@Graph at a Glance

@Graph is a macOS application for scientific data visualization, analysis, and charting. It is particularly suited to time-series measurements and has deep support for common neurophysiology file formats (ABF, AXGX, HDF5, NWB, and others) as well as fluorescence microscopy image sequences. Its five workspaces (the Data Log, Spreadsheets, Charts, Graphs and the Image Window) share data through a common flow: import → visualize → measure → chart and analyze. The omics plots are chart types, with their own page, Omics Visualization.

The Five Workspaces

  • Data Log & Text Editors: The hub of every session. Results, statistics and exported values collect here as plain text, and you can type or paste data too. Selected text can be sent to a Chart or Graph. The built-in Math Evaluator computes values inline.
  • Spreadsheets: A table editor for pasting or tidying tabular data and sending it to a Chart, Graph or Statistics. The Chart Spreadsheet edits a chart's data, the Graph Spreadsheet edits a graph's sweep data, and New Spreadsheet (⌥⌘N) handles general tables.
  • Charts: A charting workspace for summary data. Accepts data from the Data Log, a pasted table, or a Chart Spreadsheet. Basic types are Bar / Dot, Horizontal Bar, Line, Scatter (X,Y), and Pie; statistical types add Estimation Plot, Box Plot, Ridge Plot, and Kaplan-Meier Curve; and an Omics Visualization group covers Volcano Plot (with MA Plot as a display mode), Heatmap, Dot Plot, Cluster Scatter, GSEA Bar, Grouped Diverging Bar, Stacked Composition Bar, PCA Plot, Manhattan Plot, Venn Diagram (2 or 3 sets), and UpSet Plot (any number of sets, clearest with 4 or more). Effect size brackets, estimation statistics, and a full statistics engine are built in.
  • Graphs: The time-series waveform viewer. Opens ABF, AXGX, MAT, HDF5, NWB, NCS, ATF, Igor, HEKA and many other formats. Visualize, navigate, filter, baseline-correct, and measure recordings. Cursor measurements, peak detection, averaged sweeps, PSD/FFT, histogram, curve fitting, AP detection, synapse detection, and VI/IV plots all live here. Results flow automatically to the Data Log.
  • Image Window: A fluorescence imaging workspace for TIFF, CZI, LSM, ND2 and LIF files, folders of TIFFs, and Prairie View T-series and Z-stacks. Channels are shown with selectable LUTs (including Emission λ, a pseudocolor matching the fluorophore's emission peak) and display ranges. A channel's Raw checkbox shows it in plain grayscale (on by default for single-channel images), and Smoothing is off by default, so pixels are shown as recorded. Draw ROIs (rectangle, ellipse, polygon, freehand, line) or detect them automatically, correct XY drift, and extract intensity traces over time; background ROIs have a dashed border. A scale bar appears automatically when pixel size is known. Sweep Mode options are Raw Intensity, ΔF/F₀, ΔF and Z-score. Traces can be sent to a Graph window or exported as CSV. Export Image… saves PNG, JPEG, TIFF, GIF, PDF or SVG, and Export Movie… saves MP4, ProRes or a TIFF stack, both matching the display; Export Data as TIFF… saves the raw pixel values as OME-TIFF or ImageJ TIFF.

Typical Workflow

A typical session follows these steps:

  • 1. Import: Open a recording file (ABF, AXGX, HDF5, NWB and others) in Graphs, paste a data table into the Data Log or a Spreadsheet, drag a CSV onto a Spreadsheet window, or drop a TIFF or Prairie View XML onto the Data Log or Dock icon to open it in the Image Window.
  • 2. Visualize: Graphs displays time-series waveforms with full navigation and overlay controls. Image Window displays fluorescence images with LUT controls, Z-projection, and ROI overlays. Charts renders summary statistics as bar, line, pie, or estimation plots.
  • 3. Analyze: Graph measurements (cursors, peak detection, event detection) log automatically to the Data Log. Image ROI intensity traces can be background-subtracted and normalized before sending to a Graph window. Chart statistics (effect sizes with their CIs for differences in means, trimmed means, medians or ranks, then the matching tests) run from the Statistics tab.
  • 4. Draw conclusions: Effect size brackets and estimation plots (Gardner-Altman / Cumming) make statistical results visible on the chart. Export figures as PDF, SVG, PNG, JPEG, TIFF or GIF, and export data as CSV or native formats.

Common paths

  • Recording file → Graph → measure → Data Log → select → Chart Selection… → statistics + figure.
  • CSV or pasted table → Spreadsheet → Data to Chart → statistics + figure.
  • TIFF or Prairie View XML → Image Window → draw ROIs → Measure → Send to Graph → measure → Data Log → Chart Selection… → statistics + figure.
  • Any Bar/Horizontal Bar/Line/Box Plot chart → Statistics tab → Show Estimation Plot (in the EFFECT SIZE zone) → the same chart shown as a Gardner-Altman or Cumming plot; click the button again to switch back.

If @Graph Quits Unexpectedly

Your unsaved work isn't lost. While you work, @Graph keeps a recovery copy of each graph, chart, spreadsheet, Text Editor and image window with unsaved changes, and of the Data Log. An image window's copy holds its settings, ROIs, overlays and view, and the image itself is read again from its original file, so copies stay small however large the stack. The next time you open @Graph, choose Reopen to bring those windows back as they were, still marked as unsaved; windows that came from a file save back to it with ⌘S. If @Graph asks for access to an image's folder, choose it so the image can be read again. An image whose file has been moved or deleted can't be reopened, and the Data Log says so. Choose Don't Reopen to move the copies to the Trash. Recovery copies never change your own files, and they're removed when you save, close the window or quit normally.

VoiceOver and the Keyboard

@Graph works with VoiceOver. Each chart and each graph panel reads as one item that says what it shows: the axes, and each group's n, mean (or median) and spread. Press VO-Shift-Return on it to hear the data as an Audio Graph. With Keyboard navigation turned on (System Settings → Keyboard), Tab moves between controls; use the Step buttons to change sweeps. Manhattan Plots, UpSet plots, spectrograms, histograms, the Spike Sorter's views and PSD, cross-correlation and coherence results have Audio Graphs too. On a Manhattan Plot, each point names its chromosome and position. On a heatmap or dot plot, the Row labels and Column labels items let you pick a label with VO-Up and VO-Down Arrow and rename it with VO-Space.

Data Flow Summary

File → Graph → Data Log → Chart is the primary path for recording-based workflows. Image → ROI traces → Graph → Chart extends this to fluorescence imaging. For data from any source, paste a table into the Data Log or a spreadsheet and send it to a Chart.

Getting Started

@Graph is a macOS app for visualizing, analyzing and running statistics on time-series, X-Y and category data, fluorescence images and omics tables. It reads a wide range of recording formats (ABF, HDF5, Igor Pro, MATLAB and many more), shows multi-channel, multi-sweep data in graph windows, and gives you tools for measuring, filtering, fitting and exporting.

The Welcome Window

The Welcome to @Graph window greets you at launch with your recent files and buttons for Open File / Import Data, Open Data Log, New Spreadsheet and User Guide, plus Sample Graph, Sample Chart and Sample T-Series. Drop a supported file on it to open that file, or click a recent file to reopen it; a file @Graph can't open is named, and a recent file that has since been moved or deleted is said to be missing and taken off the list. Hover over "Drop a file anywhere on this window to open it" for the kinds of files @Graph reads. The window closes by itself once a file opens.

  • Open File / Import Data opens the Data Importer (see below).
  • Sample Graph, Sample Chart and Sample T-Series let you try @Graph without a file of your own. Sample Graph is a synthetic current-clamp recording with 10 sweeps in each of 3 channels (membrane potential, current command and an LED trace), including 10 MΩ of uncompensated series resistance to try Rₛ correction on. Sample Chart is a small bar chart. Sample T-Series is a simulated calcium-imaging movie of one neuron, 86 frames at 20 Hz in 12-bit camera counts with 0.5 µm pixels, built from a real recording's resting image: the cell brightens 1.6 s in (peak ΔF/F about 1), decays over the next 2 seconds and bleaches slightly, with the camera's own noise. As in the recording, the brightest part of the soma saturates at the peak, so measure ΔF/F on its edge or the dendrites. All three are the same every time, and 'Show sample data buttons' in Settings → General → Session hides them.
  • To skip the Welcome window, uncheck 'Show this window at launch' (or 'Show start-up window at launch' in Settings → General → Session). Help → Welcome to @Graph… (⌘@) brings it to the front at any time, or hides it when it's already in front. Help → @Graph User Guide (⇧⌘U) does the same for this guide.

Opening a File

  • Drag any supported file onto the Dock icon or any @Graph window, including the Welcome window. @Graph opens it in the right kind of window.
  • File → New Data Importer (⌘N) opens a drop zone that lists the importable file types by kind (Data Files, Image Files, Text / Spreadsheet Files). Drop a file on it or click Choose File….
  • File → Open File… (⌘O) opens one or more files of the same types.
  • Select tab- or comma-delimited numbers in the Data Log, then choose File → Graph Selected Text… (⇧⌘G) or Chart Selected Text… (⇧⌘H).
  • A .txt or .rtf file opens in a Text Editor window. Select the data in it and use its Text Tools menu to send it to a Graph, Chart or Spreadsheet.
  • File → New Spreadsheet (⌥⌘N) opens a blank grid. Type or paste your data, then click Data to Graph… or Data to Chart.
  • Fluorescence images (.tif/.tiff, .czi, .lsm, .nd2, .lif, a Prairie View .xml, or a folder of TIFFs) open the same ways, each in a new image window.

The Data Log

The Data Log collects every measurement, fit and analysis result as a table. You can also paste numbers into it and graph them, or use it as a simple text editor (see The Data Log).

It records from the moment @Graph starts but stays hidden until you ask for it: ⌘L (View → Show Data Log) brings it to the front with everything logged so far, and pressing ⌘L again while it's in front hides it again without losing anything. The Welcome window's Open Data Log button brings it forward too.

Type an expression followed by = (for example 2+2=) and the result appears in blue; Enter on the numeric keypad evaluates the whole line. ⌘Z removes a result like any typing. ⌘S saves the log. After Clear, the next ⌘S asks for a new file, so the last one is never overwritten.

Windows, Tabs and Sessions

Files open as tabs or as separate windows, as set in Settings → General → File Handling. Tabs keep each kind together: graphs tab with graphs, charts with charts, and images, spreadsheets and text editors each with their own kind. Window → Merge All Windows gathers every kind at once, each into its own tabbed window (the Data Log, Welcome and the User Guide stay as they are); Window → Move Tab to New Window, or dragging a tab out of the tab bar, makes a tab a window again. The E-Phys Tools menu acts on the graph window you used most recently.

Windows aren't reopened at the next launch, and each launch starts a new Data Log, hidden until you open it (save one you want to keep with ⌘S; quitting asks first if it has unsaved results). To reopen your files, turn on 'Restore last session on launch' in Settings → General → Session: each window's file comes back (for a graph, the .@graph file you saved it as, with your analysis, or else the original recording).

Every change to the data (filtering, smoothing, baseline, scale and offset) can be undone with ⌘Z and redone with ⇧⌘Z. Saving writes a .@graph file and never changes your original recording. A file kept in iCloud Drive or Dropbox is replaced in one step, coordinated with the sync.

AI Assistant

The AI Assistant is an optional chat assistant. Ask about your open recordings and results in plain language, and it can run real measurements and analyses for you (detecting action potentials, fitting curves, computing input resistance, running statistics and much more) with the same engines as the inspector panels. The numbers it reports are computed by @Graph, not guessed.

Opening the AI Assistant

Turn it on first in Settings → AI Assistant (Enable AI Assistant). Then choose View → Open AI Assistant, or press ⌥⌘I from any window. It opens in its own window, which you can keep beside your data.

Choosing Who Answers Your Questions

The AI service picker at the top of the window chooses which service answers (the AI Assistant Settings… link beside it opens Settings → AI Assistant). @Graph doesn't run a model on your Mac, so this choice decides whose servers see your questions:

  • Claude (Anthropic), ChatGPT (OpenAI), Gemini (Google) and Grok (xAI). Each sends your request to that company's servers using an API key you provide, which is why each needs a one-time opt-in (see below).
  • Apple Private Cloud Compute: Apple's own server model. It needs no API key, since it runs under your Mac's Apple Intelligence, with a daily limit set by Apple (an iCloud+ plan raises it). It needs macOS 27 or later and a Mac that supports Apple Intelligence, and it also asks for a one-time opt-in. See Using Apple Private Cloud Compute below.

Each backend has a model picker to trade capability against speed and cost: Claude offers Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5, ChatGPT offers GPT-6.1 Sol and GPT-6 Luna, Gemini offers Gemini 3.1 Pro (preview) and Gemini 3.8 Flash, and Grok offers Grok 4.7 and Grok 4.3. The second of each pair, marked "faster, cheaper", is the default and handles most questions; choose the first for harder ones. A model you chose earlier moves to its newer version when @Graph updates, or to the default if it's no longer offered. Changing the model starts a new conversation. Switching backends sets the conversation aside and brings it back when you switch back. Settings → AI Assistant → Default Backend sets the backend and model the window starts with the first time you open it after launching @Graph. Until you choose one, it's Apple Private Cloud Compute, which is free, on a Mac that can use it, and Claude otherwise.

Choosing Tool Groups

The Tools button, under the AI service pickers, lists the assistant's tools in groups: Graph Editing, Graph Transforms, Measurements, Events & Spikes, Folder Batch, Charts, Statistics (effect sizes with their tests, ANOVA, mixed models and regression), Omics Plots, Imaging, and Spreadsheets & Text. All are on at first. Every request sends the description of each tool that's on, so turning off the groups you won't need makes each request smaller: it costs less, runs faster, and leaves the assistant fewer similar tools to choose between. Beside the button is roughly how many tokens each request starts with, counted for the AI service you chose. With every group on, a Claude request starts near 100,000 tokens, so turning off unused groups saves real money. A question sends one request, plus one more for each round of tool calls. These tokens are sent with every request, but depending on the provider, repeats within a few minutes may cost much less.

The Basic Tools at the top of the list are always on: listing open windows, screenshots, the Data Log, saving results to a file, and saving, exporting, duplicating and renaming windows. Deselect All leaves only these. The groups are listed under Graphs, Charts and Statistics, and Other Windows, each with a line on what its tools do; Select All turns them all on again. Changes apply from your next question, in the current conversation too, and are remembered the next time you open @Graph. If you ask for something that needs a group that's off, the assistant tells you which one to turn on. Private Cloud Compute works a little differently: it uses one group at a time (see below).

Using Apple Private Cloud Compute

Private Cloud Compute's context window holds about 32,000 tokens for everything: the instructions, the tools, the whole conversation and each answer. So with it:

  • You choose one tool group at a time under Tools, where each group shows its size. Until you choose one, Send is off. The note beside Tools shows how much of the window the tools and instructions take.
  • Answers are short: the numbers you asked about, with the full result in the Data Log.
  • It never receives window screenshots.
  • When the conversation nears the limit, or you change the group, @Graph condenses it: the assistant keeps a short summary of the last few questions and answers, and its next reply says so. Everything stays in the chat and the Data Log.
  • Each person gets a daily number of requests, and every round of tool calls counts as one. The window shows when you're near or at the limit, with Apple's options for raising it, and the message at the limit says when it resets.
  • If a question still doesn't fit after condensing, or the service is busy or times out, the message says what to try: smaller steps, rewording, waiting a minute or New Conversation.

Opting In to Data Sharing

The first time you use a backend, a consent sheet appears; nothing is sent until you click Allow. It lists:

  • Provider: the company that receives the data.
  • Data sent: your questions, and what the assistant's tools read to answer them: measurements, statistics and detected events, and, when a tool reads one, a window's contents (a spreadsheet's cells, a text editor's text, a whole trace, a spike list). If 'Attach window screenshots' is on (off by default; the checkbox is at the top of the AI Assistant window, and in Settings → AI Assistant), pictures of the windows it works with are sent as well (never to Private Cloud Compute).
  • Purpose: the data are used to generate the analysis on the provider's servers.
  • Your API key: stored in the macOS Keychain and sent directly to the provider, never through an @Graph server (for Private Cloud Compute, that no key is needed).
  • Retention: a summary of that provider's data-retention policy, which differs between providers (for example, Google may use data sent with a free Gemini key to improve its products, but not with a paid key).
  • Allow proceeds. Cancel leaves that backend unavailable: click Ask Again to see the request again, or pick another backend.
  • 'Remember my choice for [Provider]' (on by default) remembers an Allow for that provider only, so you aren't asked again. Allowing Claude doesn't allow ChatGPT, and a Cancel is never remembered. Settings → AI Assistant → Reset Remembered Data-Sharing Consent… makes every backend ask again.

Every backend sends your questions to that company's servers (Apple's, for Private Cloud Compute; the others under your own API key), and that company's retention policy applies.

Bringing Your Own API Key

Claude, ChatGPT, Gemini and Grok each need your own API key from that company; @Graph does not supply one, and Private Cloud Compute needs none. The panel has a 'Get an API Key' link to the right page and a field to paste the key (then click Save Key). Keys are stored only in the macOS Keychain, never in an @Graph file, iCloud or an @Graph server. To remove a key, click Clear beside it in Settings → AI Assistant → API Keys.

Asking Questions and Running Analyses

Type a question and press Return (or click Send). While it works, Send becomes Stop; changes its tools made before you stop stay, and are listed. Long runs, such as spike sorting or measuring a whole folder of files, work in the background, so @Graph stays responsive meanwhile, and Stop ends them. New Conversation starts over: every request sends the whole conversation, so a long one gets slower and costs more. Copy Conversation copies the whole chat as plain text. When the answer needs a number from your data, the assistant runs the same measurement and analysis tools as the inspector panels and reports the result. For example:

  • "What's the input resistance of this cell?"
  • "Detect action potentials between 200 and 400 ms and tell me how many fired."
  • "Fit an exponential to the decay after the peak and give me tau."
  • "Run a t-test comparing these two columns." (or "Compare their medians": the assistant uses the same four Compare choices as the Statistics window)
  • "Fit a mixed model to this table and show the time course."

The Assistant can set a graph's title, axis title and tick label fonts, as it sets a chart's. Name a font family as Font Book lists it, such as Helvetica Neue, or say System for the default; a font that isn't installed is refused and nothing changes. It can also set a channel's gain (Channel Gain…), using only a gain you tell it. Spectrograms and power spectra requested from the Assistant run in the background, and Stop cancels them.

Statistics answers lead with the effect size and its CI, then p, judged at the α that matches Settings → Statistics → Default confidence level (0.05 at 95%).

For repeated measures, give it a table with one row per subject: a group column (codes such as 0 = control, 1 = treated, or labels such as Control, Treated), one column per time point, and optionally a column naming each subject's source (Values from the same). Its mixed model reports each group's rate of change and how the slopes differ, the difference in level and the difference at each time point (with simultaneous CIs), then the Group × Time test, and it can open the time-course chart with brackets. On a time-course chart it can change Time, Covariance and Values from the same, and switch the brackets to rate of change over the time points you name, with the fitted lines if you ask (⌘Z undoes it). It also runs split-plot ANOVA and two-way ANOVA (two crossed factors, such as genotype × drug). See Linear Mixed Model under Statistics.

It works in other windows too:

  • Imaging: draw, auto-detect, move, rename and delete ROIs; measure them (area, mean, SD, min, max and IntDen, as ImageJ's Measure gives them) and read an ROI's intensity trace; set a channel's LUT, color, visibility, Raw, Smoothing, Subtract or display range, or auto-contrast it (ImageJ's Auto: 0.35% of pixels saturated); move through Z and T, show the Z projection or Orthogonal Views, treat a stack as Z or T, and open an image's Export panels.
  • Omics: read an open Volcano Plot's results (each feature's log₂ fold change with its CI, then p) and change its thresholds, and log-transform a PCA Plot or Heatmap.
  • Charts: make a paired chart (the same subjects in every category) and set axis titles.
  • Spreadsheets and text: read and edit a Spreadsheet's cells or a Text Editor's text, insert or delete rows and columns, and open new Spreadsheet and Text Editor windows.

You can ask for specific options, such as the fractional derivative threshold method for AP detection or a particular channel or time window, just as you would set them in the inspector. See AP Detection in the sidebar for what 'fractional derivative threshold' means. If an option doesn't apply to what you asked for (robust SEs on a dose-response fit, or charge on a voltage recording, since charge is computed only for currents), the result says it was ignored and why. For input resistance it uses your Settings → Neurophysiology fit options unless you ask for others in the request. Its tools that read a file or folder (sag, firing properties, input resistance, cursor windows, event detection and the other folder tools) measure each file as its graph window opens it: a saved graph keeps its own settings (its IV Rₛ correction, Set X = 0 and junction potential correction), as in Batch Processing, and each result says which settings were used.

Times you give and get back are as the graph's X axis shows them, after any Set X = 0. Values and thresholds are in the units the graph shows (pA, mV), also for a file the assistant reads from disk without opening it. A spreadsheet's cells are named as on screen: rows from 1, columns by letter. Sweeps and channels are numbered from 1.

Spontaneous synaptic events are detected as the Synapse Detection panel's Continuous mode, Batch Processing and Protocols detect them: a channel's sweeps joined end to end (one by one when their sample rates differ), with Mean Frequency the events divided by the time searched. So the assistant's counts, amplitudes and frequency match theirs.

Name the window to work on as its title reads. If a name could mean more than one open window, the assistant asks rather than guessing. With only one window of a kind open, "the graph" or "the image" is enough for a question, but a change made in a window always names it. Each chart the assistant opens gets a short ID it uses to find that chart again, so two charts with the same title don't get mixed up.

Every change the assistant makes is listed in the chat in orange, with how to undo it: ⌘Z in that window for a change made in place (including a spreadsheet's cells and a text editor's text), or closing the new window it opened. Each change is its own step, so ⌘Z takes back one at a time, even when the assistant made several at once. If a request fails or is stopped, the next one tells the assistant what its tools had already changed.

Nothing is written to disk without you. A new file goes through a save panel where you pick it, saving over a file that already exists asks first (Save, Cancel or Save As…), and the export tools open the usual Export panels, where you choose the options and the file.

Text the assistant reads from your files, cells and windows is treated as data, never as instructions: only your own messages ask it to act. If a file's text seems to ask for something, the assistant tells you and asks.

If a tool's result is very long (a whole long trace, say), the assistant sees only the first part, says so, and reads a smaller range instead. If a request fails, the red message under the conversation says what to do next: check the API key, check billing on the provider's website, wait a minute and ask again, or start a New Conversation. Changes made before the failure stay, and are listed.

With 'Attach window screenshots' on, each tool that uses a graph or image window sends the assistant a picture of it, taken after the tool ran, so it sees what the tool did. A window that hasn't changed since the last picture isn't sent again.

Voice Input

Click the microphone button to dictate a question. The text appears as you speak, and recording stops when you finish or click the button again. Transcription happens entirely on your Mac, in US English. A Mac with no microphone connected says so instead of recording. The button is off while a request is running.

Saved Prompts

To save a question you ask often ("Detect APs on the visible sweep and summarize"), type it and click the filled star beside the input field. The star menu on the left lists saved prompts: pick one to put it in the input field, where you can edit or send it, or use Remove… to delete one.

Check a surprising number from the AI Assistant as you would any other measurement.

Supported File Formats

@Graph reads all of the following formats directly, with no conversion step. Drop a file onto the Dock icon or any @Graph window to open it.

Native File Format

.@graph@Graph Files
File → Save (⌘S) writes the front window as a .@graph file: a graph, chart, spreadsheet or image, which reopens in the same kind of window. A graph's file holds the data together with grouping, display settings, group order and analysis state. It is HDF5, so h5py, MATLAB and HDFView can read the data directly: each sweep is a float32 dataset in the /acquisition/ group (sweep_0000, sweep_0001, …) with unit and sample_rate_hz attributes. A raster's spike times are also stored at full (float64) precision under /atgraph_v2/spike_times/. Settings and other metadata live in the /atgraph_v2/ group, which only @Graph uses; X values and error bars are stored there as datasets, so a graph with many of them saves and opens quickly, and the X variable's name is saved too. A file saved by a later version of @Graph asks you to update before it opens. Names and units are UTF-8 text, so symbols such as µ read correctly in h5py, MATLAB and HDFView. A graph saved with Rₛ correction keeps the uncorrected data in /acquisition/, and @Graph applies the correction when it opens the file. A .@graph file is not an NWB file: the /acquisition/ group name is the only resemblance, and NWB software such as pynwb or MatNWB won't open it. To share data as NWB, use File → Export → NWB.

Read & Write

@Graph can both open and save back out to these formats:

.abfAxon Binary File (v1 & v2)
Clampex / pCLAMP recordings, ABF1 and ABF2. Samples are scaled to their recorded units, and each recorded channel opens as its own channel (a two-channel ABF opens as two channels) with its own units and name. Episodic, gap-free and event-driven recordings are read; event-driven sweeps keep their own lengths. The amplifier's telegraphed whole-cell Rₛ and Cₘ dials, filter and mode are read when the file stores them (ABF 1.8 keeps all but Rₛ), for voltage-clamp Rₛ correction and its clamp-mode check. The Data Log lists the protocol name, the file comment and the tags (comments typed during the recording) with their times. Sample rates show as whole numbers, such as 30 kHz, when the file's rate is within a part per million of one. On export, choose 32-bit (exact), written as ABF 2, or 16-bit (smaller, half the size), written as ABF 1.83 with each channel rounded to 16-bit steps across its range. The choice starts on the one that matches the graph: 16-bit when its samples are still a 16-bit ABF file's own, which it then writes back exactly. ABF holds at most 16 channels at one sample rate, with the same number of sweeps on each, and 16-bit can't hold gaps (missing values); a graph outside that is refused, with NWB or HDF5 suggested. Sweeps of different lengths share ABF's one sweep length: 32-bit pads the shorter ones with blanks, and the Data Log says so. pCLAMP, pyABF and neo read the exports.
.axgx / .axgdAxoGraph X
AxoGraph X files and AxoGraph 1 and 2 files, with every column type. Channels and episodes are read from AxoGraph's trace groups, or else from the column titles, and a time column in ms is converted. A file without a time column (a table of measurements), or whose time column isn't evenly spaced (0, 1, 2, 5, 10 min), opens as X-Y data with that column as X; the Data Log says so. Tags and interval bars marked during the recording are listed in the Data Log. AxoGraph's display settings are kept when a file is exported with the same channels and sweeps, and a recording exported as AxoGraph reads back with exactly the same values and its recording date.
.csvComma-Separated Values
Opens as a Spreadsheet window, with the first row as column titles unless it is all numbers. The file's own rows decide how it is split: at commas, at semicolons (a European CSV with decimal commas) or at tabs, as @Stat reads the same file. From there, Data to Graph… (which asks for the delimiter, header row and X-axis mode) or Data to Chart makes a graph or chart. To write a Graph as .csv, use the toolbar's Export menu.
.h5 / .hdf5HDF5
Every numeric 1-D or 2-D dataset in the file becomes a channel, wherever it is. Sample rate (sample_rate, fs, dt and the like), units and channel names are read from standard attributes; a dataset with no rate is plotted at 1 sample per second, and the Data Log says so. A file another program still has open (an acquisition in progress) is read too. On write: one float32 dataset per channel, [sweeps × samples] (row k is sweep k in h5py; NaN past a sweep's end), with units, sample_rate and the channel's name, plus sweep lengths and start times when known; a raster writes one dataset of spike times per unit under /units. Each channel keeps its own rate, so nothing is resampled, but one channel's sweeps must share a rate (NWB keeps each sweep's). @Graph reads its own export back as it was.
.matMATLAB
Reads level 4, level 5 (-v6/-v7, compressed or not) and v7.3 files: every numeric type, 1×1 structs (as struct.field) and Spike2 channel exports. The sample rate comes from a variable such as fs, srate or sample_rate, an interval such as dt, or an evenly spaced time vector; without one, X is the sample number. A [samples × sweeps] matrix is one channel of sweeps and a [channels × samples] matrix one channel per row. Spike-time vectors (names such as spike_times or ts) open as a raster. Variables on different time axes are not forced onto one: each set opens in its own window. Cell arrays and sparse matrices are skipped, and the Data Log says so. A text variable of several rows reads one line per row. On write: per channel a [samples × sweeps] matrix (NaN past a sweep's end) with <name>_sample_rate, <name>_units and, when known or needed, <name>_start_times and <name>_lengths, plus the recording's start (recording_start); a raster writes <unit>_spike_times vectors, with <unit>_spike_name (the unit's full name), <unit>_spike_win (the span recorded over) and <unit>_spike_t0 (its sweep's start). The file is level 5, which MATLAB and scipy.io.loadmat read; a recording too large for one level 5 variable (2 GB) is refused, with NWB or HDF5 suggested. @Graph reads its own export back as it was.
.nwbNeurodata Without Borders v2
Reads the time series in /acquisition/, including those inside containers such as LFP (or, when /acquisition/ has none, in /processing/): patch-clamp or extracellular, a 2-D series giving one channel per column, with the file's rate, units, conversion, offset and per-channel conversion (data × conversion × channel_conversion + offset, as pynwb). Spike times from /units open as a raster; a Units table without spike times isn't read as one. Each patch-clamp stimulus (in /stimulus/presentation) opens as a channel beside its recorded response, such as "Current stimulus (elec0)". Patch-clamp sweeps are lined up by sweep number; sweeps with no sweep number in common (voltage-clamp seal tests before current-clamp protocols, say) open in their own window. On write: an NWB 2 file that pynwb opens and validates, carrying its NWB schema so NWB tools and DANDI can check it on their own, with patch-clamp series (stimuli in the file's stimulus section, including the command an ABF file's protocol gives when no command channel was recorded), electrodes and the intracellular recordings tables, or ElectricalSeries with the electrodes table for extracellular channels, for a waveform, or the Units table for a raster, and the subject, session and electrode metadata entered in the export sheet (see Exporting). @Graph reopens both kinds of export exactly, sweep names included. The metadata of an NWB file it opens fills the sheet when you export it again.

Recording Times

Many formats record when each sweep started and when the recording was made. @Graph keeps both, and saves them in .@graph files and in its own NWB, HDF5 and MATLAB exports (an ABF export keeps the sweep starts, and the date to the millisecond).

  • Sweep start times, in seconds from the start of the recording, come from ABF, ATF, HEKA, SutterPatch, NWB, Intan, Open Ephys, Neuralynx, Blackrock, Plexon and TDT files. The first sweep can start after 0 when the file says so.
  • The recording's date and time come from ABF, AxoGraph, HEKA, SutterPatch, NWB, Open Ephys, Neuralynx, Blackrock, Plexon and TDT files. Files that store local time without a time zone are read in your Mac's time zone.
  • When a file opens, its Data Log line gives the date and time and the first and last sweep start times, or says the file records none.
  • Click a sweep to select it: the Plot Style tab of the inspector shows when it was recorded, below its name.
  • Some files record that each sweep's first sample isn't at time 0: AxoGraph times it one sample in, an Igor wave can have an X offset, and a single NWB series has a starting time. The graph opens with its X axis showing that true time, using the same shift as Set X = 0, so the data, cursors and measurements are unchanged. Set X = 0 works as usual, and Reset returns to this time. A saved .@graph reopens with the X shift it was saved with. Results that keep the recording's timing (Decimate, Interpolate, Resample, from the Graph Tools, protocols, batch processing or the AI Assistant) open with the same true time, and a result made from an open window also keeps that window's X view (see Cursors).
  • Synapse Detection's Results Over Time and time-binned summary can use the recorded sweep times instead of a typed interval (see Synapse Detection).
  • The AI Assistant's list of open windows includes each recording's date and sweep start times.

Plot Sweeps at Recording Time

View → Plot Sweeps at Recording Time draws each sweep at the time it was recorded, end to end along one time axis, instead of overlaid from 0. The X axis counts from the first sweep's start, and the time between sweeps shows as gaps. It is off by default, and the menu item is available only for a graph whose file records sweep start times.

  • It changes only the display: the data, and every analysis of it, stay the same. It is saved with a .@graph file and can be undone.
  • Zoom, pan and the hover readout work along the whole recording, and exported figures and pictures show the graph as the window does. Turning the option on or off returns the X axis to its full range.
  • Cursors work in the overlay view. While sweeps are plotted at recording time, the cursors, interval bars, trend lines and Export Visible are off, and so are the panels that use cursors: Baseline, Graph Tools, Normalize, Measure, Peaks, Curve Fitting, AP Detection, Synapse Detection and Set X = 0. Turning the option off brings them all back as they were.
  • A sweep with no recorded start time, such as a fit, isn't shown. The Data Log lists these sweeps when you turn the option on.

Read Only

@Graph opens the formats below but does not write them (TIFF aside: image windows export it, below). The resulting graphs can be saved as .@graph files or exported to any Read & Write format above.

Physiology / Neurophysiology Formats

Formats from patch-clamp, multi-electrode array (MEA) and extracellular acquisition systems. Continuous formats plot as waveforms; spike-event formats plot as a raster of spike trains. A file with parts that don't belong in one graph (streams at different rates, spikes beside continuous data, events) opens each part in its own window, titled with the part. A file with more than 4 parts (SutterPatch routines, HEKA series, streams) first asks which to open, all checked to start. A pause or gap in a recording starts a new sweep, named by its start time ("from 12.500 s"), never joined up as if continuous. A recording whose data, once loaded, would take more than about a quarter of the Mac's memory loads from its start, and a warning says how much was loaded. Formats that keep a recording in several files (Open Ephys, TDT, Intan's info.rhd and info.rhs, a HEKA .dat with a separate .pul, a Blackrock spec 2.1 .nsX with its .nev) ask for access to their folder when @Graph can't already read it.

.atfAxon Text Format
Tab-delimited text export from Clampex; a file saved with commas instead of tabs opens too. One time column (in s or ms), then one column per signal per sweep; several signals ('Trace #1 (mV)', 'Trace #1 (pA)', …) open as separate channels, each with the unit in its column header. A file whose first column isn't evenly spaced (uneven times, or a results table) opens as X-Y data with that column as X; the Data Log says so.
.ns1 – .ns6Blackrock NSx
Continuous multi-channel recordings, file specs 2.1 to 3.0 (including 3.0 with nanosecond PTP (Precision Time Protocol) timestamps, dated from the first packet), each channel scaled to its units from the header. Each pause in recording starts a new sweep; after a clock restart, sweeps are timed on from the end of the sweep before. Spec 2.1 files hold no calibration; it comes from the recording's .nev beside them, and without it the samples are raw ADC values.
.nevBlackrock / Ripple / Neuralynx NEV
Spike times, as a raster: one train per electrode and unit (unsorted, sorted units, noise), at their times on the recording's clock (for nanosecond PTP timestamps, from the file's first event). Digital-input events open as a second raster, one train per input value, for use as trial markers. A restarted recording's runs open separately. A Neuralynx .nev opens as a raster with one train per event string, and the events are listed in the Data Log.
.datHEKA / Pulse
PatchMaster data files, bundled (the usual) or with a separate .pul file, in either byte order and every sample format, including long continuous acquisitions stored interleaved and PatchMaster NEXT (1.6 and later) files. Each series opens in its own window, titled with its label. Each sweep is an episode, and each trace (Imon, Vmon…) is a channel with its own length and sample rate, with leak traces as their own channels. A file with more than 4 series asks which to open. The clamp command (constant, ramp and continuous segments, with every increment mode) is rebuilt for each sweep from the file's stimulus tree (.pgf) in mV or pA, shown as a "Stim-DA-0" channel when no trace records it, and used like an ABF protocol by VI and IV plots, Rₛ correction and the membrane test. Sine, square and chirp segments, template files and PGF parameters aren't synthesized, because HEKA's file description doesn't define their shape well enough to reproduce; the Data Log lists each series' protocol, its segments and increments, and why a command wasn't drawn. Each Imon or Vmon trace uses the command from the amplifier that recorded it, or that amplifier's holding potential or current when the protocol doesn't use its D/A output; the Data Log notes a protocol that links an input to a different output. An input no HEKA amplifier drives (a second amplifier wired to Adc5, say) shows as "Adc5 input", in volts at the input, with a Data Log note. Set its gain with Graph Utilities → Channel Gain….
.pxp / .h5xpIgor Pro Packed Experiment
Reads Igor Pro packed experiments (.pxp and the HDF5-based .h5xp). Each wave keeps its name, length, sample rate and units; waves named as a numbered series (RecordA0, RecordA1…) in one data folder become the sweeps of one channel, and a 2-D wave's columns are its sweeps. X scaling in ms is converted to seconds. SutterPatch experiments (.h5xp saved by SutterPatch with a Sutter IPA or dPatch) open one window per routine ("R1 VI_Ch1", "R2 …"): each of the routine's signals (Voltage1, Current1, AuxIN1…) is a channel at its own sample rate, each sweep keeps its start time in its name ("Sweep 2 (2.210 s)"), and SutterPatch's own settings and analysis waves are left out. Text waves, and waves that can't be read, are left out, and the Data Log counts them.
.ibwIgor Binary Wave
Reads single waves (versions 1–5, either byte order, every numeric type), both 2-D waves (rows = samples, columns = sweeps) and 1-D waves holding sweeps back to back, each ending in a NaN. Sample rate and units are read from the wave header.
.rhdIntan RHD2000
Every signal Intan records: amplifier channels (µV), auxiliary inputs, supply voltage, board ADC and digital inputs and outputs, and (in single-file .rhd recordings) temperature, each at its own rate (the auxiliary inputs at a quarter of the amplifier rate, supply and temperature once per data block). Also the "one file per signal type" and "one file per channel" formats: open their info.rhd. In files before version 3.0, the amplifier data are stored without the notch filter named in the file header. A recording that runs past the timestamp counter's limit (about 20 hours at 30 kHz) stays one sweep.
.rhsIntan RHS2000 (Stimulation/Recording Controller)
Amplifier channels (µV), DC amplifier channels when saved (V), board ADC and DAC (V), and digital inputs and outputs (0 or 1), all at the amplifier rate. Each channel that delivered stimulation also shows its current in µA ("A-000 stim"). The onsets of stimulation, compliance limit, charge recovery and amp settle open as events in a raster window; a flag that turned on together on several channels is one train. Each start of current is an event, so a biphasic pulse with an interphase delay gives one per phase. Also the "one file per signal type" and "one file per channel" formats: open their info.rhs. The header's settings, electrode impedances and notes are listed in the Data Log, and the notes become the experiment description for NWB export.
.ncs / .cscNeuralynx
One continuous channel per file, in µV from the header's scale (and sign, for an inverted input). Each stretch between gaps in the record timestamps is a sweep. Continuous channels are timed from the session's start, as the spike and event files are.
.nse / .nst / .nttNeuralynx spikes
Single electrode, stereotrode and tetrode spike files. The spikes open as a raster, one train per classified cell (cell 0 is unsorted), and their waveforms as a recording with one sweep per spike and one channel per wire, in µV. Times count from the session's .ncs files when @Graph can read their folder.
.continuousOpen Ephys (legacy format)
One file per channel. Opening one loads every channel of its processor in the folder: neural channels in µV, ADC and AUX inputs in V. Lost data and acquisition restarts start new sweeps. TTL events and messages (all_channels.events, messages.events) open as an Events raster, one train per line and direction ("TTL 1 on") followed by one per message; .spikes files open as a Spikes raster. Times line up with the continuous sweeps, and messages are also listed in the Data Log. Each .spikes file's waveforms open as a recording too, one sweep per spike and one channel per wire, in µV, scaled by the channels' bitVolts. (The gain stored in the file is rounded by Open Ephys and reads 2.6% high, so it isn't used.)
.oebinOpen Ephys (Binary format)
Open the recording's structure.oebin. Each stream (a probe's AP and LFP bands, an NI-DAQ board…) opens in its own window, read from continuous/<stream>/continuous.dat, with sweeps split where the sample numbers jump. A Neuropixels SYNC channel opens on its own. The recording's TTL events and Message Center text open as an Events raster, timed on the clock of the stream they were recorded with, and the Spike Detector's spikes as a Spikes raster, one train per electrode and unit. Each electrode's waveforms open as a recording, one sweep per spike (starting before its peak by the detector's pre-peak samples) and one channel per wire, in µV.
.plxPlexon
Continuous channels (one window per rate: wideband at 40 kHz, field potentials at 1 kHz…), sorted spike times as a raster, and events (including strobed values) as another, scaled as each file version specifies. If the header's gains give an implausible input range (placeholders left by a file converter), the load warning says so and JP Correction is off for the file.
.pl2Plexon PL2 (OmniPlex)
Plexon hasn't published this format, so @Graph can't read it directly. Convert the file to .plx with Plexon's free PlexUtil, or to NWB with NeuroConv, and open the converted file. Opening a .pl2 shows these steps.
.tsqTDT block
Open a block's .tsq. Stream stores (from its .tev, or their own .sev files) open one window per rate, float stores in volts and integer stores as recorded; snippets as a raster of spike times by channel and sort code, and epocs as another. One block per file opened.

Fluorescence Image Formats

Open images by dropping them onto the Dock icon or any @Graph window, or with File → Open File… (⌘O). They always open in a new image window. A file holding more than 4 images (scenes, positions or series) asks which to open, all checked to start. To write the pixel values back out, use the image window's Export menu → Export Data as TIFF…, which saves every channel, Z slice and time point as OME-TIFF or ImageJ TIFF, with pixel size, Z step, frame times and channel names and colors, for Fiji or Python (see Imaging Analysis).

.tif / .tiffTIFF, ImageJ and OME-TIFF
8-, 16- or 32-bit integer samples (signed or not), packed 9- to 15-bit samples (a camera's 10- and 12-bit stills), and 32- or 64-bit floating point, uncompressed or LZW, Deflate or PackBits, including BigTIFF; an RGB image opens as three channels, without its alpha (transparency). 1-bit, 16-bit floating-point, JPEG and zstd TIFFs are refused with a message. A palette (indexed-color) image opens as its red, green and blue, except an ImageJ image with a lookup table, which keeps its values as one channel in the table's color. A plain multi-page TIFF opens as a T-series, and the Data Log says so (or a Z-stack with 'Treat T-series as Z-stack'); a stack saved by tifffile (Python, napari) opens with its channels, Z and T. A plain ImageJ stack opens as a Z-stack, as in ImageJ, unless it records a frame interval and no Z spacing; 'Treat Z-stack as T-series' switches it. ImageJ hyperstacks and OME-TIFF open with their channels, Z and T, and their calibration (an OME-TIFF's per-plane times included); an OME-TIFF describing several images opens each in its own window. ImageJ composites open in their saved channel colors, and ImageJ and Fiji TIFFs with their saved display range.
.xml + .tifPrairie View
Prairie View 4 and 5 T-series and Z-series: the .xml and the TIFF files beside it. Drop the .xml file or its folder; @Graph asks for access to the folder if it can't already read it. Bit depth, pixel size, Z step (all Z devices added up), the time between time points and channel names are read from the XML. A bidirectional Z-series is put in one direction.
folderTIFF folder
A folder containing TIFF files is opened as a T-series, in natural name order (img2 before img10). Files holding several pages each open with every page, in file then page order. A folder of ImageJ Z-stacks, one per time point, opens as a Z-stack over time, and a folder holding an OME-TIFF set opens as that set; files with their own channels are refused (open one of them instead). A folder saved by Fiji takes its pixel size from the first file. Enable 'Treat T-series as Z-stack' in the Channels panel to reinterpret as a Z-stack.
.cziZeiss CZI
Zeiss confocal format from LSM 800 / 900 / 980 / Axioscope / Airyscan systems. Multi-channel, multi-Z, multi-T, including combined T×Z acquisitions, uncompressed or with ZEN's lossless (zstd) compression. Each scene opens as its own image, tile scans are stitched at their recorded positions, and a color camera's exposure (DAPI, GFP, DIC…), recorded in red, green and blue, opens as one channel, their sum, in the color that holds its signal (gray when none does); Settings ▸ Imaging can keep the three colors as channels instead, for a stained brightfield image. 8- or 16-bit. A line scan along Z (an XZ scan) opens as one image per time point with Z down the vertical axis, as ZEN shows it. Channel colors, names and voxel sizes (X, Y and Z) are read from embedded XML metadata, and channels open in their recorded colors. JPEG XR, JPEG and LZW compression, and floating-point pixels, are refused with a message saying how to save the file instead.
.lsmZeiss LSM
LSM 510 / 710 / 780 / 880 files: multi-channel Z-stacks, time series and T×Z, 8-bit, 12-bit or floating point, including files over 4 GB. Channel names and colors, voxel sizes (X, Y and Z) and the time between time points are read from the file. Line scans open as one image with time down the vertical axis, X-Z scans with Z down it. Only the first position of a multi-position file opens, and the Data Log says so. Channels open in their recorded colors.
.nd2Nikon ND2
NIS-Elements' native format, from NIS-Elements 3 on. Each XY position of a multipoint acquisition opens as its own image, named as in NIS-Elements. Multi-channel, multi-Z and multi-T, including a full Z-stack at every time point; the separate Z and T controls above the image (see Imaging Analysis) let you move through both. 8- or 16-bit, uncompressed or losslessly compressed; a color camera's exposure (DAPI, GFP, DIC…), recorded in red, green and blue, opens as one channel, their sum, in the color that holds its signal (gray when none does); Settings ▸ Imaging can keep the three colors as channels instead, for a stained brightfield image. Channel names (or, when every channel has the same description, as a color camera's do, the optical configurations), emission wavelengths and pixel size (X, Y and Z-step) are read from the file when present, and channels open in their recorded colors. Lossy (JPEG 2000) and floating-point files are refused with a message.
.lifLeica Image File
Leica LAS X format. Every image series in the file opens in its own window, titled with the file and series names. Multi-channel (including color camera images), Z-stacks, time-lapse and combined T×Z, 8 to 16-bit. Channel names (the dye, when the file records one), colors, pixel size (X, Y and Z) and frame interval are read from the file. FLIM acquisitions (FALCON / STELLARIS) add the images LAS X computed as extra channels, hidden at first: Intensity, Fast Flim (ps), Standard Deviation (ps) and Phasor Intensity. Spectral (λ) scans, floating-point series, the phasor coordinates and the raw photon data are not read.

Spreadsheet and Omics Matrix Formats

.xlsxExcel Workbook
Opens as a Spreadsheet window. Only cell values are read (not charts, images, drawings or formulas). Each number is its stored value, so a cell formatted as 45% is 0.45. Cells formatted as dates or times show as 2026-10-05, 14:30:00 or both, as Excel shows them (1900 and 1904 date systems), with milliseconds when they have any (14:30:00.250); elapsed times such as [h]:mm:ss stay numbers, a count of days (36:00:00 is 1.5). Error values (#N/A, #DIV/0!) show as Excel shows them and count as missing values. Hidden sheets aren't offered. Excel's escaped characters (_x000D_ for a line break) are read as the characters. Large sheets of several hundred thousand cells open; a sheet whose cells span more than 25 million (a stray value far from the data, usually) reports an error. A workbook with more than one sheet asks which sheets to open; several open as tabs of one window (see Opening Files from the Data Log). Password-protected workbooks are not supported and report an error.
.numbersApple Numbers Document
Opens as a Spreadsheet window, same data-cells-only scope as .xlsx. Dates show as an Excel workbook's do (2024-01-15 or 2024-01-15 13:30:00). A document with more than one table asks which ones to open, listing each table's size; several open as tabs of one window. A document last saved by an old version of Numbers, whose cell storage differs, reports an error (open it in a current Numbers and save it again).
.dtaStata Data File
Stata 7 and later (formats 110 to 119). Opens as a Spreadsheet window with the variable names as the header row. Numbers keep full precision, value labels are shown as their text, dates and times show as dates (%tw, %tm, %tq and %th in Stata's own 2024w12 style), and missing values (., .a to .z) are blank cells. Variable labels and notes are not imported. Files from Stata 6 or earlier: save them again from a newer Stata.
.pzfxGraphPad Prism (XML)
Prism's XML project format. Each data table opens as a Spreadsheet window; a file with several asks which to open, listing each table's kind (XY, Column, Grouped and so on) and size, and several open as tabs of one window. Columns are the X values, row titles and every data set's subcolumns, named as the R package pzfx names them (Control_1, Control_2 for replicates; Control_MEAN, Control_SD, Control_N for entered means). Values excluded in Prism are left blank, since Prism leaves them out of its analyses. Graphs, results and layouts are not read.
.prismGraphPad Prism 10 and Later
Prism's current project format. Read the same way as .pzfx, with the same column names: each data table (XY, Column, Grouped, Survival and the others) opens as a Spreadsheet window, and a file with several asks which to open (several open as tabs of one window). Entered CVs and high/low limits come in as Prism shows them. Values excluded in Prism are left blank. Graphs, analysis results, layouts and info sheets are not read. A damaged or incomplete file reports an error rather than opening with wrong values.
.gctGene Cluster Text (Broad Institute)
Versions 1.2 and 1.3. Opens as a Heatmap window with row and sample names (version 1.3's row and column fields aren't shown). The file must have as many rows and samples as its header declares. For sample class labels, select the .cls file with the same name together with the .gct and open both: each sample's class is added to its name. A .cls file on its own has no values to plot.
.txtGEO Series Matrix (NCBI)
A GEO series matrix file (…_series_matrix.txt) is recognized by its content and opens as a Heatmap window, with the samples named by their titles. GEO serves it compressed (.txt.gz): decompress it first.
.loomLoom
HDF5-based single-cell expression matrix. Opens as a Heatmap window, genes as rows and cells as columns. Use 'Hide all-zero rows/columns' in the Plot Style panel for sparse matrices.
.h5adAnnData (H5AD)
AnnData's HDF5-based single-cell format, dense or sparse (CSR or CSC). Opens as a Heatmap window, genes as rows and cells as columns. Gene and cell names are read whether stored as text, as nullable text or as categories. A file holding several matrices (X, raw/X, layers such as counts) asks which to open, with X, the main one, selected; raw counts are usually in layers/counts or raw/X. Files from anndata before 0.7 open too.

A heatmap is built from at most 100 million values, and a table of more than 3,000 rows asks before it is drawn. A single-cell file of tens of thousands of genes by tens of thousands of cells is over the limit: save a subset first, such as the highly variable genes or a sample of the cells. Loom and h5ad files compressed with gzip (the usual) are read; LZF or Blosc compression isn't.

General Text Formats

.rtfRich Text Format
Opens in a Text Editor window, as a .txt file does, with its formatting removed. Save (⌘S) asks for a new file name, so the original keeps its formatting.
.txt / .tsvPlain text / TSV
A .tsv file opens as a Spreadsheet, like .csv. A .txt (or .text) file opens in a Text Editor window named after the file, and Save (⌘S) writes back to it. Text in UTF-8, UTF-16, Windows-1252 or Mac OS Roman reads correctly, and a file over 20 MB asks before it opens. To plot a table in it, select the table and use Text Tools (Graph Selection…, Chart Selection…, Send to Spreadsheet… or Smart Visualization…). A GEO series matrix .txt opens as a Heatmap instead (above). To write text from a Graph, export as .csv; a Text Editor window's Save (⌘S) writes whatever plain-text extension you type.

The Data Log & Text Editors

The Data Log is @Graph's central hub: a plain-text window that collects every measurement result, statistical output and exported value from the other workspaces. It is also a simple text editor where you can type, paste and edit data. Any numeric table in a text window can be selected and sent to a Chart, a Graph or a Spreadsheet.

  • Open with ⌘L, or View → Show Data Log. ⌘L brings the Data Log to the front, and when it's already in front, ⌘L (View → Hide Data Log) hides it.
  • Results are always added at the end; nothing is overwritten. When the log grows past about 5 MB, @Graph offers to save it to a file and start a fresh one (it's cleared only after the save succeeds).
  • Save (⌘S) and Save As (⇧⌘S) keep it as a text file, the toolbar's Clear button empties it (after asking), and Edit → Find (⌘F, ⌥⌘F to replace, ⌘G for the next match) searches it. File → Print… (⌘P) prints it, and File → Duplicate (⇧⌘K) copies it into a new Text Editor window.
  • ⌘= and ⌘− make the text larger or smaller, and ⌘0 returns to the default size set in Settings → General → Data Log.
  • Lines are kept whole, so wide tables stay lined up: scroll sideways to read them, or turn on the toolbar's Wrap Lines button to wrap long lines to the window's width (a logged table's rows are then cut off at the edge, to keep their columns lined up).
  • Drop a supported file onto the Data Log to open it (see Opening Files from the Data Log, below).
  • Select a tab- or comma-delimited table and choose Text Tools → Chart Selection… (⇧⌘H) to open it as a Chart.
  • The built-in Math Evaluator computes expressions inline (see Math Evaluator, below).
  • New text editor windows (File → New Text Editor, ⇧⌘N) have the same Text Tools as the Data Log.

Opening Files from the Data Log

Drag any supported file onto the Data Log window to open it. Drag-and-drop, File → Open File… (⌘O) and the New Data Importer window (⌘N) all accept the same file types.

  • Recordings open as a new Graph: .abf, .axgx, .axgd, .pxp, .h5xp, .ibw, .atf, .dat, .mat, .nwb, .h5, .hdf5, .ncs, .csc, .nse, .nst, .ntt, .rhd, .rhs, .continuous, .oebin, .ns1–.ns6, .nev, .plx, .tsq (Plexon .pl2 files need converting to .plx or NWB first). Spike-event files (.nev, and the spikes in .plx and TDT blocks) open as a raster of spike trains rather than a continuous waveform; a file with several parts opens each in its own window, and asks which to open when it has more than 4 (see File Formats).
  • Opening a file that's already open brings its window forward, including one still loading. A window counts as the file it was opened from or last saved as; a duplicate or a result window (an average, Delete Sweeps, a power spectrum) only carries the file's name, so the file opens in a new window.
  • With Open files: Tabs, each kind of window tabs with its own kind: a recording opened over an image opens as a window of its own, and an image over a graph. File menu items that don't apply to the window in front (Save in the AI Assistant, Export in a Text Editor) are dimmed.
  • A graph's data table (the Spreadsheet button) is one window per graph, brought back when you click again, and closes with the graph. ⌘S and ⇧⌘S there save the graph. Tool windows (Spectrogram, Cross-Correlation, LFP Oscillations, Slow AHP, Spike Sorter) name the graph as it is called now, after a rename or Save As, in their results and Data Log lines.
  • Image files open as a new Image Window: .tif, .tiff, .czi, .lsm, .nd2, .lif, a Prairie View .xml, or a folder of TIFFs.
  • Omics matrix files open as a new Heatmap window with row and column names and values filled in: .gct (Gene Cluster Text; open it together with its .cls file for sample classes), a GEO series matrix .txt, .loom, and .h5ad (AnnData).
  • .gct: when the Name column holds database IDs (Ensembl, Entrez, probe IDs) and the Description column, or a version 1.3 symbol field, holds gene symbols, rows are labeled with the symbols; a symbol shared by several probes is shown as "symbol (ID)", and a row without one keeps its ID. A Description column that isn't used is mentioned when the file opens. In the .cls file, labels that are the class names themselves are used as they are; numeric labels take the class names in order of first appearance.
  • GEO series matrix: columns are named by sample title, with the GSM accession added when two samples share a title. An RNA-seq series matrix holds sample information but no expression values, since GEO keeps the counts in the series' supplementary files; @Graph says so and names the supplementary files the series lists, so you can download the counts table and open it.
  • .h5ad: a file can hold several versions of the data. When it has more than one, @Graph asks which to open: X, the main matrix, often normalized and log-transformed (the default); raw/X, which often has every gene; or a layer such as layers/counts, usually raw counts (integer matrices are marked "likely raw counts"). Gene names come from a gene-symbol column when the file has one (feature_name, gene_symbols, gene_symbol, symbol or gene_name), otherwise from the gene index, often Ensembl IDs. A gene with no symbol keeps its index name, and repeated names get -1, -2 and so on, as in scanpy. A note after opening says which matrix and which name column were used.
  • .loom: gene names come from the Gene row attribute, or Accession when there is no Gene, and a note after opening says which; repeated names are made unique as for .h5ad.
  • Spreadsheet files (.xlsx, .numbers) open as a Spreadsheet window (data cells only, not embedded charts or images). So do Stata data files (.dta) and Prism projects (.pzfx and .prism), one window per data table. A file with several sheets or tables asks which to open (below).
  • Delimited files (.csv, .tsv) open as a Spreadsheet. Text files (.txt, .text, .rtf) open in a Text Editor window named after the file, with an RTF file's formatting removed. To plot their data, select it and use Text Tools (Graph Selection…, Chart Selection…, Send to Spreadsheet… or Smart Visualization…). A text file over 20 MB asks before it opens.
  • Save (⌘S) in a Text Editor opened from a .txt file writes back to that file. Text from an .rtf file is saved as a new plain-text file, so the original keeps its formatting.
  • @Graph documents (.@graph) reopen in the kind of window they were saved from: Graph, Chart, Spreadsheet or Image.

Files dropped onto the Dock icon or any other @Graph window open the same way, except an empty spreadsheet, which loads a dropped table or recording into its own cells.

Choosing Sheets and Tables

When a workbook or Prism file holds several sheets or tables, @Graph lists them with a checkbox each, with the table's size and kind when the file states them. The first is checked, so Return opens it right away.

  • Check the ones you want, or click Select All (⌘A). Select All shows a dash when only some are checked; clicking it then checks them all.
  • Click Open (its title counts them, such as Open 3 Sheets): one opens as its own Spreadsheet window, several open as tabs of one window, in the file's order.
  • Space checks or unchecks the highlighted row (move with the arrow keys), Return opens, and Escape cancels.
  • Double-click a row to open just that one.
  • Opening many shows a small progress panel; Stop keeps the ones already open. Any that can't be opened (an empty sheet, say) are listed together at the end.

Automatic Logging

Every measurement (Measure, Peak Analysis, AP Detection, curve fitting, statistics, sag analysis, VI/IV plots, synapse detection) writes its results to the Data Log automatically, in order.

Sweep Numbers in Output

By default, result tables include a Sweep column only when the measured sweeps aren't an evenly spaced run (such as sweeps 1, 2, 5). For a run such as 1–10 (or every 2nd sweep) the column is left out. Turn on the 'Show Sweep Names' button in the Data Log toolbar to always include it.

Graphing Pasted Data

  • Type or paste tab-, comma-, semicolon- or space-separated numbers into the Data Log.
  • Select the numbers (optionally with column headers).
  • Choose File → Graph Selected Text… (⇧⌘G).
  • Set the delimiter, header row and X-axis mode, then click Graph Data. With 'Y columns only', give the time between samples with its unit (such as 0.05 ms or 50 µs), and the sample rate is worked out from it.

The delimiter is detected automatically, so data pasted from a spreadsheet, a CSV file or space-separated output needs no reformatting. Values copied as a spreadsheet shows them are read as numbers: "45%" is 45, and "$1,234.50" or French Excel's "1 234,5" is 1234.5. Error values such as #N/A and #DIV/0! are missing values.

For grid-based entry, use File → New Spreadsheet (⌥⌘N). Paste a table into it, then click Data to Graph… or Data to Chart.

Math Evaluator

Type an expression on a line (such as '3.14 * 25^2' or 'sqrt(144)') and press Enter on the numeric keypad (Return just starts a new line). The result appears on the next line in blue. You can use + − * / ^ (power) and %, parentheses, functions such as sqrt(), sin(), cos(), tan(), ln() and log() (natural log), log10(), exp(), abs(), round(), min(), max() and pow(), and the constants pi (or π), tau (2π) and e, written in lowercase. You can paste the signs × · ÷ and −. Write every multiplication: 2*x, not 2x.

You can also type an expression followed by '=' anywhere in a line (such as '88*3=' or 'The value is 37*81=') and the result appears in blue right after the '='. Text you type afterward is in the normal color. Whole numbers below 10^15 show in full; other results show 13 significant digits. A lone number and anything shaped like a date (2024-05-01, 1/2/2024, or a number with a leading zero such as 05) get no result, with = or with keypad Enter, so a column of numbers typed on the keypad just starts new lines. The calculator also gives no result rather than a wrong one when it can't tell where the expression starts: for a lone constant ('pi='), for an expression that follows a number, an operator or a unit ('5 mV * 2=', 'dose 1 000 * 3='), and on a line longer than 1,000 characters. Keypad Enter evaluates the whole line. Operators, functions and constants are those of Formula fits (Curve Fitting), plus tau (2π); see there for how ^, % and round behave.

Text Tools

The Text Tools menu has actions that open the selection in a new window, then text-transform utilities:

  • Graph Selection… (⇧⌘G): opens the selected text as a graph (time-series / sweep data).
  • Chart Selection… (⇧⌘H): opens the selected text as a bar, line, pie, or estimation chart.
  • Send to Spreadsheet…: opens the selected table in a new Spreadsheet window, its first row as column names when it names them.
  • Omics Plot: Volcano Plot… (⌥⌘L), Heatmap Selection… (⌥⌘M), Dot Plot Selection…, Cluster Scatter Selection…, GSEA Bar Selection…, Compute GSEA (Permutation)…, Grouped Diverging Bar Selection…, Venn Diagram Selection…, UpSet Plot Selection… and Manhattan Plot Selection…. See Omics Visualization for what each one needs.
  • Kaplan-Meier Curve Selection…: a survival curve from a Time / Status / Group table.
  • Smart Visualization… (⇧⌘V): weighs the evidence in the data's shape, values and headers for each destination: whether it suits a Graph, a Chart, a Scatter (X,Y) chart, a Kaplan-Meier curve or one of the omics plots, and opens that window. It opens the best one only when the evidence clearly favors it. For a close call it asks "What Kind of Data Is This?", names its best guesses, and offers a menu of every destination with the best guess already chosen, so Return opens it. The omics plots, Scatter, Manhattan and Kaplan-Meier read tables separated by tabs, commas or semicolons.
  • Smart Visualization examples: a recording or a column of samples that follow on from one another opens as a Graph, and one column of separate measurements as a Chart of their spread; two measures on different scales (height and weight) open as a Scatter, and two groups on one scale as a Chart; a time and status table headed so (Time, Event, Group) opens as a Kaplan-Meier curve; and long-form two-factor data with replicates (Genotype, Treatment, Response) opens as a grouped Chart.
  • Smart Visualization of long tables: a long table whose text columns are a group and a column named like where each row came from (Animal, Batch, Subject, Site…), with a value column, opens as a Chart read One Row per Value. The Statistics tab then suggests that column under Values from the same, and applies it only when you choose. Two real factors (Genotype and Treatment) still open as a Grouped Chart, which offers the One Row per Value reading if one of its factors looks like where each row came from.
  • Statistics… (⌘T): opens the Statistics window with the selected text loaded.
  • Convert Lists / Table (⇧⌘L): converts between blank-line-separated lists and a tab-delimited table.
  • Transpose Table (⇧⌘X or ⌃⇧X): swaps rows and columns of the selected tab-delimited table. In a text window ⇧⌘X works only when text is selected (elsewhere it opens a graph's Data Transform panel); ⌃⇧X always works.
  • Reverse List Order (⇧⌘R): reverses the order of rows.
  • List Stats (⇧⌘T): computes mean, SD, SEM and count for each column and adds the result inline. Decimal commas (1,5) are read as they are elsewhere in @Graph, and NA or other text cells are left out.

For separate logs for separate experiments, use File → New Text Editor (⇧⌘N) to open an independent text window.

Spreadsheets

@Graph has three spreadsheets: the Graph Spreadsheet (attached to a graph window), the Chart Spreadsheet (attached to a chart window), and the standalone New Spreadsheet window.

In all three, drag the line between two column headers to make the column on its left wider or narrower (the table scrolls sideways when it outgrows the window), or double-click the line to fit the column to its contents. Widths stay as you set them while the window is open, and stay with their columns when others are inserted or deleted. A row grows to show every line of a cell typed over several lines (Option-Return starts a new line while you edit), and the other rows keep their usual height.

Graph Spreadsheet

Click the spreadsheet toolbar button (grid icon) in a graph window to open an editable table of the sweep data. The first column is time; each other column is one sweep.

  • Double-click a cell to edit it. Press Return to commit and move to the next row. A decimal comma is read; text that isn't a number is refused and the cell keeps its value. An empty cell is a gap.
  • Click a column header to select the column, or a row number in the left gutter to select the row. Shift-click to extend, ⌘-click to add or remove one cell. Right-click a header for Copy Column, Paste Into Column (one value per line; a heading on the first line is skipped) or Clear Column (blanks the column, as Delete does).
  • Copy (⌘C) first asks whether to include headers and whether to round to a fixed number of decimal places; otherwise each value is copied exactly as stored.
  • Paste (⌘V) pastes tab-separated text at the selected cell, or the first data cell with nothing selected (a single value fills every selected cell; a heading row, as Copy with headers writes, is skipped). If the block is larger than the grid, the data grows to fit, with new sweeps as columns and new samples as rows, empty until filled. Columns can't be added while one channel of several is shown, and cells that aren't numbers are pasted blank. Either way @Graph says what was left out.
  • Delete clears the selected cells.
  • All edits can be undone with ⌘Z (a paste or Delete restores each column it changed, and the original grid size if the paste enlarged it).

Edits change the graph's data directly. Save with ⌘S to keep them in the .@graph document.

Chart Spreadsheet

Click the spreadsheet button (⊞) in a chart window's toolbar to edit the chart data as a grid, in the orientation it was pasted or typed: usually each column one category (group) and each row one replicate.

  • Double-click a cell to edit it. Press Return to commit. Delete clears the selected cells in one undoable step.
  • The Paste button replaces the whole table with the clipboard data, growing the grid to fit (a 50-row table pasted into a 5-row chart gives 50 rows). For more than one row, @Graph first asks whether the first row holds column titles. ⌘V pastes into the selected cells.
  • The Copy button copies the whole table as tab-delimited text, and ⌘C copies the selected cells.
  • Fill: drag one of the dots on the edges of a cell (or of a selected block) to copy it into the cells you drag over, or use Edit › Fill Down (⌘D) and Fill Right (⌘R), as in New Spreadsheet below.
  • Insert or delete rows and columns from the menu (the arrow) on each column header or row number. To delete several at once, select them (Shift-click or Command-click the headers or row numbers, or select a range of cells), then click the arrow on one of them and choose Delete Selected Rows or Delete Selected Columns; with several selected, the Insert items add that many at once, for example Insert 3 Columns Before. Undo brings them all back.
  • Double-click a column header to rename it; Option-Return starts a new line, which the chart stacks under the bar. The header grows to show a stacked name on all its lines.
  • Undo (⌘Z) / Redo (⇧⌘Z): up to 50 levels.
  • Transpose: swaps rows and columns in this table view only; the chart's data doesn't change. Handy if the table appears sideways compared with how you typed it.
  • Columns Are Conditions, for a label column followed by several number columns: OFF (default) treats each row as a group and its numbers as replicate values; ON treats each column as a condition and each row as one subject (repeated measures). @Graph cannot always tell these apart, so you choose. Toggling rebuilds the chart from your original paste. When the column headers look like distinct condition names, @Graph asks once when the chart first opens and remembers your answer for that chart.
  • Each Column Is a Category, for all-number data with no row of column names: ON treats each column as its own group; OFF treats the first column as row labels and the other columns as replicate values.
  • Charts whose data isn't a table of groups (Volcano, Dot Plot, Cluster Scatter, GSEA Bar, Grouped Diverging Bar, Stacked Bar, Kaplan-Meier, Manhattan and Scatter) don't show Transpose or the two switches above. Heatmap and PCA Plot show a simpler matrix table.

New Spreadsheet (standalone)

File → New Spreadsheet (⌥⌘N) opens a blank 20 × 20 grid in its own window, titled 'Spreadsheet 1', 'Spreadsheet 2' and so on until you save it. Row 1 and column A start out marked as headers; change this from the menu on a column header or row number.

  • When the grid is empty, it shows how to start: drop a data file onto the window, or paste data with ⌘V.
  • Drop a text table (.csv, .tsv, .txt, .rtf) onto an empty spreadsheet to load its cells as they are, text columns included, the first row a header when it names the columns; or a recording (.abf, .axgx, .mat, .h5, .dat, .ncs, .ibw, .atf, …) to load one column per sweep, beside a Time column. Dropping onto a spreadsheet that already has data opens a new spreadsheet window. A GEO series matrix file opens as a Heatmap instead, and workbooks (.xlsx, .numbers, .prism, .pzfx, .dta), .@graph documents and other files open in their own windows.
  • Click a cell to select it (it becomes the paste anchor). Shift-click another cell, or drag, to select a rectangle. Command-click a cell or a row number to add it to the selection or remove it.
  • Click a column header (A, B, C…) or a row number to select the whole column or row; Shift-click to extend.
  • Paste (⌘V): pastes at the selected cell, or at A1 when nothing is selected, blank cells included. Tab-separated text keeps its columns; text without tabs is split at semicolons or commas when most lines have them (commas separate cells, so use tabs or semicolons with decimal commas between columns). One column of decimal-comma numbers stays one column when a line has no comma (a heading such as "Weight", or a whole number), or when your Mac uses the decimal comma. The grid grows to fit and is never smaller than 20 × 20.
  • Copy (⌘C): copies the selection, or all non-empty data if nothing is selected (or everything is), as tab-delimited text.
  • Fill: point at a cell that holds something and a dot appears in the middle of each edge.
