Drag a dot down, up, left or right to copy the cell into the cells you drag over; with the pointer over a selected block, the dots fill with the whole block, repeated. Edit › Fill Down (⌘D) copies the top row of a selected block into the rest of it, or the row above into a single selected row; Fill Right (⌘R) does the same from the left. Formulas move their references as Copy and Paste do ($ parts stay fixed); values are copied as they are, without continuing a series. One Undo reverses a fill.
  • Insert or delete rows and columns from the menu (the arrow) on each column header or row number, which also sets a header row or column. To delete several at once, select them (Shift-click or Command-click the headers or row numbers, or select a range of cells), then click the arrow on one of them and choose Delete Selected Rows or Delete Selected Columns; with several selected, the Insert items add that many at once, for example Insert 3 Columns Before. Undo brings them all back. Deleting header rows or columns shrinks the header block.
  • Undo (⌘Z) / Redo (⇧⌘Z) and the toolbar's Undo and Redo: up to 50 levels (fewer for a very large sheet), header markings included. While you are typing in a cell, ⌘Z undoes the typing first.
  • Data to Graph…: opens the Graph Selected Text dialog for the table or the selected cells. Set the delimiter, header row and X-axis mode, then click Graph Data.
  • Data to Chart: opens a chart window, named after the spreadsheet, such as 'Spreadsheet 1 Chart'. A column title typed over several lines (common in Prism and Excel files) keeps its lines: the chart stacks them under the bar, as Prism does, while statistics, reports and the Data Log show the name on one line. Kaplan-Meier groups, heatmap and dot plot row names, cluster, series, category and gene-set names, and Venn and UpSet set names stack the same way (labels drawn at an angle, such as heatmap column names, stay on one line).
  • Omics Plot and Kaplan-Meier (toolbar): build those charts from the sheet or the selection. Transpose Table (toolbar) swaps rows and columns, and can be undone.
  • Sort (toolbar, or a column's dropdown): sorts the rows by the selected column. Whole rows move, header rows stay put, numbers come before text, and blanks go last. A formula that reads its own row keeps working; one that reads another row would read different cells, so @Graph asks first. Undo restores the order. A chart's Data window has the same Sort, and the chart redraws; sorting by X gives a scatter line that rises. A Volcano table has no Sort, as its rows are tied to their points.
  • Data Statistics opens the Statistics window with the spreadsheet data. @Graph detects whether groups are in rows or columns and asks if unsure.
  • To use only part of the table, select a rectangle of more than one cell before Data to Graph…, Data to Chart or Data Statistics. A single selected cell doesn't count, so the whole sheet is sent. A header row or column you have set is always included, so the data keeps its labels. A chart made from a selection keeps reading the same range of cells as you edit the sheet (by position: after inserting or deleting rows or columns, make it again).
  • Right-click a multi-cell selection for Chart Selected Cells, Statistics on Selected Cells and Graph Selected Cells (labeled with the selection's size).
  • The Export menu (next to Data Statistics) has Copy as PDF and Send to Keynote…, which draw the sheet (or the selection) as a clean table image with bold headers and grid lines, plus Export Data…: the sheet (or the selection) as CSV, TSV or an Excel workbook (.xlsx), from a sheet on the window (also File → Export…, ⇧⌘E). The Excel file keeps numbers as numbers, with the header row in bold and kept in view; values are exported as shown, not formulas. With Numbers installed, check Open in Numbers after exporting to open the file there too, or click Open in Numbers to skip the file: Numbers opens the table as a new spreadsheet (from an Excel workbook it converts), and nothing is saved until you choose File → Save in Numbers, which keeps it as a Numbers spreadsheet. Copy as PDF and Send to Keynote… are also on the right-click menu, for the selection or the whole sheet. A chart's data window (its spreadsheet) has Export… in its toolbar too (File → Export…, ⇧⌘E), for the table as it shows it, with the same formats and Open in Numbers.
  • Copy as PDF and Send to Keynote… warn first when the table is larger than about 60 rows or 15 columns, since the text would be too small to read on a slide. Select a smaller range, or continue anyway.
  • Save (⌘S) saves the spreadsheet as a .@graph file, header rows and columns included, and Save As (⇧⌘S) chooses a new name or location. The same file opens in @Stat.
  • A saved spreadsheet opens like any other .@graph file (drop it on the Dock, the app or a window) and reopens as a spreadsheet window.
  • Clear All: resets to a blank 20 × 20 grid (undoable).

Column headers in the first row (such as 'Voltage (mV)' and 'Current (pA)') give the channel names and units when you use Data to Graph…. Leave the first row blank if your data has no headers.

Formulas

Type = to start a formula, as in Excel or Numbers: =SUM(B2:B20), =AVERAGE(B:B), =B2/$B$1, =IF(C2>0,"up","down"). The cell shows the result and updates when the cells it uses change; double-click it to edit the formula. Use $ to keep a row or column fixed when you copy. The functions are listed below. Copy and Paste move references as Excel does, and inserting or deleting rows and columns keeps them pointing at the same cells; a reference to a deleted cell shows #REF!. Charts, Statistics and exports use the results, and a cell showing an error counts as missing. Formulas are saved with the spreadsheet. Text starting with = in an opened or imported file stays text. To type text that starts with =, begin with an apostrophe ('=). Formulas work in New Spreadsheet windows; chart data tables hold values only.

Functions

Operators: + − * / and ^ (power); % after a number is a percent (50% is 0.5) and between two values the remainder; & joins text; = <> < > <= >= compare. A range is written B2:B20, or B:B for a whole column. Where a range goes, a list of numbers can be typed in braces instead (an array constant, in Excel's words): {2,3,4} is a row and {2;3;4} a column. SUM, AVERAGE and the other summary functions skip blank and text cells in a range, and an error cell makes their result that error; COUNT skips errors too.

  • Totals and averages: SUM, PRODUCT, AVERAGE, MEDIAN, MIN and MAX. MIN and MAX of no numbers are 0, as in Excel.
  • Counting: COUNT (cells holding numbers), COUNTA (cells that aren't empty) and COUNTBLANK(range) (empty cells).
  • Spread: STDEV or STDEV.S and VAR or VAR.S for a sample (n − 1); STDEV.P or STDEVP and VAR.P or VARP for a whole population (n); SEM, the standard error of the mean (SD / √n).
  • Lines and correlation: SLOPE(y range, x range) and INTERCEPT(y range, x range) of the least-squares line, RSQ (r²) and CORREL or PEARSON (Pearson's r). The two ranges must be the same size, or the result is #N/A. Cells are paired by position, and a pair is left out when either cell is blank or text, as in Excel. The x values can be typed as a list: with nine time points in columns C to K, =SLOPE(C2:K2,{1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8,9}) is row 2's change per time point; fill it down for every row (Edit › Fill Down, or drag the cell's bottom dot), and the list stays the same in each. For unevenly spaced times, type the times themselves, such as {0,5,15,30,60}.
  • Rounding: ROUND(x, digits); ROUNDUP and ROUNDDOWN (away from and toward zero); TRUNC(x, digits); INT (down to a whole number); CEIL(x) (up to a whole number); CEILING(x, step) and FLOOR(x, step) (up or down to a multiple of step, 1 if left out).
  • Arithmetic: ABS, SIGN, SQRT, CBRT (cube root), POWER(x, y) or POW, HYPOT(x, y) (√(x² + y²)), MOD(x, y) (the remainder, with the sign of y, as in Excel) and FMOD(x, y) (the remainder with the sign of x).
  • Exponentials and logarithms: EXP, EXP2 (2 to the x), EXPM1 (eˣ − 1, accurate for small x), LN, LOG(x) (base 10) or LOG(x, base), LOG10, LOG2 and LOG1P (ln(1 + x), accurate for small x).
  • Trigonometry, in radians: SIN, COS, TAN, ASIN, ACOS, ATAN, ATAN2(x, y) (the angle of the point (x, y), in Excel's order), SINH, COSH, TANH, ASINH, ACOSH, ATANH and PI().
  • Special functions: ERF(x), or ERF(lower, upper) for the integral between two limits; ERFC; GAMMA or TGAMMA; LGAMMA or GAMMALN (the log of the gamma function).
  • Logic: IF(test, then, else), AND, OR, NOT, TRUE(), FALSE() and IFERROR(value, value if error).
  • Checks: ISBLANK, ISNUMBER, ISTEXT and ISERROR, each TRUE or FALSE; NA() gives #N/A, to mark a value as missing.

Charts

Charts are @Graph's charting workspace. They take data from the Data Log, pasted tables or a spreadsheet, and produce publication-ready figures with built-in statistics.

The Chart Inspector

The inspector has two tabs: Chart and Statistics. The Chart tab shows the Paired / Repeated, Chart Type and Data Layout controls at the top, then a three-icon tab bar that switches between:

  • Plot Style: how the data marks look (point overlay style, bar/mean display, error bars, connection lines for paired data, and for Estimation Plots the hide toggles and mean offset).
  • Axes & Labels: chart title, axis labels, fonts, colors, axis thickness, grid lines. If a new background color crosses from dark to light (or back), @Graph offers to invert all foreground colors.
  • Range: Y-axis min and max (and X-axis min and max for Scatter (X,Y), Volcano and Cluster Scatter; Kaplan-Meier has only Time Axis Range, below). Heatmap, Dot Plot, GSEA Bar, Grouped Diverging Bar, Stacked Composition Bar, PCA Plot, Ridge Plot and Manhattan Plot have no range settings: their axes are scaled to fit the data. Leave blank for an automatic range, or double-click the Y-axis tick values to set it on the chart. Type a plain number in the data's units, or add a unit to convert it (on an axis titled in mV, -0.08 V means -80 mV). Marks beyond a typed range are cut at the axis. If a bar chart's range leaves out zero, a warning explains that bar lengths then mislead. Logarithmic scale is here too (see Logarithmic Axes).
  • Zero in an automatic range: a filled bar always includes zero, whichever side the data sit on, because a bar's length is its value. An all-negative bar chart runs from its lowest point up to 0.
  • Other marks (box, violin, mean symbol, mean line, points alone) show value as a position, so zero is included only when it costs no more than half again the height the data need. A box plot of values between −25 and −5 gets its zero line; one over −90 to −60 does not. A typed min or max is always used as given.
  • Line charts simply pad around the data, since a line's shape carries the meaning.

Controls that don't apply to the selected chart type are hidden.

Long and nested tables: Data Layout (Chart tab) can read a table as it stands. When a table read automatically looks like one of the layouts below, the Data Layout box offers to read it that way, and nothing changes until you choose. With either layout, the Chart Data window shows and edits the table itself. When a chosen layout can't read the table, Data Layout says why.

  • One Row per Value is for a long table, one row per measurement: a column naming each row's group (WT, KO; or codes 1, 2) and a column of values, with any other columns, such as the batch, plot or classroom each row came from, kept beside them. Choose the Groups and Values columns under it.
  • Nested is Prism's nested table: a heading row of categories, each name over its subcolumns (the cells after it left blank), an optional second heading row titling each subcolumn (Mouse 1, Mouse 2…), then the values. Each category's values are its subcolumns' values together, and each value keeps its subcolumn as where it came from (Values from the same). A subcolumn title used under two categories is the same source, such as a batch measured under both, or an animal recorded in both. Nested refuses a table with a heading over every column, and says so.
  • Where each row came from: a table with one column per category can also say this, such as a Batch column beside one column per treatment, or an Animal column beside an IT and an ET column (one paired recording per row). When a column's labels repeat beside 2 or more columns of measurements, Data Layout asks what it is: Data (a category of its own) or Names, as it was read, or Where each row came from. The last leaves it out of the chart, keeps each row's label with its values and sets Values from the same to it. With Paired / Repeated on, each row also becomes the second level, so the brackets fit (1 | source/row), such as (1 | animal/row).

Double-click the title, an axis label or a category label on the chart to edit it directly. In a category label, Option-Return starts a new line, for a stacked label like Prism's. When category names are too long to sit side by side, they are turned 45° (Bar/Dot, Box Plot and Line). On a Horizontal Bar, a long name wraps onto two lines. A name too long for the room is shortened with an ellipsis (…); the data table keeps the full name.

Click a dot of a beeswarm, jitter or strip overlay to see its value. Where dots overlap, the one nearest the pointer is chosen.

Legend (Axes & Labels → Appearance): Show legend and Size apply to every chart's legend. A Bar/Dot, Horizontal Bar, Line or Box Plot chart of two or more series, and a Pie, also offer Layout (Side by Side or Top to Bottom), and their legend can be dragged anywhere on the chart. A time-course Estimation Plot's legends take Layout too and stay over their panels. The other charts keep their legend in one place: in a row above a Volcano Plot or under a Venn Diagram, and at the right of the plot for the rest (the color scale of a Heatmap; the size and color keys of a Dot Plot). A Ridge Plot's names beside its ridges are its legend, so Show legend shows or hides them. PCA, Manhattan and UpSet plots, and a chart with one series, have no legend.

Logarithmic Axes

Turn on Logarithmic scale in the Range panel (or in the sheet you get by double-clicking the tick labels) to draw the value axis, or a scatter's X axis, on a base-10 log scale. Decades are labeled (1, 10, 100, or 10ⁿ for very wide ranges) with unlabeled minor ticks between; a narrower range gets 1, 2, 5 or round-number labels. Available for Bar/Dot, Horizontal Bar, Box Plot, Line and Scatter (X,Y) charts.

  • A log axis can't show zero or negative values, so they are left out, and the Range panel and the figure caption say how many. A typed Min or Max must be above zero.
  • Bars are shown as mean symbols, since a bar's length from zero means nothing on a log axis. Your bar setting comes back when you turn the log scale off.
  • Error bars are the mean ± SD, SEM or CI as computed, so they look longer below the mean. One that reaches zero or below is cut at the axis with no cap.
  • Error bars around a geometric mean (× / ÷ a factor) are symmetric on a log axis.
  • Violins are drawn from a density of the log of the values, so their shape matches the axis. Box plot quartiles and whiskers come from the values as they are.
  • A scatter's fit line is the least-squares line of the values as they are, so on log axes it curves. For a straight line on log axes, fit the log10 of your values instead.

Chart Types

Bar / Dot

The primary chart type. Each column of data is one category. Rows are replicates (individual data points). The chart shows raw data points overlaid on a mean marker and error bar.

  • Display (Mean / Bar panel): Bar, Violin (density shape; see Violin Plot), Mean symbol (shape set by the Data Points panel's Symbol picker), Mean line (horizontal tick), or Points only.
  • Error bar modes: SD, SEM, 95% CI, or None.
  • Center (Error Bars panel): Mean, or Geometric mean for log-normal data such as concentrations or titers. The geometric mean is exp of the mean of ln x, as in Prism and R. Its error bars are × / ÷ the geometric SD factor, exp(SD of ln x), or its confidence interval, the t-interval of ln x back-transformed. They look longer above the mean on a linear axis and symmetric on a log axis. SEM has no geometric counterpart. Values at or below zero have no logarithm, so they're left out, and the panel and caption say how many. Geometric mean needs individual values: it isn't available for charts made from means with SD, SEM or n, for 0/1 data, or for violins. Copy Geometric Means copies each group's numbers. Tests and effect sizes still use the values as entered. To compare geometric means, take LN of the values in a spreadsheet and test those: the difference of log means, exp(Δ), is the ratio of geometric means.
  • A table of means with error values (a label column, then value and error columns in pairs) draws the errors as given. The error column's header says what they are: "SEM" or "SE" is read as SEM, "SD" as SD, "CI" or "95% CI" as a CI half-width. With no such word the bars are drawn as supplied and the methods caption says their type was not stated. When the header names a confidence level, such as "99% CI" or "CI90", the bars are drawn as given and the caption states that level, whatever the level in Settings; a CI header with no level says the level wasn't stated.
  • The chart's data table and CSV export include a mean-and-error chart's error columns (for example "Control SEM"), so they can be edited there. The data table stays in step with the chart: renaming a category, wedge or series, Undo in the chart window, or an Assistant edit shows in the open table right away, and Undo in the table undoes only the table's own edits. In the chart window, ⌘Z takes back changes to the chart's settings, anything in its Chart and Statistics tabs, one step per change, and leaves alone a data edit made since. ⌘S in the data table saves the chart.
  • Numbers may use a decimal point or a decimal comma; the convention is read from the table's number cells themselves (names such as "Dose 0,5" don't count), not the Mac's region setting, and a semicolon-separated table is split on its semicolons. "45%" is read as 45 and "$1,234.50" as 1234.5. A blank or non-numeric cell (such as NA, N/A, NaN, NULL, None, ".", "-", "--" or a spreadsheet error such as #N/A or #DIV/0!, in any capitalization) is a missing value that keeps its row, so each row stays one subject in a paired design.
  • Mean offset: moves the mean marker sideways, away from the data points, when they overlap.
  • Bar width sets the width of each bar. With 2 or more series, Bar gap is the space between bars within a group and Category gap the space between groups.

Horizontal Bar

Same as Bar/Dot but with categories on the Y axis and values on the X axis. Useful for ranked comparisons or when category names are long.

Estimation Plot

Follows the estimation statistics approach of Cumming & Calin-Jageman (2024) and DABEST (Ho et al. 2019; see References). Instead of asking only whether an effect is non-zero (the p-value), it shows how large the effect is and how precisely it is estimated.

  • Turning it on: click the Show Estimation Plot button, at the foot of the EFFECT SIZE zone on the Statistics tab, of a Bar, Horizontal Bar, Line or Box Plot chart with one series and at least 2 categories, or of a time course. An estimation plot compares the categories of one series, so it is grayed out (with the reason in its tooltip) for a chart with 2 or more series; for those, the brackets give the differences with their CIs. A time course is the exception: its estimation plot shows its groups over time (below). Hide data points leaves each group's center and CI; Hide mean marker & error bars hides those. The chart itself switches to an Estimation Plot, and the button stays highlighted while it is on. Click it again to return to the previous chart type, with its bar display restored. Choosing another type in the Chart tab also turns it off.
  • What it plots follows the chart's Compare choice (Statistics tab, EFFECT SIZE zone), once the chart's statistics have been opened; until then it plots means. Each group shows its points and its center on that scale: the mean, the 20% trimmed mean, or the median (Medians and Ranks). The difference axis shows the Compare estimate: the mean difference (M diff), the trimmed-mean difference (Trimmed M diff), the Hodges-Lehmann shift, or for paired data the Hodges-Lehmann median of the differences (HL diff), or dominance δ on its own −1 to 1 axis.
  • Every CI on the plot, for each group's center and each difference, is a bootstrap interval from 10,000 resamples at the Default confidence level, and each curve is the bootstrap sampling distribution of that estimate, drawn from the actual resamples as DABEST does. Means and dominance δ use the plain bootstrap with the Bootstrap CI method in Settings → Statistics (BCa by default). Medians and trimmed means (and the group medians under Ranks) use the smoothed bootstrap (Silverman & Young 1987): each resampled value gets a little Gaussian noise, scaled so the sample's variance is kept. A plain bootstrap of a median from a small sample can only land on a few values (a median of 10 values is always one value or the average of two), so its curve breaks into lumps at those values and gaps between them; the smoothed curve is single-peaked. Its interval is the percentile interval, which in simulations at n = 10 covers as well as the plain bootstrap's (93–95% for a nominal 95%), where BCa would over-correct (82–86%). Paired data are resampled as whole subjects. These CIs are the plot's own; brackets and the Statistics window report the test-based CIs that go with their p-values, so the two can differ slightly.
  • Values from the same: when the values come from shared sources (cells from one animal, plants from one plot), chosen in the Statistics tab, the plot draws the same linear mixed model as the brackets instead of a bootstrap, which would treat values from one source as independent and give intervals that are too narrow. Each group's center is its model-based mean and each difference the difference in model-based means, each with its own t-based CI (Satterthwaite df), and each curve is the t distribution of that estimate (Cumming 2012). Show each source's mean (on by default, under Values from the same) draws each source's mean over the data as a larger hollow marker (a SuperPlot; Lord et al. 2020). Compare must be Means; the figure legend states the model.
  • Time courses: the Estimation Plot of a time course (groups of subjects measured over time) has two panels on one time axis, with times placed at their values when the column names give them (0, 1, 2, 4, 8 h). Everything comes from one fit of the mixed model the brackets and the Statistics window use (Time, Covariance, Values from the same and Spreads are in the same tab), checked against R (lme4, lmerTest, mmrm and mvtnorm). Copy Chart Legend describes the model, both panels and the intervals drawn.
  • Upper panel (time courses): each group's mean at each time point from the chart's mixed model, with its CI, over each subject's line (Show each subject's line).
  • Lower panel (time courses): the differences between the groups at each time point: each group against one group, or every pair with 3 or more groups (Differences, as for the plot above, at any number of groups), side by side and colored by group (with every pair, a ring in the other group's color). Each difference has the t distribution of its estimate as a half curve on its outer side and up to two intervals.
  • Intervals (time courses), in the Statistics tab: At each time (on by default) is that difference's own CI. All times together is the simultaneous CI over every difference drawn (single-step, from the multivariate t), wider, for saying where the groups differ without the risk of looking at many time points. With both, the thick bar is each time's and the thin bar the simultaneous one, as the legend says.
  • What the lower panel shows (time courses): At each time compares the difference itself, or the change since the first time point (the difference at each time minus the difference at the first), for groups that start apart. Scale shows the difference in the data's units, or standardized as Hedges' g* or Cohen's d* as the brackets give it (not for a change since the first time point).
  • Legends (time courses): the legends over the two panels (the groups above, the comparisons below) follow Axes & Labels › Appearance: Show legend, Layout (Side by Side, wrapping onto more lines, or Top to Bottom, one per line) and Size. They stay over their panels rather than being dragged.
  • Gardner-Altman (2 groups): three columns, Group A, Group B and Difference. The left axis shows the raw data; the right axis shows the difference, with 0 aligned to Group A's center (δ has its own −1 to 1 axis).
  • Cumming (3+ groups): the upper panel shows all groups, with a dashed line marking the reference group's center; the lower panel shows the differences, each against one group or all pairwise, each with its sampling distribution, CI bar and effect size. The Differences picker in Plot Style compares each group with one group (vs Control, vs Drug A and so on), or every pair (All pairwise). Until you choose, it is the control chosen for the chart's brackets, else a group named like a control (Control, Vehicle, Sham, WT and the like), else the first. The choice is kept by the group's name, so reordering or hiding groups doesn't change it, and in the plot it is also the control Glass's Δ uses.
  • Paired / Repeated data: individual difference scores appear in the Difference column, and connection lines link each subject's own points (the values in one table row). Differences use the subjects with values in both groups.
  • Each difference is the later group minus the earlier one, and the effect size beside it has the same sign. It follows the chart's EFFECT SIZE settings: for Means, Hedges' g (g* when Spreads is May differ) or the chosen d, or Glass's Δ over the chart's control for a difference that includes it, or gav/dav or gz/dz when paired; for Trimmed means, AKP robust d; for Medians and Ranks, the plotted estimate itself (HL, δ).
  • 0/1 data: when every value in the compared categories is 0 or 1 (the rule that switches the brackets to Fisher's exact or McNemar's test), the plot shows proportions instead, after DABEST's proportion plot, in two panels for 2 groups as for 3 or more. The brackets keep their own effect measure, the odds ratio with Fisher's exact p (McNemar's test when paired), so their p and CI need not agree with the Δp interval.
  • Upper panel (0/1 data): each group's proportion of 1s as a bar filled to that height (outlined up to 1), with its Wilson score CI beside it and the count (successes/n) above it.
  • Lower panel (0/1 data): each difference in proportions (Δp, the risk difference) with Newcombe's hybrid score CI (method 10). With Paired / Repeated on, it uses Tango's score CI for paired proportions (Tango 1998) on the subjects observed in both groups, which keeps a useful width even when no subject changes (10 subjects staying at 1 and 10 at 0 give Δp = 0, 95% CI −0.16 to 0.16; in exact calculations at 10 pairs it never covered less than 93% for a nominal 95%, where Newcombe's paired interval fell to 72%). The label beside each difference is Δp, or Cohen's h (2·asin√p₂ − 2·asin√p₁, Cohen 1988) when Label is set to Cohen's h in the Statistics tab, which replaces the data checks and Compare choices for such a plot.
  • Why score intervals (0/1 data): with few 0/1 values a bootstrap interval is too narrow (in exact calculations at 5 per group a percentile bootstrap covered 84% on average for a nominal 95%, and as little as 40%; Newcombe's covered 95% on average, never below 90%), so no sampling distributions are drawn and the Bootstrap CI method doesn't apply.
  • Paired flows (0/1 data): paired data also get DABEST's flows. Between neighboring bars, bands show the fraction of subjects going 1→1, 1→0, 0→1 and 0→0, in the bar's color for those starting at 1 and gray for those starting at 0 (the bar's 0s are shaded gray to match), and each Wilson CI moves onto its bar to make room. The flows link the groups in order, like the connection lines of paired data, whichever differences the lower panel shows. A pair of bars gets flows only when every subject has a value in both, so the bands always add up to the bars. Turn them off with Show paired flows in Plot Style.
  • The Statistics tab of an Estimation Plot shows the data checks, the EFFECT SIZE zone and each plotted difference's effect size. Turning the plot on stores the Compare choice in force, as the first bracket computation does, so a later data edit cannot change what it plots. Only the first series' categories are compared (a time course compares its groups).
  • A difference that cannot be estimated (fewer than 2 values, or fewer than 2 matched subjects; for 0/1 data, no values or no matched subjects) is left off the plot and shows as n/a in the Statistics tab's effect-size list, which lists the same comparisons the plot shows.
  • The CI bars are set by the data and by the Settings → Statistics choices above, not by an error bar setting.
  • Mean offset moves the center marker and sampling distribution sideways, away from the data points. It stays available when the marker is hidden, because it also positions the distribution curve.
  • Mean line is the default marker style. Hide data points, hide mean marker & error bars, and hide sampling distributions can be combined freely.

Box Plot

The classic Tukey box-and-whisker chart: a box from the first to the third quartile (Q1–Q3), a line at the median, whiskers reaching the most extreme value within 1.5 × IQR (the interquartile range) of the box, and points beyond them drawn as outliers. It uses the same data as Bar/Dot, so switching a chart to Box Plot only changes how each column is drawn.

Notches (Plot Style → Box) narrow each box at median ± 1.58 × IQR/√n, as R's boxplot(notch = TRUE) (McGill, Tukey & Larsen 1978), using the IQR of the box drawn. Boxes whose notches don't overlap suggest their medians differ (roughly 95% confidence; an informal guide, not a test). With small samples a notch can reach past its box. It's then drawn folded outward, as in R, and a note says so. The axis widens to show the notches. Copy Box Statistics copies each box's n, whiskers, quartiles, median and, with notches on, the notch ends.

  • Quartiles use the (n + 1) rule (Hyndman–Fan type 6), as in GraphPad Prism and Excel's QUARTILE.EXC (NumPy, pandas and ggplot2 default to type 7). The Data Points panel restates these conventions. The rule decides which points fall outside the fences, so state it in your methods; the "Copy Chart Legend" button on the Statistics tab writes the sentence for you.
  • A category with a single value is drawn as a flat tick instead of a box.
  • Raw data points can be overlaid on the box (Data Points panel, same Strip/Jitter/Beeswarm styles as Bar/Dot).
  • The Mean / Bar and Error Bars panels are hidden, since the box, median, whiskers and outliers are always drawn.
  • Axis labels, fonts, colors and grid work as for Bar/Dot.
  • The Statistics tab works as for Bar/Dot: effect size brackets, Open Full Statistics Window and the Show Estimation Plot button.

Scatter (X,Y)

A standard X,Y scatter plot: one or more series, each with its own X values, on a pair of numeric axes. Use it when series were measured at different X values (for example two dose-response curves at different concentrations), which the category/replicate layout of the other basic charts can't hold. It differs from Cluster Scatter, which is one point cloud colored by a label.

  • Data are read as X,Y column pairs: column 1 is the X for column 2, column 3 the X for column 4, and so on. A leftover unpaired column is dropped. Each series is named after its Y column's header, and the X axis after the first X column's header. Pairs may differ in length.
  • A table whose first column is the X for every other column (Prism's XY table: Age, Weight (female), Weight (male)) makes one series per Y column, all against that X. A table is read this way by itself when it has a header row and its first column plainly holds an X: values increasing down the rows, and either named as one (time, age, day, dose, concentration, voltage, a time unit and the like) or evenly or geometrically spaced (3, 4, 5 … or 1, 10, 100). A first column named like an identifier (Subject, Mouse, ID, Sample) is never taken as X. Otherwise set Data Layout to "Scatter Plot (First Column Is X)". A new chart read this way joins each series' points, as Prism draws an XY table; Connect Points turns that off.
  • Four ways in, all reading the table the same way: send a selected X,Y table to a chart; pick Scatter (X,Y) in the Chart Type picker of an existing chart; set Data Layout (Chart tab) to "Scatter Plot (X,Y Column Pairs)"; or choose "X,Y Column Pairs (Scatter Plot)" in the "How Should This Table Be Read?" dialog, offered for a headerless all-numeric table with an even number of columns. A chart switched to Scatter (X,Y) plots two columns one against the other, and columns that are X,Y pairs as pairs. A chart of several series plots, within each category, every other series against the first. Other columns, such as one per group, are plotted against the first, so none is dropped.
  • Connect Points joins each series' points in the order given, not sorted by X, so a time course or loop that doubles back keeps its shape. Curved lines smooths the line. Where a series' X values double back, the curve follows the points in table order and can swing past them, and a note says so.
  • In the Data Layout panel, "Switch X and Y" replaces "Switch Row/Column". It swaps X and Y for every point and the axis titles with them, and removes any fit lines, since a fit of Y on X does not describe X on Y. Switching again restores the original.
  • Without a header or typed title, the axes are titled "X Value" and "Y Value".
  • Graphs support the same layout: Import Data's "X,Y column pairs" mode builds a Graph whose sweeps have their own X values, and Graph Utilities' Combine and Concatenate keep them.

Linear regression is on the Statistics tab. Each series has its own Linear Regression button (series are never pooled). It reports the equation with the slope's confidence interval (t-based), r with its confidence interval (Fisher z), r² and p (two-sided test of slope ≠ 0), at the confidence level in Settings, and draws a dashed line over the range of your X data (as ggplot2 and Prism do, never extrapolated). Click Keep Fit to make the line solid and save it with the chart, or Remove to discard it. A dashed (not kept) line stays dashed in print, PDF and SVG, so a preview can't be mistaken for a result. The band and the options for robust SEs, the standardized slope and residual diagnostics appear once a series has a fit. A line from Connect Points is not a fit.

  • Values the data can't define show as "n/a". p needs at least three points (with 3 it is a t test on 1 degree of freedom, as in R's lm); with 2 the line is drawn but not tested. r, r² and p are n/a when Y does not vary (Y values that differ only by rounding count as not varying). A fit needs at least two points with different X values (X values that differ only by rounding count as the same).
  • r takes its sign from the slope, so a perfect negative correlation is −1.
  • Open Full Statistics Window passes the X,Y columns to the Correlation and Regression analyses (pick one X and one Y). These add Spearman ρ and Kendall's τ-b (for curved but monotonic relationships), confidence intervals, a 4-parameter logistic dose-response fit (Bottom, Top, LogEC50, Hill slope) and, with more than one pair, multiple regression and ANCOVA.
  • The Chart Legend box writes a methods sentence, with coefficients, for each kept fit. Fits still in preview are left out.
  • Show confidence band (below the description) shades a band around every fit line, in its series' color. Choose a confidence band for the mean response (where the true line lies, the default) or a prediction band for new observations (where a new single point would fall, so always wider), and a level (95% by default, 50 to 99.99%). The band is ŷ ± t · s · √(1/n + (x − x̄)²/Sxx), with 1 more under the root for prediction, t on n − 2 degrees of freedom: the band R's predict.lm and GraphPad Prism draw. It spans the same X range as the line, is exported to PDF, PNG and SVG, is saved with the chart (off by default), and the Chart Legend box describes it.
  • Below the band settings are the same options as Simple Linear in the Full Statistics window, for every fit on the chart (all off by default and saved with the chart). Standardized slope (β) adds the beta weight, B × SD(X) / SD(Y), which with one X equals Pearson r. Robust (heteroscedasticity-consistent) SEs (HC0 to HC3, HC3 recommended) replace the slope's CI and p with sandwich ones, as R's sandwich and lmtest give, labeled (HC3) in the panel and the Chart Legend; r's CI is unchanged. The confidence band then uses the same robust covariance, ŷ ± t · √(x₀ᵀV̂x₀), and the legend says so; a prediction band stays the ordinary one, since it needs one error variance for a new point. Residual diagnostics adds a Residual Diagnostics… button to each fit, with how many points are flagged: it opens R's four residual plots and the influence table (leverage, studentized residuals, Cook's D; Row is the point's number in the series) in a window of their own, updated as the data change. In a chart's diagnostics window, Send to Data Log adds the table, Export Table saves it as text, and Export Plots saves the four plots as a PDF or PNG.

Switching an existing chart to Scatter (X,Y) keeps its original data, so you can switch away and back without loss. The Chart Type entry is available only when the data have at least two numeric columns.

Kaplan-Meier Curve

The standard time-to-event survival plot: one step curve per group, starting at 100% and dropping at each observed event (not at a censoring).

  • Build it with File → Kaplan-Meier Curve from Selected Text…, Kaplan-Meier Curve Selection… in the Text Tools menu, or the Spreadsheet toolbar's Kaplan-Meier button. The table (separated by tabs, commas or semicolons) needs a header row and 2 to 4 columns in this order: Time, Status, Group, Stratum. Group is optional (without it everyone is one group), and so is Stratum (used only by the stratified tests below). Status accepts 1/0 (1 = event), event/censored, dead (or death)/alive, yes/no or true/false, in any case. A Status column coded 1 and 2 is read as in R's survival package, 2 = event and 1 = censored. Time must be 0 or more. Rows that can't be read are skipped, and the Data Log says how many. The first row is always the header, so if it looks like a subject (a time and a status), the Data Log says it was left out.
  • Small ticks mark censored subjects (those who left the study or were event-free at last follow-up). Each group's median survival (the first time survival drops to 50% or below, or "not reached"; where the curve sits exactly at 50% over an interval, the midpoint of that interval, as in R) is shown in the legend and optionally as a dashed line, with its Brookmeyer-Crowley CI read off the confidence band: from the first event time whose band contains 50% to the last. An upper limit the band never reaches (for example when the curve falls to 0) is shown as NR, and a lower limit the band leaves undefined (the curve drops straight to 0) as NE (not estimable); R reports NA for both. At 99% with small groups the band's lower edge can dip below 50% at the first event and rise again; that first time is the lower limit here, while R's quantile() reports a later one.
  • With 2 or more groups, a log-rank test compares all groups at once. Its χ², degrees of freedom and p-value appear under the legend, or inside the plot at the bottom left when the legend is hidden, and can be turned off. The log-rank p is a large-sample approximation that runs too small with few events (about 11% false positives at the 5% level for 3 vs 3), so with fewer than 10 events in all the legend adds "Approximate p".
  • Groups left out of the log-rank test: a group with nobody at risk at any event time is left out, as R does, and so is a group that is alone in every risk set it is in (the only group in its stratum, say). Its observed and expected counts are equal and it carries no information, so leaving it out recovers the test, with one fewer degree of freedom, where R reports none.
  • With exactly 2 groups a hazard ratio with 95% CI is also shown, labeled with its direction ("HR (B vs A)", vs. the reference group: the first unless you pick another under Tests). It comes from a Cox proportional-hazards model with group as its only covariate, with Efron's handling of tied times and a Wald confidence interval, matching R's survival package. With 3 or more groups, Show Cox model (below) gives one ratio per group.
  • When a ratio has no finite estimate: if one group has no events, or every event in one group comes after the last subject of the other has left follow-up, the ordinary ratio is 0 or infinite. The legend then shows Firth's penalized estimate instead (see Firth-penalized Cox under Tests), labeled "Firth-penalized", with a line saying why. With strata, where Firth is not available, the legend says the ratio is not estimable.
  • Plot Style also offers a 95% confidence band (Greenwood's variance), a numbers-at-risk table under the time axis (subjects whose time is at or after each tick, as R's summary(fit, times =) counts), curve and median-line widths, and per-group color overrides (otherwise groups use the shared category palette).
  • Band scale sets how the confidence band and the median's interval are computed. Log-log (recommended, as in SAS and Stata) stays within 0 to 100% and covers best in small samples; choose Log to match R's survfit, or Plain (linear).
  • Range ▸ Time Axis Range cuts the curves at a fixed follow-up time, e.g. Max 60 for five years in months; numbers at risk follow the axis ticks. The survival axis is always 0 to 100%.
  • Tests (Plot Style, Kaplan-Meier box): Weighting is the log-rank test by default, the most powerful when the hazards are proportional (one group's risk a constant multiple of the other's). The weighted alternatives are Gehan-Breslow (Wilcoxon) and Tarone-Ware, which give early times more weight, and Fleming-Harrington, which weights each event time by S(t−)^ρ · (1 − S(t−))^γ for the pooled survival S just before it (ρ = 1, γ = 0 is R's survdiff(rho = 1); a γ above 0 stresses late differences). Choose the weighting before looking at the curves: trying several and reporting the smallest p inflates false positives. The legend names the test used.
  • Stratify by the Stratum column compares the groups within each stratum (such as a study site) and combines the results, as R's survdiff(Surv(time, status) ~ group + strata(stratum)). It also stratifies the Cox model; the curves themselves stay pooled. It needs the 4th column; blank Stratum cells form a stratum of their own.
  • HR reference: the group the hazard ratios are relative to. It is the first group unless you pick another; a group named like a control is offered with a Use button, never picked for you.
  • Show Cox model adds each group's hazard ratio vs. the reference with its CI (Efron ties, Wald interval, as R's coxph), the likelihood-ratio test of all groups at once, and the Schoenfeld-residual test of proportional hazards (as R's cox.zph: global, plus one per group with 3 or more groups, which is cox.zph(terms = FALSE)). A note appears when a PH p is below α (0.05 at the default 95% level): the hazard ratio may change over follow-up, so it is an average. When a ratio has no finite estimate (a group with no events, or one group's events all happening after the other has nobody left at risk), the model is refit by Firth's method (below) automatically and labeled so; with strata such a ratio reads "not estimable (0)" or "(infinite)" instead.
  • Firth-penalized Cox (off by default) fits every hazard ratio by Firth's penalized likelihood (Heinze & Schemper 2001), as R's coxphf with its defaults. The penalty keeps an estimate finite where the ordinary one runs off to 0 or infinity, and it reduces small-sample bias, so it also suits small studies. Each ratio comes with a profile penalized-likelihood CI (not a Wald interval) and a penalized likelihood-ratio p for that group; Show Cox model adds the penalized likelihood-ratio test of all groups at once. Tied times use Breslow's method, as coxphf does (the ordinary fit uses Efron's), and the legend says so. The proportional-hazards test is not computed for Firth fits, since coxphf has none. Firth is not available with strata (coxphf has no strata): the ordinary stratified fit is shown, with a note.
  • Show pairwise tests (3 or more groups) tests every pair of groups with the same weighting and strata and adjusts the p-values by Holm's method, as survminer's pairwise_survdiff. A pair that can't be tested reads "not testable" and counts as p = 1 in the adjustment, as there.
  • Up to 5,000 subjects; above that the chart shows a message instead.
  • The data (Time, Status, Group, Stratum) can be edited in the chart's spreadsheet like any other chart.

Smart Visualization opens a table here when its header names the time or status column (Time, Months, OS, Event, Status, Censored…) and its second column holds two status codes. Otherwise use one of the Kaplan-Meier commands above.

Ridge Plot

Compares distributions as stacked, slightly overlapping density curves (one kernel density estimate per category, with Violin's kernel and bandwidth; each curve runs a little past the data, where a violin is cut at them). It uses the same data as Bar/Dot and Box Plot.

  • All ridges share one height scale, as in R's ggridges: the tallest density peak sets it, so a narrow distribution stands taller than a spread-out one and heights can be compared between ridges.
  • A category needs at least 2 values; categories with fewer are skipped.
  • Overlap sets how tall the tallest peak is relative to the row spacing: 1.0 just touches the row above, higher values let curves flow into it.
  • Rug marks (optional) put a small tick at each data point along the ridge's baseline.
  • Category labels run down the left side and serve as the legend; "Show legend" hides them to free the space. The X axis is the shared value scale.
  • The X axis label titles the value scale; the Y axis label draws a rotated title for the categories (such as "Class").
  • Switching to or from Ridge Plot swaps the X and Y axis labels, since the axes swap roles. Switching back restores them.
  • The Mean / Bar, Error Bars, Data Points and Connection Lines panels are hidden.
  • To make one, switch an existing Bar/Dot or Box Plot chart's Chart Type to Ridge Plot, or ask the AI Assistant for a ridge plot.

Violin Plot

A display mode of Bar/Dot (choose Violin in the Mean / Bar panel). Violins show a kernel density of each group (Gaussian kernel, Silverman's bandwidth as in R), cut off at the smallest and largest values, as ggplot2 and Prism draw them: the width at each Y value shows how dense the data are there. The solid line marks the median and the dashed lines the quartiles (the same quartiles as Box Plot); 'Median and quartile lines' (on by default) shows or hides them. The bandwidth is 0.9 × min(SD, IQR/1.34) × n^(−1/5), R's default, also used by ggplot2 and ggridges. Because it uses the smaller of the SD and the interquartile range, an outlier or a second peak does not smooth away the real shape. The curve is evaluated at points no more than a quarter of the bandwidth apart, so a long tail or a far outlier cannot hide the peak. All bar chart controls, including effect size brackets, still apply.

  • Violin width (30–100%, 90% by default, as in ggplot2's geom_violin) is the widest violin's share of the space each group has. At 100% or less, neighboring violins never overlap.
  • Each violin is scaled to its own widest point, so violins compare in shape.
  • Violin opacity sets how solid the fill is.

Line

Connects data points with lines, for time series or dose-response data, with one line per series. Line charts show error bars (SD, SEM or CI, set under Error Bars). A missing value breaks the line. The Category gap slider (Category Spacing panel in Plot Style) sets the padding at each end of the X axis: at 0% the first and last points sit on the edges; the default is 15%. With several series, Offset the series slightly (Category Spacing panel in Plot Style) draws each series' points and error bars a little to the side of the category, so overlapping error bars stay readable. For a time course of groups of subjects, the Statistics window's Make Chart gives a fuller chart (each subject's line, the group means, and brackets from the mixed model). Curved lines joins the points with a smooth curve through every point instead of straight segments. It never rises above or falls below its neighbors, so it shows no peak the data don't have.

Pie

Each category becomes a wedge. Pie charts show positive values only: negative values are left out, and the inspector says how many.

  • Wedges: Plot Style › Wedges lists every wedge of a pie (one per category, or one per series when there are several series). Set each wedge's color, rename it (the label, legend and data table all follow), or click ↺ to restore its default color.
  • Explode: check Explode on a wedge to pull it out of the pie; Axes & Labels › Shape › Explode sets how far. With no wedge checked, the Explode slider pulls out every wedge. Shape also sets the inner radius (for a donut) and the wedge stroke color and width.
  • Labels: Axes & Labels › Labels chooses what each wedge shows (None, Name, Value, Percent, Name + % or Name + Value). Position, Size and Font appear when labels are shown, and Text color sets the color of the wedge labels and the legend's names.
  • Legend: it follows Axes & Labels › Appearance: Layout (Side by Side or Top to Bottom), Size and Show legend. Drag the legend anywhere on the chart and the pie moves to make room for it; Reset Position puts it back at the bottom (Side by Side) or the right (Top to Bottom).

Volcano Plot

For differential expression and similar large-scale comparisons. Described on the Omics Visualization page, together with its MA Plot display mode.

Heatmap

A grid of colored cells for expression matrices, correlation matrices or any other numeric row × column table, with optional clustering and dendrograms. Described on the Omics Visualization page.

Point Overlay Styles

  • Strip: all points in a single vertical line, with opacity scaled so denser regions look darker. Best for large n.
  • Jitter: small random sideways spread, full opacity. Best for small n.
  • Beeswarm: each point moves sideways just enough to clear the others, keeping its exact value. Where a group is too crowded for its column, the extra points are spread across it and may overlap, and a note in the chart's bottom-right corner says how many. A smaller point size gives them more room.
  • None: points hidden; only the mean marker and error bar are shown.

When newly pasted or imported data are binary (only two distinct values, such as 0/1), Data Points starts at None instead of Beeswarm, since two dense bands add nothing that the bar height doesn't show. You can change it at any time.

Data Point Symbol

The Data Points panel's Symbol picker sets the shape of the overlaid points and of the Mean symbol marker. Ten shapes: Circle, Square, Diamond and Triangle, each filled or open (outline), plus Cross and Plus.

  • New charts start with Settings → Appearance → Charts → Default data point symbol (circle unless changed).
  • With one series, a single Symbol picker and Point color swatch apply to the whole chart. With several series, each series is listed with its own color swatch and symbol picker. On an Estimation Plot, Group colors sets each group's color (its points, interval and curve) and Symbol the points' shape; on a time-course Estimation Plot, each group's color and its subjects' point color.
  • Opacity: strip points (and an Estimation Plot's) start out lighter the more of them share a column, so piled-up values stay visible. Moving the slider sets the opacity exactly; Automatic Opacity brings the thinning back.
  • Mean / Bar sets the color of what is shown, a bar, mean line, violin or mean symbol (one per series when there are several); Box Plot's Box panel sets the boxes' color, opacity and width; a Line chart's Lines panel each series' line and point colors, solid or dashed, and the point size. Effect-size brackets are drawn on Bar/Dot, Horizontal Bar, Box Plot and Estimation charts, not on Line charts.
  • Exported SVG files use the same shapes as the screen.
  • The AI Assistant can also change the point shape on an existing chart, and set a chart's fonts, including the legend font and size. Name a font family as Font Book lists it, such as Helvetica Neue, or say System for the default.

Paired / Repeated Measures

Check 'Paired / Repeated' at the top of the Chart inspector (or the Statistics tab). This affects: (1) connection lines linking each subject's points; (2) the paired effect sizes for Means (Hedges' gav / Cohen's dav, or gz / dz); (3) the paired form of every Compare choice, for brackets and in the Statistics window: paired t-test and repeated-measures (RM) ANOVA for Means, Yuen's paired test for Trimmed means, Wilcoxon signed-rank and Friedman for Medians, the exact sign test and Friedman for Ranks; (4) what the data checks look at (the paired differences, or the within-subject residuals).

Paired values are matched by table row: row r of every column is the same subject, and a blank cell is that subject's missing value. Each comparison uses the subjects observed in both of its groups (RM ANOVA's omnibus test uses subjects with every value). A chart whose values did not come from such a table (for example, one built from separate lists of values) has no row information, so paired brackets and connecting lines are not drawn and the panel says why, rather than pairing values by their position in each list, even when the lists are the same length.

@Graph can't tell from the numbers whether rows are the same subjects, so Paired / Repeated stays off until you tick it, or until you answer "Rows Are Subjects" when @Graph asks how to read a table.

Values from the Same (Clustered Data)

In the chart's Statistics tab, under Paired / Repeated. When values aren't independent (samples from one batch, plants from one plot, students in one classroom, cells from one animal), choose the column naming each value's source, or Subcolumns for a Nested table. It needs a table read as One Row per Value or Nested (Chart tab › Data Layout) and Compare set to Means; a likely column (named like Animal, Subject, Classroom or Plate) is offered with a link. Within category and 0/1 data can't be combined with it, and the Statistics tab says why.

Brackets then come from a linear mixed model with a random intercept for each source, as lme4's lmer fits value ~ category + (1 | source). Each shows an effect size (Hedges' g by default, below) with its CI and the p for the difference in model-based means, simultaneous over the comparisons (multivariate t) when there are 3 or more categories. The bracket header gives the ICC and the counts of values and sources, and the figure legend states the model. The numbers equal the Full Statistics window's Mixed model with one value column.

Second Levels and Repeated Measures
  • Within that chooses a second level inside the first (classrooms within each school, plants within each plot, slices within each animal). Each gets its own random intercept within its source (lme4's (1 | source/unit), such as (1 | school/classroom)); the same label in two sources is two units. The result then gives both SDs, the share of the variance at each level, and an ICC for rows from the same source and from the same second-level unit, with the counts at every level.
  • Repeated measures in a long table (Participant | Condition | Score, with Values from the same set to Participant): a random intercept per participant, which with two conditions and complete data gives exactly the paired t, and unlike the paired t keeps participants with a missing condition. With Paired / Repeated on, say which rows go together under Within that.
  • One column per category, with a column saying where each row came from (Data Layout): turning on Paired / Repeated makes each row the second level within its source, so the values in one row count as one pair or subject.
  • Nested tables work the same way: with Paired / Repeated on, row 1 of a subcolumn under each category is one pair within that subcolumn (animal A's first cell before and after the drug), and so on down the rows. The numbers equal a long table read In order within each source.
Effect Size

EFFECT SIZE asks how big the typical difference was. The Statistics window's table reports g, d and Δ.

  • Hedges' g, the default, is Cohen's d with Hedges' exact small-sample correction at the df of the SD it divides by, ν = S²/(2·Var S), as Hedges (2007) corrects dT in clustered designs.
  • Cohen's d divides the difference by the two categories' total SDs, each the SD of a single value with every level's variance included, √(σₐ² + σₑ²) or √(σₐ² + σᵦ² + σₑ²) (Hedges 2007; Westfall, Kenny & Judd 2014). They are combined as the design asks: their average, (S₁ + S₂)/2, when the two categories are measured within the same sources (gav and dav, as for paired data; Lakens 2013), or their root mean square, √((S₁² + S₂²)/2), when they come from different sources (g* and d*, as for independent groups; Delacre et al. 2021). The bracket's label says which.
  • Their CIs are approximate (delta method): a little wider than the difference's own, as they also count the uncertainty in the SDs, so one can reach 0 when p is just below α.
  • Δ is the difference between the model-based means in the data's own units.
  • Spreads works as for independent groups. May differ (the default) gives each category its own residual SD (nlme's varIdent), the mixed model's counterpart of Welch's test, so the two SDs combined can differ. Assumed equal uses one residual SD for every category; the two total SDs are then equal and d is the difference in units of the SD of a single value.
On the Plot
  • On an estimation plot, the means, the differences and their CIs come from the same model instead of a bootstrap (which would treat values from one source as independent). Each curve is the t distribution of its estimate (Cumming 2012), each CI is the comparison's own rather than simultaneous, and the label is the effect size chosen under EFFECT SIZE.
  • Show each source's mean (on by default) draws each source's mean over the data as a larger hollow marker (a SuperPlot; Lord et al. 2020), so the plot shows how many sources there are as well as how many values.

Time Course by Group

For subjects in 2 or more groups, each measured at several time points: a table with one row per subject, a column of groups, as codes (0, 1, 2…) or labels that repeat (WT, KO; shown as written, in the order they first appear) and one column per time point. The chart puts the time points across, one color per group, with each group's mean ± error bars joined over time and each subject's own line faint behind them. Its brackets come from a linear mixed model (see Statistics → Linear Mixed Model), not from a separate test at each time point.

  • Making one: on a chart of such a table, the Time Course by Group box in Plot Style lists the time points. Pick the Group column and, if several subjects come from one source (a batch, plot, site or animal), Subjects from the same (the column naming each one's source), then click Make Time Course. Back to One Column per Category (in the Subjects and Means box) or ⌘Z undoes it. A Statistics window set to the Split-plot or Mixed model design makes the same chart with Make Chart. For such a table (the group column first, or named like a grouping such as Group, CellType or Treatment, beside measurements), the Chart tab's Data Layout box and the top of the Statistics tab offer this box with a note, so the mixed model is two clicks away.
  • Subjects and Means (Plot Style): Each subject's line, Group means ± error bars (with Join the means over time), Offset the groups slightly, Data points and Subject line opacity. The Error Bars box sets the error bar type.
  • On a time course, Paired / Repeated shows checked and can't be changed: each row is one subject followed across the time points in its group, which the mixed model accounts for. Its brackets come from that model, with simultaneous CIs (Statistics tab › Compute Brackets): the groups compared at each time point, or their rates of change over the time points you choose (see Rate of change below).
  • Brackets (Statistics tab → Compute Brackets): the MIXED MODEL zone replaces Compare. Choose Time (Time as categories (any shape), or a linear, quadratic or cubic trend), Covariance (Unstructured (MMRM) by default), Spreads (Assumed equal by default, or May differ: each group its own residual SD or covariance) and an optional Values from the same column. Each bracket joins two groups at one time point and shows an effect size for the difference in their model-based means (first group minus second), chosen under Effect size: Hedges' g* (the default), Cohen's d*, or Δ in the data's units, with its simultaneous CI, then its p, adjusted over the whole family (Δ's CI matches it exactly; g*'s and d*'s are a little wider).
  • Every time point's brackets are drawn at first. The Show menu above the bracket list narrows them (Chosen in the list, Adjusted p < α, CI excludes 0, or an effect at least a size you type), says how many are drawn, and has Show All; the methods text states which were drawn and why. Under Header (BRACKETS → Display), Rate of change (difference in slope) adds, for brackets at each time point, each pair's difference in slope, with its CI and p, above the brackets.
  • Rate of change: Brackets (in the MIXED MODEL zone) chooses Each time point (the default) or Rate of change. Rate of change asks whether one group rises or falls faster than another, which a single time point tests only weakly. Each group's rate is the least-squares slope through its model-based means, in the data's units per unit of time, and there is one bracket per pair of groups, spanning the time points the slope covers, labeled with the difference in slope (first group minus second), its CI and its p, adjusted over the pairs (simultaneous CIs to match). The methods text describes whichever was drawn.
  • Slope over … to … limits the slope to part of the time course, such as the response after a drug is applied; it starts as every time point, and the model is still fitted to them all. A time point left out of the model (every subject the same, such as a normalization baseline) is left out of the slope too.
  • Show each group's fitted line (off at first) draws each group's straight line over those time points, dashed in its color, so the slopes being compared can be seen. The legend then adds a dashed Fitted lines entry and the figure legend says what the lines are.
  • Effect size (under the window), with Rate of change chosen, sets what each bracket reports: Δ change over the window (the default), how much more one group changed than the other there, in the data's units (the difference in slope times the window's length, the gap the fitted lines open); Δ slope per time unit, to 3 significant figures; or dGMA, that change in SDs of a single value (Feingold 2009), with the model's SD of a single value over the window's time points in place of the baseline SD, so it works for data normalized to a baseline, and an approximate CI. The time-point Effect size choices (g*, d*, Δ) are hidden then.
  • Counts (Header, on by default) adds the counts behind the model above the brackets: the subjects (rows) used and, with Values from the same, the sources they come from, in all and per group (“n = 60 subjects from 17 animals”, then “CellType 1: 16 from 6 · …”), and which groups share sources, whose comparison is then partly within sources. The figure legend states them too.
  • Each bracket in the list shows its Satterthwaite df beside its p, which reflects how much the comparison rests on: with several subjects per source it lies nearer the number of sources than the number of subjects. Group names keep their underscores as typed in bracket labels.
  • Linked: the chart, its spreadsheet and the Statistics window are one analysis. The chart's spreadsheet shows the subject table (the group column, the time points and any source column), and editing it updates the chart. Changing the data, the group or source column, Time or Covariance in a linked Statistics window updates the same chart and recomputes its brackets.

Effect Size Brackets

On the Statistics tab, set up and compute effect size brackets. For continuous data the tab is laid out in zones, in the same order and with the same wording as the Statistics window's Compare analysis: Paired / Repeated at the top, then DATA, DATA CHARACTERISTICS, EFFECT SIZE, TEST and BRACKETS. BRACKETS starts with the Compute Brackets button, so the zones above are set up first, then has two parts: Display (what each bracket shows) and Position (the list of brackets). Show Estimation Plot sits at the foot of EFFECT SIZE, since the Compare choice there decides what the plot's difference axis shows. Below the zones are Open Full Statistics Window and the Chart Legend box. Each bracket shows the effect size above and the p-value below by default; either can be hidden or moved. The test name is shown once, above the top bracket.

Compute Brackets runs the test named on the "Will run" line. There is no test picker: the test is the one that goes with the Compare choice (see Statistics → Compare), so each bracket's p-value and its effect size's CI answer the same question. Compare is Means until you choose (the data checks' suggestion applies only when you click Use), and the first computation stores the choice in force, so the saved chart keeps it.

Brackets are computed in the background, so the chart stays responsive while bootstrap intervals run; a small spinner beside Compute Brackets shows while they do. Brackets stay current. Editing the data, flipping Paired / Repeated, or changing the Compare choice, question, effect size, post-hoc, comparisons, control, tail, Spreads or CI setting recomputes brackets already on the chart; if the data can no longer be tested as set up, they are removed and the reason is shown under Compute Brackets. Each bracket's CI remembers the confidence level it was computed at.

  • DATA: the Groups checklist (collapsed, all checked): uncheck a category, or on a chart with 2 or more series a series, to take it off the chart and out of every statistic; check it again to put it back in its place. Two categories always stay. The data of an unchecked group are kept and saved with the chart. On a chart with 2 or more series, Within category (compare series, not categories) compares the series with each other separately in each category (such as WT vs. KO at each time point); off (the default), the categories are compared using the first series. With Paired / Repeated on, the number of subjects with a value in every group is shown.
  • DATA CHARACTERISTICS: labeled checks of normality, variance, outliers and shape, and the Compare choice they suggest, with a Use button when it differs from yours. Advisory only; see Statistics → Data Checks Are Advisory. Show outliers rings each flagged point in orange, without changing its own color or symbol; it is the same setting as Mark outliers in the Chart tab's Data Points panel (off by default; not for Box Plot, which marks its own outliers). When Trimmed means is in effect (your choice, or the suggestion once you click Use), a cyan ring also marks the points the 20% trim sets aside, and a note in the chart's bottom-right corner explains the ring.
  • EFFECT SIZE: Compare (Means, Trimmed means, Medians, Ranks) and the estimate it reports; for paired Means the Question, Magnitude (gav or dav, the default) or Consistency (gz or dz); for independent Means, Spreads (May differ, the default, or Assumed equal, with Levene's result beside it for information), then Hedges' g* or Cohen's d* (g or d with Spreads Assumed equal), or Glass's Δ with a control, and a line saying what the chosen one divides by. Trimmed means, Medians and Ranks have one effect size each: AKP robust d, the Hodges-Lehmann estimate and dominance δ. The zone ends with Show Estimation Plot (see Estimation Plot).
  • TEST: for 3 or more groups, Comparisons: All pairwise (the default) or vs. Control (the control is the first group unless you pick another; a group named like a control is offered as a suggestion with a Use button, never chosen by itself); when brackets are computed per category, Adjust p across categories (below); the "Will run" line; Multiple comparisons for Means with 3 or more groups (see Statistics → Multiple Comparisons); and Tails, for Means only. The test follows Spreads (in EFFECT SIZE): Welch's t, Welch's ANOVA, Games-Howell and Dunnett's T3 by default.
  • BRACKETS: Compute Brackets, with the reason shown under it when the data can't be tested as set up. Display: the 95% CI checkbox (with 3 or more groups the intervals are simultaneous, Bonferroni, like the adjusted p-values); whether to show the effect size and the p-value, and on which side of the line; and the Header drawn once above the brackets: Test name, Omnibus test (3 or more groups: the overall test's name, statistic, df and p, plus ω² or partial η² for Means; paired trimmed means has none), both, or neither.
  • A CI in square brackets [lo, hi] comes from a formula or the test itself; one in curly braces {lo, hi} is a bootstrap (AKP robust d, dav/gav; BCa or Percentile, set in Settings → Statistics). Where a rank-based (Hodges-Lehmann) CI cannot reach the requested level at very small n, the methods caption states the level it reaches.
  • Proportions charts (two 0/1 series, whatever the chart type): the test is chosen automatically, McNemar's when Paired / Repeated is checked (on the subjects, i.e. rows, with values in both series) and Fisher's Exact otherwise (see Categorical Tests in the Statistics section). All effect sizes are signed by direction, and the other bracket style controls work as for continuous data. Labels compared within each category sit side by side; when one is wider than its category, all of them shrink together (to no less than 65%) so they don't overlap.
  • Magnitude (McNemar's Question selector, the default) shows Δp, the difference in proportions (first series minus second) with Tango's paired score interval: how much more often one condition responded, in the chart's own units, using every subject. It is what reviewers of paired 0/1 data usually expect, and stays finite when no subject changed in one direction. Magnitude's menu also offers the paired odds ratio (b/c, labeled OR) and ln(Odds Ratio), with exact intervals; they are 0 or ∞ when a discordant cell is empty.
  • Consistency (McNemar's Question selector) shows Cohen's g, the share of the changed subjects that changed toward the first series, minus ½. It describes only those subjects, so one changed subject gives its maximum, 0.5. Because a plain "g" on other charts is Hedges' g, the first McNemar bracket (the leftmost) spells it out as "Cohen's g" and the rest say "g".
  • Discordant pairs: when Cohen's g or the odds ratio rests on fewer than 10 discordant pairs, the bracket adds their number, such as "g = 0.50 [−0.48, 0.50] (n=1)", and the Statistics tab notes it; "Always show discordant-pair count (n)" adds it to every McNemar bracket. Copy Chart Legend lists each comparison's discordant pairs (first only vs second only).
  • Fisher's Exact always uses ln(Odds Ratio), with the exact CI matched to Fisher's p (Fay 2010), so it excludes 0 exactly when p is below α.
  • p-values are printed to 3 decimals (p < 0.001 and p > 0.999 at the ends), with more decimals when rounding would land on or across 0.05 or 0.01 (0.04996 prints as 0.04996, not 0.050). A p-value outside 0 to 1 shows as n/a. The bracket list uses neutral colors: no p threshold is highlighted.
  • Copy Chart Legend writes the methods sentence from what ran: the test (with the omnibus test and post-hoc for 3 or more groups), one- or two-sided, the comparisons (all pairs or each group against the named control), how a paired design handled missing values and its complete-case n, the question the effect size answers and its full name with its CI level (simultaneous for 3 or more groups, with the bootstrap method where used), and, when brackets are computed once per category, whether p-values were also adjusted across categories. For an Estimation Plot it describes the centers, the estimate and the bootstrap CIs.
  • Adjust p across categories (Holm): shown when brackets are computed once per category (proportions charts, and "Within category" comparisons). Off by default, so each category is its own family, which suits categories that are separate outcomes or time points. Turn it on when the categories together answer one question: the comparisons of all categories are then one family, Holm-adjusted once from each comparison's unadjusted p-value (never adjusted twice; for Means with 3 or more series each pair's t-test takes the post-hoc's place), so the chance of any false positive in the whole figure stays at your significance level. The effect-size CIs then cover the same family: each is at the Bonferroni level for all M comparisons (1 − α/M), since Holm has no exactly matching intervals. Where the CI inverts the test behind the p-value (t-tests, Fisher's and McNemar's exact tests), a CI that excludes the null (0, or 1 for an odds ratio) always has a Holm-adjusted p below α, but a Holm-adjusted p below α can still have a CI that reaches it. The methods caption says so.
  • 0/1 data on one series (or in "Within category" mode with 3 or more series): when every compared value is 0 or 1, the brackets compare proportions, as the Statistics window does, whatever Compare is set to. Independent groups: Fisher's exact test, with the odds ratio (the conditional maximum-likelihood estimate, first group over second) and its exact CI matched to the test (Fay 2010, as R's exact2x2 gives it): the odds ratios the same two-sided test does not reject, so the CI excludes 1 exactly when p is below α. When a group is all 0s or all 1s the odds ratio and one bound are 0 or ∞. The difference in proportions (Δp, first group minus second) comes first, without a CI, since no interval for it matches Fisher's p: 7/12 vs 2/11 shows "Δp = 0.40, OR = 5.77 [0.87, 52.2]" beside p = 0.089. Paired / Repeated: McNemar's exact test, with Δp, the paired odds ratio, ln(Odds Ratio) or Cohen's g as for proportions charts. With 3 or more groups the pairs are Holm-adjusted, the CIs are simultaneous (Bonferroni, each at 1 − α/m, so it excludes 1 exactly when the pair's unadjusted p is below α/m), and the Omnibus test header shows Fisher's exact test of the whole table or Cochran's Q (paired).
  • Position: nudge a bracket with the up/down arrows beside it in the list, or move all visible brackets together with the arrows in the "All" row. Recomputing keeps your positions and show/hide choices.
  • Show (above the bracket list) chooses which brackets are drawn: All comparisons (the default), Chosen in the list (checking or unchecking a bracket switches to this), Adjusted p < α (offered while p is shown), CI excludes 0 (1 for an odds ratio), or an effect at least a size you type (for example |g*| ≥ 0.5, either direction). Every comparison is still computed and adjusted as one family. A line says how many are drawn (Showing 4 of 27 comparisons), with Show All, and Copy Chart Legend says which were drawn and why. With many columns, choosing which comparisons to make before looking at the results (vs. Control, or the list) keeps a chart clear more defensibly than hiding results.
  • Nudging, removing a bracket, Clear All and Reset Positions can all be undone with ⌘Z.

Data Entry

  • Select a tab-delimited table in the Data Log or any text editor, then choose Chart Selection… from the Text Tools menu, or File → Chart Selected Text… (⇧⌘H).
  • Or use File → New Spreadsheet (⌥⌘N), enter the data and click Data to Chart in its toolbar.
  • Each column = one category. Each row = one replicate. First row can be column headers. For groups measured over time (a group column, codes or labels, and one column per time point), see Time Course by Group.
  • Double-click category labels on the chart to rename them.

Inspector Reference

Plot Style panel controls by chart type

  • Bar/Horizontal Bar: point overlay style, point size/opacity, Mean / Bar display (with Median and quartile lines, Violin width and Violin opacity for violins), bar opacity, bar width, bar gap, mean offset (with Mean symbol or Mean line), error bar mode and cap width, connection lines (when Paired is on). A time course adds Subjects and Means, and a table with a group-code column offers Time Course by Group.
  • Estimation Plot: Differences (vs. any one group, or All pairwise, with 3 or more groups), point overlay style, point size/opacity, hide data points, hide mean marker & error bars, hide sampling distributions, mean marker style, mean offset, connection lines (when Paired is on).
  • Box Plot: point overlay style, point size/opacity, connection lines (when Paired is on). Box: Notches, Copy Box Statistics.
  • Kaplan-Meier Curve: censor marks, median lines, log-rank result, confidence band (with Band scale) and numbers-at-risk table toggles; curve and median line widths; Tests (weighting, strata, HR reference, Cox model, Firth-penalized Cox, pairwise tests); per-group color overrides.
  • Ridge Plot: overlap, rug marks. Ridge colors follow the category palette or your per-category overrides, as for Pie wedges.
  • Manhattan Plot: genome-wide and suggestive threshold values, line colors and visibility, the Bonferroni line (α, color), the genomic inflation λ, Open QQ Plot, the two chromosome colors, equal-width chromosomes (on by default), Read 23–26 as X, Y, XY, MT (PLINK, human), point size/opacity, automatic top-hit labels (one per locus) and the maximum label count.
  • Line: error bars (SD, SEM or CI), Category gap (padding at each end of the X axis), Curved lines, and with several series Offset the series slightly. Curved lines joins the points with a smooth curve through every point instead of straight segments. It never rises above or falls below its neighbors, so it shows no peak the data don't have.
  • Scatter (X,Y): point size and opacity, Connect Points, per-series color overrides. Fit lines are on the Statistics tab.
  • Pie: Wedges (each wedge's color, name and Explode checkbox), and a note when negative values are left out.
  • Volcano Plot / MA Plot and Heatmap: see Omics Visualization.

Axes & Labels panel controls

  • All types: chart title (text, font, size, color).
