  • Bar/Line/Scatter (X,Y)/Estimation/Box Plot/Volcano/Heatmap/Kaplan-Meier Curve/Ridge Plot/Manhattan Plot: X axis label, Y axis label, axis label font, tick value font, axis color, tick color, axis line width, tick length/width. Kaplan-Meier's Y axis always ticks at 0, 25, 50, 75 and 100%. Ridge Plot's Y axis and Manhattan Plot's X axis show names instead of numbers, but their axis label fields still draw a title.
  • Tick values: automatic ticks step by 1, 2 or 5 times a power of ten (25, 250 and so on also appear, since they stay whole numbers), so labels rarely need two decimals. A Min or Max you type is kept as typed.
  • Rescale units automatically (Tick Values box; Bar, Horizontal Bar, Box Plot, Line and Scatter (X,Y)): when an axis title ends with a unit in parentheses or brackets, such as "Current (nA)", the axis switches to the prefix that avoids long decimals or long numbers, and the title changes to match: 0.02 nA reads 20 pA, 0.25 V reads 250 mV, 0.005 s reads 5 ms, 25000 pA reads 25 nA. It works for V, A, s, Ω (or ohm), M, mol, g, L, Hz, S, F, W and m, with or without a prefix. Other units (%, a.u., mV/ms, min, cm) and titles without a unit are left alone. Only the axis changes: the data, the Range fields and all statistics keep the unit you typed, and a clicked point's value is shown in the axis's unit. Exports match the screen. On by default; turn it off to keep the unit as typed.
  • Bar/Line/Scatter (X,Y)/Estimation/Box Plot/Kaplan-Meier Curve/Ridge Plot/Manhattan Plot: grid color/width/dash/count/show. Heatmap has its own "Show cell grid" controls for the lines between cells. Volcano Plot and MA Plot have no grid, as is conventional.
  • Pie: Labels (label mode, Position, Size, Font, Text color), Shape (inner radius, Explode distance, wedge stroke color and width), and the legend's Layout and Size under Appearance.
  • All: show legend, background color (with dark/light invert offer).
  • Legend font (Appearance) sets the font of the legend and of the text a chart adds itself, such as Venn and UpSet counts, gene and cluster labels, color-scale titles and effect-size brackets. A new chart starts in Settings' default font.
  • Axis titles default to the table's column headers ("Dose (mM)" rather than "Categories"). A title you typed is never overwritten, even when the data are re-read. Without a header, each chart type gets a suitable generic title (such as X/Y for Cluster Scatter, Rows/Columns for Heatmap, Gene Set/Score for GSEA Bar, X Value/Y Value for Scatter (X,Y)).

Copy / Paste Chart Style

Copy a chart's look and paste it onto another chart: fonts, colors, sizes, axis styles, error bar mode and point overlay style. The target keeps its data, axis ranges, chart type, title and axis titles, and a Volcano Plot's color, size, shape or opacity mapping travels only to a column the target has.

  • Press ⌥⌘C, or choose Copy Style from the Graph Utilities menu or the chart's right-click menu.
  • In the target chart window press ⌥⌘V, or choose Paste Style (Graph Utilities menu or right-click), available once a style has been copied.
  • Text and shape (line/arrow) annotations come along and replace the target's annotations, in the same positions. To copy a single annotation within one window, use the Duplicate button in its editor (click the annotation). See Text & Shape Annotations.
  • Data, category labels and effect size brackets are not changed.
  • ⌥⌘C / ⌥⌘V copy and paste the style of whichever window is in front: chart, graph or image.
  • Graph windows: Paste Style brings the look (symbols, lines, colors, with per-sweep colors repeating across the target's sweeps, fonts, axes, ticks, the scale bar's look and the legend) and the annotations. Values in the source graph's data units stay the target's own: the X tick spacing, scale bar lengths and logarithmic axes, as do hidden legend entries. A sweep with its own line or symbol shape keeps its whole style: kept AP markers, a result graph's points, a fit's line, or a sweep you styled on its own. ⌘Z undoes the paste by itself, apart from a change made just before it.

References

Breslow N (1970). A generalized Kruskal-Wallis test for comparing K samples subject to unequal patterns of censorship. Biometrika, 57(3):579–594. The Gehan-Breslow weighting of the Kaplan-Meier test.

Brookmeyer R & Crowley J (1982). A confidence interval for the median survival time. Biometrics, 38(1):29–41. The median survival CI on Kaplan-Meier charts.

Brown LD, Cai TT & DasGupta A (2001). Interval estimation for a binomial proportion. Statistical Science, 16(2):101–133. https://doi.org/10.1214/ss/1009213286 Why the Estimation Plot uses the Wilson interval for a proportion.

Cohen J (1988). Statistical Power Analysis for the Behavioral Sciences, 2nd edition. Hillsdale, NJ: Lawrence Erlbaum Associates. Cohen's h for a difference in proportions.

Cox DR (1972). Regression models and life-tables. Journal of the Royal Statistical Society, Series B, 34(2):187–220. The proportional-hazards model behind the Kaplan-Meier hazard ratio.

Cumming G & Calin-Jageman R (2024). Introduction to the New Statistics: Estimation, Open Science, and Beyond, 2nd edition. New York: Routledge. The basis of the Estimation Plot.

Efron B (1977). The efficiency of Cox's likelihood function for censored data. Journal of the American Statistical Association, 72(359):557–565. The handling of tied event times in the hazard ratio.

Fagerland MW, Lydersen S & Laake P (2014). Recommended tests and confidence intervals for paired binomial proportions. Statistics in Medicine, 33(16):2850–2875. Tango's score interval among those recommended for a difference of paired proportions.

Fagerland MW, Lydersen S & Laake P (2015). Recommended confidence intervals for two independent binomial proportions. Statistical Methods in Medical Research, 24(2):224–254. Newcombe's interval among those recommended for a difference of independent proportions.

Firth D (1993). Bias reduction of maximum likelihood estimates. Biometrika, 80(1):27–38. The penalized likelihood behind Firth-penalized Cox on Kaplan-Meier charts.

Gehan EA (1965). A generalized Wilcoxon test for comparing arbitrarily singly-censored samples. Biometrika, 52(1/2):203–223. The Gehan-Breslow weighting of the Kaplan-Meier test.

Grambsch PM & Therneau TM (1994). Proportional hazards tests and diagnostics based on weighted residuals. Biometrika, 81(3):515–526. The Kaplan-Meier chart's proportional-hazards test.

Harrington DP & Fleming TR (1982). A class of rank test procedures for censored survival data. Biometrika, 69(3):553–566. The Fleming-Harrington weighting of the Kaplan-Meier test.

Heinze G & Schemper M (2001). A solution to the problem of monotone likelihood in Cox regression. Biometrics, 57(1):114–119. Firth's penalized Cox regression (R's coxphf), used when a hazard ratio is not estimable or Firth-penalized Cox is on.

Ho J, Tumkaya T, Aryal S, Choi H & Claridge-Chang A (2019). Moving beyond P values: data analysis with estimation graphics. Nature Methods, 16(7):565–566. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41592-019-0470-3 DABEST; the Estimation Plot's bootstrap intervals and sampling-distribution curves.

Hotelling H (1931). The generalization of Student's ratio. Annals of Mathematical Statistics, 2(3):360–378. The PCA chart's Confidence (group mean) ellipses: the T² region, exact at any group size.

Holm S (1979). A simple sequentially rejective multiple test procedure. Scandinavian Journal of Statistics, 6(2):65–70. The adjustment of the Kaplan-Meier pairwise tests.

Lê S, Josse J & Husson F (2008). FactoMineR: an R package for multivariate analysis. Journal of Statistical Software, 25(1):1–18. The PCA chart's Confidence (group mean) ellipses follow its shape; the radius is Hotelling's exact one instead (see Hotelling 1931).

McGill R, Tukey JW & Larsen WA (1978). Variations of box plots. The American Statistician, 32(1):12–16. Box plot notches.

Moher D, Hopewell S, Schulz KF, Montori V, Gøtzsche PC, Devereaux PJ, Elbourne D, Egger M & Altman DG (2010). CONSORT 2010 explanation and elaboration: updated guidelines for reporting parallel group randomised trials. BMJ, 340:c869. https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.c869. Item 17b: for a binary outcome, report an absolute effect size, such as the difference in proportions (Δp), beside any relative one, such as an odds ratio.

Newcombe RG (1998a). Interval estimation for the difference between independent proportions: comparison of eleven methods. Statistics in Medicine, 17(8):873–890. The hybrid score interval (method 10) for the Estimation Plot's difference in proportions.

Silverman BW & Young GA (1987). The bootstrap: to smooth or not to smooth? Biometrika, 74(3):469–479. The smoothed bootstrap behind the Estimation Plot's medians and trimmed means.

Tango T (1998). Equivalence test and confidence interval for the difference in proportions for the paired-sample design. Statistics in Medicine, 17(8):891–908. The score interval for the paired difference in proportions: McNemar's Δp and the Estimation Plot's paired difference.

Tarone RE & Ware J (1977). On distribution-free tests for equality of survival distributions. Biometrika, 64(1):156–160. The Tarone-Ware weighting of the Kaplan-Meier test.

Venables WN & Ripley BD (2002). Modern Applied Statistics with S, 4th edition. New York: Springer. cov.trob, used for the PCA chart's t ellipses.

Wilson EB (1927). Probable inference, the law of succession, and statistical inference. Journal of the American Statistical Association, 22(158):209–212. The Wilson score interval for a proportion.

Graphs: The Waveform Viewer

Each graph window has a toolbar at the top, a bar of sweep controls above the plot, the plot itself, a status bar at the bottom and the inspector on the right. Every window keeps its own inspector settings. For zooming, cursors and moving through sweeps, see Navigating Graphs.

Toolbar Buttons (left to right)

  • Export: save the graph as an image (PNG, JPEG, TIFF, GIF, PDF, SVG), copy it as PDF, send it to Keynote, or save its data as @Graph, ABF, AxoGraph, HDF5, MATLAB, NWB or CSV. File → Export… (⇧⌘E) opens the image export panel directly.
  • Spreadsheet: shows the sweeps in an editable grid, one channel at a time (turn on 'Show all channels' to see them all). See Spreadsheets.
  • Cursors (⌘K): show or hide cursors A and B.
  • Inspector (⌘I): show or hide the inspector.

Export Visible…, in the bar above the plot, copies just the sweeps you're showing into a new window, a new .@graph file, or both. With the X axis zoomed, it can also cut each sweep to the range on screen.

The status bar shows the number of channels and sweeps, the samples per sweep and the duration. On its right are JP Correction (below) and, for a recording with two or more channels or while an Rₛ correction is applied, the Rₛ button (see Modifying Graphs).

The Inspector

The inspector opens when a file is loaded. Click a category icon at the top, then a tab below it:

  • Display: Plot Style (symbols, lines, legend, sweep names), Axes & Labels (axes, ticks, grid, labels, title, background, scale bars, interval bars, error bars, trend/base lines), Range (axis limits, log scale), Groups (grouping, group order).
  • Modify: Data Transform (Y scale and offset, X scale, Set X = 0), Graph Tools (Decimate, Interpolate, Resample, Integrate, Differentiate), Filter/Smoothing, Baseline, Rₛ Correction.
  • Signal: Average, Normalize, Align at Peak, PSD / FFT.
  • Analyze: Measure, Peaks, Curve Fitting, Synapse Detection, AP Detection, LFP / Spike Train Analysis.

In any inspector panel, pressing Return (↩) in a number field runs the panel's main action (Apply Filter, Measure, Fit and so on). The LFP / Spike Train Analysis panel has many actions, so there you click the one you want.

Graph Title and Axis Labels

In the Axes & Labels panel, 'Show title' adds a title above the plot with its own font, size and color. With 'Show axis labels' on, the X and Y label fields set the text along each axis (for example 'Voltage (mV)'), with the name and unit entered separately; a time axis picks its unit for you. You can also double-click an axis label on the plot to edit it.

Window Size

The small round button at the top right of the window sets its exact size in points (1/72 inch), the unit of PDF and SVG export. Match window, Copy as PDF and Send to Keynote use the size of the graph area inside it, so windows given the same size (with the inspector shown or hidden alike) export as figures of the same size. Any size from the window's minimum up to 10,000 points works.

Junction Potential Correction

Click 'JP Correction' in the status bar to turn it on, and type the liquid junction potential (LJP) in the mV field next to it. While it's on, membrane-potential channels show V(corrected) = V(recorded) − LJP, and measurements use the corrected values. Your data isn't changed: turning it off brings back the recorded values, and switching it or changing the value can be undone with ⌘Z. New graphs start with the on/off state and value set in Settings → Neurophysiology.

Only a voltage channel that looks like a membrane potential is corrected: a channel in mV or V whose values all lie between −120 and +180 mV and whose typical level (its median) is at least 20 mV away from 0, such as a cell at rest or a voltage-clamp holding potential. Current channels are left alone, and so are voltages near 0 or outside that range: extracellular recordings, TTL and trigger lines, and traces already relative to their baseline. A file with no such channel, or whose reader couldn't confirm the voltage scaling, opens with correction off.

Imaging Analysis

The Image window loads, displays and analyzes fluorescence microscopy data: single TIFFs, TIFF sequences, T-series and Z-stacks, including Leica, Nikon, Prairie View and Zeiss files. Intensity traces measured from regions of interest (ROIs) can be sent straight to a Graph window.

The window shows the image with its playback controls below it, and an inspector on the right with four tabs: Channels (display), ROIs (drawing, detecting and listing ROIs, pixel size and scale bar), Analyze (how traces are measured, and Measure) and Register (drift correction). The sidebar button at the top right shows or hides the inspector. After Measure, the traces appear in a graph below the image.

Opening Images

Image files can be opened in several ways:

  • Drag a TIFF (.tif/.tiff), Zeiss CZI (.czi) or LSM (.lsm), Nikon ND2 (.nd2), Leica LIF (.lif), Prairie View XML (.xml), or a folder of TIFFs onto the Data Log, any open @Graph window, or the Dock icon.
  • File → Open File… (⌘O) lists the image formats alongside the recording formats, and image files appear in File → Open Recent.
  • File → New Data Importer (⌘N) opens a drop-zone window listing every importable file type by kind (Data Files, Image Files, Text / Spreadsheet Files); it closes once a file is accepted.
  • Prairie View: drag the .xml file. @Graph asks for access to the folder of TIFFs beside it, if it can't already read it; click Open Folder in the panel that appears.
  • Folder of TIFFs: files load in natural order, as in Finder (frame_2 before frame_10). Files holding several pages each load every page, in file then page order. A folder of ImageJ Z-stacks, one per time point, opens as a Z-stack over time, and a folder holding an OME-TIFF set opens as that set. A folder saved by Fiji takes its pixel size from the first file.
  • A file holding more than 4 images (CZI scenes, ND2 positions, LIF or OME-TIFF series) shows Choose What to Open, listing each image with its size, channels, Z and T. All are checked to start: uncheck any you don't need (Check All and Check None help), then click Open. A file with 4 or fewer opens one window for each.

A folder of TIFFs is treated as a T-series by default. Enable 'Treat T-series as Z-stack' in the Channels panel to reinterpret the same folder as a Z-stack.

Automatic Display Setup

When an image loads, @Graph sets up the display automatically:

  • Scale bar: shown when the file records a pixel size. Its length is a round number, 1, 2 or 5 × a power of ten (such as 20 µm or 50 µm), about 15% of the image width. For a file with none (such as a plain TIFF), enter it, or choose the objective, coupler, camera and binning, in the ROIs tab under Microscopy Setup (pixel size = camera pixel × binning ÷ (objective × coupler)); until then the scale bar has no length and ROI sizes aren't shown in µm. When the image is the camera's sensor size divided by 2, 3, 4 or 8, Microscopy Setup asks whether the camera was binned: leaving 2 × 2 binning out halves every length and quarters every area.
  • Non-square pixels: when the file records different X and Y pixel sizes, each is used for its own axis. ROI widths use the X size, heights the Y size, areas both, and a line's length combines them. The scale bar uses the size of whichever axis is horizontal on screen, so it stays right after rotating by 90°. When the Y axis isn't a length (a Leica XT line scan, where Y is time), only widths are given and a scale bar is shown only while X is horizontal.
  • Scale bar color: 'Auto' (on by default) picks white or black on every frame from the image behind the bar, so it stays legible. Turn Auto off to choose the color yourself with the 'White' checkbox.
  • Display range: an ImageJ or Fiji TIFF opens with the display range it was saved with (each channel's, for a composite), as in Fiji. Other images open spanning the first time point's lowest to highest value. Click Auto to fit the range to the data instead.

Channels Panel

Each channel has its own row: a checkbox to show or hide it, its color swatch and name, the LUT, Raw and Smoothing, the display range (Min, Max and Auto) and, for a Z-stack, Z proj. Below the rows are Frame avg., Interval and the Z and T options.

  • Raw (on by default for single-channel images): shows pixel values as a linear gray ramp from Min (black) to Max (white), with no color table. ROI measurements always use the raw detector values, whatever this setting.
  • LUT (color table): used when Raw is off. Emission λ gives the hue of the fluorophore's emission peak; Channel Color shows the channel in its own color (picking a color with the channel's swatch selects it); Grays, Green, Red, Blue, Cyan and Magenta are single-color ramps; Fire (ImageJ's, ending in white) and Hot bring out detail in bright areas; Viridis looks even to the eye, though its lowest color is a dark purple rather than black, so in a multichannel composite it tints the background.
  • Emission λ: shows a spectrum slider; drag it to the fluorophore's emission wavelength, or type one (380–700 nm) in the field beside it. Defaults: Ch1 = 520 nm (GFP/FITC), Ch2 = 610 nm (mCherry), Ch3 = 670 nm (Cy5), Ch4 = 460 nm (DAPI).
  • Smoothing (default off): one setting for the whole image, so any channel's box turns it on or off for every channel. Off, each pixel is a sharp square, exactly as recorded. On, neighboring pixels are blended when the image is enlarged, on screen and in exports; this can make gradients easier to see but adds no real detail.
  • Display range Min / Max: the range is cut into 256 equal steps, one per color, so pixels at or below Min are black and those in the top step, up to Max and above, take the LUT's peak color (white in Raw). A Max below Min inverts the ramp. Auto sets the range so 0.35% of the pixels are saturated, half shown black and half at the top color, as ImageJ's Enhance Contrast (saturated=0.35) does. It reads the whole series, not just the frame on screen: every time point, and for a Z-stack what the canvas shows (the channel's Z projection at each time point with Show Z Projection on, otherwise every slice). It is computed exactly from every pixel value and not limited to the file's declared bit depth. For a floating-point, signed or calibrated image, Min and Max are shown in the image's true values.
  • Z proj.: how Z slices are combined, both for display (with Show Z Projection on) and for ROI measurements of a Z-stack. Changing it re-runs Auto while the Z projection is shown. It appears only for a Z-stack.
  • Subtract (images with 2+ channels): subtracts another channel's signal from this channel's measured traces, ROI by ROI and frame by frame, for example to remove bleed-through or a reference signal. With Subtract background on, each channel's own background is taken off first; ΔF/F₀ is computed after. It is saved with the image and copied with Copy Style, and leaves the image itself unchanged.
  • Frame avg.: averages this many consecutive frames into each point of the measured traces, reducing noise at the cost of time resolution. Each point is plotted at the mean time of the frames it averages (the bin's center), and frames at the end that don't fill a whole bin are left out, so every point averages the same number of frames; the Data Log says how many. The displayed image and exported movie are not averaged. 1× (no averaging, the default) to 10×.
  • Interval: time between frames, read from the file when it records one, and used for the time axis. A file without one (such as a plain multi-page TIFF or a folder of TIFFs) shows 'not set': its traces are then plotted against frame number (counted from 0), there is no time readout, Time Window baselines and "Real time" playback are unavailable, and movies play at 10 fps. Type the interval here to set it; it shows up to 6 significant digits, so a 0.5 ms interval reads 0.0005. Prairie View, Nikon ND2, Zeiss CZI and LSM, and Leica LIF files record each frame's time. When those frames are evenly spaced, the interval is their mean. They count as even when every frame lies within 5% of an interval of an even grid from the first frame to the last, allowing more for timing jitter (8 times its SD, up to a quarter of an interval), so jitter alone doesn't make a steady recording uneven. When they are not (repeated cycles with a pause between them, or acquisition phases at different rates), each time point keeps its own recorded time: the time readout, Time Window baselines, the trace graph, Send to Graph, CSV export and the movie clock all use it, the Interval field shows their median interval, marked "median", and the Data Log gives the range of intervals. Typing another Interval replaces the recorded times with even spacing.
  • Treat T-series as Z-stack: treats every frame as a Z slice of a single time point. It is offered only for a plain time series (one without Z slices). Only needed for a Z-stack saved without Z information (such as a folder of TIFFs); files with real Z depth (ND2, CZI, LSM) don't need it. When checked, Orthogonal Views come on, showing slice 1 (Show Z Projection shows the stack collapsed). Every Z-stack has a 'Z step' field, read from the file when it records one: enter the real slice spacing there when it doesn't, or the XZ/YZ Orthogonal Views draw each slice as tall as one pixel is wide, and look squashed or stretched.
  • Treat Z-stack as T-series: the reverse, shown for a Z-stack of one time point. Every Z slice becomes a time point, for a time-lapse saved as a plain stack (ImageJ saves any stack that isn't a hyperstack as Z slices). Set the frame interval above it.

Raw shows what the detector measured. Use a LUT for figures or multi-channel composites; measurements are unaffected either way.

Image Canvas

The image is centered and scaled to fit. The surround is dark charcoal rather than black, so the image edge is always visible. ⌘= and ⌘− zoom in and out, ⌘0 fits the image again, and the arrow keys pan. The toolbar's Rotate 90° button turns the image, with its ROIs, text and arrows, clockwise (Option-click turns it the other way), Metadata shows what the file records about the acquisition, and Scale Bar shows or hides the scale bar. Right-click the canvas for Copy Image, Copy as PDF, Copy Style and Paste Style (and, on an ROI, Delete ROI and Duplicate ROI).

Hover over the image to read the pixel under the pointer in the top-left corner: its position in pixels (and in µm when the pixel size is known) and each visible channel's value, in the image's true values (the Z projection's, when one is shown). The readout is on screen only and never appears in exports.

ROIs stay within the image. If you draw or drag an ROI partly outside, it snaps back inside.

Drawing ROIs

Pick a tool from the toolbar (Rectangle, Ellipse, Polygon, Freehand, Line, or Pointer for selecting and moving) and draw on the canvas. ROIs stay in place when the window is resized.

  • Freehand: drag around a region to trace its outline as you go; release to close it. Escape cancels a freehand or polygon ROI in progress.
  • ROIs are named ROI 1, ROI 2, and so on (one more than the highest number on the image), each with its own color, matching its trace in the sweep graph.
  • To move an ROI, drag it, with any tool: pressing on an existing ROI selects and moves it instead of drawing a new one (the pointer turns into a hand over an ROI). Hold Option while dragging to duplicate it, or hold ⌘ to draw a new ROI starting inside an existing one. A move stays inside the image and can be undone with ⌘Z.
  • To delete the selected ROI, press Delete, or right-click it and choose Delete ROI. The right-click menu also has Duplicate ROI.
  • You can also delete an ROI from the ROIs tab, or remove them all with the toolbar's Clear ROIs button (which also clears the measured traces) or the ROIs tab's Clear All.
  • Every change to the ROIs can be undone with ⌘Z and redone with ⇧⌘Z: drawing, duplicating, moving, deleting, renaming, setting the background ROI, Load ROIs, Auto-Detect, Paste Style, and the AI Assistant's ROI tools.

Text & Shape Annotations

The Add Overlay menu at the top right of the image adds movable text labels, lines and arrows on the image, separate from ROIs. It works the same way as on Graph and Chart windows.

  • Choose Add Text, Add Line or Add Arrow. Drag an overlay to move it; click it to open its editor for font, color, size and style.
  • Drag the editor by its ≡ handle if it covers something. It returns to its usual spot when you select another overlay.
  • Drag the middle of a line or arrow to move it whole, keeping its length and angle.
  • When dragging an endpoint: hold ⇧ Shift to snap to horizontal or vertical; hold ⌥ Option to rotate around the other end at a fixed length; hold both to do both.
  • Duplicate (in the editor) makes an independent copy with the same style, slightly offset and already selected.
  • On a Graph or Chart window, 'Position by data value' in the editor pins a label, or both ends of a line or arrow, to X and Y values, typed in the units the axes show (a graph's X in its ms or s, its Y in the axis title's unit, after any junction-potential correction). An image window, and a spike-train raster, place overlays on the canvas only.
  • Overlays stay in place when the window is resized, appear in every export (image files, clipboard, PDF, movie) and are saved in the .@graph file.
  • Delete an overlay from its editor. Every change to an overlay can be undone with ⌘Z: adding, moving, editing, duplicating and deleting. In an image window, so can changes to a channel's display (LUT, color, Min, Max, Auto, Z proj) and name, rotation, the scale bar (with its Auto and White), Frame avg. and the measuring settings; changes made in quick succession, such as a slider drag, undo as one step.

ROIs Panel

The ROIs tab starts with Microscopy Setup (the pixel size) and the scale bar settings, then lists all ROIs under Regions of Interest, each with its color, its name (editable), a BG button, a show/hide (eye) button and a delete (−) button. A hidden ROI (one saved hidden, say) isn't drawn or measured; its eye button shows it again. When the pixel size is known, each row also gives the ROI's size in µm (a line's length), and for an area ROI the number of pixels it measures and their area.

  • Rename an ROI by editing its name and pressing Return (or clicking elsewhere).
  • Select an ROI to see its statistics below its row, for each visible channel on the plane shown (the current frame and Z slice, or the Z projection when one is shown): mean, standard deviation (sample, n − 1), min, max and integrated density (area × mean), as ImageJ's Measure gives them, with the ROI's pixel count and area. They update as you move through the image. The AI Assistant reads the same statistics for any ROIs.
  • Click BG to make an ROI the background reference. The row turns orange and the button reads Background; click it again to make the ROI a normal one. Only one ROI can be background at a time.
  • The background ROI has a dashed border and a '◆BG' label on the canvas and in exports.
  • The background ROI gets no trace of its own; it is used only for background subtraction (Analyze tab).
  • Save ROIs / Load ROIs: saves all ROI positions and names as a .atgroi file, or loads one and adds its ROIs, with their colors, to those already on the image, so the same ROI set can be reused on other recordings. A file saved from an image of another size has its ROIs rescaled to the same relative positions; any part of an ROI outside the image is cut off, and an ROI wholly outside is left out, with a note.
  • Clear All: removes every ROI after a confirmation (⌘Z brings them back).

Auto-Detect ROIs

Auto-Detect ROIs (ROIs tab; click it to show its settings) finds bright regions in a channel and adds one polygon ROI per region. The settings start at usable defaults, so you can click Detect ROIs straight away; the ROIs appear on the image at once. Treat the result as a starting point: every detected ROI can be edited by hand.

  • Channel: the channel to threshold.
  • Detect from: Current Frame uses the frame on screen; Mean of All Frames averages the whole series, usually the better choice for finding where cells sit, and the default for a time series (two frames or more). Three activity images find cells that fire but are dim at rest, which a mean misses: SD over Time (each pixel's sample SD through the series), Max − Mean (how far it rises at its peak), and Correlation with Neighbors (the mean Pearson correlation of each pixel's time course with its eight neighbors': a cell's pixels rise and fall together, while background pixels share only noise, near 0; the local correlation image of CaImAn and Suite2p). In a simulation with 15 bright labeled cells and 15 dim cells that fired a few times, the mean found none of the dim ones; each activity image found all 15. They are offered for a series of more than two frames.
  • Smoothing (σ, px): the image is smoothed with a Gaussian of this width before it is thresholded (default 1 px; 0 turns it off; up to 10). On a single frame at ordinary photon counts the noise otherwise dominates the histogram, and Otsu's threshold lands inside it: in simulated fields of 60 cells of varied brightness, a raw frame found as few as none of them among hundreds of noise specks, and a 1-px Gaussian found 98–100%. Area, circularity and outlines are measured on the thresholded smoothed image; the ROIs' traces are measured on the raw data.
  • Background radius (px, 0 = off, the default): before thresholding, the background is removed, the brightness a square of this radius fits under at each pixel (a grayscale opening, the "top hat"), so uneven illumination or a broad glow doesn't decide which cells pass one threshold. About 3 times the cells' radius suits. In simulated fields under a strong vignette, a single threshold found 76% of the cells; with a 20-px background radius, 98%. A manual threshold is then measured up from 0 instead of the display Min.
  • Split touching cells (on by default): a region where cells touch is cut along the neck between them, a watershed of the region's distance map, merging any two parts whose peaks rise less than half a pixel above the saddle between them (ImageJ's binary Watershed rule). A neck narrower than the cells splits; an oval, whose distance map has a single ridge, doesn't. It judges shape only. In simulation on thresholded, smoothed, noisy fields, every single cell stayed whole, 97% of ovals 1.8–2.5 times as long as wide stayed whole, and 81% of touching pairs with centers 1.6–2 radii apart were split. The area, edge and circularity filters then judge each part.
  • Auto threshold (Otsu), on by default: picks the threshold from the image's brightness histogram. For a single frame every brightness level gets its own histogram bin, as in scikit-image, so the threshold lands on an exact level even in 12- and 16-bit images; a mean of all frames uses 256 bins. The histogram covers the 0.1st to 99.9th percentile of the plane, values beyond clipped to it, so one hot or saturated pixel can't stretch it. Turn it off to set the Threshold slider yourself, as a percentage of the channel's Min to Max display range (50% to start); on an activity image (SD, max − mean, correlation), which is not on the channel's scale, as a percentage of that image's own 0.1st to 99.9th percentile range.
  • Min Area and Max Area (px², 9 and 5,000 by default): discard regions outside this size range, such as single-pixel noise or a thresholded background. With a pixel size, the panel gives the same range in µm². A Min Area above Max Area is flagged in the panel and refused by Detect.
  • Min Circularity: 4π × area ÷ perimeter² of each region's outline (see the tip below), from 0 to 1, with holes filled, so a cell with a dark nucleus (a ring) or a few noise pinholes is judged by its outline. About 1.00 for a round cell at any size, 0.85 for an oval twice as long as wide, 0.67–0.70 at three times, about 0.56 for a crescent, lower for a thin streak. Regions below the setting are discarded. The default, 0.5, keeps round and oval cells; 0 turns the filter off. Two touching cells merge into one region, which the smoothing rounds out to score 0.67–0.75, like a long oval, so circularity can't reliably separate them; Split touching cells (above) does.
  • Exclude regions touching the frame edge (on by default): a region cut off by the border has an unknown true size.
  • Detect ROIs adds the regions found to the image and says how many. Change a setting and click Detect Again to replace the ROIs the last run added with a new set (ROIs you drew or loaded are kept). The AI Assistant's auto-detect works the same way, replacing the previous run's ROIs. ⌘Z undoes each run. The same image and settings always give the same ROIs.
  • Detect from Mean of All Frames with drift correction on averages, for each pixel, only the frames that imaged it: the border uncovered by a frame's shift is left out of that frame instead of repeating the edge pixel. Frames that can't be read are left out and listed in the Data Log.

Each outline is the line where the smoothed image crosses the threshold, traced between pixel centers (marching squares). The pixels inside it are exactly the detected region, holes filled, so an ROI measures that region and no more, and two ROIs never share a pixel; touching cells that were split meet halfway between their pixels.

Drift Correction (Register Panel)

The Register tab corrects XY drift (thermal creep, stage settling, vibration) that would otherwise move cells out from under their ROIs during a long time-lapse. Each frame is shifted, without rotation or warping, to line up with one reference frame.

  • Registration Channel: the channel drift is measured from. Prefer a bright, structural channel over a sparsely active indicator.
  • Reference Frame: the frame every other frame is aligned to, numbered from 1 as in the frame counter. Use Current picks the frame on screen. Average (10 by default; 1 for none): the reference is the mean of this many frames centered on it, each first aligned to the reference frame (frames that fail are left out), and every frame, the reference frame included, is then registered to that mean. A single frame's noise limits accuracy at low photon counts: at 5 photons per pixel, a 10-frame mean halved the error (from 1.0 to 0.5 px) and cut frames off by more than 1 px from 48% to 8% in simulation. It also dilutes cell activity in the reference, which pulls the estimate when an indicator channel is used.
  • Max Drift Search (50 px by default): the largest distance from the reference frame searched, in pixels, measured as a distance (not per axis). Set it comfortably above the largest drift you expect: a larger setting is slower and gives more room for a wrong match. The slider runs from 5 px to half the image's short edge.
  • How it searches: first on a copy reduced by 2 at a time, only as far as the search distance needs and never below a 64-px short edge (so sparse fields keep enough structure), then refined level by level within the search distance, to a sub-pixel estimate. A frame is accepted only when its best match stands out clearly from the next-best match elsewhere and lies inside the search distance; a dark frame, or a shift beyond the search distance, fails instead of returning a wrong shift.
  • Before registration each plane is passed through a 3 × 3 median filter, which removes hot pixels: they don't move with the tissue and pull the estimate toward zero (0.2% hot pixels gave a mean error of 3.8 px in simulation; filtered, 0.06 px). It also lowers the error from ordinary noise. Changing the registration channel's Z projection clears a computed correction, as changing the channel does.
  • Compute Drift Correction processes the whole series, shows its progress, turns the correction on and reports the largest shift found ("Max drift: N px"). Frames that could not be registered are counted in the status line and listed in the Data Log; their shift is interpolated in time from the nearest registered frames (held at the ends of the series), and Measure lists them again with the traces, as those samples are less certain.
  • Apply Correction turns the computed correction on or off without recomputing, to compare the two.
  • Clear discards the computed correction.

When applied, the correction is used everywhere: the canvas, image and movie export, Measure, and Auto-Detect ROIs' Mean of All Frames. Export Data as TIFF is the exception: it saves the pixels as recorded.

Measure takes each ROI's values from the shifted frame without rounding them. A fractional shift is applied by bilinear interpolation between the four nearest pixels, which averages a little noise away: for a one-pixel ROI at a half-pixel shift the frame-to-frame SD halves, while an ROI of many pixels is barely affected. Pixels shifted in from beyond the frame's edge were never imaged, so they are left out: an ROI that drift moves partly out of view in some frames is measured, in every frame, on the pixels that stay in view in all of them, so a brightness gradient across the ROI can't make a trend as the edge passes through it. If no pixel stays in view throughout, the frames that see only part of the ROI are gaps, like a frame with none of it in view. The Data Log says how many frames this affected for each ROI and channel. On screen and in exports, the uncovered edge is filled in for display only.

Only shifts are corrected. Rotation or non-rigid tissue deformation needs a dedicated registration tool.

Export Image, Movie and Data

The Export toolbar menu has two parts. As Shown (for figures and talks) has Export Image… and Export Movie…, which save what the window shows. Raw Data (for analysis) has Export Data as TIFF…, which saves the measured pixel values. File → Export… (⇧⌘E) also opens Export Image.

  • As Shown exports look as they do on screen: current colors and display range, the Z slice or projection shown, rotation and drift correction, ROIs (background ROI dashed with its ◆BG label), overlays, the scale bar if shown, and the Smoothing setting. Text, outlines, ROI labels and the scale bar keep the size they have against the image in the window (at its fit, before zooming), whatever the export size. With every channel hidden, the image is black with its ROIs and overlays, as in the window. The pixel readout is never included.
  • Export Image saves the current frame as PNG, TIFF, JPEG, GIF, PDF or SVG. PNG or TIFF is best for figures. JPEG and GIF lose detail or colors, so the panel marks them with a warning. PDF and SVG hold the picture as pixels, not vector shapes. Images are saved without transparency.
  • Scale: a whole number. 1× keeps native pixels; 2×, 3×, 4×, 6× or 8× draws each image pixel as a sharp square block instead of blurring it (unless Smoothing is on). A small image starts at a scale that brings its long edge to about 512 pixels, so labels and the scale bar stay legible. The panel shows the output size in pixels.
  • Resolution (300 dpi by default) sets only the print size stored in the file, shown in cm and inches; the pixels stay the same. For PDF and SVG it sets the size of the page. JPEG also has a quality slider (92% to start).
  • If the scale bar is on but the image has no pixel size, the panel warns you and the bar is left out. Set the pixel size under Microscopy Setup in the ROIs tab.
  • Export Movie saves every time point, or a fly-through of the Z slices. For a file with both, choose Time Points or Z Slices under Animate. A time movie shows the Z slice or projection on screen; a fly-through shows the current time point.
  • Movie formats: MP4 (H.264) is small and plays everywhere, for sharing and talks. ProRes 4444 (.mov) is visually lossless, for editing. TIFF Stack keeps every frame exactly as shown (uncompressed RGB), and Fiji opens it with the frame interval or Z step set.
  • Frame rate: 1, 2, 5, 10, 15, 24, 30 or 60 fps, or Real time when the recording's own rate is in that range. It starts at real time when it can, at 60 fps for a faster recording, and at 10 fps for a slower one or a file with no frame interval. The panel says how much faster or slower than real time the movie plays. The movie contains every acquired frame (Frame avg. does not apply).
  • Show the elapsed time (or the Z position) on each frame burns in each frame's own recorded time since the first frame, or its depth (z = 2.50 µm; the slice number when there is no Z step). The elapsed time needs a frame interval.
  • A progress bar shows while the movie is written. Cancel Export stops it and leaves no file. H.264 needs even frame sizes, so an odd size gets one black row or column, never a resample, and it allows at most 4096 pixels on a side: for larger frames, choose a smaller scale, ProRes 4444 or TIFF Stack.
  • Export Data as TIFF saves the measured pixel values of every channel, Z slice and time point, for analysis in Fiji, napari or Python. Choose OME-TIFF (written as BigTIFF above 4 GB) or ImageJ TIFF (limited to 4 GB). The file keeps the pixel size, Z step, frame interval, and channel names and colors (an ImageJ TIFF also keeps each channel's display range); for unevenly spaced frames, an OME-TIFF keeps each frame's own time. 8- and 16-bit values, signed ones included, are saved exactly. For a floating-point or calibrated image, choose Calibrated values (32-bit floating point, as the measurements use them) or Stored levels (16-bit). Colors, contrast, rotation, drift correction, ROIs and annotations are not applied. A progress bar and Cancel Export are shown here too.

For figures, turn Raw off, choose a LUT (Emission λ recommended) and optionally turn on Smoothing. For sharing data, keep Raw on and Smoothing off. To analyze the pixels elsewhere, use Export Data as TIFF, not an image or movie.

Analyze Panel

The Analyze tab controls how intensity traces are computed and displayed.

  • Sweep Mode: Raw Intensity (ADU, analog-to-digital units), ΔF/F₀ (fractional change from baseline), ΔF (absolute change), or Z-score. A Z-score's mean and SD come from the Whole Trace (SD ÷ n, as scipy.stats.zscore) or from the Baseline frames (sample SD, before frame averaging), chosen under Sweep Mode. Whole Trace compares shapes, but every transient raises its SD and lowers every z-score. Baseline reads each point in baseline noise SDs whatever the events do: in a simulation, one transient scored 6.2 against the whole trace and 14.2 against the baseline, and with two more transients, 3.9 and still 14.2.
  • Baseline (F₀): shown for ΔF/F₀, ΔF and a baseline z-score. Options: First N Frames (10 by default), Frame Range (first and last frame, numbered from 1 as in the frame counter), Sliding Percentile (below), or Time Window (start and end, in seconds: the frames whose time lies in that range, ends included, with the first frame at 0 s; each frame's recorded time when the frames are unevenly spaced). Frame counts and numbers are acquired frames, even when Frame avg. is above 1×: F₀ is the mean of exactly those frames, taken before averaging, so it never takes in frames past the end of the baseline. A Time Window needs a frame interval; without one, ΔF/F₀ and ΔF are not computed and the Data Log says so. The same happens if the baseline holds no measured frame.
  • F₀ from the Mean or the Median (window baselines): the median, with 1.4826 × the median absolute deviation as its spread, is barely moved by a transient that falls inside the baseline; its standard error, for the zero test below, is about 1.25 times the mean's.
  • A baseline of a single frame (First N Frames with N = 1, say) has no spread, so whether F₀ differs from zero can't be checked; the Data Log says so. N can't be set below 1.
  • Sliding Percentile baseline: for each frame, F₀ is a low percentile (8th by default) of the measured frames within half the window (30 s by default; frames with no frame interval) on either side, cut at the ends of the series, so it follows slow drift and photobleaching instead of fixing F₀ at the start (the usual calcium-imaging baseline, as in Dombeck et al. 2007). A low percentile of noisy frames sits below the baseline itself (1.41 noise SDs at the 8th), so F₀ is raised by that amount, using the trace's robust frame-to-frame noise: in simulation, ΔF/F₀ between events read 0.03–0.14 uncorrected and within ±0.01 corrected. Keep the window several times longer than a transient and well under the bleaching time constant. A point whose F₀ can't be told apart from zero (3 standard errors of the percentile) is a gap. With a baseline z-score, the scale is the robust SD of the trace about F₀(t).
  • Background: turn on 'Subtract background' to subtract the background ROI's mean intensity from every ROI, frame by frame, before Sweep Mode is applied. Mark a BG ROI in the ROIs tab first (the panel names the one in use). If Subtract background is on but there is no visible background ROI, the Data Log says so. When a channel also has Subtract set, each of the two channels has its own background taken off before one is subtracted from the other.
  • Subtract neuropil: F − r × Fneuropil, with r = 0.7 by default (the usual two-photon value) and editable from 0 to 2. The neuropil is a ring around each ROI from 2 px out, widened 5 px at a time until it holds 350 pixels (up to 50 px), leaving out every ROI's pixels so no cell counts as another's neuropil; it is drift-corrected like the ROI, and background-subtracted too when that is on: (F − B) − r·(Fnp − B). With a channel's Subtract set, each channel is corrected before one is subtracted from the other. The Data Log gives each ring's size. In a simulated cell picking up 0.7 of the neuropil's fluctuation, the trace's correlation with the neuropil fell from 1.00 to 0.00 and the transient kept its size.
  • Photobleaching (Off, Single or Double Exponential): an offset plus one or two exponential decays is fitted to each trace after background and neuropil subtraction, robustly (iteratively reweighted with Tukey's bisquare, so transients barely pull it), and the decay of the bleaching part is undone, F' = C + (F − C)·g(0)/g(t), so the trace keeps its starting brightness and a constant offset isn't scaled. Time constants between 1/50 of the recording and 10 times it are searched; a trace with no clear decay is left as it is, with a note, and the correction is capped at 20×. In simulation, the robust fit's time constant was within 1.3% (median; at most 7%) of the true one with transients present, against 7% (at most 21%) for a plain least-squares fit. The Data Log gives each fit's time constants. For ΔF/F₀, a Sliding Percentile baseline already follows bleaching.
  • Background-subtracted traces can go below zero, and should: near background level, noise swings both ways. Clipping at zero would bias F₀ and every ΔF/F₀ upward.
  • If F₀ is not clearly above zero (not more than three standard errors above it, as happens when background subtraction removes almost all the signal), ΔF/F₀ is not computed for that trace, because dividing by it gives meaningless values. The trace is drawn as a gap, not a flat line that would look like a non-responding cell, and the Data Log records which ROI and channel was affected. Use ΔF or Raw Intensity for such traces.
  • Measure: measures every visible, non-background ROI in every frame, for each visible channel (for a Z-stack, on the channel's Z projection), and plots the traces in the sweep graph below. Everything is measured with the settings as they are when you click Measure (ROIs, channels, Sweep Mode, baseline, frame averaging, drift correction): changing one while it runs doesn't affect that run. The traces keep those settings; after changing one, click Measure again. With more than one channel, each trace is named by ROI and channel.
  • How an ROI is measured: each frame's value is the mean of the pixels whose centers lie inside the ROI (an ROI too small to hold any pixel center takes the pixels it touches; a line takes the pixels along its center line). Parts of an ROI outside the image are left out. The result is the same for every file format, and with drift correction on it is taken on the corrected frame. The ROIs list gives each area ROI's size and the number of pixels it measures, with their area in µm², which is what the trace averages; for a small or hand-drawn outline this can differ from the outline's own area. With the view turned 90°, width and height are given as seen on screen.
  • Gaps: a frame whose plane, or any Z slice of it, can't be read is a gap in the trace, whatever the Z projection, and the Data Log counts them.
  • Image Info: size, frame count, channels, Z slices, bit depth (the detector's real depth, such as 12-bit, when the file records it), pixel size (X × Y when the pixels aren't square) and frame interval ("not set" when the file has none; marked as the median when the frames are unevenly spaced).

A Whole Trace z-score expresses each trace in its own standard deviations, which helps compare ROIs with very different baseline brightness; to judge how far an event stands above the noise, use a Baseline z-score.

The Sweep Graph

After Measure, a graph below the movie controls shows one sweep per ROI, in the ROI's color; with more than one channel, each channel of an ROI has its own dash pattern (the first solid). The Y axis label follows the Sweep Mode (F (ADU), ΔF/F₀, ΔF or Z-score). The X axis is time, or the frame number (counted from 0) when the file has no frame interval; Send to Graph and CSV export use the same X, and a graph opened by Send to Graph keeps the unit (frame or s). With frame averaging, each point sits at the center of its frames, and Send to Graph shows that through the graph's X offset. Unevenly spaced frames are sent as X-Y data, each point at its own time; filters, spectra and other analyses that assume evenly spaced samples will be wrong on them, as the Data Log notes.

Sweep navigation (as in Graph windows):

  • Tab / Shift-Tab: next / previous sweep. Stops at the first and last sweep.
  • Space (images with one time point; in a T-series Space plays the movie): show one more sweep, returning to all sweeps after the last.
  • Comma key: first sweep. Period key: last sweep.
  • The header bar has matching navigation buttons, an All button to show every sweep again, and Clear, which removes the traces.
  • While stepping through sweeps, the others stay in the legend, dimmed.

Axis interaction on the sweep graph:

  • Drag in the plot area: zoom both axes to the rectangle.
  • Drag on the Y-axis strip: zoom Y only.
  • Drag on the X-axis tick numbers: zoom X only.
  • Double-click the Y-axis strip or X-axis tick numbers: type exact axis limits.
  • Double-click the plot area: zoom to fit the data.
  • The X and Y pencil buttons above the graph rename the axis labels: X takes a name and a unit (such as "Time" / "s"), Y a single label (such as "ΔF/F₀"). Only the label changes, not the numbers. Reset restores the default label.

Send to Graph

Send to Graph (in the sweep graph header) opens all traces together on one graph in a Graph window, with the Y unit of the Sweep Mode the traces were measured with, so you can use its grouping, statistics and export tools. If the window from the last Send to Graph is still open, it is reused.

After sending to Graph, use the Grouping panel to arrange sweeps, or Groups mode to compare ROIs side by side.

Export CSV

The Export CSV toolbar button (available after Measure) saves the measured traces as a CSV file: a Time(s) column (Frame when the file has no frame interval), then one column per trace, labeled with the ROI name (and channel, when there is more than one). Values are exactly those in the sweep graph, with the Sweep Mode (ΔF/F₀, Z-score and so on) applied.

Z-Stacks and Z Projection

For a Z-stack (such as an ND2, LSM or CZI file), the main canvas shows one Z slice by default. Check 'Show Z Projection' in the Channels panel to show the stack combined using each channel's Z proj. setting. For a Z-stack saved as a folder of TIFFs, open the folder, then check 'Treat T-series as Z-stack' in the Channels panel.

Z projection modes:

  • Max Intensity: the brightest value through the stack at each pixel. Best for showing the full extent of labeled structures (dendrites, fibers).
  • Mean: the average through the stack. Less noisy, but can look dim because background counts equally.
  • Sum: the total through the stack, proportional to the photons detected at each position. When the largest sum the stack could hold, slices × the bit depth's maximum, is too large for a 16-bit image, the image shows the sum divided by the smallest whole number that fits, and the Channels panel says by how much ("Sum shown ÷ 2"). The factor is the same at every time point, channel and in exported movies, so displayed sums compare across the series. Measure an ROI for the true sum.
  • Min Intensity: the dimmest value through the stack. Useful for transmitted-light images or dark objects on a bright background.
  • Standard Deviation: how much each pixel varies through the stack (the sample SD, ÷ (n − 1), as in ImageJ), highlighting regions that change most. The image shows it rounded to whole counts; ROI measurements use the exact value.

Auto sets the range for the selected projection, since projections (especially Standard Deviation) can have very different value ranges from a single slice.

With more than one channel, an All Channels row below the channel rows has its own Auto button and Z proj. picker, which apply to every channel still following them. Using a channel's own Auto or Z proj. makes just that channel stop following them.

T-series, Z-stacks, and Combined T×Z Files

A badge next to the frame counter shows what is loaded: "T-series: N", "Z-stack: N", "T×Z series: N×N" or "Single frame". Hover over it for the counts; for a T×Z file it also explains the two playback bars.

The main transport below the canvas always steps through time (T). When the file has more than one Z slice, a separate Z transport with its own Play/Pause and speed control appears above it. The two can play separately or together. When the file records a Z step, the Z transport shows the current slice's depth from the first (z = 2.5 µm).

Each transport has Play/Pause (Space plays a T-series), step buttons, a scrubber and a Speed menu (1 to 30 fps); the time transport also has a Loop button. For time, the Speed menu also offers Real time when the file has a frame interval: it plays at the recording's own rate, so a slow time-lapse plays as slowly as it was recorded, and a fast recording skips frames to keep time.

Orthogonal Views

'Show Orthogonal Views (XZ/YZ)' (Channels panel, on by default for Z-stacks; a saved or duplicated stack keeps it as it was shown, with its Show Z Projection, XZ/YZ projection, Z slice and Min/Max) shows XZ and YZ views below the canvas, which is the XY view. Each has two panes: on top, the whole stack projected using the 'XZ/YZ proj:' setting; below, the slice at the current Y (for XZ) or X (for YZ). XZ and YZ are drawn at true physical proportions, using the X and Y pixel sizes and the Z step.

  • The X / Y / Z steppers above the panes move the position, and all panes update together. The Z transport's Play button animates through Z.
  • Show Z Projection (Channels panel, available for any Z-stack) switches the main canvas from a single Z slice to the Z projection chosen by each channel's Z proj. setting, whether or not Orthogonal Views is showing. The top XZ/YZ panes use their own 'XZ/YZ proj:' setting. While the canvas shows the projection with Orthogonal Views off, the Z slider and its buttons are dimmed, as there is no slice to step through.
  • The XZ panes show a yellow line for X and the YZ panes a light-blue line for Y, matching the main canvas crosshair, plus a white line for the current Z plane.
  • To move the position, use the steppers, drag anywhere in an XZ or YZ pane, or drag the crosshair on the main canvas (with any tool). Grabbing near one line moves only that axis; grabbing near the crossing moves both.
  • All panes use the same LUT, display range and colors as the main canvas, and follow drift correction when it is on.
  • Scale bars: a checkbox next to the 'XZ/YZ proj:' picker adds a scale bar to each pane. The panes share one physical scale, so the bar reads correctly along either axis. Each bar picks black or white from its own pane.

Orthogonal Views works on any Z-stack: a native one (ND2/CZI/LSM) or a folder of TIFFs opened with 'Treat T-series as Z-stack' checked.

Supported File Formats

  • TIFF (.tif, .tiff): 8-, 16- or 32-bit integer samples, signed or not, and 32- or 64-bit floating point, uncompressed or compressed with LZW, Deflate or PackBits, in strips or tiles, including BigTIFF. 12- and 14-bit cameras store theirs as 16, or packed: packed 9- to 15-bit samples open as they are, a 10-bit image with its 0–1,023 range. Other kinds (1-bit, 16-bit floating point, and JPEG or zstd compression) are refused with a message saying why.
  • TIFF channels and stacks: the red, green and blue of an RGB image are its three channels. A multi-page TIFF with no ImageJ or OME information opens as a T-series, and the Data Log says so (check Treat T-series as Z-stack for a Z-stack). A stack saved by tifffile (Python, napari) with its axes opens with its channels, Z and T.
  • TIFF values: signed 8- and 16-bit samples (in plain, OME and ImageJ TIFFs, folders of TIFFs and Prairie View) are stored shifted up by half the range (32,768 for 16-bit), and Min, Max and every ROI measurement use the true signed value, exactly. 32-bit integer and floating-point data (deconvolved or processed stacks, say) is read once for each channel's range. Whole numbers spanning at most 65,535 are kept exactly; other data is kept as 65,536 evenly spaced levels across each channel's own range, so rounding is at most half a level, 1/131,070 of that channel's range (below the noise of nearly all data), and a ratio channel beside an intensity channel keeps its precision. Min, Max and every ROI measurement are in the file's values. The Data Log gives each channel's range and level spacing, and counts any NaN or infinite values, which are stored as the lowest value.
  • ImageJ TIFF: an ImageJ hyperstack opens with its channels, Z slices and time points, pixel size, Z spacing and frame interval; stacks over 4 GB (which ImageJ writes with a single page header) open too. An ImageJ composite opens in its saved channel colors, and an ImageJ or Fiji TIFF opens with the display range it was saved with (each channel's, for a composite), as in Fiji; click Auto to fit the range to the data instead. A description written in Latin-1, as older ImageJ versions did, is read too, so its µm is recognized.
  • ImageJ calibration: ImageJ's straight-line value calibration (value = c0 + c1 × stored) is applied to every ROI measurement, so a "16-bit signed" image, which ImageJ stores with an offset of −32,768, measures its true values instead of 32,768 too high (which shrinks every ΔF/F₀), and Min and Max are shown in calibrated values. The Data Log names the calibration, or says when one isn't applied (a non-linear function).
  • ImageJ stack layout: a header whose channels × slices × frames don't match the file's pages isn't used: the pages open as time points, without the header's frame interval, and the Data Log says so. A stack whose header gives only its image count opens as a time series, where ImageJ shows a Z-stack; check Treat T-series as Z-stack for one. A plain stack (slices only, no hyperstack) opens as a Z-stack, as in ImageJ, unless it records a frame interval and no Z spacing, which marks a recording over time: its images then open as time points and the Data Log says so. Treat Z-stack as T-series switches any other.
  • OME-TIFF (.ome.tif): channels, Z and T in any DimensionOrder, including sets split across several files. Channel names, colors and emission wavelengths, pixel size, Z step and frame interval are read from the OME-XML, in any of OME's length units from femtometers to kilometers (inches and feet too) and time units from femtoseconds to days; a size or interval in a unit @Graph doesn't know is not used, and the Data Log says so. A file describing several images opens each in its own window. Signed 8- and 16-bit and 32- and 64-bit floating-point OME-TIFFs open as plain TIFFs of those types do. When a file's OME-XML can't be used, it opens as a plain TIFF only if it has no separate channels or Z planes; otherwise it is refused with the reason, since its channels and planes would be taken for time points.
  • Prairie View (.xml plus its .tif files): T-series and Z-series from Prairie View 4 and 5, with any number of channels and cycles. Drop the .xml file or the folder. Bit depth, pixel size, channel names, the Z step (from the recorded stage positions, adding up every Z device) and the time between time points (from the recorded frame times) are read from the XML. A Z-series opens as a Z-stack per cycle. A bidirectional Z-series is put in one direction, and the Data Log says which stacks were turned. Image files the XML names but the folder lacks, and planes with no file, show as blank and are listed in the Data Log.
  • Folder of TIFFs: opens as a T-series in natural name order ('Treat T-series as Z-stack' turns it into a Z-stack). A folder of ImageJ Z-stacks, one per time point, opens as a Z-stack over time; a folder holding an OME-TIFF set opens as that set. A folder of TIFFs saved by Fiji takes its pixel size from the first file. Files with their own channels, or with both Z and T, are refused (open one of them instead).
  • Zeiss CZI (.czi): from Zeiss Axioscope / LSM 800 / 900 / 980 / Airyscan. Multi-channel, Z, T and combined T×Z acquisitions, 8- or 16-bit, uncompressed or saved with ZEN's lossless (zstd) compression. Channel colors, names, voxel sizes (X, Y and Z) and the time between frames are read from the file. Each scene opens as its own image, a tile scan is stitched from its tiles at their recorded positions, and a color camera's exposure (DAPI, GFP, DIC…), recorded in red, green and blue, opens as one channel, their sum, in the color that holds its signal (gray when none does); Settings ▸ Imaging can keep the three colors as channels instead, for a stained brightfield image. A line scan along Z (XZ or XZ-T) opens as one image per time point with Z as the vertical axis. CZIs compressed with JPEG XR, JPEG or LZW are refused with a message saying what to do (save from ZEN uncompressed or with zstd, or export as OME-TIFF).
  • Zeiss LSM (.lsm): LSM 510 / 710 / 780 / 880. Multi-channel Z-stacks, time series and T×Z, 8-bit, 12-bit and floating point, including files over 4 GB. Channel names and colors, voxel sizes (X, Y and Z) and the time between time points (from the recorded time stamps) are read from the file. A line scan opens as one image with time down the vertical axis, and an X-Z scan as one image per time point with Z down it. Only the first position of a multi-position file opens; the Data Log says how many more it holds.
  • Nikon ND2 (.nd2): files from NIS-Elements 3 and later, 8- or 16-bit, uncompressed or with lossless compression, multi-channel, including combined T×Z acquisitions (a Z-stack at every time point; see "T-series, Z-stacks, and Combined T×Z Files" above). Z and T are taken from the acquisition's own loops. Channel names, colors, emission wavelengths, pixel size (X, Y and Z step) and the time between time points (from the recorded frame times) are read from the file. Each XY position of a multipoint acquisition opens as its own image, named as in NIS-Elements, and a color camera's exposure (DAPI, GFP, DIC…), recorded in red, green and blue, opens as one channel, their sum, in the color that holds its signal (gray when none does); Settings ▸ Imaging can keep the three colors as channels instead, for a stained brightfield image. When every channel has the same description (a color camera calls each exposure "Brightfield"), channels are named by their optical configurations instead. Lossy (JPEG 2000) files are refused with a message.
  • Leica LIF (.lif): LAS X files. Every image series in the file opens in its own window, titled with the file and series names. 8- to 16-bit, multi-channel (including color camera images), Z, T and combined T×Z. Channel names (the dye when the file records one, else the detector), pixel size (X, Y and Z step) and the time between time points are read from the file. An XT line scan opens as one image with time as the vertical axis, and an XZ or XZ-T line scan as one image per time point with Z as the vertical axis (at the Z step); a tile scan shows its first tile. Series that can't be shown (spectral λ scans, floating-point data) are skipped, with the reason in the Data Log.
  • Leica FLIM (FALCON / STELLARIS): the images LAS X computes join the series as extra channels, hidden at first (check Show FLIM channels in the Channels panel, then each channel's own box): Intensity and Phasor Intensity in photon counts, Fast Flim and Standard Deviation as lifetimes in picoseconds. Phasor Real and Phasor Imaginary (signed values) and the raw photon data are not read.

Files that record channel colors (CZI, LIF, LSM, ND2, OME-TIFF) open in those colors. Otherwise a single-channel image opens in Raw, and channels 1 to 4 of a multi-channel image use default emission colors.

Saving and Reopening Image Sessions (.@graph)

An Image window can be saved as a .@graph file that recreates it exactly: pixel data, ROIs, display settings and analysis settings. Use File → Save (⌘S).

  • What is saved: all pixel data, losslessly, as the exact detector values, and the original bit depth, so 8- and 12-bit data keep their display scale; all ROIs (position, name, color, background flag, visibility); each channel's name, LUT, color, display range, visibility and Subtract setting; the frame interval; Sweep Mode, all baseline settings, background and neuropil subtraction, photobleaching correction and frame averaging; drift correction; the microscope setup and pixel size; the scale bar and its position and color; text, line and arrow overlays; axis labels; smoothing and rotation; which channels are FLIM channels, each channel's link to the master Auto and Z proj, and the Auto-Detect ROIs settings.
  • Large images save and open one plane at a time, so a big multichannel series never has to fit in memory all at once.
  • Unsaved changes: the window title shows • only when something that is saved has changed. Moving through frames, playing, or opening Orthogonal Views does not count.
  • Self-contained: the file includes the pixel data, so it does not need the original image files.
  • Reopening: double-click the file or drag it onto @Graph. The window reopens with its ROIs and settings; click Measure to extract the traces again.
  • File size grows with image size and frame count: a 600-frame, 64×106-pixel single-channel recording is about 8 MB (2 bytes per pixel, per frame, per channel).
  • Save (⌘S): once a window has been saved or was opened from a .@graph file, ⌘S saves to that file without asking. The first save asks where to save.
  • Save As (⇧⌘S): saves to a new name or location; the window then belongs to the new file, and later ⌘S saves go there.

Save with ⌘S after drawing ROIs and setting up the display; reopening the file later brings you back to exactly that point.

Copy and Paste Image Style

Display settings and ROIs can be copied from one Image window and pasted onto another, without touching the pixel data, to give a set of recordings the same LUT, display range and ROI layout.

  • Copy: press ⌥⌘C, click Copy Style in the toolbar, or right-click the image and choose Copy Style.
  • Paste: in the target window, press ⌥⌘V, click Paste Style, or right-click and choose Paste Style. Paste Style is grayed out until a style has been copied.
  • What is copied: each channel's LUT, color, display range, emission wavelength, Raw, Smoothing, Z projection and Subtract channel; Sweep Mode, baseline, Subtract background, neuropil subtraction, photobleaching correction and the scale bar; all ROIs. Pasted ROIs replace the target's ROIs; a style copied with no ROIs leaves the target's. ⌘Z undoes the whole paste.
  • If the source has more channels than the target, settings go to the first channels and an alert says so. Extra channels in the target are left unchanged.
  • If the images differ in size, ROIs are rescaled to the same relative position (an ROI 25% in from the top-left stays 25% in).

Close and Quit Protection

@Graph warns before discarding unsaved work:

  • Closing a window (⌘W) with unsaved changes (a • in its title) asks whether to Save, Don't Save, or Cancel.
  • Quit (⌘Q): if any window has unsaved changes, an alert lists them and offers Review Changes… (the default: brings each window forward in turn with its own Save / Don't Save / Cancel; canceling stops the quit), Cancel, or Discard Changes and Quit. A window that has never been saved stops the review: save it, then quit again.

The • appears in a window's title after any change since the last save, including display changes such as the LUT, display range or ROI edits. Save with ⌘S to clear it.

Omics Visualization

These chart types open in an ordinary chart window with the same Chart inspector: Volcano Plot (with its MA Plot mode), Heatmap, Dot Plot, Cluster Scatter, GSEA Bar, Grouped Diverging Bar, Venn Diagram, UpSet Plot, Manhattan Plot, Stacked Composition Bar and PCA Plot. They are grouped under "Omics Visualization" in the Chart Type picker. All except MA Plot (a display mode of Volcano Plot), Stacked Composition Bar and PCA Plot (which you reach by switching chart type) have a build command in the Omics Plot menu: File → Omics Plot, the Text Tools menu's Omics Plot submenu, or the Omics Plot button in the Spreadsheet toolbar. The same menu also has Compute GSEA (Permutation)….

Volcano Plot

Plots log₂ fold change (X) against −log₁₀(p-value) (Y), the standard view for differential expression and other large-scale comparisons. Points are colored Up, Down or Not Significant by adjustable fold-change and significance thresholds, and significant points are drawn on top of the gray ones.

  • Two ways to build one. 'Compute from Replicates': assign columns to Group A and Group B (at least 2 each; fold change is B relative to A) and choose a Significance Test: Moderated t-test (eBayes), the default for new analyses, or Welch's t-test. The moderated t-test (limma's empirical Bayes method) borrows information about variability from all features, so it gives steadier and more powerful results with the few replicates typical of omics experiments. A saved chart keeps the test it was made with. Either way, p-values get Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) FDR correction. 'Already Have Fold Change / p-values': choose existing columns (such as DESeq2, edgeR or limma output), which are plotted as they are; if there is no adjusted p-value column, BH-FDR is calculated from the raw p-values.
  • Column roles are pre-filled from header names (such as 'log2FC', 'padj', 'FDR', limma's 'P.Value'). Check them before generating. A table exported from R, whose header has one name fewer than the rows (no corner cell), is read with the names in the right columns; quoted cells and decimal commas are read too.
  • The setup window warns when row names repeat (every row is still plotted) and counts cells it could not read as numbers; "NA" and blank cells are treated as missing.
  • 'Read as' says how the fold-change column is read: Log₂ fold change (used as is), Signed linear (4 is 4-fold up, −4 is 4-fold down) or Ratio (4 is 4-fold up, 0.25 is 4-fold down). The setup window guesses and says why: a header that says log2 means log₂; otherwise negative values together with values between −1 and 1 mean log₂, negative values with none between −1 and 1 mean signed linear, and all-positive values mean a ratio. Change it if the guess is wrong. Values that are invalid for the chosen reading (a signed value between −1 and 1, a ratio of 0 or below) are counted and left out, and the chart's statistics note states the reading used.
  • A p-value outside 0 to 1 is not a probability: it is counted in the setup window and left out of the plot, the significance count and the Benjamini-Hochberg correction.
  • 'Compute from Replicates' asks what scale the values are on: Already log₂, Linear intensities or Raw counts. Linear intensities are log₂-transformed before the test. Zeros (and negative values) have no log, so you choose what happens to them: Treat as missing (the default, since a zero intensity usually means not detected) leaves them out like empty cells, and Add pseudocount adds half the smallest positive value in the table to every value first. With a pseudocount, a row with a zero gets a fold change that depends on that offset: (83, 0, 135, 104) against (30, 59, 83, 112) gives log₂FC +0.57 with a pseudocount of 3.3 and −0.72 without. A fixed +1 on small linear values (TPM, ratios, relative qPCR quantities) would shrink every fold change, so it is not used. The chart's statistics note states the choice, the offset and how many values were left out.
  • Raw counts (RNA-seq read counts) follow the edgeR and limma workflow: features with too few counts to analyze are filtered out (edgeR's filterByExpr), libraries are normalized for sequencing depth and composition (TMM), counts are converted to log₂ counts per million (log-CPM, prior count 2), and the moderated t-test runs with an intensity-dependent prior (limma-trend), so the Significance Test is set to the moderated t for counts. The fold change is the difference in mean log-CPM between the groups. The statistics note reports how many features were filtered and the normalization factors. Counts must be complete: a feature with a blank or negative count is left out, and the setup window says how many.
  • Intensity-dependent prior (limma-trend): with the moderated t-test on log or linear intensities, this checkbox (off by default) lets the prior variance follow average intensity, since low-abundance features are noisier (limma's eBayes with trend = TRUE). It is recommended for proteomics and other intensity data, and always on for raw counts.
  • Minimum valid values per group (2 by default; 0 turns the filter off): features with fewer valid values than this in either group are left out before testing, so they enter neither the variance prior nor the FDR correction (common proteomics practice, as in Perseus and DEP). With zeros treated as missing, a zero is not a valid value. The setup window shows how many features the setting removes. Raw counts are filtered by filterByExpr instead.
  • Scale checks: @Graph checks your values against the scale you chose and lets you know when they don't seem to match: values that look already log-transformed when you chose Linear intensities or Raw counts (the check used by NCBI's GEO2R), values that look linear when you chose Already log₂, and whole numbers that look like read counts when you chose Linear intensities or Already log₂.
  • Moderated t-test (eBayes) follows limma. With the minimum-valid filter off, a row with values in only one group is not tested, but its variance still helps estimate the shared prior. With the filter at 1 or off, a row with exactly one value in each group has no variance of its own, so, as in limma, it is tested on the shared prior variance alone, and its p-value rests on the other rows (with no prior, it is not tested); a row with one value in one group and two or more in the other is tested as usual.
  • The moderated t matches limma, lmFit then eBayes. The shared prior is fit by limma's fitFDist when every row has the same residual df, and by fitFDistUnequalDF1 when missing values make the df differ (limma's default since 3.62). With limma-trend, the prior follows average expression, as eBayes(trend = TRUE) fits it: a natural-spline trend when every row has the same residual df, and fitFDistUnequalDF1's weighted lowess trend when missing values make the df differ, as in current limma. As in limma, any 3 or more rows give a prior. The chart's statistics note gives the prior (d₀ and s₀²) and how many rows it rests on, and calls it weak with fewer than 10, and unreliable when more than half of the rows' variances are exactly zero (where limma warns that eBayes is unreliable). With fewer than 3 rows, or too few nonzero variances, no prior can be fit, and the note says each gene got an ordinary pooled t-test instead.
  • Effect sizes: for statistics computed by @Graph, each feature gets its log₂ fold change with a confidence interval (at the level in Settings → Statistics), its standard error, t statistic and degrees of freedom. They appear in the point popup, the chart's spreadsheet and exported tables (log2FoldChange, its CI bounds, SE, t and df, then pValue and adjustedPValue). For the moderated t-test the interval matches limma's topTable(confint = TRUE); for Welch's t-test it is the Welch interval, as R's t.test gives it.
  • Imported p-values without adjusted p-values: @Graph computes BH adjusted p-values over the rows you pasted. If the list was filtered (only significant genes, say), that correction is too lenient, so @Graph warns when the p-values look filtered, and you can enter the full count in 'Tests in the original analysis' so the correction counts every test, as R's p.adjust with n does. The figure caption states the number of tests used.
  • When nothing is significant, @Graph explains why. If some features pass the p-value cut-off but none changes enough to pass the fold-change cut-off, it says so and gives the largest fold change among them, so you can lower the fold-change cut-off in Plot Style.
