  • A p-value of exactly 0 (numerical underflow) is plotted just above both the smallest non-zero p-value and the significance line, so it is never drawn below a line it counts as passing; the figure caption says so when it applies.
  • Thresholds (Plot Style): a point is Up or Down when its |log₂FC| is at or above Fold change (1 by default, a 2-fold change) and its p-value is below Significance (0.05 by default). "Use adjusted p-value for y-axis and thresholds" is on by default; turn it off to use raw p-values. The Up, Down and N.S. colors, point size and opacity are set there too. The Y axis title follows the choice ("-Log10(Adjusted P-value)" or "-Log10(P-value)") unless you typed your own. A feature with a log₂ fold change of exactly 0 is never Up or Down.
  • The figure caption states how the statistics were obtained (computed here, with the test, filters, normalization and prior used, or imported), the thresholds used and how many features are up and down.
  • Volcano Plot can be chosen as the Chart Type only for a chart that already holds volcano data.
  • The AI Assistant can read an open Volcano Plot's results (each feature's log₂ fold change with its CI, then p and adjusted p) and change its thresholds.

Build one from selected text with File → Omics Plot → Volcano Plot from Selected Text… (⌥⌘L), the Text Tools menu's Omics Plot submenu, or the Spreadsheet toolbar's Omics Plot menu. Smart Visualization also sends data with fold-change and p-value columns here.

When @Graph calculates the adjusted p-values itself, a note says so in Plot Style and in each point's popup.

MA Plot

Plots average expression (X, log₂) against log₂ fold change (Y). M is the log ratio and A the average log intensity, terms from microarray analysis. MA Plot is a display mode of a Volcano Plot, using the same data; switch with the Display control at the top of Plot Style.

  • Mean expression (A) is on the log₂ scale. With 'Compute from Replicates' it is the average log₂ value over all samples, the same as limma's AveExpr (mean log-CPM for raw counts). With 'Already Have Fold Change / p-values', choose a Mean Expression column in the setup window (DESeq2's 'baseMean', limma's 'AveExpr' and edgeR's 'logCPM' are recognized); the header decides the scale first: one that says log or AveExpr is used as is, and baseMean or any other mean without "log" is converted to log₂ (a mean of 0, such as a gene with no counts, has no log₂ and is left off the MA plot). For any other header, negative values mean the column is already on a log scale. Without one, MA mode is unavailable and the Display control explains why.
  • Reference lines: a solid line at M = 0 (no change) and the fold-change thresholds as two horizontal dashed lines.
  • Switching modes resets the title and axis labels to suit the new mode; later edits are kept.

Aesthetic Mapping

Works in both Volcano and MA modes. Any extra column in your data (one not used for name, fold change, p-value or mean expression) can drive point color, size, shape or opacity. Extra columns are those marked Exclude in 'Compute from Replicates', or left unassigned in 'Already Have Fold Change / p-values'. Their numbers are read with the table's own decimal separator, so decimal commas (12,5) work.

  • Color: a gradient between two colors you choose, replacing the Up/Down coloring. The legend becomes a gradient bar with the column name and its low/high values.
  • Size: scaled between a minimum and maximum, with a min/mid/max legend.
  • Shape: each distinct value gets its own symbol (circle, square, triangle, diamond, cross), with a legend. Beyond five values the rest share the fifth symbol, labeled "Other" in the legend. Best for a column with a few categories.
  • Opacity: scaled between a minimum and maximum.
  • The four are independent and optional; with none mapped, the plot uses the plain Up/Down coloring.

Mappings can also be set in the setup window's 'Aesthetic Mapping' section (shown when extra columns exist), so the plot opens with them. In the setup window, a column can drive only one aesthetic at a time.

Labeling Individual Genes

Works in both Volcano and MA modes. Click a point to see its name, log₂ fold change (with its confidence interval, SE, t and df when @Graph computed the statistics), p-value and adjusted p-value, plus its value for any mapped column. Check 'Show name on plot' in that popup to label the point on the chart.

  • Labels are saved with the chart.
  • Plot Style lists the labeled genes under 'Labeled Genes (n)'; Clear All removes them all.
  • Removing one label or clearing all can be undone with ⌘Z.

Type in 'Search / highlight gene…' (Plot Style) to outline and label the matching points, whatever the case. Exports, prints and copies show them as the window does; clear the search to leave them out.

Labeling Genes by Criteria

'Label Genes Matching Criteria' in Plot Style labels many genes at once.

  • Two optional thresholds: minimum |log₂ fold change| and a p-value the gene must be below (shown as 'Adj. p-value' when adjusted p-values are in use), the same strictly-below rule as the significance threshold.
  • A category filter: Any, Significant (Up or Down), Up only, or Down only.
  • The button shows how many genes match, such as 'Label Matching Genes (47)', and adds them all to the labels in one click.

Heatmap

A grid of colored cells, one per row × column value, for expression matrices, correlation matrices or any numeric table.

  • Data: a table with row names in the first column and column names in the first row; every other cell is a number. Opening a .gct, GEO series matrix, .loom or .h5ad file builds one directly (see Supported File Formats).
  • Normalize (Plot Style): None (raw values), Z-score per row, or Z-score per column (each value minus the row or column mean, divided by its sample SD, as pheatmap and seaborn scale). A row or column whose values are all the same has no variation to scale, so its z-scores are 0 (the mid color; R gives NaN there), and the inspector says how many there are. Z-score per row is the default. A separate Log2 transform toggle is applied first (with "Add 1 first" it is log₂(x + 1), which keeps zero counts); leave it off if the data are already log₂.
  • Color scale: Low/Mid/High colors, blue–white–red by default, or a preset (the colorblind-safe ColorBrewer Red–Blue and Purple–Green, or the classic Blue–Black–Red). The value range is automatic unless you set Min and Max.
  • The automatic range follows ComplexHeatmap's default. Data of both signs in similar amounts (between 25% and 75% of the non-zero values positive: z-scores, fold changes) get a scale centered on 0 that reaches the 99th percentile of |value|; any other data, including mostly-positive data with a few negatives, count as one-sided. One-sided data (raw or log₂ expression) run from the 1st to the 99th percentile, with the mid color halfway. With fewer than 100 distinct values the full data range is used. Using percentiles rather than the extremes stops one outlier from washing every other cell out toward the mid color: cells beyond the range take the end color, and the legend marks a clipped end with ≥ or ≤. A manual range puts the mid color at 0 when it spans 0, otherwise halfway. A manual Min at or above Max has no scale: the inspector says so and the automatic range is used until it is fixed. The methods sentence states the limits and how they were chosen.
  • 'Show axis' (Plot Style) shows or hides the border, and 'Show legend' (Axes & Labels) the color scale. Row and column names are always shown, thinned when there are too many to fit. Swap Rows and Columns transposes the matrix.
  • 'Hide all-zero rows' and 'Hide all-zero columns' hide rows or columns with no non-zero values, useful for sparse single-cell matrices. Clustering is unavailable while either is on.
  • Cell grid lines are on by default but switch off automatically when the table has 30 or more rows or columns; turn 'Show cell grid' (Axes & Labels) back on if you want them.
  • Drag a rectangle to zoom (drag again to zoom further), and double-click to return to the full view. Dendrograms are hidden while zoomed.

Build one with Smart Visualization (⇧⌘V) on a pasted matrix, with File → Omics Plot → Heatmap from Selected Text… (⌥⌘M), or by opening a .gct, GEO series matrix, .loom or .h5ad file.

Clustering & Dendrograms

Rows and columns can each be hierarchically clustered, so similar ones sit together, with a dendrogram drawn alongside.

  • 'Cluster rows' and 'Cluster columns' (Plot Style) are independent.
  • Linkage (Complete, Average (UPGMA), Single or Ward) appears when either is on. New heatmaps use Complete, the default of pheatmap, ComplexHeatmap and R's hclust; a saved heatmap keeps the linkage it was saved with.
  • The tree and leaf order are exactly those of R's hclust (the order pheatmap draws), including how tied distances are resolved. Results are reused until the data or clustering settings change, so redrawing a large clustered heatmap is fast.
  • Clustering changes only the display order; turning it off restores the original order. Labels, search and click-to-inspect follow the displayed order.
  • Clustering works on the plotted values (after Log2 and normalization) and is limited to 2,000 rows or columns.
  • Distance (Plot Style, under Linkage): Euclidean (the default) compares levels; Correlation (Pearson) is 1 − r, so rows that rise and fall together sit together whatever their level (pheatmap's "correlation", R's as.dist(1 − cor())); Correlation (Spearman) is 1 − ρ on ranks, so one extreme value matters less.
  • Ward is Ward's method (R's hclust "ward.D2"). Ward needs Euclidean distances, so with a correlation distance it uses √(2(1 − r)), the Euclidean distance between the standardized profiles, and rows that correlate best still merge first. This matches R's hclust on sqrt(2 * (1 - cor)) with "ward.D2" (pheatmap applies Ward to 1 − r directly, so its tree can differ); a note under Linkage and the methods sentence say so.
  • With Euclidean distance, a pair with missing values is compared over the coordinates both have, scaled up to the full length, as R's dist() does. With correlation, it is correlated over the coordinates both have (R's use = "pairwise.complete.obs"). A correlation with a constant row or column has no value (R stops with an error there), so that pair is treated as uncorrelated (r = 0).
  • Optimal leaf ordering (off by default) flips dendrogram branches so the most similar neighbors sit side by side (Bar-Joseph et al. 2001, as R's seriation and cba packages do). The tree and its clusters stay the same; only the drawing order changes. Off, each merge's earlier cluster is drawn first. Above about 1,000 rows or columns it takes a few seconds the first time.
  • The Chart Legend box's methods sentence states the transform, the z-scoring, the distance, the linkage and the leaf ordering.

Search & Highlight

The 'Search rows/columns…' field in Plot Style finds row and column names containing the text (any case). Matches are shown in bold orange with an orange band across the row or column.

Label Density

When there is less than about 8 px per label, @Graph draws every Nth row or column label instead of all of them, and a caption below the chart says so ('Showing every Nth label…'). Enlarge the window to see more labels.

Click-to-Inspect

Click any cell to see its row and column names and raw value, plus its log₂ value when Log2 transform is on and its Z-score when a Z-score normalization is on.

Editing the Underlying Table

The spreadsheet button (table icon) in the chart window's toolbar opens the matrix in a table that handles genomics-scale data (tens of thousands of rows, thousands of columns).

  • Double-click any cell to edit its value directly.
  • Click a row to select it; click a column header to select the whole column.
  • Click and drag across cells to select a rectangular block, then ⌘C to copy it as tab-delimited text.
  • The leftmost columns show the row index and row (gene/feature) name side by side.
  • Edits can be undone with ⌘Z.

To save a heatmap matrix (or any chart's data) as a file, use Export → CSV (.csv) or TSV (.tsv); see Exporting. This works even for very large matrices.

Venn Diagram

Compares 2 or 3 named sets of items (gene names, protein IDs, sample lists) as overlapping circles, each region labeled with its count. Matching is exact and case-sensitive, as in R and UpSetR, ignoring only surrounding spaces: ' TP53 ' and 'TP53' are the same item, but 'TP53' (human) and 'Tp53' are not, so use one naming convention across all sets. Missing-value placeholders (NA, N/A, NaN, NULL, None, ".", "-" and "--", in any capitalization) are not items, and quote marks around a name (as R's write.csv writes them) are removed. Venn Diagram can be chosen as the Chart Type only for a chart that already holds set data.

  • Build one with File → Omics Plot → Venn Diagram from Selected Text… (or the Text Tools menu's Omics Plot submenu, or the Spreadsheet toolbar's Omics Plot menu) from a table with one set per column: the set name in the header row and one item per line, 2 or 3 columns.
  • With 2 sets, circle sizes and overlap are drawn to scale. Each count sits in the middle of its region; a region too small for its count (or with no area, such as an empty overlap) is labeled just outside, with a thin leader line to it. With 3 sets a fixed triangular layout is used, not to scale. Counts and percentages are always exact.
  • A set with no items yet is not drawn: a note above the diagram names it, so it can't be mistaken for a set of one item. Add items with Edit List or Pull from Volcano Plot. (With 3 sets its circle stays in the layout and its regions show 0.)
  • Each set's row in the inspector shows its item count, a color picker, 'Edit List…' (replace the items, one per line) and, when there are 3 sets, Remove (undo with ⌘Z). Click a set's name to edit it. 'Add a 3rd Set' appears when there are only 2.
  • 'Show percentages' adds each region's share of all distinct items under its count.
  • The figure caption states the layout (to scale with 2 sets, schematic with 3), what each count means, the matching rule and, with a background size, the fold enrichment and overlap test.
  • Export → CSV or TSV, and the chart's spreadsheet, give the sets in the paste format: one column per set, its name in the header and its items below. Editing them in the spreadsheet updates the diagram (keeping 2 or 3 sets).

Overlap Significance

To test whether an overlap is larger than chance, @Graph needs the size of the background the sets were drawn from (such as every gene tested). Enter it as 'Background size' in the Plot Style panel's 'Overlap Significance' box; leave it blank to skip the test.

  • Click an overlap region to see its items (Copy puts the list on the clipboard, one per line). With a background size entered, the popup also shows the fold enrichment (items the sets share, divided by the number expected if they were independent samples from the background, as SuperExactTest reports it) and a one-tailed hypergeometric p-value for over-representation (below 0.001 in scientific notation, such as 6.5e-12). Both count every item the sets share, including any also in another set. The background must be at least the number of distinct items across all the sets; if it is smaller, the inspector says so and no p-value is shown.
  • 2-set overlaps use the standard hypergeometric test; the all-three overlap uses its exact multi-set extension (as in SuperExactTest).
  • The test asks only whether the overlap is larger than expected by chance, not whether its items are individually significant.
  • When a set is pulled from (or sent from) a Volcano Plot, the Overlap Significance box suggests the number of features that plot tested as the background, with a Use button: every tested feature could have made the list, so they are the population the overlap is tested against. With sets from two Volcano Plots it suggests the features tested in both.

Sending / Pulling from a Volcano Plot

A Venn set can be filled with the significant genes of an open Volcano Plot, for example to compare hits across several differential-expression analyses.

  • Pull (from the Venn diagram): each set row has a 'Pull from Volcano Plot…' menu listing the open Volcano Plots. Choose one and a direction (Up only, Down only, or Up or Down).
  • Send (from the Volcano Plot): in its 'Send to Venn Diagram' section, pick a direction, then a set in an open Venn diagram, or 'New Venn Diagram…' to create one with Set 1 filled.
  • The genes follow the Volcano Plot's current thresholds; after changing them, pull or send again to update the set.
  • In a region's popup, names link to an open Volcano Plot point only when they match exactly, case included, as the counts do.

UpSet Plot

Compares any number of named sets of items (2 or more), and is clearest with 4 or more, where Venn circles become unreadable. Each set's size is a horizontal bar on the left; each combination of sets that occurs is a vertical bar on top, largest first, with a dot matrix below showing which sets it includes. It uses the same set data and matching rules as Venn Diagram.

  • Build one with File → Omics Plot → UpSet Plot from Selected Text… (or the Text Tools menu's Omics Plot submenu, or the Spreadsheet toolbar's Omics Plot menu) from a table with one set per column (set name in the header row, one item per line), 2 or more columns (above 8 it asks first). Smart Visualization sends 2 or 3 columns to a Venn Diagram instead.
  • Each combination bar counts the items in exactly those sets and no others (exclusive intersections, what UpSetR draws, called "distinct" mode in ComplexHeatmap's make_comb_mat), so the bars add up to the number of distinct items; the axis is titled Exclusive Intersection Size. Plot Style's Count items can switch to counting the items in at least those sets, whatever else they are in (make_comb_mat's "intersect" mode; ComplexUpset calls it inclusive intersection), titled Inclusive Intersection Size. Every combination that shares an item then gets a bar, even one no item is in exactly (such as A and B when every shared item is also in C), and an item is counted in several bars.
  • Click a bar or its column of dots to list its items, filter them and copy them, one per line. The AI Assistant can switch the counting mode and read the same item lists, for a Venn diagram too.
  • Up to 40 combinations are drawn, largest first (UpSetR's default; equal sizes in set order); a note below the chart says how many were left out and how many of the items the bars drawn cover. The figure caption states the counting mode, the limit and how many combinations there are.
  • Up to 63 sets.
  • A 0/1 membership table also works, the format UpSetR's upset() reads: an optional first column of item names, then one column per set marked 1 for members and 0 otherwise. A set no item belongs to (a column of 0s) is read as an empty set.
  • Colors are set automatically and can't be changed in the inspector.
  • Export → CSV or TSV, and the chart's spreadsheet, give the sets as Venn Diagram does: one column per set.
  • Smart Visualization and drag-and-drop send a table of 4 or more named, mostly non-numeric columns here.

Dot Plot

A grid of circles, one per group × feature (such as cell type × gene). Circle area shows one value (usually percent expressed) and fill color a second (usually average expression), as in Seurat.

Scale each feature (on for new plots, as Seurat's DotPlot does by default) colors each feature by its z-score across the groups (standard deviation with n − 1, as R's scale), clipped at ±2.5, so each gene shows where it is high or low whatever its overall level. It uses the diverging Low/Mid/High colors centered on 0; the color legend reads Color (z per feature) and spans −2.5 to 2.5. A feature with fewer than 2 values, or the same value in every group, has no z-score and draws gray. Turn it off to color by the values themselves (scanpy's default) on a sequential scale, light (the Mid color) for low values and dark (the High color) for high ones. The table and export keep the values as entered either way.

log1p before scaling (off by default) takes log(1 + x) of the color values first (the legend then reads Color (z of log1p), or Color (log1p) unscaled). Seurat averages expression on the linear scale and takes log1p before scaling, so turn it on for linear-scale averages to match Seurat, and leave it off for values already on a log scale. A value of −1 or below has no log1p and draws gray.

  • Data (preferred): one table (separated by tabs, commas or semicolons) with a header row, one row per group × feature, 4 columns: Gene/Feature, Cell Type/Group, Percent Expressed, Average Expression. The size and color columns are recognized from their headers (such as "pct"/"percent" vs "avg"/"mean"); if unclear, @Graph asks once. If a gene and group pair appears on more than one row, the first row is used and the Data Log lists the repeats. Values may carry a percent sign (45%). A row with a size but no color value (NA) draws a gray dot; rows that can't be read are counted in the Data Log.
  • Data (alternative): two tables, each with a header row and a name column, separated by a blank line. The first gives dot size, the second dot color. They must list the same rows and columns, in any order: @Graph pairs them by name, and if the names differ it says which ones instead of drawing the chart. Column names may be numbers, such as Seurat's and scanpy's numbered clusters (0, 1, 2): two headers listing the same names mark two tables.
  • Each dot has a thin outline, so a dot colored like the background (the mid color is white by default) still shows. Every positive size gets a dot at least 1 pt across, so a very small value is never lost (only those tiny dots are larger than their area says). A size of 0 or a missing size draws no dot; a negative size draws none either, and the Data Log counts them.
  • Order Groups by Similarity and Order Features by Similarity put similar groups (or features) next to each other: hierarchical clustering (complete linkage, Euclidean distance) of their colors as drawn, z-scores when Scale each feature is on, as Seurat's cluster.idents and scanpy's dendrogram order groups. Each is a one-time reorder of the data, undone with ⌘Z, so the order can still be changed by hand. No tree is drawn.
  • Hide dots below size draws no dot for sizes below it (Seurat's dot.min, scanpy's dot_min), in the size values' units, such as 5 for 5% expressing. Hide features and groups with no dots leaves out every feature and group whose sizes are all zero, missing or below that size, as Heatmap hides all-zero rows and columns; the inspector says how many are hidden, and the table and export keep them.
  • The AI Assistant can set the scaling, the size threshold and the hiding, and order groups or features by similarity, each undone with ⌘Z.
  • Export and the chart's spreadsheet give one row per dot as Feature, Group, Size, Color, the order the long-format paste reads, so an exported table pastes back as the same chart. A feature and group repeated on several rows keep the first row everywhere: in a paste, in the spreadsheet and from the AI Assistant.
  • "Swap Rows and Columns" transposes both matrices if features and groups came in the other way round.
  • The Color Preset menu sets the Low/Mid/High colors, which can then be adjusted individually (with scaling off, only Mid and High are used, labeled Low values (light) and High values (dark)). The color range is automatic unless you set it: −2.5 to 2.5 for z-scores, and for unscaled values the 1st to 99th percentile (the full range with fewer than 100 distinct values). The legend marks a clipped end with ≥ or ≤.
  • The figure caption states that dot area is proportional to size (and the largest value), the Hide dots below size threshold, the coloring (the z-score with its n − 1 SD and ±2.5 clip, of log1p when on, or the sequential range) and how many features and groups are hidden.
  • "Show legend" (Axes & Labels → Appearance) shows the size and color legends. The size legend's dots are drawn at the plot's own scale, so a plot dot the size of the legend dot labeled 50 stands for 50. Its values are round numbers (such as 100, 75, 50, 25). When the plot's dots are too large for the legend column, it shows smaller values at the same scale.

Build one with File → Omics Plot → Dot Plot from Selected Text…, the Text Tools menu's Omics Plot submenu, or the Spreadsheet toolbar's Omics Plot menu.

Cluster Scatter

The UMAP/t-SNE-style scatter plot of single-cell studies (as in Seurat): points at their embedding coordinates, colored by cluster or cell type, with an outline around each cluster.

  • Data: a table (separated by tabs, commas or semicolons) with a header row and 3 columns in this order: X, Y, Cluster (number, number, text), one row per point.
  • "Swap X and Y" swaps the coordinates of every point, if the columns came in the other order.
  • Axis ranges are automatic unless set in the Range panel. A range whose minimum isn't below its maximum (for example, one entered the wrong way round) is ignored and that axis stays automatic, as in Volcano Plot.
  • "Show Cluster Outlines" draws a straight-edged outline (convex hull) around each cluster of 3 or more points.
  • Label clusters (off by default) writes each cluster's name at the median of its points, as Seurat's DimPlot(label = TRUE) does.
  • Same scale on both axes (on for new charts) keeps one unit of X as long as one unit of Y, so cluster shapes are not stretched. A typed axis range is used as entered instead.
  • Point size and opacity are adjustable, and each cluster's color can be overridden (↺ resets it to the palette). With more than eight clusters, each cluster gets its own color from ggplot2's default palette, as in Seurat.
  • Smart Visualization and drag-and-drop recognize an X/Y/Cluster table and send it here. Smart Visualization also recognizes numbered clusters (0, 1, 2…) when a header names the clusters or the embedding (UMAP, tSNE, PC).

Build one with File → Omics Plot → Cluster Scatter from Selected Text…, the Text Tools menu's Omics Plot submenu, or the Spreadsheet toolbar's Omics Plot menu.

GSEA Bar

A diverging horizontal bar chart for gene set enrichment results: one bar per pathway or gene set, extending left or right of zero according to the sign of its score, sorted from highest to lowest.

  • Data: a table (separated by tabs, commas or semicolons) with a header row and 2 or 3 columns: Name, Score and an optional Significance (p-value or FDR). With 3 columns, score and significance are recognized from the words in their headers, as fgsea, the GSEA desktop application and clusterProfiler name them: "NES", "ES" or "enrichmentScore" for the score, and "padj", "pval", "FDR q-val", "NOM p-val", "p.adjust" or "qvalue" for significance. Only whole words count, so "Genes" is not read as "ES" and "Adjusted NES" is still a score; if unclear, @Graph asks once.
  • A whole results table works too (4 or more columns under a header row), such as fgsea's pathway, pval, padj, log2err, ES, NES, size, leadingEdge, the GSEA desktop application's NAME, ES, NES, NOM p-val, FDR q-val, FWER p-val, or clusterProfiler's ID, Description, setSize, enrichmentScore, NES, pvalue, p.adjust, qvalue. Columns are picked by header, not position: the name from pathway, term, description or name (before an ID, else the first text column), the score from NES (ES only when there is no NES), and opacity from an adjusted p (padj, p.adjust, FDR q-val, then qvalue, then FWER) before a raw one (pval, NOM p-val). Other columns are ignored. @Graph asks only when no single score column stands out, and the Chart inspector and figure caption state the columns used, for example "Score: NES; opacity: padj (BH-adjusted)".
  • Positive scores use the Up color and negative scores the Down color, both adjustable.
  • If significance is given, it sets each bar's opacity on a log scale: solid at 0.001 or below, about 78% at 0.01, 63% at 0.05, fading to 35% at 1 (not adjustable), and the legend shows this key; bars without it are fully opaque.
  • A header row is recognized only when its second field is not a number (for a table of 4 or more columns, when none of its fields is); quoted cells and decimal commas are read.

Build one with File → Omics Plot → GSEA Bar from Selected Text…, the Text Tools menu's Omics Plot submenu, or the Spreadsheet toolbar's Omics Plot menu.

Computing GSEA Directly (Permutation)

'Compute GSEA (Permutation)…', next to 'GSEA Bar from Selected Text…' in the Omics Plot menu, runs a preranked Gene Set Enrichment Analysis (Subramanian et al. 2005; see References) and plots the result as a GSEA Bar chart.

  • Input is two pasted blocks: a Ranked Gene List (tab-delimited Gene, Score, such as log fold change or a t-statistic) and a list of gene sets, one per line. Choose the gene-set layout: ".gmt: name, description, genes" (the MSigDB format; the second column is always the description and is skipped, whatever it says) or "Name, then genes". If most .gmt descriptions look like genes from your list, @Graph asks you to check the layout first.
  • A gene listed more than once keeps the score with the largest absolute value, and the caption says how many were combined. If 1% or more of the scores are tied, the caption notes it, because the order of tied genes is arbitrary and can shift the enrichment scores. When the highest and lowest points of the running sum are equally far from zero, ES is 0, as in fgsea.
  • Gene sets are tested when 15 to 500 of their genes are found in the ranked list, the GSEA default; change the range with Gene-set size in the setup window.
  • For each gene set it reports the enrichment score (ES), the normalized enrichment score (NES, comparable between sets), a permutation p-value, a Benjamini-Hochberg FDR and the leading-edge genes: the genes ranked before the running-sum peak for a positive ES, or after its lowest point for a negative ES, which drive the enrichment. As in the fgsea package (Korotkevich et al. 2021) and the GSEA desktop application, the p-value compares a positive ES only with positive null scores (and a negative ES only with negative ones), because the two tails of the null differ in shape.
  • Significance comes from comparing each set with random gene sets of the same size drawn from the ranked list (gene-set permutation), as in the GSEA desktop application's Preranked mode and fgsea. All sets of the same size share one set of random draws, which makes large analyses fast, and the same data give the same results every time.
  • Permutations are chosen automatically for the number of sets tested: 10 × (sets tested) / 0.05, at least 10,000. The smallest possible p-value is about 2 / permutations and the FDR multiplies it by the number of sets, so this lets a strongly enriched set reach an FDR well below 0.05 however many sets you test. Very large runs are limited to a few minutes of computing, and the caption then says so. You can also turn off Automatic permutations and enter a number (at least 1,000).
  • When no random set scored as extreme as a gene set, its p-value is at the permutation floor, 1/(n + 1), and the true p may be smaller. The caption counts these sets, and the data table's Note column marks each one.
  • Bars are sorted by NES, with FDR setting each bar's opacity. The chart's data table (and Copy Data or export) holds the full results table: gene set, NES, FDR (BH), size, ES, nominal p, leading-edge size and leading-edge genes ("/" separated), and the note. Cancel closes the setup window and opens nothing. The Chart inspector and figure caption record how the analysis was run: permutations and how they were chosen, the size range, how many sets were tested, duplicates, ties and the FDR method.
  • The AI Assistant's compute_gsea follows the same rules (automatic permutations, size range 15 to 500 unless set).

Grouped Diverging Bar

Like GSEA Bar, bars extend either side of zero by sign, but each category can hold several series, each drawn as its own thin sub-bar within the category's band. Useful for comparing, say, fold changes under several conditions for each gene.

  • Data: a table (separated by tabs, commas or semicolons) with a header row and 3 columns: Category, Series, Value. Categories appear in the order they first occur in the table. A wide table also works: Category, then one numeric column per series named in the header, one row per category. Values may carry a percent sign (45%); rows that can't be read are counted in the Data Log. Axis titles default to the headers (such as "Log2FoldChange", "Gene"), otherwise "Value" and "Category"; edit them in Axes & Labels.
  • Orientation: Horizontal (default) puts categories in rows with bars going left and right; Vertical puts categories along the X axis with bars going up and down and the category names rotated below. Switching it swaps the X and Y axis titles too, so each stays with the axis it names (the X title is always at the bottom).
  • Each series has its own lane in every category, in the order the series first appear, so a series is always in the same place and color. A category without a value for a series leaves that lane empty, marked NA.
  • A category and series repeated on several rows: the first row's value is used, in Grouped Diverging Bar and Stacked Composition Bar alike (first row wins). The inspector, the Data Log and the figure caption say how many rows repeat; combine them in the table first if they should be added.
  • "Swap Category and Series" swaps the two text columns if they came in the other order. It also clears per-series color overrides.
  • Each series' color can be overridden (↺ resets it to the palette). With more than eight series, each series gets its own color from ggplot2's default palette.
  • Smart Visualization and drag-and-drop recognize a Category/Series/Value table and send it here. When a category and series pair repeats (replicates of a two-factor experiment), Smart Visualization opens a grouped Chart instead.

Build one with File → Omics Plot → Grouped Diverging Bar from Selected Text…, the Text Tools menu's Omics Plot submenu, or the Spreadsheet toolbar's Omics Plot menu.

Manhattan Plot

The standard genome-wide association study (GWAS) display: one point per variant, placed by chromosome and position along X, with −log₁₀(p-value) on Y, threshold lines for genome-wide and suggestive significance, and labels on the strongest hits.

  • Data: a table separated by tabs, commas, semicolons or spaces (as PLINK writes it). Columns are found by their header names: chromosome (CHR, CHROM, #CHROM, Chromosome), position (BP, POS, Position, base_pair_location), p-value (P, PVAL, PVALUE, P-value, PBOLT_LMM) and, optionally, a name for labels (SNP, ID, RSID, variant_id, MarkerName), so PLINK's CHR SNP BP … P order reads as it is. Without those names, the columns are read in the order Chromosome, Position, P-value, Name. A header row is recognized by its content, so a table without one keeps its first row. Rows that can't be read are counted in the Data Log.
  • X axis: chromosomes run in genome order (numbered chromosomes, then X, Y, XY, Z, W, then MT, then any others alphabetically), and neighboring chromosomes alternate between two colors you can set. By default ("Equal-width chromosomes" on in Plot Style) every chromosome gets the same width and each variant sits at its relative position within its chromosome, so small chromosomes stay readable. Turn it off for cumulative base-pair positions, as qqman and topr draw them: each chromosome is as wide as its largest position in your data, so chr1 is drawn about five times as long as chr21. If any chromosome has no positive position, equal widths are used either way. The figure caption states the layout. Chromosome names that would overlap their neighbor are left out, as R does; every chromosome keeps its tick.
  • Chromosome names: "chr1", "Chr1", "CHR1", "01" and "1" are one chromosome, as are "M" and "MT". "Read 23–26 as X, Y, XY, MT (PLINK, human)", on for new charts, shows PLINK's numeric codes as X, Y, XY (pseudo-autosomal) and MT. It applies only when no chromosome is above 26 and none is already named X, Y, XY or MT, so other species keep their numbered chromosomes; turn it off for a species with more than 22 autosomes.
  • Genome-wide (default 5×10⁻⁸) and suggestive (default 1×10⁻⁵) thresholds are dashed lines, each with its own value, color and visibility in Plot Style. 5×10⁻⁸ is the convention for human GWAS; other species or study designs may need another value. The figure caption states the thresholds drawn.
  • "Show Bonferroni line" (off by default) adds a line at α / m, where m is the number of plotted variants (α is 0.05 unless you change it). That is the p-value each test must beat to keep the chance of any false positive among them at α. The inspector and the figure caption state m.
  • Genomic inflation λ: Plot Style and the figure caption report the median of the χ² statistics the p-values imply (1 degree of freedom), divided by 0.4549, its median when no variant has an effect (Devlin & Roeder 1999). Near 1 is what the null gives; well above 1 (say 1.1 or more in a large scan) suggests population structure or other confounding inflating every test.
  • Open QQ Plot (Plot Style) opens the p-values as a QQ plot in a new window: observed −log₁₀(p), sorted, against the value expected if no variant had an effect (ppoints, as qqman), with the y = x line and a shaded 95% band for each point (its Beta(i, m − i + 1) order-statistic interval, as GWASTools draws it). Under the null the points follow the line inside the band; a lift along the whole range points to inflation, a lift only at the top to real hits. Its point color, size and opacity are set in its own Plot Style, and it exports like any chart.
  • Top-hit labels name one lead variant per locus: the smallest p at or below the genome-wide threshold, then the next one more than 500 kb from every labeled variant on its chromosome, up to Max labels (10 by default). Turn off Auto-label top hits to label none. Labels need a name column (SNP, ID or the 4th column). Labels never overlap: one that would is moved up, with a line to its point, or left out. The figure caption describes the rule.
  • Point size and opacity are set in Plot Style.
  • A p-value of exactly 0 (numerical underflow, not a true zero) is plotted just above both the smallest non-zero p-value in your data and the genome-wide line, so it never sits below the line it is labeled for. Its height is a lower bound on its significance rather than a measured value. Plot Style and the figure caption say how many variants this applies to.
  • Up to 250,000 variants; above that the chart shows a message instead.
  • The data can be edited in the chart's spreadsheet.
  • The AI Assistant can build a Manhattan plot and adjust its thresholds (the Bonferroni line included), colors, chromosome widths, points and labels.

Build one with File → Omics Plot → Manhattan Plot from Selected Text…, the Text Tools menu's Omics Plot submenu, or the Spreadsheet toolbar's Omics Plot menu. Smart Visualization opens a table here when its first three headers are a chromosome, a position (BP, Pos, Position) and the p-value.

Stacked Composition Bar

Shows Grouped Diverging Bar data as one stacked bar per category, for composition (such as cell-type proportions per sample) rather than signed differences. To make one, build a Grouped Diverging Bar, then switch its Chart Type to Stacked Composition Bar.

  • Series stack in the same order in every bar (the order they first appear in the data), so each series is easy to compare across categories.
  • "Show as percentage" (Plot Style) scales every bar to 100%. Off by default, so bar heights show the raw totals.
  • Negative values are left out of the stack, and the inspector says how many were left out.
  • A category and series repeated on several rows: the first row's value is used, as in Grouped Diverging Bar, so stacks, totals and percentages match it. The inspector, the Data Log and the figure caption say how many rows repeat.
  • Show totals (n), on for new charts, writes each bar's total at its end: "n = …" in percent mode, where 50% of 4 and 50% of 4,000 look the same. Label segments writes each segment's share of its bar inside it, where it fits. Sort bars by orders the bars by one series' share, largest first. Error bars are deliberately not offered: a stacked segment has no common baseline to read them against; use Grouped Diverging Bar or a bar chart of the replicates for variability.
  • In percent mode the value axis's title gets "(%)" unless it already says percent. A category with no positive value draws an empty bar, and the inspector says how many there are.
  • Orientation, "Swap Category and Series" and per-series colors work as for Grouped Diverging Bar.

PCA Plot

A principal component analysis biplot computed from the matrix: samples plotted at their scores on two components (PC1 and PC2 unless you choose others), each axis title giving that component's % variance explained, and features' loadings drawn as arrows from the origin. To make one, build a Heatmap from a numeric matrix with row and column names, then switch its Chart Type to PCA Plot.

  • "Samples are: Columns / Rows" (Plot Style) says which way the samples run. The default, Columns, matches the usual layout of genes in rows and samples in columns.
  • "Log2 transform" runs the PCA on log₂ of the values. It is the same setting as the Heatmap's, so both chart types analyze the same numbers. Turn it on for raw counts or intensities, so a few very high values don't dominate; leave it off for data already on a log scale. A zero or negative value has no log₂ and counts as missing. For counts, also check "Add 1 first: log2(x + 1)", which keeps zeros (only negative values count as missing).
  • "Scale variables (unit variance)" standardizes each feature to unit variance first (as R's PCA does when asked to scale). Off by default, as log₂ expression data are usually on comparable scales; turn it on for features measured in different units.
  • "Top N most variable features" (500 by default): PCA can run on only the features with the highest variance across samples (after the Log2 transform, if on), as DESeq2's plotPCA does with its default of 500. Those features carry most of the structure, while thousands of low-variance genes mostly add noise. It is on automatically for new charts with more than 500 complete features and at least 10 times as many features as samples (a typical gene-expression matrix). Turn it off to use every feature, or change the number; the inspector says how many features are in use.
  • X and Y: choose any two components to plot. PC1 and PC2 is the default; PC1 vs PC3 or PC2 vs PC3 can show structure the first two miss. Each axis title shows that component's % of the variance.
  • Variance explained: small bars show the % of variance of the first 10 components, with the plotted pair highlighted. Copy Variance Table or Save as CSV… gives every component's standard deviation, variance, % of variance and cumulative %, the same numbers as R's summary(prcomp(...)).
  • The figure caption states the samples, the features used, the transform, centering and scaling, the plotted components with their % of variance, the % for PC1 to PC5, and the sign convention (each component's largest loading is positive).
  • "Show loading vectors" draws arrows for the features with the largest loadings on the two plotted components (the number shown is adjustable).
  • "Show sample labels" writes each sample's name beside its point. Samples are not grouped automatically, but you can recolor individual samples to highlight a subset.
  • Ellipse per color group (off by default) draws one ellipse around each group of samples sharing a point color, so give each group's samples the same color first. Type: Normal (the default) is ggplot2's stat_ellipse(type = "norm"), from the group's mean and covariance. t is stat_ellipse's default, a multivariate t fit (MASS::cov.trob), so an outlier stretches it less. Both are expected to hold about Level of the group (radius √(2·F(level; 2, n − 1))) and need at least 4 samples. Confidence (group mean) is the confidence ellipse for the group's center: Hotelling's T² region (covariance ÷ n, radius √(2(n − 1)/(n − 2)·F(level; 2, n − 2))), which covers the true mean at the stated level for any group size. FactoMineR and factoextra draw the same shape with the large-sample radius √χ²(level; 2), whose "95%" ellipse covers the mean only about 67% of the time with 4 samples and 84% with 8. It also needs 4 samples and shrinks as the group grows. Level is 95% by default. Plot Style and the caption say which groups got no ellipse. The axes widen to fit the ellipses.
  • The PCA is recalculated whenever the matrix changes, such as after editing a cell in the spreadsheet.
  • Missing values: a feature (such as gene) with a missing value in any sample is left out, as PCA in R and DESeq2 works on complete data; filling the gap with the feature's mean would pull that sample toward the center and understate the feature's variance. Plot Style says how many features were left out.
  • A feature counts as constant only when its spread is at rounding level next to its own values (under 10⁻¹³ of them), so features in small units, such as a capacitance in farads, or tightly spread on a large offset, are analyzed like any other. A constant feature takes no part in the PCA, and Plot Style says how many there are.
  • Needs at least 3 samples and 2 complete features; shows a message instead above 500 samples or 50,000 features, or if no feature varies.

Build a Heatmap first (File → Omics Plot → Heatmap from Selected Text…, ⌥⌘M), then switch its Chart Type to PCA Plot.

Inspector Reference

  • Plot Style for Volcano / MA Plot: Display mode, fold-change and significance thresholds, use-adjusted-p toggle, Up/Down/Not-significant colors, point size, point opacity, aesthetic mapping (color/size/shape/opacity by extra data columns), Label Genes Matching Criteria, labeled genes list with Clear All, the 'Search / highlight gene…' field, and Send to Venn Diagram.
  • Plot Style for Heatmap: normalization mode, Log2 transform (with Add 1 first for log₂(x + 1)), color scale and value range, Swap Rows and Columns, cluster rows/cluster columns toggles, linkage method, distance, optimal leaf ordering, search field, Show axis (border), hide all-zero rows, hide all-zero columns. Show legend and Show cell grid are in Axes & Labels.
  • Plot Style for Dot Plot: Swap Rows and Columns, Order Groups by Similarity, Order Features by Similarity, Hide dots below size, Hide features and groups with no dots, color preset menu, Low/Mid/High color pickers, Scale each feature (z-score), log1p before scaling, auto/manual color-scale range.
  • Plot Style for Cluster Scatter: Swap X and Y, Show Cluster Outlines, Label clusters, Same scale on both axes, point size, point opacity, per-cluster color overrides.
  • Plot Style for GSEA Bar: Positive (Up) and Negative (Down) colors.
  • Plot Style for Grouped Diverging Bar / Stacked Composition Bar: Horizontal/Vertical orientation, Swap Category and Series, per-series color overrides, plus Show as percentage for Stacked Composition Bar.
  • Plot Style for PCA Plot: Samples are Columns/Rows, Log2 transform (with Add 1 first for log₂(x + 1)), Scale variables (unit variance), Top N most variable features, X and Y components, variance-explained bars with Copy Variance Table and Save as CSV…, Show loading vectors (with a count of features shown), Show sample labels, Ellipse per color group (Type, Level), default point color, point size, point opacity, per-sample color overrides.
  • Plot Style for UpSet Plot: Count items (In exactly these sets / In at least these sets). Sorting, the 40-combination limit, colors and the layout are automatic.
  • Axes & Labels: X axis label, Y axis label, axis label font, tick value font, axis color, tick color, axis line width, tick length/width. Volcano and MA Plot have no grid. Dot Plot, Cluster Scatter, GSEA Bar, Grouped Diverging Bar, Stacked Composition Bar, PCA Plot and Manhattan Plot have grid controls (show/hide, color, width, opacity, dash, line count). Heatmap has cell grid controls (show/hide, color, width, opacity, dash). PCA Plot's axis titles are set by the plotted components (such as "PC1 (x%)", "PC3 (x%)"). UpSet Plot, like Venn Diagram, has no editable axis labels or grid.
  • Copy / Paste Chart Style (⌥⌘C / ⌥⌘V) copies the look (colors, fonts, thresholds, mapping, normalization, clustering) without the data.

Navigating Graphs

Zooming

  • Drag a rectangle on the plot to zoom into that region.
  • Drag along a Y axis's tick area (left of the plot) to set that panel's Y range, or along the X axis's tick area (below the plot) to set the shared X range.
  • Double-click an axis's tick area to type exact limits.
  • ⌘= (or ⌘→) and ⌘− (or ⌘←) zoom X in and out, ⌘↑ and ⌘↓ zoom Y, and ⌘0 resets the zoom. Zoom In X, Zoom Out X and Reset Zoom are in the Keyboard Shortcuts menu too.
  • Double-click the plot background to step back one zoom. Each zoom is remembered, so repeated double-clicks retrace them until the plot fits the data again.
  • The Range panel in the inspector sets exact limits for any panel, and its Match Y Ranges button gives every group with the same unit the same Y range.

Each group panel has its own Y range, and all panels share the X range, so they always show the same time window. In the Range panel, turning Y auto-range off keeps every panel's current range for editing, and typing a limit for one panel leaves the others as they are. Limits can be typed with a unit, such as '-80 mV', '-0.08 V' or '2 min', and are converted to the axis unit. On a logarithmic axis, zooming works in decades, and values of zero or below are left out (see Logarithmic Axes under Graph Display Settings).

Cursors

Show cursors A and B with the toolbar button (⌘K): two dashed vertical lines, labeled in the X axis's unit. Drag them, or type positions in an inspector panel's cursor fields; positions round to the nearest sample. ⌘K places them over the middle 25% of the visible window.

A result that keeps the graph's time axis opens with the same X view: Decimate, Interpolate and Resample, Integrate and Differentiate, Average, Normalize, Delete Sweeps and Export Visible, whether run from a panel, a menu, a protocol or the AI Assistant. Its X axis has the same shift (a file's true time plus any Set X = 0), cursors A and B sit at the same times (on the same samples, to within one sample of the result), and a zoomed X range stays zoomed (Export Visible shows its whole range instead). A cursor past the end of the result moves to its edge, as when dragging. Results with another X axis (Align at Peak, a spectrum, histogram, I-V or phase plot, Combine, Concatenate, Continuous to Episodic and back) start from their own. Opening a result changes nothing in the original window, and its undo history is untouched.

Panels with a 'Full sweep' / 'Between cursors A – B' choice follow the cursors: choosing 'Between cursors' shows them and 'Full sweep' hides them. Each of these tools remembers where you last left the cursors, at the same relative place in the current view, even after you quit @Graph.

Sweeps

  • Type which sweeps to show in the 'Review sweeps:' field above the plot, for example '1-10, 15, 20-25', and press Return. ⌥⌘R jumps to this field, and the All, Odd and Even buttons beside it are quick choices.
  • Tab / Shift-Tab: show the next or previous sweep on its own.
  • Comma / period: show the first or last sweep.
  • Space / Shift-Space: add the next sweep to those shown, or remove the last one added.
  • Arrow keys: pan a zoomed axis.
  • Click the plot first, so these keys go to it rather than to a text field.

Multi-channel Files

Files with more than one channel open with one panel per channel. In the Groups panel, 'Split by channel' gives each recorded channel its own panel, Y axis and unit, and 'Single group' overlays everything in one panel. See Grouping Modes under Graph Display Settings for all the choices.

Graph Display Settings

All display options are in the Display category of the inspector, and the plot updates as you change them. Plot Style holds symbols, lines and the legend; Axes & Labels holds the axes, ticks, labels, title, background, scale bars, interval bars, error bars and trend/base lines; Range sets axis limits and log scales; Groups sets the grouping and group order.

Plot Style

The top of the panel says what you're editing: a sweep, the selected group, or 'All sweeps (global)' when nothing is selected. Click a sweep or a group panel to select it; Clear returns it to the global style.

  • Symbol: shape (Dot, Circle, Square, Triangle, Diamond, Cross, Plus, Tick, and filled circles, squares and triangles), size (1–20 pt) and color. Choose None for lines only. A raster always draws ticks.
  • Line: Solid, Dashed, Dotted, Dash-Dot or None; width (0.5–5 pt) and color. Curved joins the points with a smooth curve that never passes its neighbors. If the X values double back, the curve follows the table's order instead and can swing past the points, and the inspector says so; sort the table by X to avoid that. Traces of more than 2,000 samples stay straight.
  • Wavelength color: beside each color well, type a wavelength from 380 to 700 nm in the λ field, step it with the arrows, or drag along the spectrum, and @Graph picks the matching color. Handy for coloring fluorescence traces by excitation or emission wavelength.
  • Palette colors: 'Use rainbow spectrum for overlaid sweeps' (named for the active palette, chosen in Settings → Appearance) gives each sweep a different color from the palette, which it keeps when other sweeps are shown or hidden. Choosing a line color for a group or for all sweeps turns it off. Turn on 'Color by position within group' to restart the colors in each group, so the first sweep of every group shares a color.
  • Apply current style to Sweep / Group / All: apply the current style to the selected sweep only, to its whole group (clearing per-sweep styles in it), or to every group (clearing all per-sweep and per-group colors, symbols and lines). Y ranges, ticks, scale bars, error bars, trend lines and visibility stay per group.
  • Rename a sweep: click the sweep on the graph, then edit the 'Name' field at the top of this panel. The name ("Sweep N" by default) appears in the legend and wherever else the sweep is listed. Renaming can be undone with ⌘Z and is saved in the .@graph file.

Axes & Labels

  • Show or hide the X and Y axes, and choose whether an axis crosses at zero. Log axes are set in the Range panel (see Logarithmic Axes below).
  • Grid: show or hide it. Grid lines fall on the major ticks.
  • Axis line width, and the colors of the axis lines, axis text and grid.
  • Tick marks: major and minor length, direction (inside, outside or symmetric) and minor ticks per major.
  • Tick spacing: 'auto' for rounded spacing, or an exact major interval (linear axes only; a log axis places its own ticks). X is shared by all panels; Y is set per panel.
  • Automatic ticks step by 1, 2 or 5 times a power of ten, so labels rarely need two decimals. The X axis picks the time unit that fits (a 3 ms zoom reads in ms, a long recording in min). 'Rescale Y units automatically' (on by default) does the same for Y: when a small range would need two or more decimals, the axis switches to a smaller prefix of the channel's unit, so 0.05 mV steps read 50 µV and the title reads Voltage (µV). A Y scale bar label follows the same rule. Only the labels change: the data, the Y range fields and every measurement stay in the channel's own unit. A log Y axis, or a Y interval you typed, keeps the unit as it is.
  • Labels: show or hide tick values and axis labels, and set their font, size and text. The left margin widens when a Y tick label is long, so the labels never run into the axis title. All panels share it, so their plots line up.
  • Graph Title: 'Show title' adds a title above the plot, with its own font, size and color.
  • Background color: when you switch between a dark and a light background, @Graph offers to invert the axis, text, grid and title colors so they stay readable.

Logarithmic Axes

Each axis has a Logarithmic scale checkbox in the Range panel, and in the range box that opens when you double-click its tick labels, as on charts. A log axis is labeled at powers of ten, with minor ticks at 2 to 9 times each; a narrower range is labeled 1, 2 and 5 times a power of ten, so there are always at least three labels. A Min or Max of zero or less is ignored and that end fits the data. Values at or below zero, and samples at time zero or earlier on a log time axis, are not drawn, and the Range panel says how many.

A log time axis shows s, ms, µs or ns, chosen from its upper end. Scale bars are hidden while an axis is logarithmic, and a log Y axis shows values without junction-potential correction.

Legend

Turn on 'Show legend' (Plot Style panel) to draw a key on the plot with one row per sweep, showing its name and a sample of its line and symbol, so sweeps that share a color but differ in line or symbol can still be told apart.

  • The key always matches what is drawn, including rainbow colors and per-sweep or per-group styles.
  • Drag the key to move it.
  • Double-click a row to select that sweep and open a small editor: rename the sweep, or click 'Remove from Legend' (or press Delete) to drop the row from the key without affecting the sweep.
  • 'Legend title' (optional) adds a heading above the rows, such as 'Trial 1'.
  • Removed rows are saved with the file and carried by Copy/Paste Style.
  • The legend is included in every export (PDF, SVG, PNG, clipboard) as shown on screen.

Scale Bars

Turn on 'Show scale bars' to draw L-shaped scale bars, as in many neuroscience figures. For the classic axis-free look, also turn off Show X axis and Show Y axis. Enter lengths as a number in axis units, a value with a unit such as '500 ms' or '50 pA', or 'auto' for a round length in the unit the axis shows. Drag the corner to move the bars. Each group can have its own Y bar length when units differ. The bars have square ends and are drawn as one L, so each is exactly the length its label states. Scale bars aren't drawn when either axis is logarithmic, since a length has no single size there.

Scale bar and interval bar labels share one font and size. The screen and every export use the chosen font (PDF embeds it). The X scale bar's label uses the same unit as the X axis, such as ms.

Interval Bars

Horizontal bars mark a time interval, such as a drug application, at a chosen height, with an optional label above or below. Add as many as you like. They're drawn in the Axis lines color, like the axes and scale bars, with square ends, so each covers exactly the interval it marks.

  • Turn on 'Show interval bars' and click 'Add Bar'.
  • Double-click a bar on the graph to set its start time, end time, Y value, label text and label position. The numbers are in the axes' own terms: times as the X axis shows them (after Set X = 0, in its unit, such as ms) and Y as the Y axis shows it (junction-potential corrected when that is on). The inspector list shows them the same way.
  • Delete a bar from that popover or with the trash icon next to it in the inspector list. ⌘Z undoes a deletion.
  • In a graph with stacked panels, bars are drawn (and editable) only on the top panel, using its Y scale. Reordering panels in Group Order & Visibility moves them to the new top panel.

Interval bars are included in PDF, SVG and image exports.

Trend/Base Line

A reference line for each group: 'Trend' fits a straight line, 'Base' draws a flat line at the mean Y value. It is computed from the group's average trace over an X range you choose and drawn across the full width of the panel.

  • Turn on 'Show for [group]' (or 'Show trend/base line' for a single-group graph) and choose Trend or Base.
  • Set the X start and end of the data used for the fit, as the X axis reads them (in its unit, after any 'Set X = 0' shift); the fit stays on the same stretch of data if the shift is changed later. The line itself always spans the whole panel.
  • On a logarithmic axis the fitted straight line is drawn as the curve it becomes there.
  • Color and line style can be set per group (default: axis color, dashed).
  • Works best on a group that is already a single averaged trace. For a group of raw sweeps, the fit uses their sample-by-sample average.

Error Bars

Draws vertical error bars with caps at every visible point, using another sweep's values as the ± size, such as a separately loaded SEM or SD column. These are separate from the SD/SEM bars drawn automatically on time-binned summary graphs (Synapse Detection).

  • For each group, turn on 'Show for [group]' (or 'Show error bars') and pick the source sweep.
  • Values are paired sample by sample, so the source sweep should have the same number of samples and sample rate as the sweep it annotates.

Grouping Modes

  • Single group (all sweeps): all sweeps overlaid in one panel (⌥⌘G).
  • Combine groups of N: each run of N consecutive sweeps forms one group.
  • Combine every N: sweeps are dealt out in turn (sweep 1 → group 1, sweep 2 → group 2…). Used for multi-channel files.
  • Split by channel: one group per recording channel, whatever order the sweeps are in. Offered when the file has more than one channel.
  • Separate all sweeps: one panel per sweep (⌥⌘S).
  • Clear all style overrides, in the same panel, removes every per-sweep and per-group color, symbol and line. Each group keeps its own Y range, ticks, scale bar, error bars, trend line and visibility.

Group Order & Visibility

With more than one group, the Groups panel has a 'Group Order & Visibility' section. Use each group's ↑ and ↓ buttons to change the top-to-bottom order ('Reset order' restores it), and its Show/Hide button to hide a group ('Show all' shows every group again). The order is saved in the .@graph file.

To make one stacked panel taller and its neighbor shorter, drag the gap between them up or down (the pointer shows ↕ over it). The other panels keep their heights, and every panel keeps room for its axes. Double-click a gap, or choose Reset Panel Heights from a panel's right-click menu, to make all panels the same height again (⌘Z undoes either). Only the panels' heights change, not their Y ranges. The heights are saved with the graph, used in every export, and scale with the window. While the number of panels differs (a group hidden, another grouping), the panels share the height equally.

The Groups button in the header bar (the eye) lists every group of the current grouping, with its sweeps (such as 'Sweeps 1, 4, 7' for every-third grouping), and shows or hides each with a click; the list stays open for several changes. Right-click a panel to hide it, or to show all groups again.

Match Y Ranges, in the Range panel, gives all groups with the same unit the same Y range, so figure panels can be compared by eye.

Modifying Graphs

Modify operations change the sample values in the graph (never the original recording file). Each one can be undone with ⌘Z and redone with ⇧⌘Z.

Filter, Smoothing and Baseline have an 'Apply to' menu: Selected group, All groups, All sweeps, Visible sweeps or Selected sweep. Filter and Smoothing start on Selected group, and Baseline on All groups. When the sweeps come from more than one recorded channel, a Channel menu below it limits the change to one channel, so that All sweeps need not filter a command or stimulus channel; All channels is the default. Fit overlays and AP-marker sweeps are never changed, as in Protocols and Batch Processing: they are derived from the data, not recorded.

In any Modify panel, pressing Return (↩) in a number field (frequency, window size, region start/end, scale factor, offset) runs the panel's main action.

Saving is disabled while a filter, smoothing or baseline operation is still running. Wait for the 'Applied…' message before saving. An Undo or another edit made while it runs cancels it, and nothing is applied.

Filter / Smooth (⇧⌘F)

Choose a Type, enter its Frequency (Hz) (or band edges, or Points), choose Apply to, then click Apply Filter. The types (Butterworth filters are zero-phase, run forward and backward, unless Causal is on):

  • Low-pass: removes high-frequency noise (typical cutoff: 1–5 kHz). A Butterworth filter: the flattest passband and the steepest roll-off for its order, but it overshoots a step (by about 7% at order 4, run forward and back) and rings after it.
  • Low-pass (Bessel): the filter patch amplifiers use (their 4- and 8-pole settings). It barely overshoots a step (0.5% at order 4, forward and back), so rise times, action-potential shapes and event kinetics come through undistorted; the price is a gentler roll-off, so it removes less just above the cutoff. Its cutoff is the −3 dB point, as amplifier filters are specified (SciPy's norm='mag'). Orders 1–8. Use it to match an amplifier's filter, or whenever waveform shape matters more than a sharp cutoff.
  • Order (low-, high-, band-pass and band-stop): 1–8, 4 to start. A higher order cuts more steeply but rings more after a sudden step; run forward and back, the roll-off far beyond the cutoff is 12 dB per octave per order (for a band-pass or band-stop, whose order is that of the whole filter, 6 dB per octave per order on each side of the band).
  • Causal (one pass, forward only), for every low-, high-, band-pass and band-stop filter: off by default. Off, the filter runs forward and back, so nothing shifts in time, but each event's filtered edge starts slightly before the event itself, which can make an onset or latency read early. On, it runs once, as an analog filter does: nothing appears before an event, but the whole trace is delayed, and each cutoff is the −3 dB point of the one pass (the roll-off is then 6 dB per octave per order, half that of forward and back). For a low-pass, the panel and the message give the delay (the filter's group delay at low frequencies, nearly constant across a Bessel filter's passband); subtract it from measured times. A causal high-pass or band filter shifts each frequency differently, so it changes event shapes.
  • High-pass: removes slow baseline drift (typical cutoff: 0.1–1 Hz).
  • Band-pass: keeps a frequency band.
  • Band-stop (notch): attenuates the band between the two frequencies you enter and leaves the rest alone. Use it when the interference covers a range. For a single interfering frequency, the Mains hum and Single-frequency options are more precise.
  • Band-pass and band-stop are true Butterworth band filters (the standard low-pass-to-band transform, as MATLAB and SciPy build them), so a narrow band keeps a flat passband. Order is the order of the whole filter, as for low- and high-pass, and is even (2, 4, 6 or 8), since the band is built from a prototype of half that order.
  • A cutoff or band edge must be below the Nyquist frequency (half the sample rate) of every sweep filtered, and a band's high edge above its low edge; otherwise the panel says so and nothing is changed. The panel shows the Nyquist frequency, or its range when sweeps have different rates. The filter itself applies the same rule, so no entry point (Protocols, the AI Assistant) can have a cutoff quietly moved below Nyquist.
  • A low-pass filter's gain at 0 Hz is exactly 1, so even a very low cutoff leaves the baseline level untouched (−70 mV stays −70 mV).
  • Every cutoff and band edge you enter is the −3 dB point of the filter as applied. Running forward and backward squares the response, which would otherwise put the entered frequency at −6 dB (as forward-backward filtering in MATLAB and SciPy does), so @Graph designs each filter to make the two passes together 3 dB down there.
  • Sweep ends: as in SciPy's sosfiltfilt, each end of a sweep (and of each stretch between blank cells) is extended by a mirror image of the trace, so its level and slope carry on past the end, long enough for the filter to settle; the extension is removed afterwards. The mirror turns about the end of a straight line fitted to the first (or last) 2 ms rather than about the single end sample, so one sample's noise shifts a sweep's filtered baseline less (about 2.5 times less for a high-pass), while a trace that starts or ends on a slope, such as a ramp, is still continued exactly. Within about one time constant of each end the result is still an estimate: a low-pass or band-stop ends near that line's end, and a high-pass or band-pass near 0.
  • Mains hum (50/60 Hz): removes power-line interference. See below.
  • Single-frequency: removes one exact frequency by subtracting a fitted sine wave, and its harmonics only if you ask (Harmonics: Fundamental only, the default; Those present; or First N). Use it when you know the interfering frequency precisely (a stimulator, pump or chopper) and it doesn't drift. It removes the frequency whether or not it is there, so it suits short sweeps the Mains hum filter cannot test. How the sine waves are fitted, and where from, is as for Mains hum below.
  • Boxcar: running average; fast but distorts the edges.
  • Median: keeps sharp transitions better than boxcar, and stays quick on long recordings (a 201-point window over 10 million samples takes under 2 seconds).
  • For Boxcar and Median, Points is the window in samples: at least 3, and odd (an even value runs one point longer). The message after filtering states the window used.
  • Three-point: the lightest possible smoothing, each sample replaced by a ¼, ½, ¼ weighted average of it and its two neighbors.

The Butterworth filters have an accurate flat passband at every order. The Smoothing section, below the filters, offers Boxcar (moving average), Gaussian and Savitzky-Golay (the default), with a Window (ms) field (2 ms to start), its own Apply to menu and an Apply Smoothing button. Savitzky-Golay is best when peak heights must be preserved: it fits a polynomial over the window, with the order set in the panel (2–6, default 4). Higher orders keep peak heights and widths closer to the data but smooth less at the same window, and 2 smooths most, so 4 suits spikes and synaptic events. The window needs at least order + 2 samples; below that a lower order is used, so it always smooths. At the first and last half-window it uses the polynomial fitted to the end window, as the method is defined, rather than a mirror image of the trace. Gaussian's window spans ±3 standard deviations of the kernel. The window is typed in ms and converted for each sweep at its own rate to the nearest odd number of samples, at least 3 (1.2 ms at 5 kHz is 7 samples); the line under the field and the message after smoothing show the samples and the time actually used. A window of 0 ms or less is refused. Smoothing, Boxcar, Median and Three-point all compute in double precision, and each stretch between missing samples is smoothed on its own, so a missing sample stays missing instead of spreading over a window.

Mains Hum (50/60 Hz)

Enter the nominal line frequency (60 Hz in North America, 50 Hz in most of Europe and Asia). The filter then:

  • Measures the frequency actually present within ±0.5 Hz of the nominal value. Grid frequency drifts, so subtracting at exactly 60.000 Hz can leave most of the hum behind. When the strongest frequency in that range sits at its edge, there is no hum peak in the range (a real 59.4 Hz rhythm, say), and nothing is removed.
  • Tests whether hum is there at all, and which harmonics: each one's power, through a Hann window, must exceed 20 times the background power 3 to 5 frequency bins either side (an amplitude about 4.5 times the background; on noise alone about 1 harmonic in 2,000 passes). The fundamental is tested too, so a sweep without hum is left unchanged. Harmonics: Those present (the default) removes each harmonic that passes, up to the 40th; Fundamental only and First N remove those once the fundamental passes.
  • The test needs at least 8 cycles of the line frequency in the stretch it looks at (133 ms at 60 Hz, 160 ms at 50 Hz). On a shorter sweep nothing is removed and the message says why; use Single-frequency, which does not test, if you know hum is there.
  • Fits a straight line plus the sine waves to the data, jointly, and subtracts the sine waves (not the line) from the whole sweep. This removes almost no bandwidth: a genuine 58 Hz oscillation survives intact. The line keeps a membrane potential of −65 mV or a slow drift from being mistaken for hum.

The fit is robust. An ordinary least-squares fit would take part of the response as hum: an action potential has a component at every frequency, and a train of responses at 20 Hz has its own components at 60, 120, 180 Hz…. Instead, samples that a line plus hum cannot explain are set aside (a Huber fit, then Tukey's bisquare, the standard robust regression), so on a hum-free 0.2 s sweep with one action potential the filter changes the trace by under 0.02 mV, where least squares would change it by 0.8 mV.

Estimate from chooses which samples the hum is fitted to; it is always subtracted from the whole sweep. Whole sweep (the default) suits recordings that are mostly baseline. When responses fill much of the sweep, as in a stimulus train or rhythmic activity, no fit to the whole sweep can tell them from hum: choose Between cursors A–B and place the cursors on a quiet stretch, such as the baseline before the stimulus, or Outside cursors A–B and bracket the response. Fitted on a stretch and subtracted beyond it, the hum's frequency and phase are carried over; the longer the stretch, the more precisely (a 0.2 s stretch measures the frequency to a few hundredths of a hertz, which can leave a fraction of the hum a second away). Protocols take the same choice as two times in ms.

The message after filtering gives the frequency and harmonics removed, and the Data Log lists each sweep: frequency, harmonics, the fundamental's amplitude, the samples fitted, the share set aside as signal, and why any sweep was left unchanged. When more than 10% of a sweep was set aside, the message suggests fitting a quiet stretch instead. Missing samples stay missing and do not affect the fit.

Because hum frequency and amplitude drift over time, long records fitted to the whole sweep are fitted in overlapping two-second segments, run in parallel and blended smoothly, so the removal tracks the hum along the record. Which harmonics to remove is decided once for the whole record, by majority over the segments. Sweeps shorter than one segment, and fits to a cursor stretch, are fitted in one piece.

Hum removal is a remedy, not a substitute for a clean recording. Grounding, shielding and moving the offending transformer or lamp away from the rig remove the hum at its source, along with anything else that path was adding.

Data Transform: Scale/Offset Axes (⇧⌘X)

This panel holds Y axis scale and offset, X axis scale, and Set X = 0.

  • Y Axis Scale & Offset: each value becomes value × scale + offset (the same formula in Protocols, and in Batch Processing, which applies it to the recorded values). Useful for unit conversion or inverting polarity (scale = −1). The Scope checkboxes below it, 'Visible sweeps only' and 'Apply to all groups', choose the sweeps; by default it changes the visible sweeps of the selected group. It works on the values as the graph shows them, junction-potential correction included. Error bars scale with the values. The ⓘ button shows the formula. Scale and Offset must be plain numbers: text the parser doesn't read as a number, such as '1/1000', '50%' or '1.5k', is refused rather than read as its leading digits. The unit is not changed; if the scale changes it (A to pA, say), edit the Y-axis label.
  • X Axis Scale: multiplies every X value (time, or the X column of X-Y data) by the factor. Applies to the whole file. For evenly sampled data this is the same as rescaling the sample rate, so the factor can't be negative. The cursors, the Set X = 0 shift, a fixed X range, X error bars, and the sweeps' recorded start times scale too, so the view and the recording-time layout stay on the same data. The unit is not changed; edit the X-axis label if the scale changes it.
  • Set X = 0: shifts the X axis so a chosen time becomes zero (see below).

Baseline Correction (⇧⌘B)

Choose a Method, set the window in the Start and End fields (or with cursors A and B, or Set from cursors), choose Apply to, then click Apply Baseline. ⇧⌘B opens the panel with the Start field ready for typing.

  • Mean subtraction: subtracts the mean of a time window, in ms as the X axis shows it (after Set X = 0). You can also type a unit, such as '0.1 s'. A sweep that ends before the window starts is left unchanged, and the panel says how many. A window that starts before the sweep's first sample is refused.
  • Median subtraction: the same with the window's median, which a spontaneous event or an artifact inside the window barely moves (a 15 mV event across 4% of a window moved the mean by 0.67 mV and the median by under 0.01 mV).
  • Line through two windows: give a window before the response (the region fields) and one after it (the second window; it starts on the last 10% of the sweep). The straight line through each window's mean, at the window's middle, is subtracted from the whole sweep: a drift that runs straight across the response is removed without fitting the response itself (a 20 mV step on a 4 mV per 100 ms drift reads 19.96 mV). Protocols and the AI Assistant offer it too; Batch Processing, which has one region, does not.
  • The line under the fields lists the samples averaged, numbered from 0 at the sweep's start: 'Samples 31–32' means samples 31 and 32. Each end goes to its nearest sample and both are included, the rule Measure uses, so a baseline and a Measure mean over the same times average the same samples. A window that runs past a sweep's end uses that sweep's samples up to its end, and the message says how many sweeps that happened to.
  • Single point: set Start = End (or cursors A and B to the same place) to zero the trace on the one recorded sample nearest that time, for example 'Sample 32 (1600 ms)' at 20 Hz. Set from cursors moves both fields to that sample's time so you see exactly which point is used; the cursors stay where they are. A time exactly halfway between two samples uses the later one. If the sweeps in Apply to have different sample rates, each uses its own nearest sample. A window shorter than one sample interval uses the one sample nearest it. One sample's noise sets the zero, so a short window is steadier when the trace is noisy.
  • Linear detrend: fits and removes a straight line, eliminating slow drift. Polynomial fit does the same with a curve. Fit to: the whole sweep (the default), only the region in the Start–End fields, or everything except it. A fit to the whole sweep fits the response too and takes part of it away (a 20 mV step on a drifting baseline came out 17.6 mV after a linear detrend and 5.4 mV after order 2); fit the stretches without a response and the step stays 20.0 mV. The fitted curve is always subtracted from the whole sweep.
