  • All baseline arithmetic is in double precision, so a 10-million-sample sweep is corrected as exactly as a short one. Missing samples are skipped in the mean and the fits and stay missing. Baseline correction works along a time axis, so X-Y data are refused.
  • Polynomial: fits and removes a polynomial of order up to 8. Fitted to a stretch, it is computed on that stretch's own scale, so even a high order on a short stretch gives the true least-squares curve. When a sweep (or the region fitted) has too few samples for the order, or the fit is singular, a lower order is used, down to a straight line, and the message names the sweeps and the order used. A sweep with too few usable samples is left unchanged and named.
  • The window follows cursors A and B. Opening the panel shows the cursors, at the baseline's last spot or over the middle of the view, so drag them onto a quiet stretch before the stimulus. You can also edit the Start and End fields and press Return (↩) to apply.
  • After correction the Y axis rescales to the new data, even if you had set a manual range. Junction-potential correction is turned off, since both set the reference level. Undo restores both with the data.

Set X = 0 (Data Transform panel, ⇧⌘X)

Shifts the X axis so a chosen time (or X value, on X-Y data) becomes x = 0, for example to align sweeps to a stimulus onset. The data values are unchanged; only the axis labels move. Measure and Peaks report times on this shifted axis. It sits in the Data Transform panel, below X Axis Scale.

  • With the cursors showing, drag cursor A to the new zero point (the field follows it), then click Apply.
  • Or type a value as the axis shows it, in the axis unit, and press Return (↩) or click Apply.
  • 'Active offset' shows the current shift in the axis unit.
  • Reset removes the shift, back to the recording's own time: 0 at each sweep's start, or, for a file whose first sample isn't at 0, that first sample's time (see Supported File Formats → Recording Times).
  • Apply and Reset can both be undone.

Series Resistance Correction: Rₛ (⇧⌘I)

The Rₛ Correction panel has two modes, chosen at its top: Current clamp corrects a recorded voltage (as bridge balance does), and Voltage clamp corrects a recorded current (Traynelis 1998). The panel picks the mode that matches the recording, from the amplifier's telegraphed mode when the file stores it, else from how fast its voltage steps are (as VI and IV Plot judge it), and warns when the other mode is chosen. Both corrections stand apart from the data: Remove and ⌘Z undo them, and a saved graph keeps them.

Current Clamp

In current clamp, current flowing through the electrode's series resistance (Rₛ) adds an instant voltage jump at the start and end of each current step (ΔV = I × Rₛ). This artifact distorts resting potential, AP threshold, sag ratio and steady-state voltage. Rₛ correction removes it from the voltage sweeps: check the Injected Current box, then click Apply Rₛ Correction. The message and the Data Log give the Rₛ applied.

It needs a voltage channel (mV) and the injected current, which can come from three places. The Injected Current box at the top of the panel says which one is in use:

  • A recorded current channel (pA or nA). Used whenever there is one.
  • The file's protocol. An episodic ABF file stores its current-clamp protocol in its header, so even a voltage-only recording gives the exact timing and size of each step. The panel summarizes it, such as 'steps of −100 to +400 pA across 17 sweeps (sweep 1: −100 pA at 146.8 ms)'. Each sweep's Rₛ is measured on its largest step, and the correction follows the whole protocol.
  • A step you enter. For files with neither, or to override the protocol, check 'Describe the current step' (or 'Use a step I enter instead') and give the start and end times (as the X axis shows them), the step on sweep 1 and the change per sweep: −50 pA and +10 per sweep gives −50, −40, −30 … pA. The voltage jump is looked for within 2 ms of the start you enter, and the step is moved to where it is (the message says so); with no jump there, the automatic estimate is refused rather than measured at the wrong place.
  • How Rₛ is measured: for each sweep, a straight line is fitted to the voltage just after the step and extrapolated back to the step's edge, the clock tick of the last sample before the step. The size of that jump, divided by the current step, is Rₛ. Extrapolating separates the instant ohmic jump (Rₛ) from the membrane charging that starts right after it; reading the jump at the first sample after the step would add a sample of charging (22.6 MΩ for a true 20 MΩ at 20 kHz on a 20 pF cell). The correction subtracts Rₛ × ΔI for the duration of the step.
  • 'Log per-sweep Rₛ estimates to Data Log' (on by default) lists each sweep's fit: where it starts and ends after the edge, and its slope.
  • The estimate assumes the electrode and amplifier follow the step within one sample. A slower response, such as an uncompensated pipette capacitance, leaves the jump incomplete where the line starts and Rₛ reads low (about 86%, 72% and 44% of the true value for electrode time constants of 40, 100 and 200 µs at 20 kHz). The panel checks for it over all the sweeps together, from how far the first samples after the step fall short of the line, and warns when they do; neutralize the pipette capacitance when recording, or use the amplifier's bridge-balance value as a manual Rₛ.
  • Post-onset window: how long after onset the voltage is fitted. It is set automatically from the file's sample rate to give about ten samples (1.0 ms at 10 kHz, 0.4 ms at 25 kHz and above), and the panel shows how many samples it holds. If you type your own value, keep it under about a tenth of the membrane time constant; beyond that the fit starts measuring membrane charging instead of Rₛ.
  • Sample rate: accuracy barely changes between about 8 and 25 kHz and improves above that. Below 8 kHz the window reaches its 1.0 ms limit and the estimate is roughly twice as noisy; the panel warns you when this applies.
  • Baseline window: the time before step onset used for the pre-step voltage and current (default 5 ms).
  • Finding the step in a recorded current: its start and end are where the mean current over the next millisecond differs most from the mean over the previous one, so a current monitor that was low-pass filtered (its edges spread over several samples) or is noisy is handled; an unfiltered step is placed on its exact sample. The step's size is read after the edge has settled, 1 ms after onset. The whole sweep is searched, so a step starting in its first milliseconds is found, and in a biphasic step the first phase is taken. The step should last at least about 2 ms.
  • Correct: 'Every current step, relative to the sweep's start' (the default), 'The measured step only', or 'The whole current, holding current included'. Rₛ is measured on one step, but every change of the injected current drops across it, so with every step a multi-step or biphasic protocol is corrected throughout. With the measured step only, the second phase of a biphasic pulse keeps its error. 'Holding current included' removes I × Rₛ everywhere (2 mV for −100 pA at 20 MΩ); use it only when the current channel reads 0 with no current injected. The Data Log names the choice; 'Log per-sweep Rₛ estimates' adds the per-sweep table to its summary.
  • The recorded current is read as constant levels between its steps, each level its own mean, so the corrected trace has sharp edges and none of the current's noise. With a protocol (or a step you enter) instead of a recorded current, the whole command is always followed: 'The measured step only' isn't offered, and the choice sets whether its holding level counts.
  • Each sweep gets its own Rₛ estimate, so Rₛ drift during a recording is followed. The message after applying, and the Data Log, report the Rₛ actually applied: the mean over the measured sweeps and its range.
  • This mode removes the Rₛ drop from a recorded voltage; for a voltage-clamp current, use the Voltage clamp mode (below). A voltage that jumps at once to 90% or more of its final deflection, with no membrane charging after it, looks like a voltage-clamp command (or a pipette without a cell), and the automatic estimate is refused with that explanation.
  • A sweep with no clear current step (such as the 0 pA sweep of a family), or whose jump is inside its own noise (a small step), has no Rₛ of its own. It is left out of the mean, and the channel's pooled Rₛ, to which a small step's jump still contributes, is applied to it, scaled by its own step (so nothing changes for 0 pA). The Data Log names these sweeps below the table instead of listing them in it.
  • The pooled Rₛ is the weighted least-squares slope of the measured jumps on their current steps, each sweep weighted by the inverse variance of its own jump. It is set to 0 only when it lies within 3 of its standard errors of 0 (on the summed degrees of freedom of the sweeps' fits).
  • If amplifier bridge balance was used, the remaining Rₛ may be zero (fully compensated) or negative (over-compensated); both are handled. Only when the pooled Rₛ is itself within the noise is the correction zero. The test for a jump matches 3σ (two-sided p = 0.0027), as Student's t on the noise level of the 5 ms baseline pooled with the fit's own scatter. The short fit window alone has only 2 to 7 degrees of freedom, and on 2 a real 1 mV jump at 5 kHz was set to zero in most sweeps.
  • An estimate that can't be Rₛ is refused with an explanation: a jump in the opposite direction to the current step (over-compensation, swapped channels or an artifact), or an Rₛ larger than the input resistance implied by the steady-state response. Use the manual override in those cases.
  • Manual override: check 'Use manual Rₛ instead of auto-detect' and enter a value (in MΩ) to apply to every sweep. Use it when automatic estimates are unstable or refused, or when you measured Rₛ another way (such as bridge balance at the amplifier). Zero and negative values are allowed. A manual Rₛ needs no step: with a protocol of ramps or pulse trains, it is applied along the whole command.
  • Re-applying replaces the current correction rather than adding to it: with a correction active the button reads 'Re-apply Rₛ Correction', so changing from 50 MΩ to −10 MΩ leaves −10 MΩ applied.
  • The Rₛ button in the status bar appears for recordings with two or more channels and whenever a correction is applied; it shows the active value and turns orange while a correction is on. Click it to open the Rₛ panel.
  • Remove restores the uncorrected voltage exactly, as long as nothing else has changed the samples since (a filter, baseline correction or scale/offset). After such a change, use ⌘Z to step back instead.
  • Saving keeps the correction. While it is still the last change to the samples, the file holds the uncorrected voltage and the correction itself (each sweep's Rₛ step and where it applies), and reopening rebuilds the corrected trace exactly, so Re-apply and Remove work as before saving; the file stays its usual size. After a later change, the corrected samples are saved as they are and the Rₛ button in the status bar records the correction.

Typical Rₛ values: 5–20 MΩ for whole-cell patch, 30–100 MΩ for perforated patch, 50–200 MΩ for sharp electrodes. Rₛ is only part of the input resistance (steady-state deflection divided by current) and always smaller than it. If a computed Rₛ looks implausible, check the channel units first.

Voltage Clamp

In voltage clamp, the current flows through whatever Rₛ the amplifier left uncompensated, so the membrane sits at Vₘ = Vcmd − I·Rₛ instead of at the command, and the membrane capacitance charges through Rₛ, which filters fast currents with a time constant of Rₛ·Cₘ. A 500 pA current through 20 MΩ moves the membrane 10 mV toward the current's reversal potential, and at 30 pF an EPSC's 0.3 ms rise slows to about 0.7 ms. The Voltage clamp mode corrects the recorded current for both, point by point, as Traynelis (1998) describes:

  • Vₘ = Vcmd − I·Rₛ; the ionic current Iₘ = I − Cₘ·dVₘ/dt, which removes the Rₛ·Cₘ filtering; and Icorr = Iₘ · (Vcmd − Erev) / (Vₘ − Erev), the current the cell would have passed at the command potential. 'Fraction to correct' (default 100%) applies part of it, as Traynelis's program does.
  • Rₛ and Cₘ, Measure automatically (the default): from a voltage-step test pulse in the same sweeps (a step of 0.5 to 25 mV, 2 ms or longer, that returns to the level before it), fitted as Membrane Test fits it. Recorded with the same amplifier settings as the data, the pulse gives the Rₛ the amplifier left uncompensated, which is exactly what needs correcting, so no compensation % is needed. Rₘ comes with it. By default the sweeps' pulses are averaged first, as Membrane Test does; 'Use each sweep's own test pulse' follows an Rₛ that drifts. The Data Log warns when Rₛ changes by more than 20% across the sweeps.
  • Other sources: 'Measure in a window I set' (the test pulse that starts in that window), 'From the last Membrane Test' (run E-Phys Tools → Membrane Test… on a membrane-test recording made with the same settings, then come back), 'From the amplifier's telegraphs' (the whole-cell Rₛ and Cₘ dials an ABF file stores) and 'Values I enter'. Only when the Rₛ is a total, the telegraphed dial or a dial reading you type, does the panel ask for the online Rₛ compensation % that was set on the amplifier, and it corrects the remainder.
  • With whole-cell capacitance compensation on, the amplifier cancels the test pulse's transient, so there is nothing to measure. When the file's telegraphs say compensation was on, Measure automatically says so and uses the telegraphed values (enter the compensation %). When a pulse shows no transient that can be fitted, the panel says so plainly and asks for another source. ABF 1 files telegraph Cₘ but not Rₛ: 'Values I enter' starts with the telegraphed Cₘ filled in. Measure shows what will be used, and where it came from, before you apply.
  • Vcmd: the file's protocol (an ABF's epochs), else a recorded command or Vₘ channel (read as its constant levels, so its noise isn't differentiated), else a holding potential you enter. The panel says which.
  • Erev: typed, or 'Erev from the last IV Plot' (see IV Plot below). About 0 mV suits AMPA and NMDA receptor currents. The Data Log reports how sensitive the result is to it, computed from your data, for example 'a 5 mV error in Erev changes the corrected peak by about 1.1%'. When the command is within 5 mV of Erev the correction is refused: there is too little driving force for it to mean anything. Samples whose Vₘ comes within 10% of the command's distance from Erev, or crosses it, are left unscaled and listed in the Data Log, and a command within 5 mV of Erev gets only the capacitive correction.
  • Noise: undoing the Rₛ·Cₘ filtering takes the derivative of Vₘ, which amplifies high-frequency noise in proportion to frequency and Rₛ·Cₘ. So the derivative is taken through a zero-phase low-pass (Gaussian). With 'Choose the dV/dt low-pass automatically' on (the default) it is the highest that at most doubles the sample-to-sample noise, starting from a quarter of the sample rate or the amplifier's filter; a lower one is quieter but slows fast rises, and spreads sharp edges slightly both ways in time. The Data Log gives the low-pass used and how much the noise grew. For a very noisy recording, low-pass filter it first (Filter/Smoothing), or average sweeps.
  • 'Correct the leak separately (uses Rₘ)', on by default: with Rₘ known (from the test pulse, a Membrane Test, or entered), the resting leak is moved to the command potential on its own and only the rest of the current is rescaled with Erev. Traynelis's original rescales the whole current, holding current included, as one conductance; with Rₘ = 300 MΩ that left EPSCs 1.5 to 8% small (amplitude and charge) at Rₛ 5 to 25 MΩ in simulations, which the separate leak removes. Off, the Data Log says how much the holding current was rescaled.
  • Accuracy, on simulated single-compartment cells (Rₛ 5 to 25 MΩ, Cₘ 10 to 50 pF, Rₘ 300 MΩ, a 20 kHz recording behind a 5 kHz filter, 2 pA noise, an AMPA-like EPSC with 0.2 ms rise and 3 ms decay at −70 mV, Rₛ and Cₘ measured from a −5 mV test pulse): the test pulse gave Rₛ and Cₘ within 2%; the corrected EPSC's amplitude came within 2% and its charge within 0.3% of the true current, where the raw recording was 5 to 44% small in amplitude and 3 to 15% in charge, and its 10–90% rise time within 6 to 34% (raw: 10 to 260% slow), the remainder being the low-pass's price for keeping the noise within 2×. Only the 5 MΩ, 10 pF cell's test pulse couldn't be measured: its transient (0.05 ms) lasts one sample at 20 kHz, and the panel says so. Without noise the amplitude and charge come within 0.1%. With the leak not separated and no low-pass, the result matches Keine's MATLAB implementation of Traynelis's program (Zenodo 2538615) in charge to 0.05%.
  • Assumptions and limits: a single-compartment cell (space-clamp errors in dendrites can't be fixed offline), and a current channel whose zero is right (Vₘ uses the absolute current). The driving-force step treats the conductance as linear, so it is exact for ligand-gated currents but only approximate for voltage-gated currents, whose gating also depends on the true Vₘ: on a simulated delayed-rectifier K⁺ family at Rₛ 15 MΩ, steady-state currents 13 to 48% small became 9 to 35% small after correction, and the activation kinetics stayed distorted. For voltage-gated currents, correct the IV Plot's voltage axis instead (below), which is exact at steady state.
  • Long recordings are corrected in the background; the panel shows a spinner and the window stays usable. The status bar's Rₛ button reads 'Rₛ = … MΩ (VC)' while a voltage-clamp correction stands. Saving keeps it as a recipe (each sweep's Rₛ and Cₘ, Erev, the fraction, the low-pass, the command source) with the uncorrected current, and reopening recomputes the same corrected trace.
  • The AI Assistant can apply it too (apply_vc_rs_correction), with the same choices.

Graph Tools

Graph Tools work on the graph whose window they were opened from and open the result in a new window; the original is unchanged. Each sweep is processed at its own sample rate and length, so a recording whose channels were sampled at different rates stays correctly timed. If a tool can't run (for example, the graph already has the requested rate, or it is an X-Y graph with its own X values), the sheet stays open and says why.

  • Decimate… reduces the point count by a whole-number factor N. By default each sweep is filtered against aliasing and every Nth point kept (described below). With 'Average Y values within each bin' on, each new point is the mean of N samples, placed at the center of its bin, optionally with a second graph of the SD or SEM of each bin for error bars; a partial bin at the end of a sweep is dropped. Averaging is only a weak anti-alias filter: content just above the new Nyquist frequency is only about 4 dB down (about 10 dB at 1.5 times it) and folds back into the result, so turn averaging off when the trace has content there. Off, each sweep is low-pass filtered (zero-phase, at 80% of the new Nyquist frequency) and every Nth point kept, including the last one when it falls on that grid, so nothing aliases. This gives the same points as Resample to the same rate.
  • Interpolate… inserts 1 or more new samples between each pair: linear by default (never overshoots, but flattens a peak between samples), or band-limited (a windowed-sinc kernel), which recovers such a peak at the cost of ringing near steps and clipped samples. Batch processing, protocols and the AI Assistant offer the same choice.
  • Resample… converts every sweep to one new sample rate, with anti-alias filtering when lowering it and windowed-sinc reconstruction, accurate to about 0.02% for content up to 30% of the source rate. A whole-number reduction (such as 20 kHz to 2 kHz) keeps every Nth filtered sample instead, as Decimate does. Each sweep keeps its duration. Near the source's Nyquist frequency, and within about 8 samples of each end of a sweep, the reconstruction is less exact.
  • Results open at the source's true time: a recording whose first sample isn't at 0 keeps that shift, and averaged Decimate points sit at their bin centers. They also keep the source window's Set X = 0 shift, cursors A and B and X zoom (see Cursors).
  • Integrate: the running integral (trapezoidal rule) of each visible sweep, over the full sweep or between cursors A and B. The Y unit becomes unit·s (such as mV·s). Between cursors, the final value equals Measure's Area for the same window. A missing sample stays missing in the result, and the intervals on either side of it add nothing to the total. On X-Y data the points are taken in increasing X (sorted first), and points with a missing X are left out.
  • Between cursors, a sweep that has no sample between them is left out of the result and named; when no sweep has one, nothing is computed. On a graph whose sweeps have different sample rates, the result's time axis starts at the first sweep's first sample in the window, so another sweep can be shown up to one of its sample intervals early.
  • Differentiate: the first derivative of each visible sweep, over the same choice of range. The Y unit becomes unit/s (such as mV/s), and on X-Y data unit per X unit, labeled d/dX (an I-V curve's slope, in pA/mV). 'Pre-smooth (pts)' (None, 3, 5, 9, 15, 25) applies a boxcar smooth first (Protocols and the AI Assistant accept any count; an even one runs one point longer, and their report says so), recommended for noisy traces because differentiating amplifies noise. The whole sweep is smoothed before a cursor window is cut, so the window's ends average real samples on both sides, and near the sweep's own ends the average narrows symmetrically, which leaves a straight line's slope intact. The derivative is the three-point difference over the actual X spacing (exact for a parabola, as numpy.gradient computes it), so unevenly spaced X-Y data are differentiated as accurately as evenly sampled sweeps.

The derivative of a raw voltage trace sampled at 25 kHz shows large values (thousands of mV/s). This is correct. Pre-smoothing (9–25 points) brings out features such as action potential upstrokes.

Signal Processing

Signal Processing operations open the result in a new graph window and leave the original unchanged. They're in the inspector's Signal category, and the most common ones also have keyboard shortcuts.

Average Sweeps (⇧⌘A)

Averages the visible sweeps into one sweep per group (so a voltage + current recording gives one average of each) and opens the result in a new window. The sweep name gives the number of sweeps behind each sample, such as 'Group 1 avg (n=30)', or its range, such as 'n=24–30', where sweeps have unequal lengths or gaps (after Align at Peak, for example); with a shaded band it also names the spread, such as '± SEM', so the legend says what the shading is. A group whose sweeps are in different units (mV with pA) or have different X values is refused with the reason. Sweeps at different sample rates are interpolated linearly onto the group's highest rate, never past a sweep's own last sample, and the Data Log says so. Click Average Visible Sweeps in the Average panel, or press ⇧⌘A to run it right away without opening the inspector.

The Average panel has more choices, which ignore which sweeps are visible and give one average per recording channel. On a multi-channel recording, sweep numbers here count trials within each channel, as Review sweeps does: All Odd on a voltage + current file averages trials 1, 3, 5… of the voltage channel and, separately, the same trials of the current channel:

  • All, All Odd, All Even: every sweep, or sweeps 1, 3, 5… or 2, 4, 6….
  • Every N: each run of N consecutive sweeps separately (N = 3 on 9 sweeps gives averages of 1–3, 4–6 and 7–9).
  • Every Nth: sweeps N, 2N, 3N….
  • Average Custom: the sweeps you list, such as '3, 5-7, 33-41'.

Variability (in the Average panel, and used by ⇧⌘A too) shows how much the sweeps spread around each average: None, SD, SEM, or a Confidence interval (95% unless you type another level). Each is worked out at every sample from the sweeps that have a value there, so where sweeps have different lengths or gaps each sample uses its own n:

  • SD: the sample standard deviation, with n − 1 in the denominator.
  • SEM: SD/√n.
  • Confidence interval: mean ± t(n − 1)·SEM. It is pointwise: each sample's interval covers the true mean at that sample 95% of the time, but the band as a whole is not a simultaneous band for the entire trace.
  • Samples with fewer than 2 sweeps get no error (the band has a gap there).

Show it as a Shaded band around the mean, in the mean's color, or as Mean + and − sweeps: two extra sweeps, such as 'Mean + SEM' and 'Mean − SEM', drawn dashed and thinner in the mean's color, in the same panel. The band is saved in a .@graph file, drawn in every figure export (PDF, SVG, PNG and the other image formats), kept by Export Visible and Scale / Offset, and hidden if you change the mean in any other way. The sweeps are ordinary sweeps, so every file format, export and measurement sees them. The Data Log states the measure and the n behind each average.

Normalize (⇧⌘R)

Divides each sweep by a reference value and opens the dimensionless result in a new window. Two modes:

  • Per sweep: each sweep is divided by its own value, so differences in amplitude are lost. Choose its largest absolute peak (the default), positive peak, negative peak, its value at cursor A, or a number you enter. Handy for comparing shapes, such as AP waveforms across conditions.
  • Per sweep, relative to a baseline window: Percent of baseline (100 × value / baseline mean, its own baseline reading 100%, the way synaptic plasticity is plotted; a negative baseline, such as a field EPSP's slope, still reads +100%); ΔF/F₀ ((F − F₀) / F₀, with F₀ the baseline mean, for fluorescence traces that arrive as sweeps; F₀ must be positive, so subtract background first); and z-score ((value − mean) / SD of the baseline window, SD with n − 1; of the whole sweep without a window). The first two need a baseline window. Peak points and polarity don't apply to them.
  • Per sweep, Min–max: each sweep scaled from 0 (its smallest value) to 1 (its largest).
  • Global (all sweeps): the sweeps are divided by the largest peak among them (absolute by default, positive, or negative), so relative amplitudes are kept and only the largest sweep reaches ±1. Handy when amplitude differences matter, as in dose-response or run-down. Each unit is scaled on its own, so a current sweep is never divided by a voltage peak.

Measure from: a baseline window (the default, the first 5 ms of the sweep to start with) or zero. With a baseline, each sweep's own mean over the window is subtracted first and the trace becomes (value − baseline) / (reference − baseline), so a response is scaled by its amplitude, not by where it sits. Measured from zero, an action potential from −70 mV with an afterhyperpolarization to −78 mV has its 'largest peak' at −78 and comes out upside down, and an EPSC on a holding current is divided by whatever sample is furthest from 0; the panel warns when the sweeps sit far from zero.

Peak = mean of N points: the reference is the mean of N samples centered on the peak (or on cursor A). With one point, the single most extreme sample, noise biases the reference outward: a 50 pA EPSC in 5 pA noise comes out at 0.85 rather than 1. A few points spanning the top of the peak reduce that.

Keep polarity (on by default): each sweep is divided by the size of its reference, so a negative-going response stays negative and its peak becomes −1. Turned off, it is divided by the signed reference and a negative-going trace is turned upside down (peak +1). Protocols keep the setting they were saved with.

A sweep with no reference (no positive peak above the baseline, an empty baseline window, cursor A missing) is left unchanged and named in the message; the Data Log lists each sweep's reference. Fit curves and AP markers are not normalized. By default it uses the visible sweeps of every group. Values are taken as the graph shows them, with junction-potential correction if it's on. ⇧⌘R opens this panel in the inspector, where you choose the options and click Normalize → New Window.

Align Sweeps at Peak (⌥⌘K)

Shifts each visible sweep so its main peak falls at the same time, X = 0 in the new window (Set X = 0 moves it like any other), for overlaying action potentials, EPSPs or other stereotyped events to compare their shape. Set the options below in the Align at Peak panel and click Align at Peak, or press ⌥⌘K to align on the largest absolute peak immediately, without opening the inspector. Each sweep moves by its own amount, so the new window doesn't take the original's cursors, X shift or zoom.

  • Peak direction: Largest absolute peak (default), Positive peak or Negative peak. With the largest absolute peak, the polarity is chosen once for all sweeps (from their median excursion above and below rest), so a biphasic event aligns on the same phase in every sweep instead of on its peak in some and its trough in others.
  • Search for the peak between cursors A and B only: otherwise the whole sweep is searched, and a stimulus artifact, a spontaneous event or, in pure noise, the largest noise sample becomes the 'peak'.
  • Align on: The peak (default) or Half-way up the rising phase, which is the first sample before the peak's top that is half-way from the resting level (the median before the peak in the search window) to the peak. The single peak sample moves with the noise, and lining up noise maxima inflates an average of the aligned sweeps (500 aligned 20 pA EPSCs averaged to 23.8 pA); the steep middle of the rise barely moves. Either way, sweeps are shifted by whole samples.
  • Fit curves, AP markers and flat sweeps are left out, and the message and Data Log say so. X-Y data have no time axis and are refused.
  • By default every sample is kept: each sweep is shifted, not cut, and the result is as long as the longest lead-in plus the longest tail. This works at any time scale, from a millisecond spike to an event lasting minutes.
  • Trim the lead-in before each peak: keeps only the time you enter in 'Keep before the peak (ms)' ("5 ms", "2 s" and "1 min" are all accepted) and drops earlier samples. The tail is never cut.
  • Where a shifted sweep has no data, it has a gap, which Measure and the other tools skip.
  • Sweeps are shifted by whole samples, so each peak lands on the nearest sample to the common time, not exactly on it. For brief events at low sample rates this blurs an average of the aligned sweeps: for a 0.25 ms action potential at 10 kHz, the average's peak reads about 3% low and its half-width about 5% wide; at 50 kHz the effect is under 0.3%. For brief events recorded below about 20 kHz, raise the rate first (Graph Tools → Interpolate, band-limited) and then align.

Power Spectral Density / FFT (⌥⌘P)

Computes the power spectral density (PSD) of each visible sweep by Welch's method (Hann-windowed segments, averaged) and opens it in a new graph window with frequency (Hz) on the X axis. In the PSD / FFT panel, set the options below and click Compute PSD / FFT; ⌥⌘P runs it with default settings immediately.

The spectrum is computed in the background, so @Graph stays responsive on a long recording. A progress bar shows in the panel (for ⌥⌘P, at the bottom of the graph) with a Cancel button; canceling, or closing the graph window, stops it, and no window opens and nothing is added to the Data Log.

Settings

  • Segment size (Welch FFT window): Auto, or a fixed size from 1024 points up to the length of the shortest sweep. Each choice shows its frequency resolution (Hz/bin). Auto uses the largest power of 2 up to a quarter of the shortest sweep, so at least 7 overlapping segments are averaged (such as 131,072 points, about 0.19 Hz, for a 30 s sweep at 25 kHz), but never below 64 points, so a sweep shorter than 256 samples has fewer segments. Larger segments give finer resolution but a noisier spectrum. A sweep shorter than the smallest segment (64 points) is one segment of its own length, with the Hann window and its normalization at that length, zero-padded to 64 points (as SciPy's welch does when the data are shorter than a segment). Sizes are powers of 2, so the samples after a sweep's last whole segment are left out (18% of a 10,000-sample sweep at the largest size); the Data Log says how many.
  • Segments overlap by 50%, and each segment's mean is removed first so the resting potential doesn't swamp the low frequencies. 'Remove from each segment: Linear trend' removes each segment's least-squares straight line instead, so a slow drift (5 mV across a sweep, say) doesn't raise the lowest few bins.
  • The whole spectrum is computed in double precision (it is stored at single precision afterwards), so bins far below the peak, and the lowest bins of a trace on a large offset, are as reliable as the rest.
  • Amplitude (√ of power): shows the square root, the amplitude spectral density in units/√Hz (such as µV/√Hz), the way amplifier and electrode noise is usually quoted; with Normalize off, the RMS amplitude spectrum in units. In dB the two read the same, so it applies to linear values only.
  • Confidence interval: shades each spectrum's pointwise interval at the level typed (95% by default), from the χ² distribution on the estimate's equivalent degrees of freedom: 2 per segment (per taper with multitaper), less for overlapping segments, by Welch's (1967) formula including the overlap between different tapers; half at 0 Hz and Nyquist. Seven half-overlapping Hann segments give about 13.4 degrees of freedom. In simulated white noise the intervals covered the true spectrum 95.0–95.1% of the time for 1–20 segments, with or without multitaper. The interval is for each frequency on its own, not a band for the whole spectrum at once. The Data Log gives the degrees of freedom.
  • Normalize to PSD (on by default): gives true power density in units²/Hz (such as mV²/Hz), which doesn't depend on the sample rate or segment size. Off gives the power spectrum in units², where a sinusoid of amplitude a peaks at a²/2 (its mean-square power) when its frequency falls on a bin; half a bin away the Hann window's peak reads up to 1.4 dB lower, so sum the bins around a peak for its exact power. Use it for sizing a line-noise or stimulus peak. These match the 'density' and 'spectrum' scalings of Welch's method in SciPy and MATLAB.
  • Y scale: Linear; Logarithmic axis, which keeps the power values linear and draws them on a log axis, the way to see the wide range of a noise spectrum; or dB values (10·log₁₀ of the power), for export. A bin with no power is shown at a floor set by the spectrum's own peak (300 dB below it), so a trace in A reads like the same trace in pA. Don't average dB spectra: the mean of dB values is a mean of logarithms and reads low (2.4 dB for single-segment spectra of noise, 0.3 dB with 7 segments); Average says so in the Data Log.
  • Fit curves and AP markers are left out, so a short fit sweep can't shrink everyone's segment.
  • Max frequency: limits the X axis. At 0, the spectrum runs up to the Nyquist frequency, and the window opens zoomed to where the power is (to three significant figures, so a 0.2 Hz rhythm opens on 0–0.381 Hz).

Interpreting the output

Whole-cell voltage recordings typically show a 1/f² (brown noise) spectrum: power falls steeply with frequency. Typical values for a patched neuron at rest:

  • 1–5 Hz: 0.1–1 mV²/Hz (slow membrane potential fluctuations, drift)
  • 10–50 Hz: 0.001–0.1 mV²/Hz (synaptic noise, channel noise)
  • 100–500 Hz: 10⁻⁵ to 10⁻³ mV²/Hz (amplifier + pipette noise floor)
  • Single-sweep PSDs are inherently noisy. The log-log slope of −2 is only apparent when averaging many sweeps or using a longer recording.

For a smoother spectrum, average the PSD sweeps (linear power, not dB values): press ⇧⌘A in the PSD window. To compare spectra from several files, use Combine Open Graphs.

Multitaper

An alternative to the single Hann window above, useful when the spectrum still looks noisy after averaging every available segment (a short recording, or a large segment size chosen for fine resolution). Turn on Multitaper and each segment is analyzed with several tapers instead of one; their spectra are averaged before Welch's own segment averaging runs, trading some frequency resolution for a further reduction in noise.

  • Time-bandwidth product (NW): sets how many tapers are used (K = 2 × NW − 1, shown next to the field) and how much the frequency resolution widens (roughly 2 × NW divided by the segment duration). NW runs from 1 to 10. Start at NW = 3 (5 tapers), the standard default for LFP/EEG-scale work.
  • Lower NW (2–2.5, fewer tapers): sharper resolution, for when nearby spectral features need to stay distinguishable, or the segment is already long enough that Welch's own averaging is doing most of the noise reduction.
  • Higher NW (4–6, more tapers): more smoothing, for short segments, noisy signals, or when only the power summed across a broad band matters rather than its fine structure.
  • The info button next to the NW field shows the resulting resolution in Hz for the current segment size.
  • Protocols, Batch Processing and the AI Assistant offer the same Multitaper option and NW.

@Graph builds the tapers from a closed-form formula (sine tapers, Riedel & Sidorenko 1995) rather than the Slepian/DPSS tapers of Thomson's original method. They optimize different things: Slepian tapers keep the most energy inside the band ±W (least leakage from far away), while sine tapers minimize the local bias, the smearing of the spectrum's shape within the band, and leak a little more from far away. For smooth spectra the two give nearly the same result, and the closed form avoids an eigendecomposition over segments that can run past half a million points.

Histogram (⌃⌘H)

Graph Utilities → Convert to Histogram… bins the amplitude values of the graph's sweeps and opens the result in a histogram window. Handy for looking at the distribution of membrane potential or noise. With several groups, choose the selected group or all groups. Fit curves and AP markers are left out: they aren't recordings.

  • Set either the bin width or the bin count, and the histogram is built from the one you set last. A width gives bins with edges at whole multiples of it (a width of 5 mV gives edges at …, −70, −65, …); a count gives exactly that many equal bins from the smallest value to the largest. The starting width (about 30 bins) and the range shown come from the group being binned, and follow when you choose another group, until you type a width or count yourself.
  • Each bin holds values from its lower edge up to, but not including, its upper edge, as R's hist(right = FALSE) makes them (R's default closes bins on the right instead). With a width, a largest value lying exactly on an edge gets a bin of its own, so integers 0 to 10 at width 1 give eleven bars of 1 (NumPy puts 10 in with 9). With a count, the last bin also holds values equal to its upper edge, so the largest value is counted.
  • Values are compared with the edges at the precision they are recorded in (32 bits), so data on a decimal grid, such as 0.1 mV steps, bin evenly at any decimal width.
  • Values: optionally bin only values between two limits (the bins then span the limits, and values outside are counted and reported), and only samples between the graph's cursors. Limits keep one outlier from squeezing the rest into a few bars.
  • When the recording's values come in fixed steps (a digitizer's resolution), the setup form says so and offers the nearest width that is a multiple of the step, so every bar spans the same number of levels rather than alternately 16 and 17. The starting width is such a multiple.
  • With All groups and a bin count, "Same bins for every group" (on by default) bins every group over the range of all of them, so their bars and percentages line up. A width gives every group the same edges anyway.
  • Each histogram shows n, the values binned, and how many missing values were skipped or fell outside the limits. Frequency (%) is of n.
  • Y axis: Count, Frequency (%), or Density (count ÷ (n × bin width), per unit of X, so the bars' areas sum to 1). Density is the one to use for comparing groups of different size or histograms with different bin widths, as R's hist(freq = FALSE) and matplotlib's density = True plot it. Log axis (in the histogram window) shows the counts on a logarithmic scale, for heavy-tailed data such as event amplitudes or intervals; empty bins have no bar on it. Copy as Table includes the density.
  • Curve (in the histogram window): Normal draws a normal distribution with the binned values' mean and SD; Kernel density draws a Gaussian kernel density estimate with Silverman's bandwidth (0.9 · min(SD, IQR/1.34) · n^(−1/5), R's default bw.nrd0), computed from the values counted in 4,096 fine bins as R's density() bins them. Either is scaled to the Y axis (× n × bin width for counts), and its parameters are shown under the chart. Read them as a guide to the shape, not as a test of whether the data are normal. The AI Assistant's histogram can open with any Y axis, the log axis and either curve.
  • The values are those the graph shows: junction-potential corrected on a voltage panel. The X axis carries the panel's own Y title (mV, pA and so on), and the Y axis shows Count or Frequency (%).
  • Copy as Table copies one row per bin (center, edges, count, percentage) as plain tab-separated text, with as many decimals as the bin width needs.
  • Cumulative Probability, in the same menu, plots the cumulative distribution (0 to 1) instead, of the same values: as the graph shows them (junction-potential corrected), without fit curves or AP markers.

For inter-event interval or dwell-time histograms, copy the event times from the Data Log into a Spreadsheet and use Data to Chart.

Other Graph Utilities

  • Channel Gain… sets the gain of the amplifier connected to a channel's input when the file doesn't record it. Type the gain as the amplifier states it (0.5 mV/pA, 10 mV/mV), the unit to show and an optional offset. The channel's values and unit change as if it had been recorded that way, so analyses and exports use them. Undo reverts it, and Remove Gain gives the input back. For HEKA files, 'Use for every series of this file' remembers the gain on this Mac for that channel of that file; Forget This File's Gains clears it. It is also in Modify → Data Transform, under Scale / Offset.
  • Copy Style / Paste Style (⌥⌘C / ⌥⌘V, the Graph Utilities menu, or right-click on the plot) copies all visual settings (colors, line widths, fonts, axes) from one graph to another, so your figures match.
  • Combine Open Graphs overlays the visible sweeps of all open graph windows in one window, keeping their colors, sample rates and lengths. Each graph's groups are kept; groups that share a unit are pooled, one from each graph, so two voltage + current recordings give one voltage and one current group, while a paired recording's two voltage channels stay separate.
  • Combine Selected Graphs… does the same for the windows you choose, in the order you set.
  • Concatenate Open Graphs joins open graphs end to end in time, such as a recording split across files: group by group and episode by episode, in window order. The pieces joined into one sweep must have the same sample rate and unit; if not, nothing is joined and the message names the mismatch (resample first, or check the channel order). A graph with fewer episodes leaves a gap as long as its own sweeps.
  • Delete Sweeps… lists all sweeps and groups; check the ones to remove (such as sweeps with artifacts) and click Delete Selected. The result opens in a new window.
  • Swap X and Y Axes exchanges the X and Y data, for example to show an I(V) curve as V(I).
  • Continuous to Episodic… cuts a long continuous sweep into equal episodes (length in ms, s or samples) in a new window. A channel recorded at a different rate is cut into episodes of the same duration, so episode N covers the same time on every channel. The last episode keeps whatever remains.
  • Episodic to Continuous joins each group's sweeps into one continuous trace, for example for LFP or EEG saved as episodes. The sweeps of a group must share a sample rate.
  • Decimate, Interpolate, Resample: see Modifying Graphs → Graph Tools.
  • VI Plot, IV Plot, Firing Properties, Sag Analysis and Membrane Test are in the E-Phys Tools menu.

References

Riedel KS & Sidorenko A (1995). Minimum bias multiple taper spectral estimation. IEEE Transactions on Signal Processing, 43(1):188–195. https://doi.org/10.1109/78.365298 (the Multitaper tapers above).

Traynelis SF (1998). Software-based correction of single compartment series resistance errors. Journal of Neuroscience Methods, 86(1):25–34. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0165-0270(98)00140-X (voltage-clamp Rₛ correction above).

Keine C (2019). Offline series resistance correction, v1.1 (MATLAB). Zenodo. https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.2538615 (the reference implementation the voltage-clamp correction was checked against).

Measuring & Analysis

The Measure, Peaks and Curve Fitting tabs of the inspector measure the sweeps of the front graph and write their results to the Data Log. Press Return (↩) in any number field of an analysis panel (including the cursor A/B fields) to re-run the panel's main action (Measure, Run Peak Analysis, Fit, Detect APs and so on).

The range choice in Measure, Peaks, Curve Fitting, AP Detection, Synapse Detection and Graph Tools (Integrate and Differentiate) is linked to the cursors: choosing 'Between cursors A – B' shows the cursors and 'Full sweep' hides them, and turning the cursors on or off with ⌘K switches the choice to match. A panel opens with the choice that matches the cursors, so opening one never hides cursors you placed. Measure, Peaks and Curve Fitting use the visible sweeps of the selected group unless you change their Scope. AP Detection uses the visible voltage sweeps of every group, Synapse Detection the selected group's visible sweeps, and Graph Tools the visible sweeps of every group.

Measure Panel (⇧⌘M)

Measures each sweep over the full sweep or between cursors A and B. Check the metrics you want, choose the Scope and click Measure:

  • Average amplitude: the mean sample value.
  • Area: the integral of the waveform (amplitude × time) by the trapezoidal rule, from the sample nearest cursor A to the sample nearest cursor B when measuring between cursors. It equals the last value of Integrate over the same window.
  • Maximum and Minimum, each with an optional Location on the X axis as shown (so it follows Set X = 0).
  • Number of points, Variance (n − 1), Standard deviation and Std error of mean. A single point has no spread: its variance, SD and SEM read N/A.
  • Gaps (missing samples, such as the edges Align at Peak leaves, or a missing Y value in X-Y data) are skipped: every statistic uses the real samples, and the area is not integrated across a gap.

Peaks Panel: Peak Detection (⇧⌘D)

The Peaks panel finds peaks in each sweep and measures each one. Set the options below and click Run Peak Analysis:

  • Direction: Auto (the default: whichever direction swings further from the reference level in each sweep, meaning the baseline window's mean, or the resting level without one), Positive or Negative.
  • Which peaks: Maximum (the default: the largest peak in each sweep, with no threshold; the middle of a flat top), First (the first peak beyond the threshold) or All (every peak beyond it).
  • Threshold: Absolute (a level in the trace's unit) or Relative % (that percentage of the way from the reference level to the largest peak). With a baseline subtracted, an absolute threshold is a signed distance from the baseline: type it negative for downward peaks, or check 'Threshold is a distance in the peak's direction' to have it read toward the peaks whatever its sign. The Data Log warns when the threshold is on the wrong side of the reference to separate anything.
  • Separate peaks by (First and All; default 50% of threshold): a peak counts only if the trace falls back on both sides, before reaching a larger value, by this share of the threshold's distance from the reference level (a prominence, as in SciPy's find_peaks). Noise around the threshold then doesn't split one event into several peaks, and two events whose dip stays beyond the threshold are still told apart. Min. spacing (default 0, off) keeps only the larger of two peaks closer than that. A peak on the first or last sample of the window (an event cut by its edge) is not reported, and a sweep with no peak gets a 'no peak' row, so the rows line up with the sweeps.
  • 'Subtract baseline before threshold': set the window with 'Baseline from … to …' (default: the first 10% of the sweep). The window is shaded on the graph while the panel is open, and each sweep's mean over it is subtracted. The Data Log records the window and warns if a detected peak falls inside it. A sweep with no data in the window gives N/A amplitudes (and no peaks for an absolute threshold) rather than measuring from zero.
  • Output measurements: Peak (Amplitude when a baseline is subtracted) and Location are on by default. You can add Rise time (10–90% by default), Width at (50%, the half-width), Decay time (100–50%), Onset at (5% of the rise, timed from cursor A when detecting between cursors, otherwise from the start of the sweep), Inter-event interval (IEI, with Which peaks set to All), Area (Charge on a current) and Max rise and decay slopes. Location follows Set X = 0.
  • Rise, width, decay and onset levels are percentages of the peak's height above a reference level: the baseline if one is subtracted, otherwise the resting level, not zero. A spike from −70 mV therefore has its 50% level halfway up the spike, not halfway to 0 mV. The resting level is the median of the first or the last 5% of the window, whichever lies on the resting side of the peak. If the two ends differ by more than a quarter of the peak (the window starts or ends during a response), the Data Log suggests a baseline window. Without a baseline, the Peak column reports the raw peak value.
  • Each level is timed where the trace crosses it, interpolated between samples (as Clampfit, Stimfit and eFEL do), so the kinetics aren't rounded to whole samples. A phase only a sample or two long still reads long, so sample fast events densely.
  • Junction potential: on a voltage trace, peaks are measured on the corrected values when JP correction is on, like the Measure panel, and the Data Log says whether it was applied. Amplitudes from a baseline and all the kinetics are differences, so the correction doesn't change them.
  • Area: the trace minus the reference level, integrated by the trapezoid rule from where the trace last crosses that level before the peak to where it first returns after it. On a current (voltage clamp) it is reported as charge in pC; on any other trace as an area in the trace's unit × s (× the X unit on X-Y data). It reads N/A when the trace doesn't return to the level before the neighboring peak or the window's edge. Max rise and decay slopes are the steepest dV/dt (or dI/dt) on each side of the peak, per ms, from central differences as NumPy's gradient computes them.
  • Noise and drift (optional, off by default): on a noisy trace the most extreme sample sits on a noise peak, so the peak reads high and the kinetics read off (an EPSC at 20 times the noise SD: amplitude +8%, 10–90% rise +76%). 'Peak = mean of N points' takes the peak as the mean of N samples centered on it (Stimfit's convention). 'Smooth kinetics over N points' measures rise, width, decay, onset and slopes on a copy smoothed by an N-sample moving average; it lengthens a rise shorter than about 3 times its window. 'Local baseline for each peak' measures each peak from the mean of a short stretch just before it (Mean over … ending … before the peak), for a drifting baseline; detection still uses the sweep's baseline. The Data Log states which options were used, and with them off warns when peaks are under 30 times the baseline window's noise SD.
  • X-Y data (a spectrum, an I-V curve, a dose-response) is measured along its X axis, as Measure and the fits are: the cursors, the baseline window and Location are X values, and rise, width, decay, onset and the interval between peaks are spans in the X unit (a PSD peak's width in Hz, say). Points are taken in order of increasing X. If a sweep's X runs back and forth (a ramp up and back, a phase plot), its two branches are merged, and the Data Log warns.

Curve Fitting

The Curve Fitting tab fits each sweep in Scope over the Fit range (Full sweep or Between cursors A – B) and draws each fit as a dashed curve. Its top part, Fit type, offers:

  • Linear: a straight line (y = ax + b), with slope, intercept and R².
  • Polynomial: degree 1–6 (2 by default), fitted in a numerically stable way so high degrees and short cursor windows work reliably.
  • Exponential: Single, Double or Triple terms (A·e^(−t/τ) + C and so on). Starting values are estimated from the data, so fits converge across the usual range of time constants (0.5 ms AMPA to 200 ms NMDA/GABA-B).
  • Power: y = A · x^B.

Below it, choose E-phys preset or Formula, a Weighting and a Fit range, and click Fit:

  • E-phys preset: mono-, bi- and tri-exponential decay, mono-exponential rise, mono-exponential plus linear, alpha function, Boltzmann, Hill dose-response and dose-inhibition, Hill, Gaussian, double Gaussian, Lorentzian, and a line or parabola through zero. Each preset shows its equation.
  • Alpha function: A·(t/τ)·e^(1−t/τ) + C, a synaptic event starting at t = 0: on a sweep, the start of the fit window, so place cursor A at the event's onset; on X-Y data, X = 0, so subtract the onset from X first if needed. A is the peak amplitude and τ the time to peak. Clampfit's A·t·e^(−t/τ) + C is the same curve with A multiplied by e/τ.
  • Formula: any expression in lowercase x (X and t are read as parameters). Only pi (or π) and lowercase e (Euler's number) are constants; every other name, including tau and E, is a fitted parameter. The panel lists the fitted names as you type, and the Data Log records any constants with the fit. A formula that can't be fitted is refused with the reason: the first piece that can't be read, a formula without x, or a parameter named like a function ("a*exp" with its brackets left out).
  • Formula operators and functions: + − * / ^ (power) and % (remainder), and the pasted signs − × · and ÷; write every multiplication (2*x, not 2x); exp, exp2, expm1, log or ln (both natural log), log10, log2, log1p, sqrt, cbrt, sin, cos, tan, asin, acos, atan, sinh, cosh, tanh, asinh, acosh, atanh, erf, erfc, gamma, lgamma, abs, sign, floor, ceil, round and trunc; and with two values pow, atan2, hypot, min, max and mod (also fmod). Function names may be written in any case. ^ groups from the right (2^3^2 is 512) and binds tighter than a leading minus (−2^2 is −4); % and mod give the remainder with the sign of the divisor, as in R and Python (−7 % 3 is 2), and fmod keeps C's sign of the first value (fmod(−7, 3) is −1); angles are in radians; round takes halves away from zero (2.5 → 3).
  • How a Formula is fitted: starting values come from the data (parameters the formula is linear in, such as a and c in a*exp(-x/b) + c, are solved exactly), then Levenberg-Marquardt refines the best candidates, each parameter scaled to its own size, so a time constant of 1 ms with x in seconds fits as readily as one near 1. A sweep whose fit doesn't reach a true minimum reads "did not converge" in the Data Log, with no values. Optional starting values (name = value, separated by commas) help a hard fit or choose between two fits. A fit needs at least as many points as parameters. A long fit can be stopped with Cancel.
  • Weighting: Least squares (unweighted), the usual choice, or Relative weighting (1/Y²) for data whose scatter grows in proportion to Y. The weights come from the fitted curve's Y, re-estimated until they settle, as GraphPad Prism weights; R's nls with weights 1/y² divides by each point's own Y instead, which pulls the curve toward points that happen to fall low (Carroll & Ruppert 1988). Where the curve is at or near Y = 0 a point gets a very large weight, so use least squares when the curve comes close to or crosses zero; the Data Log warns.

A new fit is drawn as a dashed curve across the sweep. Keep (Keep fits) turns it into a regular sweep you can style and save, and Remove (Remove fits) discards it. Both can be undone with ⌘Z. A fit is drawn in the panel of the sweep it was fitted to, whatever the grouping, and stays there when kept.

Fits run in the background, so the app stays responsive: a fit that takes more than a moment shows a spinner, "Fitting sweep 2 of 8…" and a Cancel button beside Fit, and Cancel leaves the graph and the Data Log as they were. Both Fit buttons wait while a fit runs, and if the graph's data change meanwhile, nothing is drawn or logged and the panel asks you to fit again.

Reading a Fit

  • After fitting, the panel shows the equation with the fitted values (such as y = 42.3 · e^(−t / 18.5) + 2.1), and the Data Log writes it above the parameter table. You can copy it from either place. Values of 10⁶ or more are shown with an exponent, to 4 significant digits.
  • R² is 1 − (residual sum of squares) / (total sum of squares). With relative weighting both sums are weighted, and for a line or parabola through zero the total is taken about Y = 0, as R reports it for a model without an intercept. The Data Log notes both, since such an R² can't be compared with an ordinary one.
  • Below the parameters, the Data Log adds a Goodness of fit table: N, df, the sum of squares (Weighted SS under relative weighting), Sy.x (the residual SD, √(SS/df)), Adj. R², and AIC, AICc and BIC as R's AIC() and BIC() compute them, counting the residual SD as a parameter. Lower is better, and only fits of different models to the same points, with the same weighting, can be compared (a mono- against a bi-exponential, say).
  • On a sweep, consecutive samples are correlated, so AIC, AICc and BIC tend to favor extra components. Keep an extra exponential only if it also removes a trend from the residuals.
  • Time constants are always positive, and a double or triple exponential lists its components fastest first. A Boltzmann slope or a Gaussian width is reported as its magnitude. On a time axis, exponential and polynomial fits take the start of the fit window as t = 0 (the Data Log says where that is). On X-Y data, an exponential fit works wherever X starts, and its amplitude is the value at x = 0, as the equation is written. If X starts many time constants away from 0, subtract a constant from X first.
  • Units don't change the result: a dose in M or nM, or a current in A or pA, gives the same parameters, intervals and band in its own units.
  • If the best fit leaves a parameter undetermined (a Boltzmann steeper than the spacing of the X values, two merged time constants, or a formula whose parameters can't be told apart, as b and c in a*x + b + c), the Data Log says "Parameters not identifiable" and gives no intervals or band: treat those values with caution. With exactly as many points as parameters, the fit passes through every point, R² is 1 and there are no intervals.
  • Missing samples (gaps) in the fit window are skipped rather than stopping the fit.

Confidence Intervals and Bands (X-Y Data)

  • Fits to X-Y data, such as a dose-response or an I-V curve, report each parameter's confidence interval beside its value, at the confidence level set in Settings. These are the asymptotic intervals GraphPad Prism and SciPy report, and they assume the points are independent. A straight line's intervals are exact (in closed form, as R's confint(lm)), even when X sits far from 0.
  • Fits to a sweep (a decay, a rise) get no intervals or band: consecutive samples of a recording are correlated, so the formula would treat thousands of samples as independent measurements and give intervals far too narrow to mean anything. For the uncertainty of a value such as τ, fit each sweep, cell or experiment and summarize those values across them (n = cells), for example with a mean and its CI.
  • Show confidence band shades a band around each fitted curve, in the curve's color: a confidence band (where the true curve lies, the default) or a prediction band (where a new single point would fall, so always wider), at a Level (95% by default). The setting is shared by both parts of the tab and is remembered.
  • A line or polynomial gets the exact band (as R's predict.lm). A nonlinear model gets the delta-method band (as GraphPad Prism and R's investr package compute it), which is approximate and most trustworthy when the parameters are well determined.
  • The band spans the drawn curve, is exported with it to PDF, PNG and SVG, and is saved with the file. Editing the curve in a way the band can't follow (a filter, say) hides the band; Y Scale/Offset keeps it in step.

For exponential fits, tau (τ) is in the recording's own X unit, usually seconds, even when the axis shows ms. The equation takes the start of the fit window as t = 0.

AP Detection (⇧⌘J), including the Phase Plot and the Intracellular and Extracellular modes, has its own page: see AP Detection in the sidebar.

All measurements are written to the Data Log. Select any column of numbers and press ⇧⌘G to graph it immediately.

Synapse Detection

The Synapse Detection panel (Analyze tab) has two modes, chosen with a picker at the top: Evoked, for responses at known stimulus times (paired-pulse and train protocols), and Spontaneous, for miniature or spontaneous events (mEPSCs, mIPSCs, EPSPs) anywhere in the trace. Both modes show their detection settings, then Measurements to Report, then a plot-over-time section (Results Over Time for Evoked, Time-Binned Summary Graphs for Spontaneous). Spontaneous mode also has Detection range (the whole sweep, or between cursors A and B), Multi-sweep mode for several sweeps (Continuous joins them end to end, Episodic analyzes each one separately), Overlap Handling and Capture Settings (saving event waveforms). Evoked detection runs on every sweep of the selected group. While spontaneous detection runs, a progress bar shows with a Cancel button, which stops it and adds nothing. The Data Log's tables name the file they came from.

Evoked Detection

Looks for a response at known stimulus times instead of scanning the whole trace. This mode is for evoked EPSCs/EPSPs/IPSCs, paired-pulse protocols, and trains.

Evoked Event Timing

  • Uniform Train: a start time, pulse count and frequency (such as "start at 550 ms, 10 pulses, 10 Hz").
  • Manual Timings: a list of pulse times (ms), separated by commas or spaces, for irregular protocols. Use a period for decimals, since a comma separates times. Entries that aren't plain numbers (such as "10ms") are ignored, and the Data Log lists them.
  • Train and manual times are in ms as the X axis shows them, like every other time in the panel: for a file with a recorded start time, or after Set X = 0, read them off the axis. Batch Processing and the AI Assistant take pulse times the same way.
  • From TTL / Stimulus Channel: pick the group that holds the stimulus trace; each threshold crossing is a pulse onset. Pulse times are found separately in each sweep, so sweep-to-sweep jitter is captured. Pulses at least Min. pulse separation apart are all kept, including ones exactly that far apart.
  • TTL Polarity: Active-high (default) takes the trace rising above threshold as the onset; Active-low takes it falling below threshold, for a TTL that is high at rest and drops during the stimulus. If this is set wrong there is no error, but every pulse time is shifted later by the pulse width, because the trailing edge is detected.
  • A uniform train or TTL detection can be combined with an extra manual pulse time (such as a train plus one late pulse).

The expected response direction and an onset threshold (relative to baseline) apply to every pulse. The direction starts out upward for a voltage channel and downward for a current channel; change it if needed. The search for a threshold crossing starts 1 ms after each pulse, so a stimulus artifact isn't mistaken for the response, and ends at the 'Search up to (ms after pulse)' time. With 'Never search past the next pulse' checked (the default), it also stops at the next pulse. 'Threshold in baseline SDs' (on by default, at 4) reads the onset threshold as a multiple of the resting noise SD, so it follows the recording's noise rather than its unit and works the same in pA, nA or mV. The SD comes from the baseline window before the first pulse: the window before a later pulse holds the previous response's decay, which would raise the threshold past the responses of a fast train. Uncheck it to give the threshold as a level in the channel's unit instead. A paired-pulse ratio's Pulse 1 check uses the level each pulse was actually searched with, in the channel's unit. Once a response is found, its peak is searched up to the next pulse, within 'Measure for (ms after onset)' (shared with Spontaneous mode's Capture length), which also bounds its half-width and decay fit.

Stimulation type sets how long after the pulse to search: Electrical (up to 4 ms, the default), ChR2 (4 ms), Chrimson (6 ms), or Custom. The response must cross the onset threshold within the window, which takes part of its rise after it starts, so each preset is 1 ms past the usual latency: electrically evoked responses usually start within 2–3 ms, ChR2 responses about 3 ms, and Chrimson ones 4–5 ms or more. A preset only fills in a starting value, and you can edit it afterward. When pulses scored as failures cross the onset threshold shortly after the search window ends, the Data Log says how many and when, so you can lengthen the window if responses are expected that late.

A pulse with no detectable response is recorded as a failure (0 amplitude), not skipped, so the failure rate is meaningful.

Evoked Baseline & Measurement

In a train, a later response starts on the decaying tail of the ones before it, so its size depends on what it is measured from. "Measure each response" offers both answers:

  • From rest (before the first pulse), the default: amplitude, rise time, half-width and decay of every pulse are measured from one baseline taken before the first pulse. A later response then includes what remains of the earlier ones: the summed depolarization the cell actually sees, which decides whether it fires.
  • From the previous response's decay: each response's decay is fitted (with the Decay model chosen under Measurements to Report) and extrapolated under the next response, which is then measured on its own. This is the response to that pulse alone, as paired-pulse and short-term-plasticity ratios usually intend. The decay needs a few milliseconds before the next pulse to be fitted; if it cannot be, the pulses after it are measured from the level just before each pulse instead.

Baseline before each pulse (ms) sets how long a stretch before each pulse is averaged. Onsets are always found against that level just before each pulse, and 'From rest' uses the same stretch before the first pulse. It's shared with Spontaneous mode's Capture baseline. Decay time constant is fit the same way as in Spontaneous mode (see Event Measurements, below).

Evoked Output

  • Per-sweep tables: one for each measurement checked in Measurements to Report (Latency, Amplitude, Rise, Half-Width, Charge, Decay τ), with one row per sweep and one column per pulse. Latency is in ms from the pulse. A failure shows as 0 in the Amplitude table. With exactly two pulses, the Amplitude table adds each sweep's own PPR. Decay τ1 (ms, fast) and Decay τ2 (ms, slow) tables are added when at least one pulse fit as a double exponential (see Event Measurements below).
  • Per-Pulse Summary: mean amplitude, its SD and coefficient of variation (CV = SD ÷ |mean|, the starting point for 1/CV² and variance–mean analyses), and failure rate for each pulse position across sweeps (failures count as 0), and mean charge when measured (failures count, at about 0, with the fixed window).
  • Paired-Pulse Ratios: for each consecutive pulse pair, the ratio of the mean amplitudes across sweeps (mean₂ ÷ mean₁). Check 'Paired-pulse ratio: 95% confidence interval' (under Summary statistics, below Measure each response) to add Fieller's 95% interval, computed from the sweeps holding both pulses and allowing for the two pulses' covariance. It needs no random resampling and covered the true ratio 93–96% of the time in simulations with 10 to 30 sweeps, where a bootstrap covered 89–94% with 10 to 20. "unbounded" means the first pulse's mean is not clearly away from zero, so the ratio is undetermined.
  • Train Summary (3+ pulses): each pulse's mean amplitude normalized to Pulse 1 (ratio of means).
  • Steady-state summary (6+ pulses): the mean of the last third of the pulses (at least the last 3) relative to Pulse 1, showing where the train settles.
  • Short-Term Plasticity (2+ pulses): each sweep's own Pₙ ÷ P₁, then averaged across sweeps. A sweep contributes only if its Pulse 1 response is a real response (in the expected direction and past the onset threshold), since dividing by a near-zero amplitude gives a huge, meaningless ratio. The "N sweeps" column shows how many sweeps contributed.

Report the Paired-Pulse Ratios (ratio of means). A mean of per-sweep ratios, as in Short-Term Plasticity, is biased upward whenever amplitudes vary from sweep to sweep: with no plasticity and an amplitude CV of 0.3 it reads about 1.1 (Kim & Alger 2001), and the table says so. The ratio of means read within 1% in the same simulations. Short-Term Plasticity is kept for comparison with work that used it.

Measurements to Report (Evoked)

Onset Times starts unchecked; check it to add onset times to the results and Results Over Time. In Evoked mode onset is measured in ms from the stimulus. In Spontaneous mode it is measured from the sweep's start, from cursor A when analyzing between the cursors, and from the first sweep's start when sweeps are joined end to end (Continuous); the checkbox says which. Instantaneous Frequency and Inter-Event Intervals are hidden in Evoked mode, since they describe spacing between spontaneous events. In Continuous mode the first event after a join between sweeps has neither, since the sweeps weren't recorded back to back.

Results Over Time

For time-course experiments (drug application, LTP/LTD) where each sweep is a time point rather than an independent trial. Check "Plot results over time", choose the Time axis, and detection opens a graph with one stacked panel per detected pulse for each metric checked in Measurements to Report (Amplitude with 3 pulses gives 3 panels, for example). The "Short-Term Plasticity (Pₙ / P₁, per-sweep)" checkbox adds those ratios too.

  • Sweep interval (s), the default: each sweep's position in the sequence × the interval you type. A sweep where no pulse was found (TTL mode) leaves a gap rather than shifting later sweeps earlier.
  • Recorded sweep times: each sweep at the start time the file records for it, with 0 at the group's earliest sweep. Choose it when the time between sweeps matters, for example a pause while a drug washes in; keep the sweep interval when it doesn't. It is available when the file records sweep start times (see Supported File Formats → Recording Times). A sweep without a recorded start time is left out, and the Data Log names it.

The X axis label and a Data Log line say which time axis was used.

Spontaneous Detection

Scans the whole trace for events using one of three methods, chosen under Detection Method: From Threshold, From Template or Deconvolution. Samples a file leaves unrecorded (blank, such as a short gap at the end of each sweep) are read as baseline, so in Continuous mode detection carries on into the sweeps after them.

Threshold Method

  • Amplitude threshold: an event starts when the signal moves this far from baseline, and it is kept only if its measured amplitude reaches the threshold. Slow drift and the resting level are removed for detection only, so enter a size, not a level such as −70 mV. It is in the channel's unit, shown beside the field. Positive for upward events, negative for downward.
  • Derivative threshold: an event starts when the slope (in the channel's unit per ms, such as pA/ms, computed over the Regression interval) crosses a set value. Less affected by slow baseline drift.
  • Separating valley (% of peak, default 50): an event ends once the signal falls back to this fraction of its peak, so the next event can be counted even if the trace has not returned to baseline. Each new event must itself rise by at least the threshold from the lowest point since the previous one, so an event's own decaying tail, or noise on it hovering near the threshold, is not counted as further events. A second event riding on a tail takes the trough before it as its onset.
  • Event direction (for measuring the peak, rise and decay) is the sign of the threshold you set: negative for downward events.

Overlap Handling

For every method: with 'Reject overlapping events' on, events closer together than Min. event separation (default 10 ms) form a cluster. The first event of each cluster is kept if 'Accept first event in an overlapping cluster' is checked, and otherwise the whole cluster is dropped. Set the separation to 0 to keep every event. Every event is measured before the overlap rule is applied, so a kept event's measurements stop at the next detected event even when that one is rejected (measured through it, the first of two 5 ms events 8 ms apart read a decay τ of 13 ms). With an amplitude threshold or a minimum amplitude, detections smaller than it are dropped first, so noise between two events can't join them into one cluster.

Rejection is off by default. Rejected events are left out of the event count, the frequency and the inter-event intervals, which then read low: in simulated 20 Hz recordings a 10 ms separation dropped 19% of events and raised the median interval from 34 to 47 ms. Keeping them costs nothing in their measurements, since an event on another's decay is measured from that decay (see Event Measurements). When rejection leaves events out, the Data Log says how many.

Template Method

Based on the optimally scaled template of Clements & Bekkers (1997; see References). A template waveform is slid along the data; at each point it is scaled and offset to fit best, and the point is scored by the signal-to-noise ratio of that fit (scale ÷ its standard error). An event is detected where the score passes the threshold (default 4, which gives virtually no false positives; Stimfit suggests 3, after Clements & Bekkers, which finds more small events). Because the template is normalized to a peak of 1 about its own baseline, the score is the fitted event's amplitude in noise SDs, whatever the template's units or holding level. Because the template is scaled, only its shape matters, not its amplitude; Event Direction (upward or downward) says which way events go and applies to every template, from a function or a graph. With several templates (for example a fast and a slow one), each point takes the score of whichever template fits best, so events of each shape are found.

Templates come from a function (synaptic: exp(−t/τdecay) − exp(−t/τrise), where Rise τ and Decay τ are time constants; the template peaks at τrise·τdecay/(τdecay − τrise)·ln(τdecay/τrise), 4.9 ms for the default 2 and 18 ms, which the Timing Hint follows) or from the sweeps of an open graph window (From a Graph). The Clements & Bekkers score stays low at the first of two events closer together than the template, so of such a pair usually only the second is found, and that one is then measured on the first one's tail; the Threshold and Deconvolution methods separate them. Keep the template short for this reason, its baseline plus one or two decay τ: on simulated 20 Hz EPSCs with a 5 ms decay τ, a template 25 ms long (5 ms of it baseline) found 70% of events and one 10 ms long 85%, with no more false ones. A template from a graph should start with the baseline length set under Template Waveform, since its level there is taken as the template's zero. A sweep no longer than the template can't be searched, and the Data Log says so.

Deconvolution Method

Based on Pernía-Andrade et al. (2012; see References). The trace is deconvolved by the event template in the frequency domain (its spectrum divided by the template's), which turns each event into a brief peak at its onset however much it overlaps the next. The result is band-pass filtered, 0.1 to 100 Hz by default as in the paper (High-pass and Low-pass; the paper doesn't name its filter type, so these are Gaussian filters applied in the frequency domain, with no phase shift and no ringing). Its noise SD is the SD of a Gaussian fitted to its all-points histogram, and every local maximum of the result above the threshold is an event, timed at its onset, as the paper defines them. The threshold is 4 noise SDs by default (the paper uses 4 to 4.5). Two events whose peaks merge above the threshold are both found as long as each keeps its own maximum (about 3 ms apart or more at 100 Hz). The template comes from the same settings as the Template method (a function or a graph; with several templates the first is used). It is followed to the end of its decay: a template that ends while still decaying is continued as the exponential its own tail follows, since one cut off early would give a false event one template-length after every real one. Detected events are then measured on the recorded trace exactly as with the other methods (peak amplitude, 20–80% rise as in the paper, half-width, decay, charge and Fitted waveform). Overlap Handling applies if turned on.

On simulated recordings (events with 0.5 and 5 ms time constants and amplitudes around 15 pA, at 2, 10 and 20 Hz, in 2 kHz-filtered noise of 1 to 3 pA, at 20 kHz) it found 93 to 99% of events with 0 to 3 false events per minute, against 49 to 97% (and none) for the Template method, which misses events that follow closely on another. Onset times were within 0.05 ms on average (SD 0.07 to 0.18 ms). On the paper's own recipe (time constants of 0.4 and 5 ms, each event's scaled by a random factor of mean 1 and SD 0.3, 10 Hz, signal-to-noise ratio 5) it found 97 to 99% of events in white noise and 96 to 98% in mixed white and 1/f noise, with about 1% false events (the paper reports 98 to 99% and 1 to 2%). In noise already filtered below the events' frequencies, the 100 Hz low-pass costs events (about 80% found), and raised to 2 kHz it found them all, which is why the paper low-pass filters “as required”; in white noise, though, a 2 kHz low-pass finds nothing. A 1 to 200 Hz band found up to 3% more events where the noise was low, but 3 to 9 false events per minute. Set the template's rise and decay τ close to your events'.
