Minimum Amplitude

For the Derivative threshold, Template and Deconvolution methods, Minimum amplitude (default 0, off) drops every detection whose amplitude, measured from its baseline, is smaller than the value you set, sign ignored, as an amplitude threshold does; the rest are then measured again. Their own criteria are unit-free (a slope, or a signal-to-noise score), so small noise detections pass them. A few times the baseline noise SD works well: on simulated 15 pA EPSCs in 2.2 pA noise, 5 pA raised the share of detections that were real events from 50% to 95% for a −12 pA/ms derivative threshold and from 94% to 100% for deconvolution at 3.5 SD, missing none. The test uses the amplitude read from the samples, so it applies the same way with Peak Amplitude unchecked or Fitted waveform on.

Choosing a Method and Its Limits

  • Amplitude threshold: simple and fast, but its threshold is in the recording's unit, so set it from the noise (about 3 to 4 noise SDs). Events smaller than it are missed, and a threshold near the noise lets noise peaks through as small events.
  • Derivative threshold: less affected by slow drift, but the slope of fast noise is large, so on its own it detects many noise events; pair it with a Minimum amplitude.
  • Template (Clements & Bekkers): the most selective when the template's kinetics match the events, and its threshold carries between recordings. It misses the first of two events closer together than the template, and events whose shape differs from every template.
  • Deconvolution (Pernía-Andrade et al.): best at events close together and at high rates. It needs a template close to the events' kinetics (a slow template on fast events gives extra false detections), and its Low-pass sets a trade-off: lower merges events closer than about 0.3 ÷ low-pass seconds, higher lets more noise through.
  • All methods: noise lifts a small event's sampled peak and shortens its half-width (see Fitted waveform), and its decay τ reads a few percent long, since the fit stops where noise first touches the baseline (+3% for 15 pA events in 2 pA noise, under 1% at 40 pA). Gaps in the recording are read as baseline, and there are no excluded regions: to leave out a stretch such as a stimulus or a seal test, analyze between cursors A and B instead.

Event Measurements

For each detected event: peak amplitude, 20–80% (or 10–90%) rise time, half-width, decay time constant, onset time, instantaneous frequency and inter-event interval. The amplitude is always measured, since the rise, half-width and decay are read relative to it; unchecking Peak Amplitude only leaves it out of the results. Rise and half-width levels are timed at interpolated crossings, found walking back from the peak (so a threshold crossing above the 20% level still gets a rise time) but never past the previous event's peak. The half-width, the decay fit and the peak search stop at the next detected event's onset (kept or not; see Overlap Handling), and in Continuous mode at the join with the next sweep, so a closely following event does not distort them. An event that starts on the previous event's decay is measured from where that decay would have been: each event's fitted decay is subtracted before the next is measured, as in Evoked mode's 'From the previous response's decay' setting. Captured waveforms can be baseline-subtracted, and aligned at their peaks for the Template and Deconvolution methods, before averaging (Threshold events are always captured from their onsets, where they cross the threshold, so that method has no Align checkbox), in every mode (single sweep, Continuous and Episodic). In Continuous mode each event is assigned to the sweep it falls in, even when sweeps differ in length.

Rise Times are timed between 20% and 80% of the amplitude by default; the picker next to the checkbox chooses 10–90% instead.

Charge (optional, off by default) is in pC (pA·ms is 1 fC) and is measured only on a current channel (pA, nA), as recorded in voltage clamp. The area under a voltage event is not a charge, so on a voltage channel the checkbox reads 'Charge (current channels only)' and is unavailable, and Batch Processing, Protocols and the summary leave it out too.

  • Onset to return to baseline: the trace minus the event's baseline, integrated by the trapezoid rule from its onset to where it first returns to baseline after the peak (interpolated between samples), within the capture length and before the next detected event. It is N/A when the trace hasn't returned by then; the Data Log says how many.
  • Evoked mode integrates over a fixed window after each pulse by default, as Clampfit and Easy Electrophysiology measure evoked charge: from 1 ms after the pulse (clear of the stimulus artifact, as the onset search) for the Window you set (default 50 ms, which holds the charge of a fast AMPA EPSC; lengthen it for slow NMDA or GABA-A responses), ending before the next pulse if that comes first. A failure then reads about 0 and counts in the mean, and the Data Log states the window and how many pulses the next pulse cut it short for. Choose 'Onset to return to baseline' to use that rule here too; a failure then has no charge.
  • Accuracy: read from the samples, noise ends the integral where the decaying tail first touches the baseline, so charge reads low on small events (15 pA events in 2 kHz-filtered noise of 2 pA: about −14%). With Fitted waveform it is the fitted event's integral instead (within 1%). Both match NumPy's trapz.

Fitted waveform (optional, off by default): read from the samples, a small event's peak is lifted by the noise (by about one noise SD when the noise is band-limited), and level crossings move, in every program that measures single events this way. 15 pA events in 2 kHz-filtered noise of 2 pA read amplitude +15%, 20–80% rise +98% and half-width −37%. With Fitted waveform checked, amplitude, rise and half-width come instead from a least-squares fit of the event shape, exp(−t/τdecay) − exp(−t/τrise), with its onset, both time constants and its scale free and the same baseline: +1%, −5% and −2% on those events. The fitted scale is the template scale, with the template's kinetics fitted to each event. The decay τ, and which events are kept (the minimum amplitude still uses the sampled peak), are unchanged. It is slower, and an event whose fit fails or is implausible keeps its sampled values; the Data Log says how many. With it off, the Data Log notes when the median event is under 20 times the baseline noise SD.

Onset: a threshold is crossed partway up an event's rise, later for a small event than a large one, so for the Threshold method the onset is moved back to where the rise begins: the line through the 20% and 80% points of the rise, extrapolated to baseline. This makes onset times, and the intervals between events, independent of event size, and keeps the rise out of the baseline window. It is never moved later than the threshold crossing or back past the previous event's peak. The Template method's onset is already the fitted template's own. Evoked onsets, and so latencies, are extrapolated the same way, to the level just before the pulse and never before the pulse itself.

Decay time constant is fit nonlinearly (Levenberg-Marquardt) from where the event has fallen to 80% of its amplitude until the signal returns to baseline, the capture window ends or the next event begins (time still runs from the peak; from the peak itself when the next event comes too soon to reach 80%). Just after the peak the rise has not finished, and a fit starting there read 5.16 ms for a noise-free 5 ms decay (0.5 ms rise); from 80% it reads 5.01 ms. Choose the model under Decay Time Constants: Single exponential (default) suits most synaptic currents; choose Double exponential when the decay has a clear fast and a slow phase, and use the same model for every group you compare. The reported Decay τ (ms) is always one number per event: the single exponential's τ, or for a double exponential the amplitude-weighted average (A₁τ₁ + A₂τ₂)/(A₁ + A₂). With the double-exponential model, two further panels appear when at least one event fits two components: Decay τ1 (ms, fast) and Decay τ2 (ms, slow). An event too short to fit two components is fit as a single exponential and shows NaN in those panels. A fit that doesn't describe a decay (a τ over 5 times the stretch fitted, as an event on a plateau or a step gives) is left out: that event's Decay τ is NaN rather than a huge number.

Overall Summary and Cumulative Distributions

In Spontaneous mode, 'Overall summary (Data Log)' writes one summary for the recording: the number of events, the frequency (events divided by the time analyzed, every sweep's searched stretch added together), and the mean, SD (n − 1), median and CV (SD ÷ |mean|) of amplitude, charge, rise, half-width, decay τ and inter-event interval, each when checked in Measurements to Report. 'Cumulative distributions (amplitude, IEI)' opens two graphs, the cumulative distribution of event amplitude (absolute value) and of inter-event interval: each value against the fraction of events at or below it. Copy or export their data to compare recordings or conditions, for example with the two-sample Kolmogorov-Smirnov test in Statistics, the usual way to compare event populations. Both match NumPy.

Time-Binned Summary Graphs

"Plot time-binned summary graphs" groups every detected event by its time in the recording (Bin width default 30 s) and opens one panel per metric checked in Measurements to Report. Its Time axis is Sweeps joined end to end by default, all sweeps treated as one continuous timeline. When the file records when each sweep started you can choose Recorded sweep times instead: each event sits at its sweep's start plus its time within the sweep, and a bin's Mean Frequency is its events divided by the time sweeps actually covered in it, so the gaps between sweeps don't count as silence (a bin wholly in a gap stays empty). Sweeps without a recorded start time are left out, with a note in the Data Log, and the axis label says which time axis was used. A "Mean Frequency (Hz)" panel is always included: event count ÷ bin width, which is not the same as Instantaneous Frequency (1/inter-event interval, per event). The bins cover the whole analyzed recording, so a quiet stretch at the end shows as zero frequency; if the bin width doesn't divide the recording, the last bin is shorter and its frequency uses its own width. Optional error bars for each bin: SD, SEM, or the confidence interval of the mean (SEM × the t critical value, at the confidence level in Settings → Statistics); the graph title says which. This section is hidden in Episodic mode, which has no continuous timeline.

References

Clements JD & Bekkers JM (1997). Detection of spontaneous synaptic events with an optimally scaled template. Biophysical Journal, 73(1):220–229. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0006-3495(97)78062-7 (the Template Method above).

Pernía-Andrade AJ, Goswami SP, Stickler Y, Fröbe U, Schlögl A & Jonas P (2012). A deconvolution-based method with high sensitivity and temporal resolution for detection of spontaneous synaptic currents in vitro and in vivo. Biophysical Journal, 103(7):1429–1439. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.bpj.2012.08.039 (the Deconvolution Method above).

AP Detection

The AP Detection panel (Analyze tab, ⇧⌘J) has two modes, chosen with a picker at the top: Intracellular, for whole-cell or sharp-electrode voltage recordings (with a phase plot of dV/dt against V), and Extracellular, for spike detection on a continuous extracellular waveform. A raster (spike times only) opens in Extracellular, the one mode that applies to it.

Intracellular AP Detection & Phase Plot

Choose a threshold method with the Method picker. Measurements per AP: threshold voltage, latency, peak, amplitude, rise time, half-width, decay time, maximum rise and decay slopes, and AHP (depth and time). Rise and decay are measured between two levels you set, each a percentage of the AP's amplitude above its threshold: 10–90% rise and 90–10% decay by default, or 20–80% and 80–20%, for example. Because both use the same reference, the decay mirrors the rise and doesn't depend on AHP depth. The table's column headers show the percentages used. Rise, half-width and decay are timed where the trace crosses each level, interpolated between samples (as Clampfit, Stimfit and eFEL do), so they aren't rounded to whole samples.

Find: First AP (the default) measures only the first AP in each sweep; choose All APs to measure every one. Detection runs on the visible sweeps of every group whose unit is a voltage (V, mV or µV; the Data Log names any sweep skipped for its unit), over the full sweep or between cursors A and B, with junction-potential correction applied when it's on. Every analyzed sweep gets a row: First AP leaves it blank and All APs counts 0 when a sweep has no AP, sweeps are then always labeled, and the table says how many sweeps fired out of how many, so the rows line up with a current-step family. Detect APs writes the table to the Data Log and marks each threshold on the graph; choose Keep markers or Remove markers afterward. Detection runs in the background (the button reads Detecting… meanwhile), so a long recording doesn't freeze the window; if you edit the graph while it runs, the Data Log says so and no markers are added. The starting dV/dt threshold comes from Settings → Neurophysiology: 20 mV/ms, the Allen Institute IPFX's dV/dt cutoff, unless you change it there. With the Derivative method it puts the threshold within about 1.5 mV of IPFX's in simulated cells, where 50 mV/ms put it 2.5 to 5 mV higher up the upstroke.

  • Derivative threshold (default): one fixed dV/dt value (mV/ms) for every AP. The rise must also be sustained: the average slope over the crossing step and the next two samples must be at least the Sustained-slope multiplier (default 2×) times the threshold. This stops small, fast oscillations on the upstroke (such as from over-compensated capacitance neutralization) from being taken as APs. The test only qualifies an upstroke: the threshold reported is where the unbroken run of samples at or above the set dV/dt begins, the first sample whose slope reaches it (as the Allen Institute's IPFX), so it doesn't move up the AP at high sample rates. Lower the multiplier if real APs are missed; raise it if brief noise is accepted as APs.
  • Fractional derivative threshold: for each AP, @Graph finds its maximum slope (the steepest point of the whole upstroke) and walks back toward baseline to the first point where dV/dt falls to a set percentage of that maximum (Fraction of max slope, default 5%, the Allen Institute IPFX convention: on clean recordings at 100 kHz it lands within about 0.1 to 0.2 mV of IPFX's threshold, and noise moves it less than a smaller percentage, which the baseline's own noisy slope can reach early). This adapts to each spike's own upstroke speed. IPFX instead takes the percentage of the average maximum slope of all the sweep's spikes: the two agree within 0.2 mV for most spikes of a regular train, but the later spikes of a burst, which rise more slowly, get thresholds up to 7 mV apart. The maximum slope is the steepest step anywhere between threshold and peak, as in IPFX, so a noise blip at the spike's foot can't set the cutoff. The dV/dt field becomes a 'dV/dt floor' that an upstroke must clear to be considered at all; the Sustained-slope multiplier isn't used. In a fast train the walk back stops at the previous AP's trough.
  • Second derivative and Phase-slope (Sekerli) find the same APs as Derivative, then place each threshold by the shape of its upstroke. Second derivative uses the peak of d²V/dt², where the rise accelerates fastest. Phase-slope uses the peak of (d²V/dt²)/(dV/dt), the slope of the phase plot, as proposed by Sekerli et al. (2004); this is the 'kink' where the spike takes off. Derivatives are fitted over 0.2 ms at every sampling rate. The search runs back from the fastest rise while the rise keeps accelerating and dV/dt stays above 5 mV/ms. On fast spikes the second-derivative peak lies well above the dV/dt threshold. Phase-slope suits cells with a sharp onset; on a slow, noisy onset its peak is poorly defined.
  • Phase-plane curvature (Sekerli) finds the same APs as Derivative and places the threshold where the phase plot (dV/dt against V) bends upward most sharply: the peak of d²(dV/dt)/dV², Method II of Sekerli et al. (2004). It needs a third derivative, so it fits a quartic over 0.4 ms at every sampling rate. Noise moves it much less than the phase slope, and it usually lands near the dV/dt threshold. With a very sharp onset it takes the start of the search. It needs about 0.2 ms of trace before an upstroke.
  • Recordings at 15 kHz and faster: the threshold is placed on the slope of a filtered copy of the trace (a zero-phase 4-pole Bessel low-pass at 10 kHz, as the Allen Institute's IPFX uses, or at 0.4 of the sampling rate when that is lower: 8 kHz at 20 kHz), so noise at high sample rates doesn't push it up the AP and the same cell gives the same threshold at any rate. Your data is never changed: the threshold's time is interpolated between samples, its voltage read from the unfiltered trace at that time, and everything else is measured on the unfiltered trace. The marker sits on the trace at the nearest sample (within half a sample of the reported time). Which events count as APs is still decided on the unfiltered trace. At 20 kHz this took the threshold's bias with 0.5 mV of noise from +1.6 mV to +0.1 mV, and the downstroke's from −31% to −13%. Recordings slower than 15 kHz are not filtered: the filter would keep too little of the upstroke.
  • Below 15 kHz the threshold is a whole sample, as in IPFX and eFEL, so it can sit up to one sample interval early or late: in a simulated cell its SD was 1.4 mV at 10 kHz. Check 'Interpolate threshold between samples (below 15 kHz)' to place it where dV/dt (a central difference) crosses the criterion, interpolated between samples: the SD fell to 0.03 mV at 10 kHz, with the threshold about 0.5 mV more negative on average (0.1 mV at 20 kHz; with the Fractional method 1 to 2 mV at 10 kHz, where the maximum slope reads low). Off by default; Batch Processing and Protocol AP steps have the same checkbox.

Min. amplitude from threshold (default 40 mV) rejects any candidate whose peak doesn't rise at least this far above its threshold, filtering out subthreshold depolarizations and noise. It applies to every method. A rejected candidate doesn't hide a spike right after it: the search goes on from the next sample. IPFX counts any event at least 2 mV high whose peak is above −30 mV, so it also counts spikelets and the shrinking spikes of depolarization block (and, in a simulated cell in block with 0.5 mV of noise, about 50 noise events); @Graph counts neither.

A spike already rising when the window starts (a sweep that begins, or cursor A placed, partway up a spike) is skipped, as IPFX skips it: its threshold isn't in the data. A spike still rising at the window's last sample (the sweep's end, or cursor B) is counted, with its threshold and latency, but its peak and the measurements taken from it are blank, and the Data Log lists it.

The peak is the highest point before the trace returns below threshold (in a fast train whose troughs stay above threshold, before the trace, having fallen a quarter of the way back, rises again), so a noise notch near the top doesn't end the search early. Half-width and decay are measured no later than the trough after the peak: when the trace doesn't fall that far before the next spike (in a burst, say), they read blank rather than being timed on a later spike.

AHP is the threshold minus the trough, as the Allen Institute's IPFX defines it: the lowest point between the peak and the next AP's threshold. Noise on a slow falling phase can't end that search early: in simulated cells at 10 to 200 kHz, clean and noisy, every trough matches IPFX 2.1.2's. For a sweep's last AP the search runs to the end of the window, as IPFX's runs to the end of its analysis window, and the AHP is blank when the trace never comes back to within 1 mV of threshold before then (IPFX's clipped spike). Choose 'Between cursors A – B' with B at the end of the current step to end it there. When the file's protocol gives the current step's timing (an ABF epoch table), the search stops at the step's end, and the Data Log lists any AHP cut short there. Without it, the last AP's search also stops where the trace, having leveled out, starts falling steeply again, as it does when a current step ends; when that can't be seen, the step's fall to rest is read as the AHP (40 mV for a true 28 mV in a simulated cell whose step ended 10 ms after its last AP), so the Data Log lists AHPs that never recovered, or that are much deeper than the rest of their sweep, to check. AHP time is the time from the peak to that trough (IPFX's trough time). IPFX's fast trough, ADP and slow trough are not reported.

Max rise and decay slopes are IPFX's upstroke and downstroke: the steepest rise between threshold and peak and the steepest fall between peak and trough (mV/ms; the fall is negative), taken between neighboring samples as IPFX takes them and, from 15 kHz, after the same filter (10 kHz, or 0.4 of the sampling rate when lower), so from 25 kHz they match IPFX's. The filter, or slower sampling, reads a fast upstroke about 10% low (4 to 13% in simulated cells), and the fall is blank when the AHP is.

Compared with IPFX: the peak and the trough are the same samples IPFX picks. Half-width is measured at half the AP's height above threshold, interpolated between samples; IPFX measures at half the height from trough to peak, on whole samples, which reads 0.1 to 0.2 ms wider in simulated cells. Below 15 kHz IPFX places the threshold about one sample earlier (it credits each sample-to-sample slope to the earlier sample), 1 to 1.5 mV lower at 10 kHz. Latency here is the time from the sweep's start; Firing Properties gives the latency from the current step's onset, as IPFX does. In simulated regular-spiking, fast-spiking, adapting and bursting cells at 10 to 100 kHz, with up to 1 mV of noise, every spike was found and nothing else was counted.

Generate Phase Plot opens a graph of dV/dt (Y, 'Derivative') against membrane voltage (X, 'Membrane Potential') for each sweep, useful for characterizing AP shape and sweep-to-sweep variability. Each trace takes the color its sweep has in the source window. Smoothing (samples, odd number, 1 = none) reduces noise in the derivative. Sweeps: Visible / selected (the default) or All sweeps. dV/dt is a central difference between neighboring samples, so it under-reads the fastest part of the upstroke: for a fast spike (about 600 mV/ms) the maximum dV/dt reads about 40% low at 10 kHz, 17% at 20 kHz and 4% at 50 kHz, and less for slower spikes. Record at 50 kHz or faster when the maximum dV/dt matters.

Check 'Average across sweeps (binned)' to plot one average trajectory instead (the button then reads Generate Average Phase Plot). Points are grouped into voltage bins (Bin width, default 1 mV) and averaged across sweeps, with error bars in both V and dV/dt. Because each voltage is passed twice (on the fast upstroke and the slower decay), the rising and falling phases are averaged separately and plotted as two traces (orange = rising, blue = falling), keeping the loop shape. The phases are told apart by position, not by the sign of dV/dt: falling runs from each spike's peak to its trough, as AP Detection finds it (the lowest point before the next spike, or before the end), and rising is the rest, so noise on a flat stretch doesn't split into a positive and a negative branch. When the file's protocol gives the current step's timing, the last spike's falling branch stops at the step's end, and the fall to rest after it is left out of both branches.

The Error bars control (SEM by default) sets what the error bars show:

  • SD: the spread of individual sweeps around the mean in that bin.
  • SEM: the precision of the mean (SD / √n), which shrinks as more sweeps are included.
  • 95% CI: SEM × the t critical value for n − 1 degrees of freedom. Wider than SEM, especially with few sweeps. It stays at 95% whatever the confidence level in Settings.

A bin that only one sweep reaches (often at the top of the spike) has no error bar, rather than a bar of zero.

For rheobase, firing rates, adaptation and the F–I curve of a current-step family, use E-Phys Tools → Firing Properties…, which finds spikes with the settings of your last AP Detection run.

All measurements are written to the Data Log. Select any column of numbers and press ⇧⌘G to graph it immediately.

Extracellular AP Detection

Requires a continuous extracellular waveform, such as a multi-electrode-array or single-electrode recording (see Supported File Formats). Set the mode picker at the top of the panel to Extracellular.

Settings: 'Pre-filter to spike band before detecting' (on, 300–3000 Hz) removes LFP and drift before thresholding. It is a Butterworth band-pass, 4th order overall, run forward and backward (zero-phase, so it doesn't delay the spikes), with both edges at −3 dB after the two passes. Polarity is negative-going by default. Threshold is Auto (a multiple of each sweep's noise SD, median(|x|)/0.6745 of the filtered trace, default 5×) or a Manual value in the first visible sweep's unit; a channel shown in another prefix of that unit (mV beside µV) is converted, so the level and the reported amplitudes mean the same on every channel. Blank (NaN) samples are left out of the noise estimate, and a sweep with no measurable noise is skipped rather than searched with another value. Dead time (default 1.5 ms, as in WaveClus) pauses detection after each spike so one spike isn't counted twice; as in the Spike Sorter, no interval shorter than it can appear in the raster.

  • Detect Spikes → Raster: threshold-based spike detection on the visible sweeps (or between the cursors). With an automatic threshold, each sweep (usually each channel) gets its own threshold: the multiplier × that sweep's own noise level over the span searched, read from the whole span when it is 30 s or shorter and otherwise from thirty 1 s stretches spread evenly across it, so the end of a long recording counts as much as its start; the panel previews it for the first visible sweep, and the Data Log records the range used (to 4 significant digits) and the cursor window as the cursor fields show it. The multiplier must be greater than zero, and a manual threshold non-zero. The report and waveform buttons act on the last successful run of this recording, with the polarity and units it used. A progress bar shows while detection runs, with a Cancel button that stops it between sweeps. Each spike's time is its peak, located between samples, on the recording's own time axis: the time the graph shows for it when it opens, including a first sample that sits past 0 (one sample interval in an AxoGraph file, an NWB series' start time, an Igor wave's X offset). The raster and the recording therefore read the same time for a spike, and a spike found at a given sample lines up with that sample in Spike-Triggered LFP. The spike times open in a new raster window (one train per sweep), already showing its LFP / Spike Train Analysis pane.
  • Amplitude/Width Report: each spike's amplitude (from its peak to the opposite peak that follows), trough-to-peak width and half-width (the time spent beyond half the peak, measured from zero), plotted as a scatter (amplitude vs. width), with each train's mean and SD of all three in the Data Log (a wide spread can mean a train holds more than one unit). Peaks are located between samples and half-width crossings interpolated, so widths aren't rounded to the sample interval (33 µs at 30 kHz, 100 µs at 10 kHz). Features are measured within ±1.5 ms of the spike, so a following peak up to 1.5 ms after the trough is found; for a spike closer than that to a sweep's start or end the span is cut short, and the Data Log counts those spikes (their width and rebound can read low). Half-width assumes a zero-centered trace, so keep the pre-filter on. A spike band reshapes the waveform (in any program that measures there): with 300–3000 Hz a wide unit's 1.10 ms trough-to-peak read 4% short and a narrow unit's half-width 48% long. Check 'Measure widths on a wide-band copy' to measure amplitudes and widths on the unfiltered trace high-passed at the frequency you set (default 100 Hz) instead: −1% and +1% on those units. Spike times still come from detection. The wide-band copy is noisier, so widths spread more and amplitudes read higher (peak-to-peak picks up more noise); the report's title says which trace was used.
  • Show Spike Waveforms…: overlays a 3 ms snippet (±1.5 ms) around each detected spike, to check spike shape and consistency.

These tools need the continuous waveform. LFP / Spike Train Analysis works from spike times alone, so it also runs on spike-sorted files (such as .nev and .plx) that have no waveform.

References

Sekerli M, Del Negro CA, Lee RH & Butera RJ (2004). Estimating action potential thresholds from neuronal time-series: new metrics and evaluation of methodologies. IEEE Transactions on Biomedical Engineering, 51(9):1665–1672. The phase-slope threshold.

LFP / Spike Train Analysis

The LFP / Spike Train Analysis panel (Analyze tab) has tools for continuous field-potential data (Spectrogram, Cross-Correlation / Coherence, Band Power Over Time, Hilbert Envelope and Phase, Phase–Amplitude Coupling), for spikes against the LFP (Spike-Triggered LFP and Phase Locking) and for spike trains (PSTH, ISI / Burst Detection, Fano Factor). Spike trains come from a raster made by Detect Spikes → Raster in AP Detection (Extracellular) or by the Spike Sorter, or from a file that stores spike times, such as Blackrock/Ripple .nev, Plexon .plx, an NWB file's units or TDT snippets (see Supported File Formats).

  • PSTH (Firing-Rate Histogram): firing rate over time from a set of spike trains. Output: Per train (one trace each) or Pooled average (the default, one population trace); click Compute PSTH.
  • Bin width (PSTH; ms, default 10): sets how finely time is divided. Smaller bins show faster rate changes but are noisier, and larger bins give a smoother trace.
  • Suggest (PSTH): fills in a bin width chosen from this data's own spike statistics (Shimazaki & Shinomoto 2007; see References) rather than a fixed default. It considers widths that divide the time window into whole bins, so the PSTH has no partial last bin. When no width shows more bin-to-bin variation in the counts than chance (Poisson) would give, the spikes are too few to resolve any change in rate: Suggest says the data are insufficient and leaves the width alone. When the best width is the coarsest it tries (a tenth of the window), it is filled in with a note that the best may be coarser still.
  • Time window (PSTH): by default, the span the trains were recorded over when that is known (a raster from Detect Spikes → Raster or the Spike Sorter), so a silent stretch at the end still counts as zero firing. Turn on 'Custom time window' to set the start and end (s) yourself; Suggest then uses that window too. Spikes outside the window are left out. If the bin width doesn't divide the window, the last bin is shorter and its rate uses its own width.
  • Spike-sorted files (PSTH): these store only spike times, so the span isn't known, and every spike-train tool follows one rule: each train is taken as recorded from its first to its last spike. The PSTH then runs from the earliest first spike to the latest last one, and each train counts only in the bins wholly between its own first and last spikes.
  • Rates (PSTH): each rate is the spike count divided by the time actually recorded in that bin, summed over the trains when pooled. So when sweeps have different lengths, a late bin is averaged over only the sweeps that reach it, instead of counting the shorter ones as silent. A bin that no train recorded (for example past the end of every sweep in a custom window) has no rate and shows as a gap. A spike exactly on a bin edge, judged in whole samples, goes in the bin that starts there, as numpy.histogram does.
  • Align to stimulus events (PSTH): a peri-stimulus time histogram (Gerstein & Kiang 1960). Each sweep's spikes are re-timed from its stimulus, every sweep (or, with 'First event in each sweep only' off, every event) is one trial, and each bin shows the mean firing rate across trials with a band of ± SEM (SD with n − 1, divided by √n); 'Also show ± SD' adds the SD.
  • Events (Align to stimulus events) can come from a TTL / stimulus channel of the recording (open it; each onset is where the channel crosses the threshold, found the same way as evoked synaptic detection), from the file's stimulus command (ABF protocols: each time the command leaves its holding level, such as a step or each pulse of a train), or from times you type (seconds from each sweep's start, as the raster's time axis shows them, the same in every sweep). Channel and command events are timed on the recording's own time axis, as the raster's spikes are, and stimulus sweeps pair with the raster's trials in order.
  • Window and bins (Align to stimulus events): Before and After (s; defaults 0.5 and 1) set the window. Bins are anchored at the event, so one bin edge sits at 0 and no bin mixes spikes from before and after it, and the window is widened to whole bins. A trial counts in a bin only if its sweep recorded the whole bin, so an event near the end of a sweep doesn't read as silence after it; the Data Log lists how many trials each bin used. For a spike-sorted file, each train counts as recorded from its first to its last spike, so an event past the end of the data is not a silent trial.
  • Smoothing SD (Align to stimulus events; ms): when above 0, smooths each trial's binned rate with a Gaussian of that SD (cut off at 4 SD) before averaging (Nawrot et al. 1999), so the SEM describes the smoothed curve. Each trial is binned beyond the window by the kernel's reach first, so the first and last bins are smoothed with the recorded data next to them. At recording gaps and ends the kernel is rescaled over the bins that have data (normalized convolution) instead of treating missing time as zero.
  • Curves (Align to stimulus events): All trains (one population curve), Per unit (one curve per sorted unit over its own trials, for a Spike Sorter raster whose trains are named '<unit>: <sweep>'), or Per train (the events within each train, such as many stimuli in one long recording).
  • Results (Align to stimulus events): the result window shows each curve's mean and its SEM band as lines, and a second window shows the aligned raster (one row per trial, time from the event). The Data Log gets each curve's pre-event rate (spikes in the Before window ÷ its length) and post-event rate (the After window), both mean ± SEM across trials, the peak, and every bin's mean, SEM and trial count. Values match numpy and the Elephant toolkit's time_histogram.
  • Stimulus times can also come from an event raster: a file's digital events (Blackrock), events and strobed words (Plexon), epocs (TDT), TTL lines and messages (Open Ephys, Neuralynx) or stimulation events (Intan RHS). Choose Stimulus times from → Event raster, then the raster (the one read from the spike raster's own file comes first) and the train. Every event is a trial. Min. interval leaves out an event that comes less than that long after the one before it, so a burst of TTL pulses gives one trial at its first pulse. 'Only events from … to …' uses one stretch of the experiment. The two rasters must share a clock: the same file, or files whose recordings started at the same moment. Otherwise @Graph says why it can't use them. A trial whose window the train didn't record at all (past its ends or in a gap) is left out, and the Data Log says how many.
  • Baseline z-score and bins that differ from baseline (Align to stimulus events, off by default): set the baseline (seconds from the event; by default the whole Before window) and α. A new window shows each bin's z-score, (bin's mean rate − the baseline bins' mean) ÷ the baseline bins' SD, on the curve as plotted, with the marked bins as points. The PSTH window marks them on the mean too, and the Data Log adds each bin's z and adjusted p and lists the periods that differ, above or below baseline.
  • The baseline test (Align to stimulus events): the z-score measures size only; the marking is the test. Each bin outside the baseline is compared with the baseline's mean rate, and its p-value comes from shuffling each trial's bins within that trial 1,000 times and taking the largest difference over all the bins tested (a max-statistic permutation test, Westfall–Young step-down; seeded from the data, so the same data give the same result). That holds the family-wise error rate at α: the chance of marking any bin when the stimulus has no effect. It uses unsmoothed rates and assumes a trial's bins are interchangeable without a stimulus (a steady rate across the window).
  • How well the baseline test works: each trial keeps its own spikes, so bursts and trial-to-trial changes in rate don't cause false marks. On simulated trains with no response it marked a bin in 4.5 to 5.7% of 1,000 runs at α = 0.05 (Poisson at 5 and 20 Hz, rates varying across trials, bursty), where a Poisson count test with Holm's correction marked 46% of the bursty ones. A response of 40 Hz for 50 ms over 20 Hz firing was found in all but 1 of 300 runs with 30 trials, and in 72% with 10 trials.
  • ISI / Burst Detection: an inter-spike-interval histogram plus a per-train burst summary. A burst is a run of spikes, at least as many as Min. spikes per burst (default 3), in which every interval is at or below Max intra-burst ISI (default 10 ms). Intervals are judged in whole samples, so one equal to the limit counts, wherever the train sits in time; an interval on a histogram bin edge likewise goes in the bin that starts there.
  • Max Interval options (ISI / Burst Detection; all 0 by default, the plain rule above) turn it into NeuroExplorer's full Max Interval method. 'Max ISI within a burst' keeps a burst going, once started, on intervals up to a longer limit. Bursts closer than 'Merge bursts closer than' (last spike to next first spike) are then joined, with any spikes between them, and last, bursts shorter than 'Min. burst duration' or with too few spikes are dropped. On simulated bursty trains the bursts matched those of Eglen's sjemea mi.find.bursts, the implementation Cotterill et al. 2016 compared, exactly.
  • Data Log table (ISI / Burst Detection): one row per train and a Pooled row, with spikes; span (the time the train was recorded over, or its first to its last spike for a spike-sorted file); bursts and burst rate (bursts ÷ span); spikes in bursts and their % of all spikes; mean spikes per burst; mean burst duration; mean intra-burst rate (each burst's (spikes − 1)/duration, averaged over bursts); mean interburst interval (from the last spike of one burst to the first of the next, as in Cotterill et al. 2016); and three irregularity indices.
  • Irregularity indices (ISI / Burst Detection): ISI CV, ISI CV2 (Holt et al. 1996) and LV (local variation, Shinomoto et al. 2003). All three are ≈1 for random (Poisson) firing, <1 for more regular firing and >1 for burstier firing. CV2 and LV compare neighboring intervals, so slow changes in firing rate affect them much less than CV; LV was designed to separate regular, random and bursty firing patterns across cortical neurons. CV2 and LV match the Elephant toolkit.
  • CV from few intervals (ISI / Burst Detection): the CV reads low from few intervals (about 0.87 instead of 1 for a Poisson train with 5), and the Data Log says so for a train with fewer than 20; CV2 and LV have no such bias. Check 'Bias-corrected ISI CV (jackknife)' to add a column with the jackknife-corrected CV, n·CV − (n − 1)·the mean CV leaving out one interval at a time. For Poisson and gamma trains with 5 intervals it read within 0.3–8% of the true CV, against 10–20% low for the plain CV, but with a wider spread.
  • Pooled row (ISI / Burst Detection): sums spikes, bursts and spans (so its burst rate is all bursts over all recorded time), averages over every burst (not over the trains' averages), and takes CV2 and LV from every pair of neighboring intervals within a train. It has no CV, because intervals pooled from trains firing at different rates look irregular even when each train is regular.
  • Method (ISI / Burst Detection): Max Interval (NeuroExplorer) or Poisson surprise (Legéndy & Salcman 1985, as Cotterill et al.'s burstanalysis code, with an indexing slip in that code's look-ahead step corrected). Poisson surprise starts a burst at three spikes whose two intervals are shorter than half the train's mean interval, adds later spikes while that makes it more surprising and drops early ones while that does. Surprise is −ln P, the chance a random train at the same mean rate fires that many spikes that quickly; bursts below Min. surprise (default 5, P < 0.7%) are left out. The Data Log lists every burst. Mark bursts on a copy of the raster opens the raster with a bar under each burst.
  • Poisson chance reference (ISI / Burst Detection, on by default): adds two columns beside % spikes in bursts, the % of spikes in bursts and the burst rate this rule gives on average for a Poisson (random) train with the same number of spikes and mean interval. Random firing makes bursts too: at 20 Hz with the default 10 ms and 3 spikes, 8.5% of the spikes of a 100-spike Poisson train are in bursts (8.7% for a long one). It is exact, worked out from the chance that an interval is at or below the maximum, 1 − exp(−maximum ÷ mean interval), and the lengths of the runs of such intervals (no simulation); it matched a million simulated trains to within their sampling error. The rate is the train's own (1 ÷ mean interval), so with few spikes the reference reads a little high (8.9% for 10 spikes at 20 Hz, where Poisson trains average 7.1%). Read the observed % against it: near it is what chance gives at that rate. It covers the plain rule only, so with Max Interval options set its columns are left empty.
  • Fano Factor: the variance of spike counts divided by their mean (variance with n − 1): 1 for a Poisson train, below 1 for more regular firing, above 1 for more variable or bursty firing. Values match numpy and scipy.
  • Consecutive windows (Fano Factor): one value per train, from back-to-back windows of the Counting window (default 100 ms) over the span it was recorded. For a spike-sorted file that span is its first to its last spike, and those two spikes are left out because they are in the span by construction; counted, they would raise F.
  • Across trials (Fano Factor): one value per unit (all trains as one unit for a raster without Spike Sorter names), from one window per trial at the same time: from Window start on each sweep's time axis, from each event you type, or, with 'Events from an event raster', from the events of an event-raster train (as above). A trial counts only if its sweep recorded the whole window. Trials of: Per train (each train its own unit), Per unit, or All trains.
  • Data Log (Fano Factor): F with its confidence interval (at the confidence level in Settings) and the p-value of F = 1, from the index-of-dispersion test ((n − 1)·F against χ² with n − 1 degrees of freedom; Eden & Kramer 2010). The interval inverts the same χ² and is exact for Poisson counts. In simulations of Poisson, gamma and bursty trains with 10 to 100 counts it covered the true value in 92 to 97% of runs at 95% (85% for short windows of very bursty trains), where bootstrap intervals (percentile or BCa) covered 61 to 90% with 10 counts.
  • Window length (Fano Factor): F tends to 1 for windows much shorter than the interval between spikes and, for long windows, to the squared CV of the intervals (0.5 for a gamma train of shape 2), while slow changes in rate raise it. Compare Fano factors at one window length, and use 'Also plot Fano factor against window length' (25 lengths on a log axis, with each CI and the Poisson line) to see how it changes.
  • Pool all trains into one ISI histogram: off by default (one histogram per train); on, combines all trains into one population histogram.
  • Log-scale ISI bins: off by default (about 100 equal-width bins). On, uses ten bins per decade (0.1 log10 units, the logISI histogram of Pasquale et al. 2010) on the same edges every time, so histograms from different runs compare bin for bin, on a log axis, so short intervals within bursts and long gaps between bursts, which can differ by several orders of magnitude, are both clearly visible. Bin heights are counts.

PSTH and ISI/Burst Detection use only spike times, so they work the same on detected spikes and on spike-sorted files. Amplitude/width and waveform-shape analysis need the waveform and are under AP Detection (Extracellular).

Recordings with gaps: when a recording was paused (Plexon, TDT) or an NWB unit lists several observation intervals, every spike-train analysis uses only the time observed. Rates are spikes ÷ observed time, a PSTH bin partly in a gap is divided by the part observed and one wholly in a gap is left blank, and no interval, burst or counting window spans a gap. The burst table then shows the time observed and spanned.

For a stimulus-aligned PSTH from a Spike Sorter raster, keep the recording window open: its TTL channel or stimulus command supplies the event times, and Per unit keeps each unit's trials together.

Spectrogram and Cross-Correlation / Coherence

The Spectrogram and Cross-Correlation / Coherence sections (Signal cross-correlation, Coherence, and Spike-train cross-correlogram) work on this window's data. The same tools are in the E-Phys Tools menu, working on the front graph window. See E-Phys Tools → Spectrogram / Cross-Correlation / Coherence for what each one computes.

Band Power, Envelope and Phase, and Coupling

Four sections for rhythms in field potentials. They work on this window's data (the spike–LFP section pairs it with a raster or an LFP graph), and the same controls are in E-Phys Tools → LFP Oscillations… for the front graph, with the same results. Bands have presets (delta 1–4 Hz, theta 4–8 Hz, rodent hippocampal theta 6–10 Hz, alpha, beta, low gamma, gamma 30–80 Hz, high gamma 80–150 Hz), and the edges stay editable. Every band-pass is the Filter panel's zero-phase Butterworth (4th order, both edges −3 dB after the forward and backward passes). Phase follows the cosine convention: 0° at each peak of the band-passed signal, ±180° at each trough.

  • Band Power Over Time: a band's power as a time series in a new window, on the graph's own time axis (Set X = 0, the cursors and the X zoom carry over). Choose either definition. Spectrogram: the power spectral density of each short Hann window (mean removed, scaled as the Spectrogram and SciPy's spectrogram), integrated over the band with each frequency bin counting by the share of its width inside the band (summing whole bins made 4–8 Hz with 1 Hz bins 5 Hz wide), which is the band's variance in that window, placed at the window's center. Band-pass + Hilbert: half the squared amplitude envelope of the band-passed signal, at every sample, whose mean over time is the band's variance. Both estimate the same quantity (a steady sinusoid of amplitude A reads A²/2 by either); the Hilbert estimate follows changes sample by sample, the spectrogram's averages over each window. Window: Auto picks the shortest power of 2 whose bins are at most a quarter of the band's width apart (for 4–8 Hz at 1 kHz, 1,024 samples, 1 s). A window longer than the sweep is cut to fit, and the Data Log says so. A window with a missing sample has no value.
  • Relative power divides by the power over a total range (default 1–100 Hz), found the same way, so a change in overall signal size doesn't read as a change in the band. Smoothing is a centered moving average (in seconds) that shrinks at the ends; for relative power the band and the total are each averaged first and then divided (a ratio of mean powers). The window is a whole, odd number of points (one per window step with the Spectrogram), so a short smoothing can round to none; the Data Log gives the window actually used. Relative power by Band-pass + Hilbert needs smoothing of at least one cycle of the band's low edge: unsmoothed it divides two instantaneous envelopes, which is not a share of the power and can exceed 1 by far (11% of samples on pink noise). dB (10·log10) comes last. With All sweeps, each sweep becomes a sweep of the result, grouped by channel; with the Spectrogram, sweeps at another sample rate are left out, and the message says so.
  • Hilbert Envelope and Phase: new channels in a new window on the graph's time base: the band-passed signal, its amplitude envelope, its phase (degrees) and, if you like, its instantaneous frequency (the rate of change of the unwrapped phase ÷ 2π, by central differences). The analytic signal is SciPy's hilbert (by FFT), with each end first extended by odd reflection for 10 cycles of the band's low edge, the same extension the zero-phase filter uses, which keeps the ends from spreading error into the rest of the trace (7× less than no extension on test data). Blank samples stay blank, and each unbroken stretch is analyzed on its own. Very long recordings are transformed in overlapping blocks, which agree with a single transform to about 0.01% of the signal's RMS.
  • Within a cycle or two of an end or a gap, the filter and the transform both have edge effects, so leave those stretches out of measurements. Phase and instantaneous frequency describe one rhythm only for a narrow band; for a band wider than an octave the Data Log says so. Instantaneous frequency is meaningful only where the envelope is well above zero: where it dips, the phase is poorly defined and the frequency can swing widely, even below 0.
  • Phase–Amplitude Coupling: the modulation index (MI) of Tort et al. (2010; see References), for a phase band (default 4–8 Hz) and an amplitude band (default 30–80 Hz); click Compute Coupling. The phase band's phase is divided into 18 bins of 20°, the amplitude band's mean envelope is found in each, and MI measures how far that distribution is from flat: (log 18 − its entropy) ÷ log 18, from 0 (no coupling) toward 1. It matches tensorpac's implementation to rounding.
  • Data and bands (Phase–Amplitude Coupling): data within two cycles of the phase band's low edge of an end or gap are left out. The amplitude band must lie above the phase band (an overlapping band picks up the phase rhythm's own harmonics, which read as coupling), as the comodulogram's empty cells already enforce. A flat phase signal, whose phase doesn't move through every bin, gets a message saying so.
  • All sweeps (Phase–Amplitude Coupling): with All sweeps (pooled) of a channel, every sweep of that channel goes into one index. On a file with several channels the Sweep menu offers one such choice per channel, never all channels together: pooling another electrode, a current or stimulus channel, or sweeps in another unit would mix unrelated signals into one index (in a test, a clearly coupled LFP's coupling vanished when a second, uncoupled channel was pooled in). Band Power and Hilbert's All sweeps likewise run on one channel's sweeps.
  • Results (Phase–Amplitude Coupling): the phase–amplitude plot opens in a new window (the normalized mean amplitude per phase bin over two cycles, with the flat 1/18 line for no coupling). The Data Log gets MI, the phase bin of largest amplitude (its center, so coupling at 0° reads ±10°), the distribution's circular mean phase (which doesn't step by bins) and the test below, and warns when the amplitude band is narrower than twice the phase band's top frequency.
  • Measure (Phase–Amplitude Coupling): Tort MI or Canolty MVL. MVL is the length of the mean of A·e^(iφ), in the signal's unit; its angle is the preferred phase (0° = peak of the phase band). It is also reported as Canolty's z-score against the time-shift surrogates and as Özkurt & Schnitzler's direct PAC (0–1). Use the p-value for significance: surrogate MVLs are skewed, so z overstates it slightly. Both measures use the same filters, edge rule and surrogates, and the comodulogram can map either.
  • Significance: each surrogate (200 by default) circularly shifts the amplitude envelope against the phase by a random lag between 10% and 90% of the data's length, which keeps each signal's own rhythm and breaks only their timing (a time-shift surrogate, as in Canolty et al. 2006; Aru et al. 2015 recommend surrogates that keep each signal's structure), and recomputes MI. The Data Log gives the surrogates' mean, SD and 95th percentile, z = (MI − mean) ÷ SD, and p = (1 + surrogates at or above MI) ÷ (1 + surrogates), so 200 surrogates can show p down to 0.005. The lags come from a fixed seed, so a run repeats exactly. On uncoupled test signals of 10 s or more, p fell below 0.05 in about 5% of runs, as it should; with each sweep's own lag, pooled sweeps are tested the same way. This p is right for a band pair you chose before looking at the data. For a pair picked from the comodulogram it is uncorrected (see below). A strictly periodic phase signal (a pure sine) can't be tested this way: shifting it lines it up with itself again, so the surrogates keep the coupling.
  • Short records: the test needs enough cycles of the phase rhythm. When the data kept (all pooled sweeps together, after the edges are left out) hold fewer than 30 cycles of the phase band's low edge (7.5 s for 4–8 Hz, plus 0.5 s at each end), the Data Log and the panel warn. On uncoupled noise, p ≤ 0.05 came up in 6.6–10% of runs with 2 to 16 cycles and p ≤ 0.01 in 2.5–7% with 2 to 12, so read p as approximate there; 20 cycles and up stayed near 5%. Pooling sweeps helps, since each sweep gets its own shift. A single record too short to hold one different shift per surrogate also gets a warning.
  • Comodulogram: MI for every pair of phase frequency (default 2–14 Hz, bands 2 Hz wide) and amplitude frequency (default 20–200 Hz), as a heatmap. Amplitude bands are widened to at least twice the top edge of the highest phase band (15 Hz with the defaults, so 30 Hz): coupling puts the fast rhythm's power in sidebands at ± the phase frequency, which a narrower band cuts off (Aru et al. 2015). A cell whose amplitude band reaches down to its phase band is left empty.
  • Whole-map test (Whole-map test surrogates, 200 by default; 0 skips it): picking the brightest of hundreds of cells and testing it alone finds 'coupling' in pure noise most of the time (about 3 runs in 4 with the default 481 cells), so the map is tested as a whole. Each surrogate shifts the amplitude by one random time, the same in every cell, recomputes the whole map and gives its largest MI anywhere on the map. A cell is significant when its MI exceeds that maximum in all but 5% of the surrogates (the max-statistic test, as in Maris & Oostenveld 2007 and tensorpac's 'maxstat'), so the chance of even one false cell anywhere on the map is 5%.
  • Whole-map results: the significant cells open as a second heatmap (other cells blank), and the Data Log lists them with MI and the whole-map p, plus the brightest cell's uncorrected p (that cell tested alone) and whole-map p. Use the whole-map p for any pair you pick from the map.
  • Whole-map performance: on uncoupled pink noise (20 s, the default grid, 200 surrogates) some cell came up significant in 6% of 200 runs (2 of 40 runs at 60 s), against 74% for the brightest cell tested alone. Coupling to a 6 Hz rhythm at modulation depth 0.6 was found at its cell in every run; at depth 0.3 in every 60 s run, but in only 17% of 20 s runs (that one pair, chosen in advance and tested alone, came out in 92%). Testing the whole map costs power, so test a pair you chose beforehand on its own.
  • Why the whole-map test uses MI: the maximum is taken over MI itself, not over each cell's z-score. One cell's time-shift surrogates slightly understate how far its MI can stray by chance (the same effect as the short-record warning, larger for slow phase bands), and the largest of hundreds of z-scores lands in exactly that tail (on 20 s of noise, a z-based maximum flagged a cell in about 30% of runs). The cost is that cells whose MI is naturally small, such as fast amplitude bands, need stronger coupling to pass.
  • Whole-map computing time grows with surrogates × cells × data length: on a 2-core test machine, 20 s at 1 kHz with the default grid and 200 surrogates took about 2.5 s, and 60 s about 5 s.
  • Spike-Triggered LFP and Phase Locking: pairs a spike-train raster (from Detect Spikes → Raster, the Spike Sorter or a spike-sorted file) with a continuous LFP graph. Start from either one and pick the other from the open windows. Spike times are lined up with the LFP on the recording clock when both carry one, as Cross-Correlation lines up two graphs, and otherwise from their sweeps' starts, each on its own time axis (the result says so). A train and an LFP sweep recorded at different times are refused, with a button to compare them from each sweep's start anyway; that result is labeled 'not simultaneous: trial-relative comparison (from each sweep's start)'. Either way a raster detected from this recording lines up with it exactly, to the sample. 'Each train with the LFP sweep of the same number' pools trials.
  • The spike-triggered average takes the LFP (band-passed first with 'Band-pass the LFP before averaging', default 1–100 Hz) from −window to +window (Window ±, default 250 ms) around each spike's nearest sample and opens the mean with mean ± SEM (the standard error across spikes) in a new window. A spike whose window runs past an end or into a gap is left out, and the Data Log gives the count.
  • Phase locking: each spike's phase in the phase band, interpolated on the unit circle between the samples either side of it (as elephant's spike_triggered_phase), leaving out spikes within two cycles of the band's low edge of an end or gap. It is summarized by the pairwise phase consistency, PPC (Vinck et al. 2010): the average cosine of the phase difference over all pairs of spikes, about 0 without locking and, unlike the resultant length R, not inflated when there are few spikes. For von Mises-locked spikes PPC estimates R². The Data Log also gives R, the mean phase and the Rayleigh test of uniformity (Zar's approximation from 10 spikes, as in CircStat, Berens 2009; it can't go below a floor at fewer, 0.135 for 2 spikes, so below 10 spikes p is exact for 2 and from 200,000 simulated sets of phases, with a fixed seed, for 3 to 9), and a histogram of spike phases (18 bins, with the flat level for no locking) opens in a new window. With 'Each train with the LFP sweep of the same number' on, the Data Log also gives PPC across trials (PPC₁, Vinck et al. 2012), the average over pairs of spikes from different trials only: spikes of one burst share a phase because they come together, which raises PPC but not PPC₁, so prefer PPC₁ for a bursty unit. It matched a brute-force average over all such pairs.

Read coupling with care. Sharp or non-sinusoidal waves (sawtooth-shaped theta, spikes, artifacts) give a significant MI without any interaction between two rhythms, and so can a too-narrow amplitude band (Aru et al. 2015). Look at the raw traces and the phase–amplitude plot before drawing conclusions. Likewise, spikes of one burst land close together in phase and can raise PPC; compare units or trials rather than reading one number alone, or use PPC across trials. A strictly periodic test signal (a pure sinusoid whose amplitude another pure sinusoid modulates) keeps its coupling under any time shift, so the surrogate test can't reject it however strong the coupling (p near 0.5 on such a signal); recordings, and test signals with some phase jitter, don't have this problem, and the MI itself is still right (it matched tensorpac's Tort MI on the same phase and amplitude).

Pouzat C, Mazor O & Laurent G (2002). Using noise signature to optimize spike-sorting and to assess neuronal classification quality. Journal of Neuroscience Methods, 122(1):43–57. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0165-0270(02)00276-5. The Spike Sorter's noise whitening.

Prerau MJ, Brown RE, Bianchi MT, Ellenbogen JM & Purdon PL (2017). Sleep neurophysiological dynamics through the lens of multitaper spectral analysis. Physiology, 32(1):60–92. Multitaper spectrogram settings.

Quiroga RQ, Nadasdy Z & Ben-Shaul Y (2004). Unsupervised spike detection and sorting with wavelets and superparamagnetic clustering. Neural Computation, 16(8):1661–1687. https://doi.org/10.1162/089976604774201631. The noise level median(|x|)/0.6745 used for automatic thresholds.

Schmitzer-Torbert N, Jackson J, Henze D, Harris K & Redish AD (2005). Quantitative measures of cluster quality for use in extracellular recordings. Neuroscience, 131(1):1–11. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuroscience.2004.09.066. Isolation distance and L-ratio.

Shimazaki H & Shinomoto S (2007). A method for selecting the bin size of a time histogram. Neural Computation, 19(6):1503–1527. https://doi.org/10.1162/neco.2007.19.6.1503 (the PSTH bin width Suggest button above).

Shinomoto S, Shima K & Tanji J (2003). Differences in spiking patterns among cortical neurons. Neural Computation, 15(12):2823–2842. https://doi.org/10.1162/089976603322518759. The burst summary's LV.

Smith MA & Kohn A (2008). Spatial and temporal scales of neuronal correlation in primary visual cortex. Journal of Neuroscience, 28(48):12591–12603. https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.2929-08.2008. The jitter-corrected spike CCG.

Suner S, Fellows MR, Vargas-Irwin C, Nakata GK & Donoghue JP (2005). Reliability of signals from a chronically implanted, silicon-based electrode array in non-human primate primary motor cortex. IEEE Transactions on Neural Systems and Rehabilitation Engineering, 13(4):524–541. https://doi.org/10.1109/TNSRE.2005.857687. The Spike Sorter's SNR (p-p ÷ 2σ).

Thomson DJ (1982). Spectrum estimation and harmonic analysis. Proceedings of the IEEE, 70(9):1055–1096. The multitaper method.

Tort ABL, Komorowski R, Eichenbaum H & Kopell N (2010). Measuring phase-amplitude coupling between neuronal oscillations of different frequencies. Journal of Neurophysiology, 104(2):1195–1210. https://doi.org/10.1152/jn.00106.2010. The modulation index and the phase–amplitude plot.

Vinck M, Battaglia FP, Womelsdorf T & Pennartz C (2012). Improved measures of phase-coupling between spikes and the local field potential. Journal of Computational Neuroscience, 33(1):53–75. https://doi.org/10.1007/s10827-011-0374-4. PPC across trials (PPC₁).

Vinck M, van Wingerden M, Womelsdorf T, Fries P & Pennartz CMA (2010). The pairwise phase consistency: a bias-free measure of rhythmic neuronal synchronization. NeuroImage, 51(1):112–122. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuroimage.2010.01.073. Spike–LFP phase locking.

Westfall PH & Young SS (1993). Resampling-Based Multiple Testing: Examples and Methods for p-Value Adjustment. Wiley. The step-down max-statistic test that marks PSTH bins that differ from baseline.

Zar JH (1999). Biostatistical Analysis, 4th ed. Prentice Hall. Chapter 27: the Rayleigh test and its p-value.

E-Phys Tools

The E-Phys Tools menu (between Graph Utilities and Lab Tools) holds specialized analyses that run on the front graph window straight from the menu, without opening the inspector. Each needs a particular kind of recording, described below. AP Detection, Synapse Detection and LFP / Spike Train Analysis are inspector tabs with their own pages in this guide. Spectrogram, Cross-Correlation / Coherence and LFP Oscillations are available both here (acting on the front graph) and as sections of the LFP / Spike Train Analysis tab; both routes give the same results. The tools that open their own window (Spectrogram, Cross-Correlation / Coherence, LFP Oscillations, Spike Sorter, Slow AHP Analysis) keep one window per graph: choosing the command again brings that graph's window forward, even when another graph has the same name.

Every command except Cross-Correlation / Coherence and LFP Oscillations needs sampled recordings and says so for a spike-train raster (for a raster, LFP Oscillations shows only its spike–LFP section). Times in the Data Log (step start and end, sag peak time) are as the X axis shows them, after any Set X = 0 shift. A command still running ignores a second request (the Mac beeps). You can keep working on the graph while one runs: it measures the data as they were when you chose it. When a family has a short (aborted) sweep, the sweeps shorter than most are left out when finding the step, so the step is still found in full. If you edit the graph meanwhile, VI / IV Plot, Firing Properties, Sag Analysis, Membrane Test, Spectrogram, Slow AHP Analysis and the LFP Oscillations tools say so in the Data Log, so you can run them again on the edited data.

VI Plot (Voltage–Current relationship)

Requires: a current-clamp Graph with a voltage-response channel and a current-command channel, recorded during a series of current steps (one step size per sweep).

  • Use E-Phys Tools → VI Plot. For each sweep, @Graph measures the injected current and the steady-state voltage response (mean over the last 20% of the step, ending 0.5 ms before the step's end; the baseline likewise ends 0.5 ms before the step).
  • The result plots voltage (Y) against injected current (X), usually linear below threshold. The slope of the linear part, from the steady-state responses, is the input resistance (MΩ). IPFX fits the peak deflection of its hyperpolarizing sweeps instead, so the two can differ where Iₕ sags.
  • VI Plot and IV Plot stop with a message when the family looks recorded in the other clamp mode, judged from how quickly the voltage reaches its new level at each step: at once for a voltage command, gradually for a charging membrane. The check needs at least 2 sweeps.
  • Input resistance is fitted over the linear range around 0 pA, found from the data because it depends on the cell (±50 pA may suit a pyramidal cell but not a high-resistance cerebellar granule cell). Starting from the step nearest 0 (or 0 itself, when no 0 pA step was run) with one step either side, the range grows a step at a time, on both sides together while it stays linear, then on whichever side still is. Growing from the center keeps the range balanced around 0 where the data allow. The Data Log reports the range and how many sweeps it held, and the plotted line spans it.
  • Linear here means the slope changes by no more than 10% from one end of the range to the other (the slope of a quadratic fitted to it, checked on the whole range and on each side of 0). With 7 or more steps @Graph estimates the noise, and only curvature larger than the noise can end the range. With fewer, noise alone can end it early (often after 3 steps), and the Data Log then says what range allowing for noise would give (see the options below).
  • Special cases for the input resistance fit: if the steps either side of 0 already bend by more than 10%, @Graph keeps them (they give the slope at 0) and says so in the Data Log. It fits one side only when that side is much straighter, as when every depolarizing step rectifies (giving −50 to 0 pA, say). When no step is near 0, (0, 0) is added as a point (the response is baseline-subtracted, so it is 0 there): the Data Log says so ('plus the origin'), the sweep count leaves it out, and R² includes it. With only 2 steps in the fit, the Data Log warns that there are too few to tell whether the range is linear.
  • A single sweep an odd event moved (a spontaneous depolarization, a large EPSP) is dropped from the fit rather than allowed to end the range early. Each sweep is compared with a curve through its six nearest neighbors, which follows rectification but not one stray point; a sweep more than 4 robust standard deviations off is dropped, one at a time, at most 20% of the sweeps and only with 7 or more. Dropped sweeps stay in the plot and are marked † in the Data Log. A single odd sweep can hide from this rule by pulling its neighbors' curves toward it, so the Data Log also names any sweep a robust fit of the whole curve would drop (see the options below).
  • Options (Settings → Neurophysiology, all off by default; they apply to the menu, Batch Processing, Protocols and the AI Assistant): 'Fit the line through the origin' fits the range's steps by a line through (0, 0), and R² is then measured about 0, as R's lm(y ~ 0 + x) reports it. 'Allow for noise with 5 or 6 steps' estimates the noise from the scatter about a quadratic through all the steps, so noise alone no longer ends the range while real curvature still does (the IV Plot always allows for noise, from the holding current). 'Flag outlier sweeps with a robust fit' drops sweeps far off a robust (Huber) quadratic through all the steps, from 6 steps: more than 4 robust SDs off (5 with 6 steps). In simulated families it caught a 2 mV error at +20 pA in 72 to 97% of runs with 7 to 11 steps, where the default rule caught 2 to 60%.
  • Sweeps that fire action potentials (a rise of more than 20 mV within 1 ms, the usual 20 V/s criterion) are still plotted, but their steady-state mean includes the spikes, so they are left out of input resistance and τ. The Data Log marks them with *.
  • Membrane τ: fitted for each sweep that didn't fire, as V(t) = V∞ + A·e^(−t/τ) by nonlinear least squares from where the response first reaches 10% of its peak deflection to that peak (the method of the Allen Institute's IPFX). Starting at 10% skips the bridge artifact at the step; ending at the peak keeps out the sag after it; and V∞ is fitted rather than taken from the end of the step. Per-sweep values go to the Data Log with the VI table; a sweep whose response is too small (under 5× the baseline noise) or too brief to fit is skipped (shown as a dash). The reported membrane τ is the mean over the hyperpolarizing sweeps, as depolarizing steps recruit active conductances that shorten it; all fitted sweeps are averaged only when no hyperpolarizing sweep was fitted.
  • Baseline Vm: the resting potential before the step, averaged over all sweeps. The summary table (Batch Processing, Protocols) says beside it whether junction-potential correction was taken off it.
  • The output graph has both axes crossing at zero, no junction-potential correction, and axis labels 'Current step' (X) and 'Steady-state voltage response' (Y), in the recording's units.

IV Plot (Current–Voltage relationship)

Requires: a voltage-clamp Graph with a current-response channel and a voltage-command channel, recorded during a series of voltage steps (one step size per sweep).

  • Use E-Phys Tools → IV Plot. For each sweep, @Graph measures the command voltage and the steady-state current (mean over the last 20% of the step, ending 0.5 ms before the step's end, so the capacitive transient at the edge stays out; the baseline likewise ends 0.5 ms before the step).
  • The result plots current (Y) against the voltage step (X, the change from the holding potential). Baseline current and holding potential (junction-potential corrected when correction is on) go to the Data Log.
  • Input resistance comes from the linear range around the holding potential, found by the VI Plot's rule with the axes swapped: from the 0 mV step and one step either side, the range grows a step at a time while the slope (here a conductance) changes by no more than 10% beyond the noise. The holding potential is the center because it is commanded, so it is known exactly, and the baseline-subtracted current is 0 there by construction; the cell's zero-current potential has to be read off the curve, and the Data Log says when it lies outside the range.
  • The noise allowed for comes from the holding current: every sweep returns to the same holding command, so the scatter of its baseline current from sweep to sweep measures the noise whatever shape the curve has. The VI Plot's estimate (the scatter about a cubic through all the steps) would take the strong bend of voltage-gated currents for noise and let the range run into them. So an IV family always allows for noise, and 'Allow for noise with 5 or 6 steps' does not apply to it.
  • The current is the measured variable and the voltage is commanded, so the current is regressed on the voltage and input resistance = 1 / slope.
  • The Data Log gives the range in command potentials and as steps from the holding potential, how many steps it held, and the steps left out on each side and why: for example 'nonlinear, the conductance rises (outward rectification)' above the range, or inward rectification (Kir) below it. A sweep beyond the range is reported there, not marked † as an outlier. When even the steps nearest the holding potential bend by more than 10%, it warns that the value includes voltage-gated current: record smaller steps near the holding potential.
  • On the simulated IV family (a 250 MΩ leak plus a delayed-rectifier K⁺ current) the range is −100 to −70 mV and input resistance 247 MΩ, as the noise-free currents give over those steps.
  • Fitting through the origin, robust outlier flagging and the clamp-mode check work as for the VI Plot. For a range of your own, run Linear Fit on the IV plot over it: the slope is a conductance, and 1 / slope is the input resistance.
  • The output graph has both axes crossing at zero, no junction-potential correction, and axis labels 'Voltage step' (X) and 'Steady-state current' (Y), in the recording's units.
  • Rₛ-corrected voltage axis: in the Rₛ panel's Voltage clamp mode, turn on 'Correct the IV Plot's voltage axis for Rₛ'. The panel finds the uncompensated Rₛ as it does for the trace correction (a test pulse, the last Membrane Test, telegraphs or values you enter) and the graph keeps it. IV Plot then plots and fits each point at Vₘ = Vcmd − I·Rₛ (as a step from the holding Vₘ, which the holding current moves too) instead of at the command; the Data Log adds a Vcmd column. This needs no Erev and is exact for steady-state currents, voltage-gated ones included: the current is plotted at the potential the membrane actually reached. Input resistance then excludes Rₛ. It is not applied while a voltage-clamp trace correction stands, which already scales each current to its command. The AI Assistant's VI/IV tools use it too.
  • Erev: with the axis corrected, the Data Log reports where the total current (holding current included) crosses zero, the zero of a line fitted to the two points either side of the first crossing, with its confidence interval by Fieller's method (at the confidence level in Settings). At zero current there is no Rₛ error, so for a single-compartment cell this is the reversal potential, and the Rₛ panel can use it ('Erev from the last IV Plot'). On a simulated I–V of a 100 MΩ leak reversing at −20 mV, recorded through 10 or 20 MΩ, the corrected plot gave −20.00 mV (CI ±0.03) and input resistance 100.0 MΩ, where the uncorrected one read 110 or 120 MΩ. On a simulated K⁺ family the corrected steady-state currents matched the true I–V within 0.4%; but only over the potentials the membrane reached (−80 to +2 mV for commands to +40 mV at 15 MΩ), since no correction can supply data at potentials never reached.

Firing Properties (rheobase and F–I curve)

Requires: a current-clamp Graph with a voltage channel, recorded during a family of current steps (one step per sweep). Use E-Phys Tools → Firing Properties…

  • Spikes are found by AP Detection with the settings of its last run (or the ones the panel starts with): every AP in each sweep, counted for a step when its threshold falls within the step, its start and end included.
  • Step currents come from the file's stimulus protocol when it has one (each step's change from the level before it), else from a recorded current channel (measured as in the VI Plot), else from values you enter: the first step and the increment per sweep, or every sweep's current, with the step's start and end as the X axis shows them. Currents are reported in pA.
  • Per sweep: the spike count; the mean firing rate (spikes in the step / step duration); the instantaneous frequency of the first and of the last ISI (1 / ISI); the first-spike latency (from the step's onset to the first threshold); the ISI CV (sample SD / mean, from 3 spikes); and the adaptation index as the Allen Institute's IPFX defines it (adapt): the mean of (ISIᵢ₊₁ − ISIᵢ) / (ISIᵢ₊₁ + ISIᵢ), near 0 for regular firing and positive when firing slows. IPFX's isi_cv uses the population SD: multiply by √((n − 1)/n), for n ISIs, to compare.
  • Rheobase: the smallest positive step that fired at least one AP. It is only as precise as the steps, so the Data Log also gives the step below it that didn't fire (rheobase lies between the two), or says that the smallest positive step tested already fired (rheobase is then that step or less). Steps within 1% of the largest of 0 count as 0, and a sweep that fires at 0 pA or below is noted (spontaneous firing).
  • F–I curve: mean rate against step current, plotted in a new window. With 'Fit the F–I slope' on (the default), a least-squares line through the steps at or above rheobase that fired gives the F–I slope in Hz/pA, as IPFX's fi_fit_slope. A rate that falls at the largest steps (depolarization block) is noted, since a line then fits only below it. Steps above rheobase that fire no AP (depolarization block, or a cell running down) are plotted at 0 Hz, left out of the F–I slope, and named in the Data Log.
  • Maximum firing rate: the highest mean rate over the step, with its current.
  • The table goes to the Data Log. Batch Processing, Protocols and the AI Assistant run it too, and Batch can save each F–I curve as a .@graph file. Checked against IPFX on simulated cells: the same rheobase, adaptation index within 0.001, and the same spike counts (so rates and F–I slope) except where a threshold falls right at the step's start or end.

Sag Analysis

Requires: current-clamp voltage sweeps recorded during hyperpolarizing (negative) current steps, showing a sag: an initial hyperpolarization that partly recovers before the step ends, typical of HCN (Iₕ) channels.

  • Use E-Phys Tools → Sag Analysis.
  • For each sweep, @Graph finds the sag peak (the most negative point of a 4 ms running mean, so noise doesn't deepen it, searching until the trace clearly starts to recover, at most 400 ms into the step) and the steady-state voltage (mean over the last 20% of the step), both relative to the pre-step baseline.
  • Step timing comes from the current channel. Without one it comes from the file's protocol when it has one, or else from the voltage sweeps themselves: each edge is where the sweeps first leave the level they held before it by more than three times that level's own 1 ms noise (usually within 1 to 2 ms of the real edge).
  • Sag ratio (%) = (peak − steady state) / peak × 100, reported for the sweep whose peak falls closest to 20 mV below its baseline. The sag slope over the 50 ms after the peak is also reported. IPFX instead uses the sweep whose peak potential is closest to −100 mV, and takes the steady state over the last 30 ms of the step.
