  • Only a sweep whose peak and steady state are both at least 1 mV below its baseline counts. A graph with none (depolarizing steps only, or a step edge found in the wrong place) is refused with a message rather than given a ratio over noise, and so is a graph that records only current.
  • Results are written to the Data Log. To plot a column, select it there and press ⇧⌘G.

Slow AHP Analysis

Requires: a current-clamp Graph with a spike train. @Graph detects the train and measures the afterhyperpolarization after its last spike: peak amplitude relative to baseline, the slow AHP at a fixed latency after the step, recovery time from an exponential fit, and the area from the peak to recovery. E-Phys Tools → Slow AHP Analysis… opens a window where you set the options and run it. All times are ms from the sweep's start.

  • Spike Detection: spikes are peaks above Threshold (mV) (default 0 mV; not found by slope, so capacitive artifacts aren't counted) and at least Min. separation between spikes apart (default 5 ms, so above 200 Hz every second spike is missed; lower it for faster firing). A spike whose peak stays below the threshold isn't counted, so set the threshold below the smallest late spike in an adapting train. Train Grouping: spikes closer than Max. inter-spike gap (default 500 ms) form one train, and trains with fewer than Min. spikes per train (default 3) are ignored.
  • Baseline, Before each train (the default): the median of up to 200 ms (Length) ending 2 ms before the train's current step starts, from the file's protocol. Without a protocol, it ends where the trace starts rising toward the first spike (the first point, walking back, where it rises less than 0.05 mV/ms). Or choose Time window (Fixed values, or Live from cursors), one per sweep, or Manual value. A median, so a spontaneous spike in the window doesn't shift it. The Data Log gives each train's window and warns when it holds a spike, overlaps a train (its step, spikes or AHP), was cut short, or drifts by more than 0.5 mV.
  • The AHP peak is the lowest point of the trace smoothed over ±10 ms, within the Peak search duration (default 100 ms). The search starts at the later of the last spike plus a gap (Start, default 5 ms after the last spike's peak) and the end of the current step: from the protocol or, without one, where the trace falls at least 5 mV off a plateau, so an adapting cell's depolarized plateau isn't read as an AHP. The amplitude is baseline minus the trace averaged over the Peak averaging window (default 20 ms, one cycle of 50 Hz mains hum, so hum doesn't bias it; 0 uses the single sample at the peak).
  • When the peak is within 10 ms of the search window's end, or the trace keeps falling past it, the Data Log warns that the true peak may lie beyond: a slow AHP often peaks several hundred ms after the train, so lengthen the window or use the fixed-latency value. A trace above baseline at the peak is flagged too.
  • Slow AHP at a fixed latency: baseline minus the mean voltage from 0.5 to 1 s after the current step ends (after the last spike when no step end is known), the usual measure of the slow AHP. The span can be changed. The peak above, found within 100 ms of the step's end, is what many studies call the medium AHP (mAHP), and this later value the slow AHP (sAHP). Both are measured from the baseline before the train, not from the spike threshold as AP Detection's AHP is. Beside it, the integral over a fixed span (from the step's end to 1 s after it by default): the area between baseline and trace, which, unlike the peak-to-recovery integral, doesn't depend on where recovery is placed, so it compares directly between cells.
  • Each train's windows (recovery, fit, integral and fixed latency) end before the next train's onset or the next spike of any kind, whichever comes first, so the next step's upstroke or a stray spike isn't fitted.
  • Recovery is fitted with a single or double exponential on the trace averaged into 1 ms bins (a 60 s sweep fits in well under a second). A single exponential starts 400 ms after the peak, skipping the fast early part; choosing the double sets the delay to 0, since it needs that part to determine its fast τ. A time constant shorter than two bins or longer than three times the fitted span is reported as "not determined", and fewer than 10 points give no fit.
  • Recovery time is when the fit reaches a set fraction (default 95%) of the way back to baseline, within the recovery window. If the fit is already past that point where it starts, the time is read from the trace smoothed over ±10 ms before it. If it never gets there, the result says "not reached" and how far it recovered.
  • It measures the selected group's visible sweeps, skipping fit curves and AP markers, and every row gives its sweep. Voltages are converted to mV whatever the recording's unit (V, mV or µV); sweeps whose unit isn't a voltage are skipped, and so are voltage sweeps with 16 or fewer distinct levels, which are a command waveform rather than a recording. The Data Log lists both. Missing samples are skipped by every average. With more than one train, the Data Log also gives the mean, SD and n of each measurement across trains (recovery time and integral over the trains that recovered).

A "not reached" result with a high recovered percentage usually means the recovery window is too short for that trace, not that the cell failed to recover. By default the search runs to the end of the sweep or the next train; if 'Limit recovery search to a maximum duration' is on, raise the limit or turn it off. Otherwise record longer sweeps, or space the trains further apart.

Spectrogram

Requires: any Graph. E-Phys Tools → Spectrogram… opens a settings window for the front graph; the Spectrogram section of the LFP / Spike Train Analysis tab has the same controls for that tab's graph.

  • Like PSD / FFT, but the spectra of successive windows are kept separate instead of averaged, giving a frequency × time heatmap. Handy for power that changes over time, such as theta and gamma power during behavior or LFP power around a stimulus.
  • Choose the Sweep, the Window size (samples) (default 256, rounded down to a power of 2, minimum 64; shorter windows give finer time resolution but coarser frequency resolution), the Overlap (default 50%), 'Restrict max frequency shown' with its Max frequency (Hz), and 'Log scale (dB)' (on by default), then click Compute Spectrogram. The status line gives the window used, in ms, and its frequency resolution. A window holding a missing (blank) sample is left out of its column's average, and a column with no complete window shows as a gap.
  • The result opens in a new heatmap window: frequency on one axis, time on the other, power as color. Power is a one-sided density (units²/Hz, or dB re 1 units²/Hz; the chart title gives the unit), scaled as PSD / FFT is, so the two agree. It opens unnormalized on a sequential purple–teal–yellow scale; the heatmap inspector's z-score options are there if you want each frequency's change over time instead.
  • Frequency runs up the vertical axis, lowest at the bottom. Times are as the graph's X axis shows them, after Set X = 0 or the file's own start time.
  • Each window's mean is removed before its FFT (Hann window). Very long windows (8192 samples or more) give more frequency bins than a heatmap can draw; adjacent bins are then averaged into one row (0 Hz keeps a row of its own), so the whole range up to the maximum frequency still shows. Likewise a long recording gives more windows than it can draw; neighboring windows are then averaged into one column, so every sample still counts and a short burst still shows. For a recording with channels at different sample rates, only each sweep's own samples are used. Values match scipy.signal.spectrogram with window 'hann', detrend 'constant' and density scaling (to single precision; the FFT is single precision).
  • Method: Short-time FFT uses one Hann window per segment. Multitaper multiplies each window of T seconds by several Slepian tapers and averages the results (Thomson 1982; as Chronux and Prerau et al. 2017), giving a smoother map. NW sets the frequency resolution, 2W = 2·NW/T, shown under the settings. The automatic taper count, 2NW − 1, is the most that stay well concentrated. Detrend removes each window's mean (default), a fitted line, or nothing. Padding gives finer bins, not finer resolution.

The spectrogram is computed in the background, so you can keep working. A progress bar shows how far it has got; click Cancel to stop it, and no window opens. Closing the window also stops it. With a long multitaper window (several seconds of samples), computing the tapers takes a noticeable share of the time, and the bar includes it.

Cross-Correlation / Coherence

Requires: two signals to compare, for example to ask whether two units fire together or two LFP channels are phase-locked. E-Phys Tools → Cross-Correlation / Coherence… compares the front graph (Signal A) with another open graph or another sweep of the same graph (Signal B). The Cross-Correlation / Coherence section of the LFP / Spike Train Analysis tab has the same controls, with that tab's graph as Signal A, and gives identical results. Choose the analysis, Sweep (or Train) A, where Signal B comes from ('this graph', or any open graph window, listed as windows open and close), and Sweep B, then click Run; results open in a new graph window with the axis in the right unit (s for lags, Hz for coherence).

  • Spike-train cross-correlogram: spike-timing correlation between two spike-train sources (such as rasters from Extracellular AP Detection, or spike-sorted units), showing synchrony, lead or lag. Both signals must be spike trains. Each bar counts B spikes at that lag from an A spike (positive lag: B after A). Choosing the same train as A and B gives an autocorrelogram; each spike's pairing with itself is then left out of the zero-lag bin.
  • Axes (spike-train cross-correlogram): Bin width (default 5 ms) and Max lag (default 200 ms) set the lag axis. Bins are centered on whole multiples of the bin width; each runs from half a bin below its center up to, but not including, half a bin above, as numpy.histogram's bins do, and a lag exactly on an edge is judged in whole samples. Y scale: Counts (the default), or Rate (Hz), which is B's firing rate around an A spike (counts ÷ number of A spikes ÷ bin width) and so compares across bin widths and train sizes.
  • Chance level (spike-train cross-correlogram): a second trace shows what each bin would hold if the trains were independent, from each train's mean rate over its recorded span (for a spike-sorted file, which doesn't store it, from that train's first to its last spike, the rule every spike-train tool follows). It is edge-corrected using both trains' spans: an A spike near the end of a sweep, or outside B's span, has less of B to pair with at long lags, so the line falls toward the ends of the lag axis (by about |lag| ÷ sweep length). On short sweeps the correlogram of two unrelated trains has the same tent shape, so compare the peak with the line, not with the outer bins. A peak well above it suggests a real timing relationship; the line is a guide, not a significance test.
  • Spike CCG over trials, shift predictor and jitter bands: two units that both respond to a stimulus make a broad CCG peak even with no connection between them (Brody 1999). 'All trials of both units' sums trial k of Train A's unit with trial k of Train B's unit over every trial; units are found from Spike Sorter names ('<unit>: <sweep>'), and a raster without them counts each train as a trial. The note under the switch shows which trials were paired, and the chance line is kept (summed over trials).
  • Shift predictor (Perkel et al. 1967): the same correlogram between different trials, which share the stimulus but no moment-to-moment coupling. All shifts, averaged (the default) sums A's trial j with B's trial k for every j ≠ k and divides by (trials − 1), the least noisy form and equal to the PSTH predictor; Next trial pairs trial k with k + 1 (the last with the first). The result adds the predictor and the corrected CCG (raw − predictor), which is near 0 where only the stimulus links the two.
  • Jitter bands (Amarasingham et al. 2012) test for fine-timescale synchrony. Every spike of B is moved to a random time within its own window (Window, default 20 ms, typically 10 to 25 ms, tiling each trial from its start) while A stays as recorded, which keeps B's firing-rate changes slower than the window and destroys only its fine timing relative to A. The CCG is recomputed, binned the same way, for each surrogate (1000 by default). The random draws are seeded from the data, so the same trains always give the same bands. An autocorrelogram jitters its one train.
  • Pattern jitter (Harrison & Geman 2009): spikes of B no more than R apart ('Preserve spike patterns shorter than R', 10 ms by default) keep their exact intervals and move together, and every longer interval stays longer than R, so B's bursts and pauses survive and only their timing relative to A is tested. Every surrogate that follows these rules is equally likely, and they are drawn exactly by the paper's algorithm (on a time grid of 1/1000 of the window, at least 10 µs, made coarser for very long trains so the tables stay under about 10 million entries).
  • Choosing R: set it to cover B's burst intervals (the ISI / Burst Detection max interval is a good guide); a larger R holds more of B in place, which leaves less room to detect weak synchrony. R = 0 is plain interval jitter, which breaks B's own bursts up: two bursty trains then cross the global band far more often than 5% (17 to 37% in simulations with bursts of 4 to 6 spikes), so use 0 only when B doesn't fire in bursts.
  • Reading the jitter bands: the pointwise band is each lag's 2.5th to 97.5th percentile, and about 1 lag in 20 crosses it by chance. The global band (Fujisawa et al. 2008) is set from each surrogate's highest and lowest bin over all lags shown, so an unrelated pair crosses it anywhere only about 5% of the time: a bin beyond it is significant. In simulations of 300 independent pairs each with R = 10 ms, 4 to 8% crossed it (where an exact test reads about 6%, with the 200 surrogates used there), whether neither, one or both trains fired in bursts (6.0% over 1,200 pairs of bursty trains).
  • Jitter-corrected CCG: the mean of the surrogates is shown too, and its name says which jitter was used. Last comes the jitter-corrected CCG (raw − jitter mean, Smith & Kohn 2008), which takes out co-modulation slower than the window and is near 0 where the two aren't synchronized more finely than that.
  • Checks on the CCG: values match numpy. Plain jitter surrogates match the Elephant toolkit's jitter_spikes, and their mean matched the analytically expected jittered CCG within its Monte Carlo error. Pattern jitter surrogates match an independent implementation of Harrison and Geman's algorithm. References are under LFP / Spike Train Analysis.
  • Signal cross-correlation: correlation between two continuous signals over a range of lags (positive lag: B follows A), up to Max lag (default 200 ms). Both signals have their mean removed. 'Normalize (±1 scale)' (on by default) divides by the square root of the product of the two mean-removed signals' sums of squares over the whole record (MATLAB's xcov 'coeff', which is xcorr 'coeff' after removing the means), so identical signals read 1 at zero lag; because the overlap shrinks at longer lags while the divisor doesn't, values taper toward the ends of the lag range. Both signals must share a sample rate. The maximum lag is rounded to the nearest whole sample. The lag range stops at the record's length (and at 500,000 samples); the result's name says when it was shortened. A missing sample inside a trace counts as its mean, adding nothing at any lag.
  • Lining up the two signals: one rule for every analysis here, spike trains included. When both sweeps carry a recording clock (a recording date and sweep start times, as two graphs of one file, two files of one session, or two sweeps of one graph do), they are lined up on that clock and only the time both cover is used, and the result's name (and the Data Log) says so. Channels of one sweep were recorded together and are compared as they are. Two sweeps recorded at different times (sweep 1 against sweep 3, say) don't overlap on the clock, so the comparison is refused with an explanation and a button, Compare from each sweep's start (not simultaneous). It lines them up from each sweep's start, as trials, and the result's name, legend and Data Log entry say 'not simultaneous: trial-relative comparison (from each sweep's start)'. When one or both have no recording clock, they are compared from their starts and the result's name says so. Missing samples at either end (a blank tail some files carry) are left out of both. For spike trains, B's spike times and recorded span are moved onto A's clock and cut to the time both cover, which the chance level and the jitter bands then use. Over trials, each pair (trial k of A with trial k of B) is lined up on its own sweeps' clocks, and the shift predictor pairs trials in that trial-relative time.
  • Coherence: magnitude-squared coherence, frequency by frequency, between two continuous signals, averaged over 50%-overlapping Hann-windowed segments (Welch's method, matching SciPy). A segment with a missing sample in either signal is left out, as in PSD / FFT, and too few samples for 2 segments gives an error rather than a misleading result.
  • Segment size (coherence; samples, 0 = auto, the default) is rounded down to a power of 2 (at least 64) for the FFT, so 1000 uses 512. Auto uses the largest power of 2 up to a quarter of the record (at most 8192 samples), which averages at least 7 segments for records of 256 samples or more (fewer below that, down to 3 at 128 samples). The result's name gives the size used, its frequency resolution and the segment count. Coherence is biased upwards with few segments: two unrelated signals read about 1 ÷ (number of segments), so 0.5 from 2 segments means nothing.
  • Significance level (coherence): a second trace is the 95% level for two independent signals, at each frequency: 1 − 0.05^(1/(K − 1)), with K the effective number of segments (the count reduced for their 50% overlap). About 1 frequency in 20 crosses it by chance, so look for a band of adjacent frequencies above it rather than a single bin. The line leaves out 0 Hz, the first frequency above it and the Nyquist frequency, where chance coherence runs higher (independent noise crossed it 15%, 8% and 10% of the time there, as SciPy's coherence gives: removing each segment's mean also reaches the first bin).
  • Phase and time lag (coherence): two more panels show the cross-spectrum's phase, as B's phase lag behind A in degrees (the angle of SciPy's csd(b, a)), and, at the frequencies whose coherence is above the significance line, the time lag that phase implies: phase ÷ (360° × frequency), in seconds (Bendat & Piersol 2010). Both are positive when B lags A, as a positive cross-correlation lag is. A phase is known only up to whole cycles, so the lag is the one within half a period of zero: for a lag longer than that (above about 100 Hz for a 5 ms lag) the value wraps, and a steady slope of phase against frequency across a coherent band is the more reliable estimate.

LFP Oscillations

Requires: any Graph (a raster shows only the spike–LFP section). E-Phys Tools → LFP Oscillations… opens a window with Band Power Over Time, Hilbert Envelope and Phase, Phase–Amplitude Coupling and Spike-Triggered LFP and Phase Locking for the front graph: the same controls as those sections of the LFP / Spike Train Analysis tab, described on its page, with the same results.

Spike Sorter

Requires: a Graph with at least one continuous extracellular channel. E-Phys Tools → Spike Sorter… opens a window that detects and clusters spikes on one channel (or a group of channels you choose, such as a tetrode) of the front graph, lets you review and edit the clusters, and commits them as named spike-train units.

  • Scope: a semi-automated sorter in the spirit of Plexon Offline Sorter, not a replacement for probe-based sorters such as Kilosort. @Graph knows nothing about electrode layout, so you choose which channels belong together.
  • Detection: choose the Primary channel, 'Pre-filter to spike band' (on, 300–3000 Hz by default), Polarity (negative by default), and Threshold: Auto (× noise SD, a slider from 3 to 8, default 5) or a Manual value. Snippet window (default 2 ms) is the length of the waveform cut out, centered on each spike; a spike closer than half of it to a sweep's start or end is left out, since its waveform doesn't fit, and the status line counts those. Then click Detect & Sort; each run replaces the current result. A Cancel button beside it stops a run, leaving the current result as it was. After each spike, detection pauses for the Dead time (default 0.5 ms), so one spike isn't counted twice. Keep it shorter than the Refractory period (default 1.5 ms), which is used only for the quality measures: a lockout as long as the refractory period would make every refractory violation undetectable. The automatic threshold is the multiplier × the median noise level (median(|x|)/0.6745) over up to 20 sweeps spread through the recording, each read from up to 30 s (thirty 1 s stretches spread across a longer sweep, so an hour-long recording isn't judged by its first half-minute), and one threshold is used for every sweep, so a unit is detected by the same criterion throughout; the status line reports the value used. Each detected spike is re-centered on its true peak, and its time is refined between samples, so small timing jitter doesn't blur the clusters.
  • Channel Group: optionally add more channels. Clustering then uses each spike's waveforms on all of them together, which helps when a unit looks different on nearby sites. The channels must share the primary's sample rate and length (otherwise the sorter says so and doesn't run), and every channel is read in the primary channel's unit (a channel shown in mV beside one in µV is converted), so a manual threshold, amplitudes, the best channel and template matching mean the same on all of them.
  • Blank data (Channel Group): a channel with no signal (a disabled or zeroed contact) adds no features, and the status line names it. A spike whose waveform window touches blank (missing) samples, such as the gap between joined recordings, is left out and counted in the status line. Background windows over a blank are skipped, and if no usable background is left the status line says the features are unwhitened.
  • Detect on (Channel Group) chooses where spikes are found. All channels in the group (the default) thresholds every channel at its own noise level (the same multiplier, or the same manual value), so a unit largest on any channel is found, not only one large on the primary channel. Primary channel only detects on the primary channel alone, as with a single channel.
  • Spikes on several channels (Channel Group) count once: of crossings closer than the dead time (at least 0.3 ms), whatever their channels, the one largest relative to its channel's noise is kept (as SpikeInterface's 'locally exclusive' detection). The spike is timed on that channel, between samples, and its waveforms are cut on every channel at that time. The dead time therefore applies per spike, not per channel, which is what the contamination estimate assumes.
  • Best channel (Channel Group): each cluster's best channel is the one where its average waveform is largest. Its SNR is measured there, the cluster list names it, and the Waveforms tab labels each channel with the clusters it is best for.
  • Clustering runs the steps below in order. Always check the automatic result. Split, overlap check and merge run only with 'Auto cluster count' on (the default); set the cluster count yourself (K) to keep the clusters as k-means leaves them.
  • Outliers (Clustering): 'Auto-exclude amplitude outliers' (on by default) keeps spikes with extreme amplitudes (usually movement or electrical artifacts) out of the clustering and puts them in their own 'Artifacts' cluster, which is left out of Commit by default, like a Noise cluster. A large real unit is not excluded: outliers whose amplitudes agree closely with each other (at least 20 spikes) are clustered like any other spikes.
  • Features (Clustering): waveforms are reduced to 3 principal components, or with a channel group to 3 per channel (each channel's own, as in KlustaKwik, MClust, Kilosort and SpikeInterface; one set of components across all channels spends them on the largest channels and can miss two units that share a best channel). The components are taken after noise whitening: the background noise is measured between spikes and divided out (Pouzat et al. 2002), so the noise no longer dominates the components and one unit forms a round cloud rather than an elongated one.
  • k-means (Clustering): spikes are grouped by the best of 5 k-means++ starts, so one unlucky start can't merge two units, with the number of clusters chosen automatically or set by you.
  • Split (Clustering): any cluster that holds two units is cut in two by k-means, and the cut is kept when its spikes, projected on the line between the two halves, form two peaks (Hartigan's dip test at the 5% level); each half is tested again. This is the other half of ISO-SPLIT's split-and-merge (Magland and Barnett 2015), and it catches k-means putting two distinct units in one cluster while spending clusters on a few outlying spikes. A single unit forms one peak on that line, so it stays whole.
  • Overlap check (Clustering): a small cluster (under a tenth of the largest) is checked for being other units' spikes landing on or next to each other, which with 3 components per channel can form a cluster of their own. Checking before the merge keeps it from joining such a cluster to a unit. Each of its spikes is fitted with the other clusters' average waveforms (templates), as template matching and Resolve Spike Collisions fit them (Pillow et al. 2013; Ekanadham et al. 2014; Kilosort): one unit at the spike's time, refitted together with any spikes of the units right next to it, or else that unit plus a second spike hidden at another lag.
  • Overlap results (Clustering): if at least half a cluster's spikes fit that way, clearly better than the cluster's own average waveform fits them, each spike goes to the unit fitted at its time, any hidden spike is added to its unit, and the cluster is removed. Spikes that nothing explains go to an 'Unresolved overlaps' cluster labeled Noise, left out of Commit by default. A small cluster with a consistent shape of its own, such as a rarely firing unit, is kept, since its own average fits it best. The status line reports what was moved.
  • Merge (Clustering): two clusters are merged when they look like one unit cut in two: their spikes, projected on the line between the two cluster centers, form a single peak (Hartigan's dip test at the 5% level, with the p-values of R's diptest). A small cluster, under a tenth of the pair's spikes, would barely change that test, so it is tested against only as many of the larger cluster's spikes as it has, those nearest to it: a tail cut off one unit continues into them and is merged back, while a separate small unit leaves a gap and stays apart. Clusters of under 10 spikes are left as they are. This keeps a unit whose size drifts, shrinks within bursts or varies unevenly as one cluster.
  • Review: a waveform overlay per channel, a PC scatter plot, an ISI histogram and a Drift tab, with a quality summary for each cluster in the list. The waveform, PC scatter and Drift views are scaled to the spikes not labeled Noise, so a few huge artifacts can't shrink the real units into a corner; Noise spikes beyond that range are clipped.
  • PC scatter plot (Review): with a channel group, pick the channel and component for each axis, or leave them on Auto: PC1 against PC2 of the selected cluster's best channel. Box moves use the axes shown.
  • ISI histogram (Review): 0.5 ms bins, with refractory violations highlighted. Intervals are measured within each sweep, never across a sweep boundary.
  • Drift tab (Review): spike amplitude against recording time (sweeps laid end to end; with 'Drift-tolerant' on, dashed lines mark its blocks). The extremum of each spike is searched for over 0.75 ms after its threshold crossing (as in WaveClus).
  • Cluster list (Review): spike count, firing rate (spikes per second over the whole recording searched), ISI-violation percentage (intervals shorter than the refractory period), an estimated contamination with the number of violations it rests on, SNR, isolation distance (higher is better separated), L-ratio (lower is better separated), amplitude cutoff and presence ratio.
  • Contamination (Review): the share of the cluster's spikes that come from other neurons, from the violations, the dead time and the recording length (Hill et al. 2011, with Llobet et al. 2022's correction for violations between two contaminating spikes, without which it reads high). With only a few violations it is very uncertain. It assumes the other neurons' spikes come at random times, independent of the unit's own, and it can't go above 100%: more violations than that model allows show as ≥100%.
  • SNR (Review): p-p ÷ 2σ, the peak-to-peak size of the cluster's average waveform over twice the noise level, on its best channel with a channel group. SpikeInterface and Kilosort divide the largest deflection by the noise level instead, which reads about 1.4 times higher.
  • Amplitude cutoff (Review): the estimated fraction of the unit's spikes that were too small to cross the threshold, from how sharply the low end of its amplitude histogram is cut off (Hill et al. 2011; 0 to 0.5, lower is better; the Allen Institute keeps units under 0.1). It needs at least 500 spikes and assumes the amplitudes spread evenly around their middle, which drift breaks. Each spike's amplitude is the least-squares scale of the cluster's average waveform fitted to it, as SpikeInterface's amplitude scalings, but without its joint fit of colliding spikes.
  • Presence ratio (Review): the fraction of 60 s stretches of the recording in which the unit fired at least once (a sweep shorter than that is a stretch of its own), so a unit lost partway through reads low (the Allen Institute keeps units above 0.9). It needs a recording of at least 60 s.
  • Isolation distance and L-ratio (Review) are measured against every other spike of the sort, the Artifacts cluster included. The often-quoted marks (isolation distance above 20, L-ratio below 0.05; Schmitzer-Torbert et al. 2005) were set with 8 features per spike, while these use 3 principal components (3 per channel with a channel group, so the chi-square degrees of freedom are 3 × the number of channels, as in SpikeInterface). The values run on a different scale, so compare clusters with each other rather than with those marks.
  • Against SpikeInterface (Review): the presence ratio is computed exactly as its quality metrics compute it, and the amplitude cutoff too, except that @Graph's smoothed histogram counts stay real numbers: SpikeInterface truncates them to whole numbers, which makes its estimate noisier (an SD of 0.079 against 0.046 at 600 spikes). The firing rate, violation count and L-ratio match SpikeInterface's too, given the same features, and so does isolation distance wherever it is defined (SpikeInterface reports a value even when there are fewer spikes outside the cluster than in it).
  • Editing: rename, relabel (good, multi-unit (MUA) or noise), merge or split clusters, mark one as noise (it stays listed and is left out at Commit), and move points between clusters by drawing a box in the PC scatter. Every edit can be undone, one step at a time, with the 'Undo: …' button under the cluster list: merge, split, delete, rename, relabel, box moves, and the spikes added by template matching or collision resolution (the last 20 steps). 'Show templates' on the Waveforms tab draws each cluster's average template over its waveforms.
  • Template matching: 'Refine via Template Matching' builds an average waveform for each cluster and scans the recording for further spikes of that shape, recovering spikes just below the threshold. Each match is aligned between samples before it is scored, and it must be a true peak of the template match, not the edge of a known spike's dead time. Where two templates match the same event, only the better match is kept, so no spike is counted twice or given to the wrong unit. Template matching and collision resolution each have a Cancel button while they run; a canceled pass adds nothing.
  • Match strength (template matching; slider, default 0.70): how large an event must be relative to the template. The template is scaled to fit the event by least squares, and an event counts when that scale is at least the Match strength (0.70: at least 70% as large as the template).
  • Shape check (template matching; on by default): rejects a match that lines up with a template but has the wrong shape, such as a spike of another unit or a slow deflection. What is left after subtracting the scaled template may be at most 'Tolerance' (default 1.5×) times as large as for a typical spike of the cluster itself, which adapts to the noise level and the number of channels. Raise the tolerance if real spikes are being missed, and lower it if matches look wrong. Clusters with fewer than 5 spikes aren't checked.
  • Drift-tolerant (template matching): turn it on first if the Drift tab shows a unit's waveform changing slowly over the session; the template then changes gradually along the recording. The recording is cut into the chosen number of Blocks (default 4), equal stretches of recorded time, each with its own template (a stretch with fewer than 5 of the unit's spikes is left out). The template at any point is interpolated between the two nearest, so it follows drift within one long continuous sweep as well as across sweeps.
  • Collision resolution: 'Resolve Spike Collisions' finds spikes hidden by an overlapping spike. It uses the Match strength set above. It first subtracts the template of every spike already in a non-Noise cluster, scaled to fit, and then looks within half a snippet of each one for another spike matching any template. A recovered spike must be at least 0.4 ms from any other spike and at least the Dead time from another spike of the same unit. It only searches next to spikes already found, and it cannot separate two spikes less than 0.4 ms apart.
  • 'Save Sort State…' saves the whole session (settings, including template-matching ones, clusters and every spike) to a .atgsort file, by default next to the recording; 'Load Sort State…' reopens one. Useful for pausing a long review or handing it to a colleague. Waveforms are stored exactly (as 32-bit binary values). A file saved by a newer version of @Graph asks you to update. A file whose clusters, spikes or waveforms don't fit together is refused with the reason. A sort state saved from a different recording is refused if its channels or sweeps don't exist here, and otherwise loads only after you confirm.
  • Template matching and collision resolution always use the channel group and band-pass filter of the last Detect & Sort run (or loaded sort state), not the current controls, so they search the recording the same way the clusters' waveforms were extracted. Change the controls and run Detect & Sort again to use new ones.
  • 'Commit Sorted Units' turns each kept cluster into a named spike train in a new Graph window, ready for Cross-Correlation, PSTH and other spike-train analyses. Its spike times are on the recording's own time axis, as Detect Spikes' are. There is one train per cluster per sweep, including sweeps where the unit never fired, so a trial without spikes counts as zero in a PSTH instead of being left out.
  • The AI Assistant can also run the sorter (detect, cluster and optionally refine) when you ask it to sort a channel. It opens the result in a new window as an unreviewed first pass; review and commit it in the Spike Sorter.

Automatic clustering and template matching are starting points. Review the waveforms, PC scatter and ISI histogram before committing. Template matching can be slow on long recordings, and Match strength is a trade-off: too loose picks up noise, too strict misses real spikes. Check the ISI-violation percentage and SNR after each pass.

Membrane Test: Rₛ, Rₘ, and Cₘ

Requires: a voltage-clamp Graph with a brief voltage step (typically 5–10 mV) and its current response. If the Graph has a voltage command channel, the step size (ΔV) is measured from it; otherwise you are asked to enter it. A graph of voltage alone (current clamp) is refused, with a pointer to the Rₛ Correction panel. Use E-Phys Tools → Membrane Test…

  • How it works: @Graph finds the step and the current transient, then fits a single exponential to its decay toward the new steady-state current by least squares, which gives the time constant τ. The recording filter rounds off the transient's instant jump but keeps its area, so the peak current is worked out from the charge the transient carries (peak = steady-state current + charge / τ), as pCLAMP's Membrane Test does. Series resistance (Rₛ) comes from that peak current, membrane resistance (Rₘ) from the steady-state current change, and membrane capacitance from τ: Cₘ = τ × (1/Rₛ + 1/Rₘ), the same formula as pCLAMP's. If the transient is cut short (by the step's end, say), the peak comes from the fit extrapolated back to where the transient's rise reaches half its height instead.
  • Because the charge doesn't depend on the filter, in simulated cells Rₛ, Rₘ and Cₘ come out within 1% for filters of 5 kHz or wider. When τ approaches the filter's rise time (τ = 0.1 ms behind a 2 kHz filter, for example), the filter shapes the transient as much as the cell does: Rₛ can then read up to 40% high and Cₘ about 10% high. When these values matter for a fast transient, record with a wider filter.
  • The R² beside each τ in the Data Log is measured on the current itself, so it says how well the exponential follows the recorded decay.
  • With a voltage channel, each current sweep takes the step and ΔV from its own voltage sweep (paired in order). Without one, the step is detected from the current trace.
  • Rₛ, Rₘ and Cₘ come from one fit to the averaged transient of all the sweeps, as in pCLAMP's Membrane Test: averaging first leaves the fit much less noise than averaging the per-sweep values afterward. Each sweep is also fitted on its own and listed in the Data Log, to follow the cell's stability. With a single sweep, or if the averaged transient can't be fitted, the per-sweep values (or their mean) are reported.

Cₘ is the least certain of the three. Capacitive transients often have a fast component followed by a slower one (such as from imperfect space clamp in cells with extensive dendrites), which a single exponential can't separate. Use Cₘ as a relative measure, for example to track a cell's stability during an experiment, rather than a precise absolute value, especially in large or complex cells.

Extracellular AP Detection is a mode of the AP Detection tab, and LFP / Spike Train Analysis (PSTH, ISI/Burst Detection, Spike CCG) is its own inspector tab. Each has its own page in this guide.

Batch Processing

Batch Processing → Batch Process Files… runs one operation on each file you choose in a folder, and combines the results into one report. The operations are grouped as in Protocols (Modify, Signal, Measure, E-Phys), plus File Handling's Convert Format. To run a sequence of steps on an open window instead, use a Protocol (below).

Choosing Files

  • Choose Folder… lists the files in that folder (not in its subfolders; hidden files are left out). Uncheck a file to leave it out; Select All and Deselect All change them all.
  • Batch reads every format @Graph opens as a graph: recordings, saved .@graph graphs, and text tables (.csv, .txt, .tsv, .text, .rtf, read with the Graph Data dialog's default settings). A file it can't read is reported as failed, and the run goes on.
  • A file that holds several parts (streams at different rates, HEKA series, SutterPatch routines, and so on, each opening in its own graph window) is processed part by part: the report has a section for each, headed with the file's and the part's names, outputs are named with the part (cell1 (R2 VI)_scaled.abf), and the summary says how many parts each such file had.
  • Show Channels reads each file's channel names and units (each part's, for a file of several), in the units its graph shows, for the Channel fields below and for checking VI/IV roles. Choosing another folder stops it reading the old one.
  • VI/IV Plot needs each file's role: mark it Voltage, Current or Skip. Files pair up in list order (first Voltage with first Current, and so on). A file that records both itself (Vm and Im channels) is marked Both and is paired within the file, as the E-Phys menu pairs such a graph.

Operations

  • Modify: Scale / Offset, Sample Rate (Decimate, Interpolate or Resample, as the Graph Tools do them; Interpolate linear or band-limited), Integrate, Differentiate.
  • Signal: Average Sweeps (one average per channel, or per group for a saved graph; optionally with its SD, SEM or confidence interval as a band or as + and − sweeps, and only a .@graph file keeps a band), Normalize Sweeps, Align Peaks (every sample kept unless you trim the lead-in, and X = 0 at the peaks).
  • Measure: Linear Fit, Curve Fit, Exponential / Formula Fit, Peak Measurement, Cursor Measurement, Synapse Detection (Spontaneous or Evoked).
  • E-Phys: AP Detection, VI / IV Plot, Firing Properties, Membrane Test, Sag Analysis, LFP Power Spectral Density. Firing Properties searches every sweep for spikes, including those of a group hidden in a saved graph, as the E-Phys menu does. The LFP Power Spectral Density step runs in the background; Stop ends it, and the step reports nothing.
  • File Handling: Convert Format saves each file again in another format, unchanged.

Each operation shows its main settings; anything not shown is as its panel starts (settings changed in a panel aren't used). Times are in ms as each file's graph shows them (a saved graph's Set X = 0 shift applies; a file of X-Y data, whose X isn't time, is refused), and a unit can be typed with a number ("2 s", "20 kHz"). Run stays disabled, with the reason beside it, until every setting reads.

  • Batch uses the values as each file's graph shows them, in the same units (a recording stored in nA shows, and is measured, in pA when that suits its values), so thresholds and values typed in the channel's unit mean what they do in the panels. Junction potential: As each graph opens (the default) measures each file with the correction its own graph opens with (Settings' default for a recording, a saved graph's own setting), so the numbers match each graph's panel. You can also choose Not corrected, or Corrected by a value for every file. VI/IV Plot's summary says beside the resting level whether it was corrected.
  • For IV Plot, Correct the voltage axis for Rₛ is As each saved graph sets it by default: a saved graph whose Rₛ panel corrects its IV Plot uses its own Rₛ, as its IV Plot does. Measured in each file finds Rₛ from each file's test pulse, as the Rₛ panel's Measure automatically does. One value for every file uses the Rₛ you enter, and Not corrected leaves the axis as recorded. A file whose Rₛ can't be measured is reported as failed.
  • AP Detection's slope threshold starts at the dV/dt threshold set in Settings → Neurophysiology, as the AP Detection panel's does. It also has the panel's sustained-slope multiplier, rise and decay percentages and measurement checkboxes.
  • Channel fields take a channel's name as Show Channels lists it (the unit after it can be left off; an unnamed channel is "Channel 2", say). Left blank, Synapse Detection uses the first channel and the baseline pre-step corrects every channel. A file without the named channel fails with its list of channels.
  • Synapse Detection finds events going up or down (Events go); by default a current goes down (an inward EPSC) and anything else up. You set the amplitude threshold (Spontaneous, in the channel's unit) or onset threshold (Evoked: in baseline SDs, 4 by default, or with 'In baseline SDs' unchecked in the channel's unit). Everything else is as the Synapse Detection panel starts: in Spontaneous mode a channel's sweeps are joined into one recording, as the panel's Continuous mode does (sweep by sweep when their sample rates differ). Mean Frequency is the events divided by the time searched, as in the panel's time-binned summary. Evoked detection takes the pulse times (in ms as the graph's X axis shows them), the Stimulation type (which sets how long after each pulse to search) and whether each response is measured from rest or from the previous response's decay. With Charge checked, evoked charge is integrated over a fixed window after each pulse (Window, default 50 ms) unless you choose onset to return, as in the panel.
  • Spontaneous detection can use the panel's Template or Deconvolution method instead of a threshold: set the template's τrise and τdecay (ms; the direction comes from Events go) and the threshold in noise SDs, and for Deconvolution its low-pass and high-pass (Hz). Both modes offer the panel's Rise (20–80% or 10–90%), Charge and Fitted waveform; Spontaneous also adds the Overall summary (mean, SD, median and CV of each measurement), the minimum amplitude (Template and Deconvolution) and Reject overlapping events. Charge is measured only on a current channel: on any other the report leaves it out and says why.
  • Peak Measurement has the Peaks panel's Area (a current's area is reported as its charge in pC, any other channel's as an area in its unit × s) and Max rise and decay slopes, and its Noise and drift options: Peak = mean of N points, Smooth kinetics over N points, and Local baseline for each peak (its length and the gap before the peak, in ms). For First and All, it also has Separate peaks by (default 50% of the threshold), Min. spacing (ms; default 0, off) and, for an absolute threshold, 'Threshold is a distance in the peak's direction'. Its rise, width, decay and onset percentages and the Inter-event interval are there too.

Saving Results

Modify and Signal operations, LFP Power Spectral Density and Convert Format save one file per input, as .@graph (recommended), Axon Binary (.abf), AxoGraph (.axgx), MATLAB (.mat), NWB (.nwb) or HDF5 (.h5). VI/IV Plot and Firing Properties can also save each plot or F–I curve as a .@graph. Files go into the input folder unless you click Choose Output Folder….

With Axon Binary chosen, Samples picks the sample format: Match each file (the default: 16-bit for a file whose samples are still a 16-bit ABF file's own, which it keeps exactly, and 32-bit for every other), 32-bit (exact; saved as ABF 2, which pyABF opens) or 16-bit (smaller: half the size, each channel rounded to 1/65,534 of its range).

With NWB chosen, NWB Metadata… opens the export's metadata sheet once for the whole batch (see Exporting). The subject ID can be the same for every file or come from each file's name: the whole name, or the part before its first space, underscore or hyphen (A150416A 001.axgd gives A150416A). Each file keeps its channels' automatic roles and any electrode details of the NWB file it came from. Without metadata, a file read from NWB keeps its own.

  • Each output is named after its input and the operation (cell1_scaled.abf, cell1_VIplot.@graph); Convert Format keeps the input's name. Decimate with an error type saves the error as a second file (cell1_decimated_SEM.abf).
  • An existing file is never replaced, whether an input, an earlier run's output, or a file written earlier in the same run: the new file's name gets a number instead (cell1_scaled 2.abf).
  • A saved .@graph opens as its panel's result would: one group per channel, a PSD with its frequency axis, an integral or derivative with its unit.

Baseline Correction Pre-Step

For the fits, Peak and Cursor Measurement and AP Detection, check 'Baseline-correct each sweep first' to correct each sweep before measuring (Mean subtraction over a region, Linear detrend or Polynomial fit), on one channel or all. This happens in memory only; the files are not changed. Sweeps that end before the region are left uncorrected and reported. A region with Start = End uses the one sample nearest that time, as in the Baseline Correction panel, and the report names it.

Running and Results

Run processes the files in order, showing which one it is on. Batch Processing works through the files one at a time and keeps @Graph responsive while it runs, so you can keep working in other windows. Long curve fits and synaptic event detection run in the background. Stop finishes the file under way and lists the rest as not processed. The report starts with the settings used, appears in the window, is added to the Data Log, and can be saved with Save Report…. Save Table as CSV… saves its tables as one CSV file, a row per file and sweep with the file's name first, and empty cells where nothing was measured; Show Saved Files shows the new files in the Finder. Each file's section lists any warnings (a recording read only in part, a paired file whose unit doesn't match its role) and, if it failed, why.

The window remembers its settings between sessions: the operation, every option (numbers as you typed them), the save format and the folders. A folder that has moved, or is on a disk that isn't connected, is named so you can choose it again. Which files are checked and their VI/IV roles start fresh each time. Reset to Defaults puts every option back as the window first starts (the chosen folder and files stay). Settings are read when you click Run, so changing Settings during a run doesn't affect it.

For VI/IV Plot, check every file's Voltage/Current role before running. Pairing goes strictly by list order, so one wrong role pairs the wrong files.

Protocols

A Protocol (Batch Processing → Protocols…) is a named sequence of steps you build once and run on any open graph window, for example Baseline Correction → Filter → Detect Events for every new cell. Batch Process Files… runs one operation on files in a folder; a Protocol runs a list of steps on a window already open.

Building and Running a Protocol

  • Add Step offers four groups of steps (listed below). Drag steps to reorder them, or right-click a step for Edit…, Duplicate, Move Up, Move Down and Delete. ⌘Z in the Protocols window undoes changes to the list, including Load and New. The protocol you're building is kept when you close the window.
  • Pick the target window under Run, then click Run Protocol (⌘R). Each step's results appear as it finishes, in the Results panel and in the Data Log, where every table names its window. A failing step stops the run. Stop (⌘.) ends the run after the current step; a fit stops after the sweep it is fitting.
  • Changes a run makes to the picked window's data are undone with ⌘Z in that window, a few at a time when fits, Detect Events or E-Phys steps come between them.
  • Save Protocol…, Load Protocol… and New (Clear Steps), in the ⋯ menu beside the protocol name, store a Protocol as a .atgproto file for reuse or start a new one. A file missing a setting still opens, with that setting as a new step starts. A file that isn't a protocol, or holds a step this version doesn't have, is refused with a message naming the step.
  • Protocols run on sampled recordings; a spike-train raster is refused.

Steps

  • Modify: Baseline Correction, Filter, Smoothing and Scale / Offset change the window's sweeps, like the on-screen tools. Sample Rate (Decimate/Interpolate/Resample), Integrate and Differentiate open a new window with the result, as their tools do.
  • Signal: Average Sweeps (with the same Variability choice as the Average panel: none, SD, SEM or confidence interval, as a band or as + and − sweeps), Normalize Sweeps, Align Peaks. Each opens a new window with the result. As with Sample Rate, Integrate and Differentiate, an average or normalized result keeps the window's X shift, cursors and X zoom.
  • Measure: Linear Fit, Curve Fit, Exponential / Formula Fit, Peak Measurement, Cursor Measurement, Detect Events (Synapse Detection). These report text without changing the window.
  • E-Phys: AP Detection, VI / IV Plot, Firing Properties, Membrane Test, Sag Analysis, LFP Power Spectral Density. VI/IV Plot, Firing Properties (its F–I curve) and PSD also open a window with the plot; the others report text. A VI/IV Plot step corrects the IV axis for Rₛ when its window does. Firing Properties searches every sweep for spikes, whatever the window shows, as the E-Phys menu does. LFP Power Spectral Density runs in the background; Stop ends it during a file, and nothing is saved for that file.
  • Baseline Correction, Filter, Smoothing and Scale / Offset steps have a channel (all channels, or one: then only its sweeps change) and an Apply to scope (all sweeps, visible sweeps, selected sweep, selected group or all groups); most other steps have the same scope options as their on-screen tool. They check what their panel checks (a filter frequency below every sweep's Nyquist frequency, a baseline region's end not before its start; End = Start uses the one sample nearest that time) and stop with a message otherwise. Baseline Correction turns junction-potential correction off and the Y axis back to automatic, as the panel does.
  • Filter steps start at order 4, as the Filter panel does; AP Detection steps start at the dV/dt threshold set in Settings → Neurophysiology. Channels without a name are numbered from 1 in reports (Channel 1), as in Batch Processing.
  • Detect Events does spontaneous synapse detection (Threshold, Template or Deconvolution, Continuous or Episodic, optionally within a window of each sweep) on one channel and reports the event count, the mean amplitude, rise time, half-width, decay τ and inter-event interval, and the frequency (events divided by the time searched). Its threshold is in the channel's unit as the window shows it; Template and Deconvolution take theirs in noise SDs, and Deconvolution its low-pass and high-pass too. Templates are always set by their rise and decay times, so a saved protocol works on any recording. It has the panel's Rise (20–80% or 10–90%), Charge (current channels only, in pC), Fitted waveform and Overall summary options; its other settings are as the Synapse Detection panel starts.
  • Peak Measurement steps have the Peaks panel's Area (a charge in pC on a current, otherwise an area in the unit × s), Max rise and decay slopes, Noise and drift options (Peak = mean of N points, Smooth kinetics, Local baseline for each peak) and, for First and All, its separation settings (Separate peaks by, Min. spacing, 'Threshold is a distance in the peak's direction'). A local baseline is a time, so on a VI / IV plot or a spectrum the step stops with a message.
  • Cursor Measurement measures between the step's saved times; the window's cursors aren't moved.
  • Firing Properties opens the F–I curve in a new window, as the E-Phys menu command does (uncheck 'Open the F–I curve in a new window' for the table only). Later steps keep running on the step's own window.

Times and New Windows

  • A step set to Between cursors A – B needs both cursors placed in its window; otherwise the run stops with a message.
  • Times a step stores (a baseline region, a cursor-measurement or search window) are in ms as the X axis of the window the step runs on shows them, after any Set X = 0 shift. A new window from Sample Rate, Integrate, Differentiate, Average or Normalize shows its source's times, so the same times work on either; after Align Peaks, 0 is the peak. Fits, Peak Measurement and AP Detection set to Between cursors A – B use that window's cursors when the protocol runs; Integrate and Differentiate can store their own times instead (Use these times instead of cursors A and B). Measurements use the window's junction-potential correction, as the panels do.
  • A step that opens a new window (Sample Rate, Integrate, Differentiate, Average Sweeps, Normalize Sweeps, Align Peaks, VI / IV Plot, LFP Power Spectral Density) has a Later steps run on choice, beside it in the list (Then: this window or Then: new window) and in its editor. This window, the default, keeps later steps on the picked window. The new window makes the steps after it work on the result instead, for example Decimate → Filter → Detect Events on the decimated data; the list marks those steps "→ new window of step N". Decimate with error bars goes on in the decimated window.
  • A new window the run goes on in opens when the run ends, already showing what the later steps did. Those changes have no Undo there (close the window to discard them), and the picked window keeps its own data.
  • A VI / IV plot or a power spectrum isn't a recording: on one, only Linear Fit, Curve Fit, Exponential / Formula Fit and Peak Measurement can run, over the whole plot (it has no cursors). The list marks any other step after it in orange, and the run stops there with a message.

Statistics

Statistics run in two places that share the same engine, zones and wording: the Statistics window, described here, and a chart's Statistics tab, which draws the results as effect size brackets (see Charts → Effect Size Brackets).

The Statistics window opens from Text Tools → Statistics… (⌘T in any text window), from the Data Statistics button in a Spreadsheet window, or from a chart's inspector via Open Full Statistics Window. Its Analysis box offers Compare (groups or conditions, including one-sample, two-way, split-plot and whole-distribution designs), Categorical (Fisher's exact 2×2 and r×c, McNemar's 2×2, Cochran's Q, chi-square goodness-of-fit and independence, one-sample binomial), Correlation, and Regression (simple linear, multiple regression, ANCOVA and a 4-parameter dose-response fit). Each reports its own effect sizes and confidence intervals. Results are shown in fixed-width columns that match the Data Log output.

Effect Size First: How @Graph Approaches Statistics

@Graph follows the estimation ("new statistics") approach of Cumming & Calin-Jageman (2024): the estimate and its confidence interval are the main result, and the p-value is supporting context. You choose what to compare (see Compare, below), and that one choice fixes the estimate and its effect size. The test follows from them, so the p-value and the CI always answer the same question: a CI that excludes zero goes with a significant p. That is why the button that runs a comparison is called Determine Effect, and why results show p-values and effect sizes side by side with no significance stars. You decide what threshold suits your field.

There is deliberately no free choice of test. The test is the one that goes with the estimate, so its p-value and the effect size's CI answer the same question; to run a different test, choose a different Compare option.

Getting Data into Statistics

  • From the Data Log or any text editor: type or paste a tab- or comma-delimited table (one group per column, optional header row). Select the block and choose Text Tools → Statistics… or press ⌘T.
  • From a Spreadsheet: click the Data Statistics button in the toolbar. Select cells first to analyze only those.
  • Either way, @Graph works out whether the groups are in rows or columns, and asks when it isn't sure.
  • From a Chart: click Open Full Statistics Window in the Statistics tab of the inspector. This opens the full Statistics window (every analysis, including Regression) linked to the chart, with its data, its Paired/Repeated setting and its comparison settings already in place (see Linked to a Chart, below). A time-course chart opens it on the Mixed model design, with the chart's group and Values from the same columns, Time, Covariance and Spreads. Each chart has one Statistics window: clicking again brings it forward.

A Graph window can't send its data to Statistics directly. To test measurements made in a Graph, select their table in the Data Log, where they are written automatically, and press ⌘T.

Running an Analysis

The Statistics window opens on the Descriptive table and the suggested settings, but runs no test. Adjust Paired/Repeated, the analysis, the design or the columns as needed, then click Determine Effect (or press Return) to show the result in the window's output panel. Click Send to Data Log to keep a permanent record there, or Export Report… (⌘S) to save it as a text or PDF file. File → Print (⌘P) prints it. Nothing is logged otherwise.

Once a result is showing, changing any setting or input (columns, design, Compare choice, effect size, post-hoc, tail, Spreads, typed counts) re-runs it, so the result always matches the panel. The report sent to the Data Log, printed or exported is headed with the settings and columns that produced it. μ₀ may be typed with a decimal point or comma; text that isn't a number stops the run with a message instead of being read as 0.

The Zones

The left panel starts with Analysis, a four-way choice: Compare (groups or conditions), Categorical, Correlation and Regression. The DATA zone of every analysis has Descriptive table and Normality tests, which show those two summaries of the checked columns at once, each with an ⓘ; looking at one does not change the analysis that Determine Effect runs. The window opens on the Descriptive table, and returns to it when the data change.

For Compare, the panel is laid out in zones, in the same order and with the same wording as a chart's Statistics tab:

  • Paired / Repeated measures data (shown for Groups or conditions): each row is one subject measured in every column. It decides whether the paired or the independent-groups form of each comparison runs, and what the data checks look at.
  • DATA: the Groups checklist and the Descriptive table and Normality tests buttons. The Groups checklist is collapsed, with every group checked; uncheck a group to leave it out. In a window linked to a one-series chart it is the chart's own checklist, so a group unchecked here disappears from the chart too, and the reverse.
  • DATA CHARACTERISTICS: labeled checks of normality, variance, outliers and shape, and the Compare choice they suggest (see Data Checks Are Advisory, below).
  • EFFECT SIZE: Compare (Means, Trimmed means, Medians or Ranks), a line stating the estimate it reports, Spreads for independent means (May differ or Assumed equal, with Levene's result beside it for information) or the Question for paired means (Magnitude or Consistency), and the effect-size measure with a line saying what it divides by.
  • TEST: the Design picker (below); then, for 3 or more groups, the comparisons: All pairwise, or vs. Control with a Control picker. The comparisons start at All pairwise and the control at the first group. A column named like a control (baseline, control, vehicle, sham, placebo, wt, pre, 0h, t0 and similar) is only suggested ("'WT' looks like a control"), with a Use button that makes it the control; a name never changes the comparisons by itself. They sit in TEST because they decide which post-hocs fit and how many comparisons the p-values and simultaneous CIs are adjusted for. Then a "Will run: …" line naming the test that follows from the EFFECT SIZE choices; Multiple comparisons, the post-hoc picker, for Means with 3 or more groups; and Tails, for Means only. The ⓘ beside "About this test" describes the test that will run.
  • DISPLAY: Show the comparison on other scales (on by default; see Sensitivity, below).

Compare: What Is Compared Decides the Effect Size

For continuous data, Compare picks what the groups are compared on: means, trimmed means, medians or ranks. Each choice fixes the estimate and its effect size, and the test follows from them, so the p-value and the CI always answer the same question. The same four choices appear in the Statistics window and on a chart's Statistics tab, and the ⓘ beside Compare explains each.

The Four Compare Choices

  • Means: the difference in means, with a standardized effect size from the Hedges' g family. Two independent groups: Welch's t-test, or Student's t with Spreads set to Assumed equal (Welch's t loses very little power when variances are equal; Delacre, Lakens & Leys 2017), reporting the mean difference with its 95% CI (on the test's own SE and df) and Hedges' g* (g with Spreads Assumed equal, or Glass's Δ). Two paired conditions: the paired t-test, with gav or gz (see Effect Sizes); rows missing a value in either column are left out, and the result says how many. Three or more independent groups: Welch's one-way ANOVA (the classical F with equal variances assumed), then the chosen post-hoc. Three or more repeated conditions: repeated-measures ANOVA with the Greenhouse-Geisser correction, then paired t-tests. The usual choice when the data are roughly symmetric without far-out values. Only Means offers one-tailed tests.
  • Trimmed means: the difference in 20% trimmed means (the highest and lowest 20% of each group set aside), with AKP robust d (Algina, Keselman & Penfield 2005) and its bootstrap CI. Tested by Yuen's trimmed t (Yuen 1974), which like Welch's t does not assume equal variances; for paired data, Yuen's paired test on the Winsorized covariance between conditions. Three or more independent groups: Wilcox's trimmed-means ANOVA as the omnibus test, then Holm-adjusted Yuen tests. Three or more repeated conditions: no established omnibus test matches this estimate, so only the Holm-adjusted pairwise tests are reported; they control the family-wise error rate on their own. For data with outliers or long tails, which pull means and SDs around.
  • Medians: the Hodges-Lehmann estimate, in the data's own units. Independent groups: the Hodges-Lehmann shift, the median of all differences between a value in one group and a value in the other, tested by Mann-Whitney U. Paired: the median of the Walsh averages of the paired differences, tested by the Wilcoxon signed-rank test (zero differences are dropped, as in R and SciPy). p is exact up to 49 values per group (or 49 non-zero differences), ties included. The CI inverts the same test, as R does: exact when there are no ties and fewer than 50 values per group (or differences); with ties in small samples (up to 20 values in all, or 30 pairs) it inverts the same exact conditional test as the p-value, so the two always agree; otherwise it comes from the tie-corrected normal approximation. Three or more groups: Kruskal-Wallis (independent) or Friedman (paired) as the omnibus test, then Holm-adjusted pairwise tests. At very small n the rank-based CI cannot reach the requested level; the table and the chart's caption then state the level it actually reaches. For skewed data, where the mean is not a typical value. With independent groups whose spreads differ (Levene), a note warns that Mann-Whitney then rejects too often for a difference in medians and that the shift is not a pure location difference, and points to Ranks or Trimmed means.
  • Ranks: dominance δ, how much more often one group's values exceed the other's, from −1 to 1, with no assumption about the data's shape or units. Independent groups: Cliff's δ = P(A > B) − P(A < B), tested by the Brunner-Munzel test, which allows unequal spreads (Brunner & Munzel 2000; Karch 2021). With 10 or fewer values in a group its p and CI come from the studentized permutation distribution (Neubert & Brunner 2007; Pauly, Asendorf & Konietschke 2016): exact when there are at most 20,000 possible splits, otherwise 10,000 random re-splits. When every value in one group exceeds every value in the other (δ = ±1) no finite-sample CI exists and "—" is shown. Paired: the share of subjects higher in the first condition minus the share higher in the second (subjects with equal values left out), tested by the exact sign test with its exact Clopper-Pearson CI, so the CI excludes zero exactly when p is significant. Three or more groups: Kruskal-Wallis or Friedman, then Holm-adjusted pairwise tests. For ordinal scores and any data whose units don't carry meaning.
CompareEstimate (effect size)2 groups: independent / paired3+ groups: independent / paired
Meansmean difference (g* or g, d* or d, Glass's Δ; paired: gav, dav, gz, dz)Welch's t (Student's t) / paired tWelch's ANOVA (classical F) + post-hoc / repeated-measures ANOVA (Greenhouse-Geisser) + paired t-tests
Trimmed means20% trimmed-mean difference (AKP robust d)Yuen's trimmed t / Yuen's paired ttrimmed-means ANOVA + Holm / Holm-adjusted pairs only

Effect Sizes

Each Compare choice has its own effect size, chosen in the EFFECT SIZE zone:

  • Means, independent groups: a Spreads selector. May differ (the default) gives Hedges' g* or Cohen's d* (Delacre et al. 2021), standardized by the average of the two groups' variances, so the effect size makes the same assumption as Welch's t, Welch's ANOVA, Games-Howell and Dunnett's T3; the Welch's t table shows the pooled-SD g beside g* for comparison. With groups of the same size d* equals d, and g* differs from g only through the small-sample correction. With a control (two groups, or vs. Control) it also offers Glass's Δ. Assumed equal gives Hedges' g or Cohen's d, over the pooled SD, with Student's t, the classical ANOVA and its pooled-variance post-hocs.
  • Means, paired: a Question selector. Magnitude (the default) offers gav and dav, "how big was the typical change?"; Consistency offers gz and dz, "how consistently did subjects change?". The Power Analysis calculator (Lab Tools) offers the same Question for paired designs.
  • Trimmed means: AKP robust d. Medians: the Hodges-Lehmann estimate itself, in the data's units. Ranks: dominance δ itself. Each has only this one effect size.
