The effect size and the test make one assumption together, set by Spreads, so an effect size never assumes something its test doesn't. Levene's test is shown under Spreads and in DATA CHARACTERISTICS for information; it never switches them. A Levene p above 0.05 doesn't show that the spreads are equal, and choosing the test from a variance pretest distorts its error rates (Zimmerman 2004), while Welch's t loses very little power when the spreads are equal (Delacre, Lakens & Leys 2017). So an ordinary two-group comparison reports g* beside Welch's t even when the spreads look alike. With groups of the same size d* and d are the same number, and g* and g differ only through the small-sample correction (g*'s correction uses Delacre's degrees of freedom); with unequal sizes and spreads they differ more. The Welch's t table shows both. To report g, set Spreads to Assumed equal, and the test becomes Student's t. Choose that only when the spreads are known to match, from the design or earlier data.

Normality and skew have no switch of their own; Compare is their counterpart. Normality mostly changes how accurate a Means CI is at small n, not what is worth estimating. Skew and far-out values do change that, so DATA CHARACTERISTICS suggests Medians or Trimmed means when they are present, and Use applies the suggestion. Compare stays on Means until you choose: the suggestion is never applied automatically, for the same reason as Levene's test (Rochon, Gondan & Kieser 2012). It is only a suggestion, based on the data's shape, and may not suit every analysis: choose the comparison from your study design.

The two paired questions can give different answers from the same data: did nearly every subject move the same way, even by a little, or how big was the typical change? A consistent but modest change can give a large dz beside a small dav, or vice versa. Neither is more correct; the Question says which one the number answers, and the effect-size menu and figure caption name it.

Before trusting a standardized effect size (d, g, dav, gav), ask whether the SD describes the spread well. For roughly symmetric data without extreme points it does. If the data are skewed, or one or two extreme points set the SD, these effect sizes can swing on those points; Trimmed means (AKP robust d) or Medians (Hodges-Lehmann) then answer the magnitude question without being dominated by them. Result tables report p-values and effect sizes only; @Graph never adds significance stars.

Standardized mean differences (Means)
  • Cohen's d (independent groups, equal variances assumed): mean difference / pooled SD. Small = 0.2, medium = 0.5, large = 0.8. Uncorrected for small-sample bias; selectable at any n, but not the default.
  • Hedges' g (independent groups, equal variances assumed): bias-corrected d, and the default. The correction is largest for small samples and fades smoothly toward Cohen's d as n grows (negligible above about n = 20), so it is never worse. Choose Cohen's d only to match a journal or field convention.
  • Hedges' g* and Cohen's d*, read "g star" and "d star" (independent groups, spreads may differ; Delacre et al. 2021): the mean difference over the average of the two groups' variances, √((s₁² + s₂²)/2), instead of a pooled SD. The pooled SD weights each group's variance by its size, so when the spreads differ, d depends on which group happened to be larger; d* weights the two groups equally and describes the populations alone. Used whenever Spreads is May differ, the default. g* corrects d* for small-sample bias on Delacre's degrees of freedom.
  • Glass's Δ (independent groups with a control, spreads may differ; Glass 1976): the mean difference over the control group's SD alone, with the small-sample correction on the control's n − 1 degrees of freedom (Hedges 1981). For a treatment expected to change the spread as well as the mean: the difference is then in units of the untreated variability. Offered with Spreads May differ, for two groups (the Control picker beside it chooses which) or each group vs. a control. Its CI is a noncentral t on the control's degrees of freedom with the sample variance ratio plugged in, as R's effectsize computes it (exact given that ratio; Hedges & Olkin 1985).
  • Cohen's dz (paired): mean difference / SD of the differences. Uncorrected; selectable, but not the default. It is not the textbook "drm" (which divides by a single measurement's SD), and the two agree only when the pre/post correlation is 0.5. Because the SD of the differences shrinks as within-subject correlation rises, a small but consistent change can give a larger dz than a bigger, less consistent one. That is why it answers the consistency question rather than the magnitude one.
  • Hedges' gz (paired): bias-corrected dz, and the default when the Question is Consistency. As with Hedges' g, the correction matters most at small n.
  • Cohen's dav (paired): mean difference / the average of the two conditions' SDs, (SD₁+SD₂)/2 (Lakens 2013). It ignores the correlation between conditions, so it isn't inflated or deflated by consistency, and it is directly comparable to an independent-groups Cohen's d. Unlike dz (which equals the paired t-test's t/√n), it has no direct link to the test's p-value. Uncorrected; selectable, but not the default.
  • Hedges' gav (paired): bias-corrected dav, the default for paired means (Lakens 2013; R's effectsize package), with the correction taken at correlation-adjusted degrees of freedom (Fitts 2025). Its 95% CI, like dav's, is a bootstrap (BCa, bias-corrected and accelerated, by default), following Fitts (2025): no validated closed-form interval exists for this denominator in a paired design.
Trimmed means, medians and ranks
  • AKP robust d (Trimmed means; Algina, Keselman & Penfield 2005): the 20% trimmed-mean difference divided by a 20% Winsorized SD, times 0.642 so it matches Cohen's d under normality (the usual small/medium/large guide applies). For paired data the denominator is the two conditions' pooled Winsorized SD, √((s²_w1 + s²_w2)/2), so it is a magnitude like gav, not the SD of the differences. Its 95% CI is always a bootstrap (10,000 resamples, BCa by default, following the Bootstrap CI method setting); the trimmed-mean difference itself gets Yuen's CI.
  • Hodges-Lehmann estimate (Medians): the median of all between-group differences (independent) or of the Walsh averages of the paired differences (paired), in the data's own units. It is the rank-based counterpart of the mean difference and needs no assumption about the SD. Its CI is the standard distribution-free interval (Hollander, Wolfe & Chicken 2014; the same interval R reports), which inverts the test. Its ends are the limits of the shifts the test does not reject, and an end can itself be rejected: a CI that ends exactly at 0 beside a significant p has 0 just outside, and the table says so. For independent groups it is a location shift only when the two groups have the same shape; otherwise read it as the typical difference. Use it when the actual size ("the typical difference was 4.2 mV") matters.
  • Dominance δ (Ranks): P(A > B) − P(A < B) for independent groups (Cliff's δ; Cliff 1993; equal to 2P − 1 for Brunner-Munzel's P), or, for paired data, the share of subjects higher in the first condition minus the share higher in the second. 0 means neither group tends to be higher; ±1 means every value in one exceeds every value in the other. Its CI comes from the same test as p (Brunner-Munzel, or the sign test's exact Clopper-Pearson interval).
Overall and other effect sizes

The other designs and analyses report these effect sizes (alternatives in parentheses). Overall effect sizes (η², Kendall's W and the like) describe the whole test; the others describe each comparison.

AnalysisDesignEffect size
One-sample t, trimmed t, Wilcoxon or sign test (by Compare)1 column vs. μ₀g (d); AKP robust d; Hodges-Lehmann difference from μ₀; δ = P(X > μ₀) − P(X < μ₀)
Welch's or classical one-way ANOVA3+ independent groups, Meansη², ω², Cohen's f
Repeated-measures ANOVA3+ repeated conditions, Meanspartial and generalized η², Cohen's f
Two-way ANOVAtwo crossed factorspartial η² and Cohen's f for each effect; each post-hoc pair: d
Split-plot (mixed) ANOVAone between-, one within-subjects factorpartial and generalized η², Cohen's f
Linear mixed modelgroups × repeated time points, missing values allowedmodel-based means and differences; marginal and conditional R²
Kolmogorov-Smirnov2 independent groups, whole distributionsD (0 to 1)
Fisher's exact2×2, independentln(odds ratio) (odds ratio, relative risk)
McNemar's2×2, pairedΔp, the difference in proportions (paired odds ratio, ln(odds ratio), Cohen's g)
Cochran's Q; χ² independence; χ² goodness-of-fitbinary or categoricalKendall's W; Cramér's V and its bias-corrected form (Bergsma 2013); Cohen's w
One-sample binomial1 binary column vs. p₀observed proportion − p₀ (Clopper-Pearson exact CI)
Correlationone X, one YPearson r (Spearman ρ, Kendall's τ-b)
Multiple Regressionone Y, 2+ predictorsR² and Cohen's f
ANCOVAgroups adjusted for covariatespartial η² and Cohen's f; each adjusted pair: d
  • η² / partial η², ω² and Cohen's f (one-way, RM, two-way and split-plot ANOVA; ANCOVA's factor): each CI comes from the noncentral F distribution (Steiger 2004; Smithson 2001), as in R's MBESS package (see “Why these intervals use λ/(λ + N)” below), at 1 − 2α: 90% when the confidence level is 95%. At that level the CI excludes zero exactly when the F test is significant at α (Steiger 2004).
  • ω² (one-way ANOVA): the less biased estimate of the population share of variance (η² describes the sample and runs high at small n). It shares η²'s noncentral-F CI, since both estimate the same population quantity.
  • Generalized η² (RM and split-plot ANOVA; Olejnik & Algina 2003; Bakeman 2005): keeps between-subject variability in the denominator, so it can be compared with η² from a between-subjects study of the same effect. Partial η² from a within-subjects design is larger and cannot be compared that way. Shown without a CI (there is no exact one).
  • Odds ratios (Fisher's Exact, McNemar's): conditional maximum-likelihood estimates with exact intervals (see Categorical Tests, below); 0 and ∞ are legitimate values when a cell is empty, and are printed as such.
  • Kendall's W (Cochran's Q), Cramér's V (Chi-square independence) and Cohen's w (goodness-of-fit; 0.1/0.3/0.5 small/medium/large): 95% CI by bootstrap (10,000 resamples keeping each test's paired or independent structure, seeded from the data so unchanged data give the identical interval), using the Bootstrap CI method setting.
  • Pearson r (Correlation): 95% CI via the Fisher z-transform. Spearman ρ and Kendall's τ-b each get a bootstrap 95% CI (resampling the (x,y) pairs together), using the Bootstrap CI method setting.
  • D (Kolmogorov-Smirnov): the largest gap between the two cumulative distributions, from 0 (identical) to 1 (no overlap).
Direction and "Always show effect sizes as positive"

Effect sizes are signed: negative when the first-named group or condition is lower. On a time course with a control group (one you chose, or one named like a control, such as Control, Vehicle or WT), a bracket with the control reads as the other group minus the control (Treatment A − Control), so each treatment's effect has its own sign; brackets between two other groups keep the first minus the second. With Settings → Statistics → Always show effect sizes as positive turned on, a result-table row whose estimate is negative is restated the other way round: the group names are swapped and the estimate, its CI, the test statistic and the effect size are all given for the reversed order (a one-tailed test is restated for the same hypothesis), never just the sign of the effect size flipped. In a one-tailed post-hoc table of all pairs, where the row order is the hypothesis (first-named group > second), rows keep their tested order, and the table says so. A chart bracket joins two groups without a direction, so its effect size and CI are shown for the order that makes them positive. An Estimation Plot draws each difference as later minus earlier group, so its labels stay signed as plotted whatever this setting says. η², ω², Cramér's V and Kendall's W are never changed.

Confidence Intervals

Settings → Statistics → Default confidence level (90%, 95% or 99%; 95% unless changed) applies to every CI. The variance-explained effect sizes (η², partial η², ω², Cohen's f and R²) get a 1 − 2α interval instead, 90% at the current level, the level at which the interval excludes zero exactly when the F test is significant (Steiger 2004). With 3 or more groups, pairwise CIs are simultaneous (see Multiple Comparisons, below). Its α (1 − the level) is also the threshold of the verdicts the tables state: Mauchly's test, the interaction verdicts of the two-way and split-plot ANOVA, and Levene's test. Changing this setting, Always show effect sizes as positive or the bootstrap settings re-runs a result already shown in the Statistics window.

Why these intervals use λ/(λ + N): every CI for η², partial η², ω² and Cohen's f is an interval for the population share of variance the effect explains, the same quantity a power analysis plans with (Cohen 1988; G*Power). It is found as Steiger (2004) describes: the noncentral-F interval for the noncentrality λ, converted by λ/(λ + N). N is the number of observations the effect is estimated from: all values for a between-subjects effect, subjects × conditions for a within-subjects effect, and the subjects for a split-plot's between-subjects effect.

  • R's MBESS package computes the same interval (Kelley 2007), so any of them can be reproduced there.
  • R's effectsize package and JASP convert by λ/(λ + df_error) instead, which carries the bounds through the formula for the sample η². Their intervals come out higher and wider, most in repeated-measures designs, so the two are not interchangeable.
  • The sample η² overestimates the population share at small n, so it can sit near or above the upper end of its interval.
  • Multiple regression's R² is the exception: its predictors are usually measured on the sampled subjects rather than set by design, so its CI treats them as random and inverts Fisher's (1928) exact distribution of R² under multivariate normality. R's MBESS (ci.R2, by default) approximates that same interval (Lee 1971) and agrees to about three decimals. With predictor values fixed by design (doses, timepoints) the noncentral-F interval applies instead; it is narrower at the top.

Confidence intervals for d and g (and the paired dz and gz, and Welch's d*/g*, which invert Welch's t on Delacre's df, as the R package deffectsize does; Glass's Δ's CI is described with Glass's Δ, above) come from inverting the noncentral t distribution (Steiger & Fouladi 1997; Cumming & Finch 2001), as in R's effectsize and MBESS packages: exact when the data are normal, and usually asymmetric around the estimate, so they are always shown as a range, never ± a margin. For g the bounds carry the same small-sample correction as the estimate. The Hodges-Lehmann CI inverts the Mann-Whitney or signed-rank test, and δ's CI comes from the Brunner-Munzel test or the exact sign test, so each normally agrees with its own p-value (with tied values the Hodges-Lehmann CI is approximate). Odds ratios get exact intervals (see Categorical Tests, below).

Which bootstrap?

Settings → Statistics → Bootstrap CI method offers BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated), the recommended default, or Percentile. It applies to every interval that is bootstrapped because no formula fits: AKP robust d; dav and gav; the Estimation Plot's intervals for means and dominance δ; Kendall's W (Cochran's Q), Cramér's V and Cohen's w; and Spearman ρ and Kendall's τ-b. Test-based intervals (t, noncentral t and F, Yuen, rank inversion, Clopper-Pearson) are never bootstrapped. A plain percentile interval is too narrow at small n; BCa corrects for bias and skew using the same 10,000 resamples (Efron & Tibshirani 1993, ch. 14). Choose Percentile only to reproduce a number computed with it. The Estimation Plot's medians and trimmed means always use the smoothed bootstrap's percentile interval. Every bootstrap is seeded from the data, so re-running an unchanged analysis gives the identical interval. The setting is global and applies the next time an analysis runs. Each bootstrap CI row names the method used, such as "(BCa bootstrap)", and a chart bracket draws a bootstrap CI in curly braces. Warn on small-sample bootstrap CIs (Settings → Statistics; below n = 5 by default) adds a note to a result table whose bootstrap CI rests on fewer values per group or pair than that.

Test: The Test Goes with the Estimate

The TEST zone sets how the comparison is tested: the design, which comparisons are made with 3 or more groups, the post-hoc and the tails. The test itself follows from the Compare choice and is named on the "Will run" line.

Design

The Design picker, at the top of the TEST zone, says which family of tests the comparison uses; what a design needs from the data (μ₀, the A×B layout, the group column) is set in DATA:

  • Groups or conditions (the default): 2 or more columns, one per group, or one per condition with Paired / Repeated on. The Compare choice decides the analysis (see Compare, above).
  • One sample vs. a value: one column against a hypothesized value μ₀, typed in the panel. Means runs the one-sample t-test with Hedges' g (or Cohen's d); Trimmed means the one-sample trimmed t with AKP robust d (Algina, Keselman & Penfield) and its bootstrap CI; Medians the Wilcoxon signed-rank test with the Hodges-Lehmann pseudo-median minus μ₀; Ranks the exact sign test with δ = P(X > μ₀) − P(X < μ₀) and its exact (Clopper-Pearson) CI.
  • Two-way (factorial): two crossed between-subjects factors, compared on means (see Two-Way ANOVA, below), with its own post-hoc picker. Set the number of levels of each factor; the columns must be ordered A1B1, A1B2, …, A2B1, … (B changes fastest). If the data are marked paired, a warning says that a two-way ANOVA treats every value as a different subject and points to Split-plot instead.
  • Split-plot (mixed): one between-subjects factor, a column of group codes (0, 1, 2, …) picked separately, and the other checked columns as the repeated conditions (see ANOVA Tables, below). The picker starts from the first column that looks like group codes. Rows missing any value are left out, and a note gives the count. The data checks and the Descriptive and Normality tables look at each group × condition cell, and the Variance line tests the groups' spreads at each condition (Levene), the homogeneity the ANOVA assumes; sphericity is tested with the result.
  • Mixed model (repeated measures): the split-plot layout (a group column, codes or labels, and one column per time point, one row per subject) fitted as a linear mixed model. Subjects with missing time points are kept. TEST then offers Time (Time as categories (any shape), the default, or a Linear, Quadratic or Cubic trend) and Covariance (Unstructured (MMRM), the default; Random intercept; Random intercept + slope; or Random intercept + AR(1)); see Linear Mixed Model, below.
  • Whole distributions (KS): the two-sample Kolmogorov-Smirnov test on exactly 2 columns. It compares the whole distributions, not their means or medians. D, the largest gap between the two groups' cumulative distributions (0 to 1), is both the statistic and the effect size. The p-value is exact (as in the exact mode of SciPy and R) unless the two group sizes multiply to more than 10 million. A significant D says the distributions differ, not how; use Groups or conditions for a difference in location with its CI.

The Compare choice applies to Groups or conditions and to One sample. Two-way, Split-plot and KS have one analysis each, and their EFFECT SIZE zone says what they report.

Multiple Comparisons (3 or More Groups)

Choose All pairwise or vs. Control at the top of the TEST zone (after Design in the Statistics window). vs. Control compares each group only with the control: a smaller family, and so more power.

Trimmed means, Medians and Ranks: the pairwise tests are Holm-adjusted, and each CI is the Bonferroni simultaneous interval at level 1 − α/m (m comparisons), the interval that goes with Holm, so a CI that excludes zero always has a significant Holm p. Paired designs use each pair's own complete subjects, as R's pairwise tests do, so a pair's n can exceed the omnibus test's.

Means: pick the method under Multiple comparisons in the TEST zone. Only the methods that fit the design and the variance assumption are listed, the recommended one first and marked "(recommended)"; the ⓘ beside the picker describes the selected method.

  • Independent groups, Spreads: May differ (the default): Games-Howell (recommended) or Dunnett's T3 for all pairs; Dunnett's T3 (recommended) or Games-Howell for vs. Control. With small groups (fewer than about 50 each) prefer T3 for all pairs too: Games-Howell can then reject slightly too often, while T3 holds the error rate. The omnibus test beside them is Welch's ANOVA.
  • Independent groups, Spreads: Assumed equal: Tukey HSD for all pairs, or Dunnett for vs. Control (each the recommended one for its comparisons), or Holm-Bonferroni, Šidák or Bonferroni on the pooled variance. The omnibus test beside it is the classical F.
  • Repeated measures: Holm-Bonferroni (recommended), Šidák or Bonferroni, applied to paired t-tests, because Tukey, Games-Howell and Dunnett assume independent groups. Each comparison uses the subjects observed in both of its conditions; the omnibus test uses the subjects observed in all of them.
  • Tukey HSD: every pair, handling unequal group sizes (Tukey-Kramer) with the exact studentized-range distribution. Games-Howell: Tukey's unequal-variance counterpart, where each pair uses its own variances and degrees of freedom.
  • Dunnett's T3 (Dunnett 1980): each pair's Welch t-test at its own degrees of freedom, referred to the studentized maximum modulus distribution for the whole family (all pairs, or each group against the control), as in SPSS, GraphPad Prism and R's PMCMRplus. It does not assume equal variances, and it is never less powerful than the Šidák-corrected Welch t-tests (Tamhane's T2).
  • Dunnett (vs control): every group against one control, using the exact joint distribution of those comparisons. Its unequal-variance counterpart is Dunnett's T3 against the control.
  • Šidák / Bonferroni / Holm-Bonferroni: general-purpose corrections. Holm-Bonferroni is always at least as powerful as Bonferroni with the same protection.

Each Means post-hoc row gives the mean difference with a CI adjusted like its p-value: simultaneous for Tukey HSD, Games-Howell, Dunnett, Dunnett's T3, Šidák and Bonferroni. Holm has no exactly matching interval, so it gets the Bonferroni simultaneous CI that goes with it: if the CI excludes zero the Holm p is significant, though a Holm p just under the threshold can sit beside a CI that still includes zero. The effect sizes' CIs are simultaneous across the family (Bonferroni, 1 − α/m), as the chart's brackets show them.

One- and Two-Tailed Tests

Only Means has a Tails picker: for the independent, paired and one-sample t-tests, and for the post-hocs with a one-sided form (Dunnett, Dunnett's T3, Holm-Bonferroni, Šidák and Bonferroni). Tukey HSD and Games-Howell have no one-tailed form, and the Trimmed means, Medians and Ranks tests are two-sided. Two-tailed is the default; a one-tailed choice (first > second, or first < second; against a control it reads as each group vs. the control) tests only that direction and shows a warning. Wherever the picker is hidden the test runs two-tailed, whatever was chosen before. With a one-tailed t-test the effect size's CI is one-sided too (the bound in the tested direction, as R's effectsize reports), so it excludes zero exactly when the one-tailed p is significant; in post-hoc tables the mean-difference CIs become one-sided and the effect-size CIs stay two-sided.

ANOVA Tables

  • One-way ANOVA (Means, 3+ independent groups): Welch's F, or the classical F with Spreads Assumed equal, with its p, η² (the sample's share of variance), ω² (the less biased population estimate; it can be slightly negative when F < 1, as SPSS also reports) and Cohen's f, the CIs of ω² and f at 90% (see Effect Sizes), then the post-hoc table.
  • Repeated Measures (RM) ANOVA (Means, 3+ repeated conditions): F, partial η² and Cohen's f with 90% CIs, and generalized η², which unlike partial η² can be compared with results from other designs (Olejnik & Algina 2003; Bakeman 2005). Includes Mauchly's sphericity test with Greenhouse-Geisser and Huynh-Feldt corrections (see below); the Greenhouse-Geisser p is the reported result. Each post-hoc row gives the mean difference with a CI adjusted like its p-value.
  • Split-plot ANOVA (Mixed) (Design: Split-plot; one between-subjects factor × 2+ repeated conditions): for example, drug vs. placebo, each measured at several time points. Reports F, p, partial η² and Cohen's f (with 90% CIs, noncentral F) for the between-subjects effect, the within-subjects effect and their interaction, plus generalized η² for each, with the same sphericity handling as RM ANOVA. Sphericity is tested on the covariance pooled within the between-subjects groups, as SPSS and R's car package do, with Lecoutre's Huynh-Feldt correction. The group column is never used as a within-subjects condition, even if it is checked. See Two-Way ANOVA below for how this differs from two independent factors.

Linear Mixed Model

Design: Mixed model (repeated measures). For subjects in 2 or more groups, each measured at several time points: neurons of different subtypes recorded at 10 time points, say. The question "do the groups' time courses differ in shape" is the Group × Time interaction.

  • Data: as for split-plot, a group column of codes or labels (picked in DATA) and the time points as the other checked columns, one row per subject. A subject missing some time points is kept, and every value it has counts (split-plot leaves the whole row out). Times come from the column names when each holds a distinct number ("0", "5 min", "Day 14"), else 1, 2, 3… A time point where every subject has the same value, such as the baseline each time course was normalized to (all 1, or 100%), is left out with a note: it holds no information about the groups, and a point with no variance would distort the covariance estimates and add comparisons that are 0 by construction.
  • Time: as categories, one mean per group and time point, with no assumption about the shape; or as a linear, quadratic or cubic trend (orthogonal polynomials over the time values), with fewer parameters and so more power when the time courses are smooth curves. With a trend, the interaction compares the groups' curve coefficients beyond the level.
  • Covariance: Unstructured (MMRM), the default (the MMRM of clinical trials: each time point has its own variance and each pair of time points its own correlation, so the tests hold whatever the true pattern of correlation). It needs at least as many subjects as time points plus one per group, at most 30 time points, and every pair of time points measured together in at least 2 subjects. When it can't be estimated, the structure with the lowest AIC among the other three is used, and a note in the result says why; random intercept (each subject has its own level; on complete data this reproduces split-plot ANOVA without the sphericity correction); random intercept + slope (each subject also has its own linear trend over time); random intercept + AR(1) (residuals correlated over time, ρ^(lag) for times lag spacings apart, so neighboring times correlate more than distant ones). The simpler structures assume a pattern; when it doesn't fit, their tests can be too liberal. The result shows AIC for all four (lower is better).
  • Spreads: Assumed equal (the default: one covariance for every group) or May differ, the time-course counterpart of Welch's test. May differ gives each group its own residual SD with the random-intercept structures (nlme's varIdent by group; the subject-level variances shared), or its own unstructured covariance with Unstructured (mmrm's us(time | group / subject)), which needs at least one more subject than time points in every group, else the lowest-AIC other structure is used with each group's own residual SD. Choose it from the design: a treatment expected to change the variability, or groups of very different sizes. The result lists each group's SDs and gives the AIC with the other choice. Checked against nlme and mmrm.
  • Fitting: REML, as R's mmrm and nlme (unstructured), lme4 (random intercept, + slope) and nlme (AR(1)) fit it. Tests: Type III F for the group, time and group × time effects, with Satterthwaite's denominator df, as R's lmerTest and mmrm report them; checked to match lmerTest and nlme on balanced, unbalanced and incomplete data.
  • With 3 or more groups, a table tests each pair's difference in shape within the one model, Holm-adjusted.
  • Rate of change: each group's slope over time (the least-squares line through its model-based means, in the data's units per time unit) with its CI, and each pair's difference in slope with a simultaneous CI and adjusted p; with 3 or more groups, a test that all slopes are equal. When one group rises or falls faster than another, this is the effect size to report beside the interaction, and a more sensitive test of a steady divergence than any single time point.
  • Each subject's slope: at the end of the table, every subject's own least-squares slope over the time points (by row, with its group and, if used, its cluster), and each group's mean and SD of them. Use these to plot or export one rate per subject (a neuron, say): Send to Data Log, select the rows, then Text Tools › Send to Spreadsheet…. They are descriptive; the group slopes above are the model's estimates. In a spreadsheet, =SLOPE(values, times) gives the same number for one row.
  • Then: the model-based mean of each group at each time with its 95% CI, and the group differences at each time point (as R's emmeans, Satterthwaite df). All time points and pairs form one family, with simultaneous CIs and adjusted p-values that match them: a difference is significant exactly when its CI excludes 0.
  • The adjustment: single-step, from the multivariate t distribution of the comparisons (R's emmeans "mvt", multcomp), which accounts for how they correlate and so is less conservative than Bonferroni with the same protection. It is integrated numerically (Genz & Bretz 2009) with a fixed seed, so a re-run gives the same p, accurate to about the 4th decimal. When that integral can't be set up, the table says the adjustment is Bonferroni instead.
  • Standardized differences: Hedges' g* and Cohen's d* divide each difference by the root mean square of the two groups' SDs of a single value at that time point, √((S₁² + S₂²)/2), every level's variance included, as for independent groups (Delacre et al. 2021; Hedges 2007; Westfall, Kenny & Judd 2014). With Spreads Assumed equal the groups share one covariance and the two SDs are one. g* adds Hedges' exact small-sample correction at the df of the SD it divides by, ν = S²/(2·Var S) (Hedges 2007). Their CIs are approximate (delta method), simultaneous like the differences', and a little wider. Checked against R (mmrm, lme4 and nlme fits, with the delta method from the REML Hessian).
  • The fit: the covariance estimates (for Unstructured, each time point's SD and the correlations between time points; otherwise the between-subject SD, slope SD and correlation, AR(1) ρ and residual SD), the between-source SD when a Values from the same column is chosen, a singular-fit note when a variance is estimated at 0, the REML criterion, AIC, BIC and marginal R², plus conditional R² for the random-effect structures (Nakagawa & Schielzeth 2013; Johnson 2014).
  • Data checks: normality and outliers in each group × time cell, using every value, and Levene's test across the groups at each time point.
  • Also: each pair of groups' difference averaged over the time points (the difference in level, with its CI; with 3 or more groups, simultaneous CIs and adjusted p, as above), the natural follow-up when two groups share a shape; and, when another covariance structure fits clearly better by AIC, a line naming it.
  • Finding it: a table whose checked columns include one of groups, as codes (0, 1, 2…) or labels that repeat (WT, KO; shown as written, in the order they first appear) beside 2 or more columns of measurements gets an offer in DATA to analyze it as a Mixed model, with a Use button. Changing the Design clears the result until Determine Effect, as changing the Analysis does; changes within a design update a result already shown.
  • Nested data (Values from the same): when several subjects come from the same source (a batch, plot, classroom, site, plate, litter or animal), choose the column naming each subject's source under Values from the same in DATA (a text column is offered too, and in a window opened from a chart, a column the chart took as row labels); it is None by default, and the layout is otherwise unchanged. The labels can be text ("M1", "M2") or numbers. The model then adds a random intercept for each source above the subjects' own, as R's lme4 fits (1 | source) + (1 | subject). Effects that differ between sources (the groups, when each source gives one group) are then tested against the spread between sources, with degrees of freedom near the number of sources, not the number of subjects. The result adds the between-source SD and the number of sources, and notes when that variance is estimated at 0 (the results then equal those without the column). A column named like Animal, Mouse, Slice, Litter, Subject, Classroom, Plate or Site whose labels repeat is offered with a link (Use “Mouse”). Checked against lmerTest and nlme with sources crossed with and nested in the groups.
  • One value per row (clustered data): when each row is one measurement (a sample, a plant, a student, a cell) and rows come from shared sources (batches, plots, classrooms, animals), check just the value column, pick the group column (or None for one group) and, under Values from the same, the column naming each row's source. A table laid out this way (a value column, a group column, codes or labels, and a column named like Animal, Subject, Classroom, Plate or Site whose labels repeat) is recognized in the Groups and One sample designs, which offer to switch.
  • The model (one value per row): value ~ group + (1 | source), as lme4's lmer fits it, with Satterthwaite CIs as lmerTest reports them. Spreads (May differ, the default, or Assumed equal) sets whether each group has its own residual SD (nlme's varIdent, the counterpart of Welch's test). A second level inside the first, and repeated measures in a long table, work as under Values from the Same in Charts. Checked against lme4, lmerTest, nlme and a dense REML fit in R.
  • The result (one value per row): each group's mean and the differences with CIs; the between- and within-source SDs; the ICC, the share of the variation that lies between sources (0 when rows from one source are no more alike than any two rows); n rows and n sources for each group; and the same comparison with the rows treated as independent, with the ratio of the interval widths. When spreads may differ, each group's residual and total SD are listed, and the rows-treated-as-independent comparison is Welch's.
  • Sources and groups (one value per row): when sources span the groups (two cell types recorded in each animal), the comparison is made largely within each source, and its interval can be narrower than when the sources are ignored. When each source is in one group only, the comparison rests on the sources more than on the rows.
  • Standardized differences (one value per row): Hedges' g (Cohen's d with Hedges' exact small-sample correction at the df of the SD it divides by, ν = S²/(2·Var S), as Hedges (2007) corrects dT) and Cohen's d. d is the difference divided by the two groups' total SDs, each the SD of a single value with every level's variance included (Hedges 2007; Westfall, Kenny & Judd 2014): their average for groups measured within the same sources (dav; Lakens 2013), their root mean square for groups from different sources (d*; Delacre et al. 2021). The CIs are approximate (delta method).
  • With the Split-plot or Mixed model design, the chart is a time course of each group: the time points across, one color per group, each group's means ± error bars joined over time, and each subject's own line faint behind them. In a window opened from a chart of the same table, choosing either design (or Use on the suggestion) turns that chart into the time course, and choosing another design turns it back (Undo in the chart works too). Otherwise Make Chart opens the time-course chart and links the window to it. Either way the chart, its spreadsheet and the window are one analysis: the data, the group and Values from the same columns, the time points, Time, Covariance and Spreads are shared both ways, and the chart's brackets come from the same model. (A window linked to a chart of a different table opens a separate time-course chart, which follows the window.) Subjects and Means, in the chart's Plot Style panel, turns the subject lines, the means and the data points on or off. Paired / Repeated shows checked and locked: each row is one subject followed over time, and the groups are different subjects. Time Course by Group, in the chart's Plot Style panel, makes the same chart from the table (pick the group column and an optional Values from the same column), and Back to One Column per Category undoes it.
  • Brackets on the time-course chart (Statistics tab → Compute Brackets) come from the mixed model, not from a test at each time point: the Statistics tab's MIXED MODEL zone replaces Compare and offers the model's Time, Covariance, Spreads and Values from the same.
  • Each bracket joins two groups at one time point and shows an effect size for the difference in their model-based means (first group minus second), with its simultaneous CI and its p, adjusted over the whole family: the numbers of the window's per-time table. Effect size chooses Hedges' g* (the default), Cohen's d* (the groups are independent, so their SDs are combined as a root mean square), or Δ in the data's units. Δ's CI excludes 0 exactly when p is below α; g*'s and d*'s are a little wider.
  • The bracket list in the Statistics tab names each comparison in full ("Hit 8: Subtype 0 vs Subtype 2") and lists them by time point, left to right. Every time point's brackets are drawn at first; the Show menu above the list narrows them, and the list turns each on or off.
  • Rate of change on the time-course chart: under Header (BRACKETS › Display), Rate of change (difference in slope) adds each pair's difference in slope, with its CI and p, below the test name. Brackets, in the MIXED MODEL zone, switches to Rate of change: one bracket per pair of groups spanning the time points of Slope over … to … (every time point at first), labeled with the difference in their slopes (least-squares slopes through the model-based means), its CI and its p adjusted over the pairs. Show each group's fitted line draws the lines compared.
  • A bracket's ends are spread across its time point so it stays readable while the groups' markers nearly overlap, and brackets at neighboring time points alternate in height so their labels don't collide. They are drawn in the SVG, PDF and PNG exports, and the methods caption describes the model.
  • One chart per window: while the time-course chart made from a Statistics window is open, that window's button reads Show Chart, and the chart follows the window: changing the data, the group or source column, Time or Covariance there updates the same chart (keeping its colors and settings) and recomputes brackets already shown. Close the chart to make a fresh one.
  • The AI Assistant fits the same model from a table you give it, can open the time-course chart, and can change a time-course chart's Time, Covariance and Values from the same (⌘Z undoes it).

Repeated-Measures ANOVA and Sphericity

Sphericity (equal variances of all pairwise differences between conditions) is an assumption of RM ANOVA. Violation makes the F-test anti-conservative (p too small). @Graph tests sphericity with Mauchly's W, shows the corrected p-values, and reports the Greenhouse-Geisser p as the result.

  • Mauchly's W: from 0 (severe violation) to 1 (perfect sphericity), matching R. p ≥ α = sphericity not rejected, where α is 1 − the confidence level in Settings (0.05 at 95%); the same α decides the two-way and split-plot interaction verdicts and the Levene line. With unequal group sizes the within-subjects effect uses Type III sums of squares (the groups weighted equally), as SPSS and afex report.
  • Greenhouse-Geisser correction (εGG): multiplies both numerator and denominator df by ε. ε = 1 means no correction needed; ε = 1/(k-1) is the most conservative possible (lower bound).
  • Huynh-Feldt correction (εHF): less conservative than Greenhouse-Geisser, and some prefer it when εGG > 0.75. The table shows both. In Split-plot ANOVA both ε are computed from the covariance pooled within the between-subjects groups (as SPSS and R's car package do), with Lecoutre's correction to Huynh-Feldt.
  • Which p to report: the Greenhouse-Geisser-corrected p, whatever Mauchly's test says, as @Graph does (Maxwell & Delaney 2004). Mauchly's test has little power at the sample sizes RM designs usually have, so a non-significant result is weak evidence that sphericity holds, and when it does hold εGG is close to 1 and the correction costs almost nothing.

Two-Way ANOVA

Design: Two-way (factorial). Tests two independent factors (such as genotype × treatment) and their interaction on means, reporting each effect's partial η² and Cohen's f with 90% CIs (noncentral F) alongside the omnibus table. @Graph detects whether cells have equal replicate counts (balanced) or not (unbalanced) and chooses the sums of squares accordingly. The table's note says which was used.

  • Balanced design (equal n per cell): the classical sums of squares (Types I, II and III all agree here).
  • Unbalanced design (unequal n per cell, every cell with at least one value): Type III sums of squares with sum-to-zero contrasts, as SPSS and R's afex report. Each effect is adjusted for every other term, and the main effects are about the equal-weight (unweighted) marginal means, the same estimated marginal means the post-hoc comparisons test. A design with an empty cell is not supported.
  • Post-hoc: Tukey HSD (the default), Dunnett (every level against the first level of its factor, so order the columns with the control first), Holm-Bonferroni, Šidák or Bonferroni. Main-effect comparisons for both factors and, when the interaction is significant (p < α, from the confidence level), simple-effects comparisons (one factor's levels within each level of the other), all using the omnibus test's pooled error term (the within-groups mean square, MSW). With unequal cell sizes the main-effect comparisons use estimated marginal means (each level's cell means averaged with equal weight, as R's emmeans does), each with its own SE. Each row gives the difference with a CI adjusted in the same way as its p-value (simultaneous within each family; for Holm, the Bonferroni simultaneous CI that goes with it), and Cohen's d (difference ÷ √MSW) with a per-comparison CI.
  • Normality/variance check: Levene's test and a per-cell normality summary are shown for information. They don't change the test, but a failing check is a reason to also weigh the Scheirer-Ray-Hare result below.
  • Sensitivity (Scheirer-Ray-Hare): with Show the comparison on other scales on, the table ends with the same sums-of-squares decomposition on ranks, reporting H and a chi-square p-value for each factor and the interaction. It needs the same number of values in every cell. It is conservative overall but prone to false positives for the interaction in small samples, so treat a significant interaction there with extra caution.
  • Two independent factors, not one factor repeated over time/conditions: if one factor is the same subjects measured repeatedly, use Split-plot (mixed) instead (see ANOVA Tables above).

Sensitivity: The Same Comparison on Other Scales

With Show the comparison on other scales on (the default), a Groups or conditions result ends with the same comparison on the other three Compare scales: for 2 groups each one's estimate, CI, p and test; for 3 or more each one's omnibus p (or, for paired trimmed means, which has no omnibus test, its smallest Holm-adjusted pairwise p). A Two-way result ends with the same analysis on ranks (Scheirer-Ray-Hare; see Two-Way ANOVA). A conclusion that holds on only one scale is worth a second look.

Data Checks and Suggestions

What the Window Suggests

When the window opens, and whenever the checked columns change, @Graph looks at the data and suggests (never forces) an analysis. A choice you have made is left alone.

  • Numeric vs. binary (checked first): if the checked columns hold only 2 distinct values between them (such as 0/1), the data are treated as categorical and routed to the Categorical analysis (Fisher's Exact, McNemar's, Cochran's Q or Chi-square independence, depending on the number of columns and pairing) instead of running the data checks. The suggested test is marked ✦ in the picker. For one binary column (One-sample Binomial) or two independent binary columns (Fisher's Exact), @Graph fills the count fields from your data; they follow a change of columns until you type in them. McNemar's picks up the two checked columns. McNemar's and Cochran's Q need 0/1 coding, and the panel says so when the two values are something else.
  • Independent vs. paired: always your choice; @Graph never changes Paired/Repeated from the data. Equal sizes can be either, so a note asks you to check the box if the data are matched. With Paired on and different numbers of values per column (a missing value, say), a note says the paired tests use only the subjects with a value in every compared column.
  • Design: one checked column opens as One sample vs. a value; a chart with several series per category (not paired) opens as Two-way (factorial); a time-course chart opens as Mixed model (repeated measures); anything else as Groups or conditions. The design follows the data until you pick one.
  • Comparisons: All pairwise. When a column is named like a control, a suggestion offers vs. Control with it as the control; Use applies it.
  • Compare: Means (Welch's t or Welch's ANOVA) until you choose. The data checks suggest Means, Trimmed means or Medians (see below), and the suggestion stays visible with a Use button, but only Use or your own choice changes Compare. A chart shows Means (every chart type, Estimation Plots included) until you first open its Statistics tab or a linked Statistics window, or ask the AI Assistant to run its statistics; from then on it follows your Compare choice, which is saved with the chart. A linked window uses the chart's own choice, and changing a shared setting there recomputes brackets already on the chart.
  • Suggestions never take you away from a Descriptive or Normality view you are looking at; they are applied to the analysis that Determine Effect will run.

Data Checks Are Advisory

The DATA CHARACTERISTICS zone, in the Statistics window and on a chart's Statistics tab, describes the data. Nothing in it changes the analysis: choosing a test from normality or variance pretests is unstable and distorts error rates (Rochon, Gondan & Kieser 2012), so the checks only inform your choice. Each line has an ⓘ with the detail.

  • Normality: Shapiro-Wilk for n ≤ 50, skewness and kurtosis above that (see Skewness and Kurtosis, below). A paired design is judged on what its test models: each subject's difference (2 conditions) or the residuals after removing subject and condition means (3 or more), not each condition's raw values. Normality affects how accurate a Means comparison's p-value and CI are at small n; it does not decide what is compared.
  • Spread (independent groups): Levene's test (Brown-Forsythe variant) and the ratio of the largest to the smallest variance, for information: the Spreads choice, not this check, sets the test (see Which effect size, when). A p-value within 40% of α (0.03 to 0.07 at 95% confidence) is marked borderline. There is no spread check for Paired / Repeated data, and none is needed: the paired tests work on each subject's difference, so the two conditions' own variances never enter them (the only spread that matters, that of the differences, is already in the test's standard error), and Levene's test assumes independent groups, so it would be miscalibrated on the same subjects measured twice. With 3 or more repeated conditions the matching assumption is sphericity, which the repeated-measures ANOVA corrects for itself (Greenhouse-Geisser).
  • Outliers: points outside their group's Tukey fences (Q1/Q3 ± 1.5×IQR; beyond 3×IQR is "far out"), with a robust modified z-score for each (Iglewicz & Hoaglin 1993). On a chart, Show outliers rings the flagged points in orange.
  • Shape and suggestion: a far-out value (in a group of 5 or more) suggests Trimmed means, since means and SDs are pulled by such points; otherwise strong skew (|skewness| ≥ 1 with n ≥ 8; Bulmer 1979) suggests Medians, since the mean is then not a typical value; otherwise Means. The reason is given in words. When your Compare choice differs from the suggestion, "Suggested: compare …" is shown in orange with a Use button that applies it, and a note says it is only a suggestion based on the data's shape: choose the comparison from your study design.
  • Nothing switches by itself. Compare opens on Means and changes only when you choose or click Use; a chart's first bracket computation (or turning on the Estimation Plot) stores the choice in force, so a saved chart reopens on the same analysis.
  • In "Within category" mode (charts with several series) each series is pooled across categories after centering each category's values on their own mean, so the checks describe the comparison that runs in every category without mixing distributions centered in different places.

Skewness and Kurtosis

@Graph reports bias-corrected skewness (G1) and excess kurtosis (G2), the convention used by R, SPSS, GraphPad Prism and Excel.

  • G1 (skewness): G1 = g1 × √(n(n−1)) / (n−2), where g1 is the uncorrected sample skewness. For n ≥ 30 the correction is about 5% or less; for n < 10 it can be substantial.
  • G2 (excess kurtosis): G2 = (n-1)/((n-2)(n-3)) × ((n+1)×g2 + 6), where g2 is the uncorrected sample excess kurtosis. A normal distribution has G2 = 0.
  • The D'Agostino-Pearson K² normality test uses the uncorrected sample skewness and kurtosis (√b1 and b2), as its formulas require, not G1 and G2.
  • Normality verdict by sample size (following Kim 2013): Shapiro-Wilk has little power at small n and flags trivial deviations at large n, so @Graph tiers the verdict. Up to n = 50, Shapiro-Wilk's p-value decides (G1/G2 are shown for context). From n = 51 to 299, the z-scores of G1/G2 are compared against a stricter 3.29 cutoff instead of 1.96. At n ≥ 300, the raw values decide: |skewness| > 2 or |excess kurtosis| > 4. The normality line in DATA CHARACTERISTICS and every normality summary in the Statistics window and chart inspector use this verdict. The Normality Tests table itself reports Shapiro-Wilk's W and p for any n from 3 to 5,000 (the range covered by Royston's method, which R also uses).

Descriptive Statistics

  • Descriptive table (in DATA): n, mean, SD, SEM, median, IQR, min, max, skewness (G1), excess kurtosis (G2) and a 95% CI of the mean for every checked column, whichever analysis is chosen.
  • Normality tests (in DATA): Shapiro-Wilk (n = 3–5,000), D'Agostino-Pearson K² (n ≥ 20) and Kolmogorov-Smirnov (Lilliefors) for every checked column. With Paired / Repeated on, the table adds the paired differences (2 conditions) or the residuals of the subject + condition model (3 or more), which is what the test assumes normal and what the data checks judge. For a split-plot design it tests each group × condition cell.

Other Analyses

Categorical Tests

For dichotomous/count data rather than continuous measurements. Routed here automatically when the checked columns hold only 2 distinct values (see What the Window Suggests above), or chosen from Categorical under Analysis.

  • Fisher's Exact Test (2×2, independent groups): compares proportions between two independent groups. Type the cell counts (a, b / c, d); when routed here from two binary columns, @Graph fills them from your data. Reports the odds ratio and relative risk, each with a 95% CI, and notes that only the odds ratio is valid for a case-control (retrospective) design. The odds ratio is the conditional maximum-likelihood estimate, as R's fisher.test reports it. Its CI is the exact interval matched to the p-value (Fay 2010, as R's exact2x2 gives it): the odds ratios the same two-sided test does not reject, so it excludes 1 exactly when p is below α, and it is the CI the chart's brackets show. (R's fisher.test prints the central interval, α/2 in each tail, which near α can contradict its own p: 1 3 / 6 0 has p = 0.033 beside a central CI of 0 to 1.17.) With a zero cell the estimate and one bound are 0 or ∞, as in R, rather than a finite number invented by adding 0.5 to every cell. The headline effect size is ln(Odds Ratio) with its own 95% CI, on which an odds ratio of 0.2 and one of 5.0 are equally far from 0 (signed, so its direction shows which group had the higher odds).
  • McNemar's Test (paired 2×2): the same subjects measured on one binary outcome twice (such as before/after), entered as two 0/1 columns. The headline effect size is Δp, the difference in proportions (b − c)/n, with Tango's paired score interval (Tango 1998): how much more often one condition was positive, using every subject; it keeps close to its level at small n and stays finite when a discordant cell is empty. The table also reports the paired odds ratio (b/c, the ratio of discordant pairs), ln(Odds Ratio), and Cohen's g = b/(b+c) − 0.5, how far the discordant pairs depart from a 50/50 split (range ±0.5), and the discordant counts b and c; a chart's brackets can show any of them. The odds ratio and Cohen's g use only the subjects that changed, so with fewer than 10 of them the table adds a note: one changed subject gives g = 0.5. Their CIs come from one exact Clopper-Pearson interval for the discordant split b/(b+c), so they exclude the null exactly when the exact binomial test is significant (a Wilson interval could disagree with it: b = 5, c = 0 gave an OR CI excluding 1 beside p = 0.0625). When c or b is 0 the odds ratio is ∞ or 0, and the CI is open on that side. The p-value is always the exact binomial test (two-sided, as R's exact2x2 mcnemar.exact gives it), at any number of discordant pairs, so p and the CIs always agree.
  • Cochran's Q (paired, k≥2 binary conditions): extends McNemar's to 3 or more repeated binary conditions per subject; the binary counterpart of Friedman's test. Reports the χ² p-value and an exact permutation p-value for the same Q (each subject's successes equally likely to fall on any condition; for 2 conditions it equals the exact McNemar test). When few subjects changed (n below 4 or n·k below 24, where n counts only the subjects whose responses differ across conditions, as in Tate & Brown 1970), the exact p is the test's p: it is the p the result, the per-category table, chart brackets and the AI Assistant report, with the χ² p beside it. Otherwise the χ² p is reported, with the exact one beside it; the result says which rule applied. With many subjects the permutation p is a Monte Carlo estimate (100,000 rearrangements, seeded from the data). Also reports a Kendall's W-style effect size with a bootstrap 95% CI, and runs pairwise McNemar post-hoc comparisons for k≥3, all pairs or each vs. a control (the Comparisons picker under Options), exactly as the chart's brackets run them: each pair uses every subject with values in both of its conditions, p is Holm-adjusted, and the paired odds ratio and Cohen's g have simultaneous (Bonferroni, 1 − α/m) exact CIs.
  • Chi-square Test of Independence (r×c, independent groups): 2 or more groups against 2 or more categories, one column per group, each cell a category code (0, 1, 2, …). Reports Cramér's V and its bias-corrected version (Bergsma 2013), each with a bootstrap 95% CI, and the smallest expected count. When an expected count is below 1, or more than 20% of them are below 5 (Cochran 1954), the χ² p-value is unreliable: the result and the panel say so and suggest Fisher's exact (r×c), with a Use button. Three or more groups of 0/1 data open here.
  • Fisher's Exact Test (r×c): the same table and effect sizes as the chi-square test of independence, with Fisher's exact p-value (the Freeman-Halton extension, as R's fisher.test): the probability, given the row and column totals, of every table no more likely than the one observed. A table too large to enumerate gets a Monte Carlo p-value from 100,000 random tables with the same totals, as R's fisher.test(simulate.p.value = TRUE), with the seed shown so it reproduces.
  • Chi-square goodness-of-fit: tests observed counts against an expected distribution (uniform by default, or typed in, one value per category, separated by commas, or by semicolons when the values use a decimal comma: 0,3; 0,5; 0,2). The first checked column holds one count per category, one row per category; the panel and the result name it, and say so when other columns are checked, since they are not used. Reports Cohen's w (the one-sample analogue of Cramér's V) with a bootstrap 95% CI.
  • One-sample Binomial Test: tests whether an observed proportion of successes differs from a hypothesized value p₀. Reports the observed proportion and its difference from p₀, each with an exact Clopper-Pearson 95% CI, and the exact two-sided p, as R's binom.test does. For p₀ other than 0.5 the CI and p can disagree just at the threshold (the CI inverts the equal-tailed test); the table then says so, and the p-value decides the test. When routed here from a single binary column, @Graph fills in the counts from your data.

Correlation and Regression

  • Correlation (Pearson r, Spearman ρ, Kendall's τ-b, linear regression): for two continuous columns. Pearson r is the headline effect size, with a 95% CI (Fisher z-transform); the regression slope and intercept each get a 95% CI too. Spearman ρ and Kendall's τ-b are reported alongside, each with a bootstrap 95% CI (resampling the (x,y) pairs together). Kendall's p-value is exact, as in R, when there are no ties and n is below 50, and by permutation for n ≤ 10 with ties; Spearman's is exact (permutation) for n ≤ 10, and above that, with no ties, comes from the AS 89 Edgeworth series as in R (Best & Roberts 1975). Otherwise they use large-sample approximations. Exact p-values are marked '(exact)'.
  • Regression: Simple Linear (one X, one Y, the default) and Dose-Response (4-parameter logistic) take one X and one Y column, like Correlation. Multiple Regression and ANCOVA take one row per observation, not one column per group. Simple Linear and Multiple Regression can add standardized coefficients, robust SEs and residual diagnostics. See Multiple Regression and ANCOVA below.

Multiple Regression and ANCOVA

The Regression analysis takes a different data shape from the group comparisons above: one row per observation (a cell, a sweep, an animal), with the outcome (Y) and each predictor in its own column. Pick the outcome in Y (outcome) column and check every predictor column in the Columns list. For a single predictor, choose Simple Linear or use Correlation, since Multiple Regression needs 2 or more.

  • Multiple Regression: models one continuous Y from 2+ predictors at once (Y = b₀ + b₁X₁ + b₂X₂ + …), so each coefficient is that predictor's effect with the others held constant. Reports R² and Cohen's f (with 90% CIs from the exact distribution of R², treating the predictors as random; Fisher 1928), adjusted R², the overall model F-test, and each term's B/SE/95% CI/t/p. Predictors on a large offset (such as 10⁸ ± 1) fit normally. If a predictor is constant, or a combination of the others, the message names it.
  • VIF (variance inflation factor): shown per predictor for up to 20 predictors (a note says when it is skipped). VIF > 5 flags moderate multicollinearity (interpret that coefficient with caution); VIF > 10 flags severe multicollinearity (that coefficient is unreliable; consider removing or combining the correlated predictors).
  • Options for Simple Linear and Multiple Regression, all off by default (with none on, the table is exactly as described above):
  • Standardized coefficients (β): adds a Std. β column beside each B, the change in Y in SDs per SD of that predictor (B × SD(X) / SD(Y) over the rows used, as R's lm on scale()d variables). It uses this sample's SDs, so it shifts with how widely each predictor happened to be sampled: compare it between predictors in one model, not across studies. With one predictor it equals Pearson r.
  • Robust (heteroscedasticity-consistent) SEs: each coefficient's SE, 95% CI, t and p use the sandwich estimator instead, labeled with its name: HC3 (the default, recommended at lab sample sizes; Long & Ervin 2000), HC2, HC1 or HC0 (White 1980), as R's sandwich::vcovHC with lmtest::coeftest (t on the residual degrees of freedom). Use them when the spread of Y changes with a predictor, such as a fan shape in the Residuals vs Fitted plot. R², the model F test and VIF stay the ordinary ones.
  • Residual diagnostics: adds R's four plot.lm plots under the result (Residuals vs Fitted, Normal Q-Q of the standardized residuals, Scale-Location, and Residuals vs Leverage with Cook's distance contours at 0.5 and 1), each with its lowess smooth in red and the most extreme rows numbered, plus a table of the rows with the largest Cook's D, leverage and studentized residuals. Each rule is listed with its cutoff and the rows it flags: Cook's D > 4/n (worth a look) or > 1 (highly influential; Cook 1977), leverage h > 2p/n (twice the average hat value, p counting the intercept; Belsley, Kuh & Welsch 1980), and |studentized residual| > 2 or > 3 (externally studentized, as R's rstudent; about 5% of rows pass 2 by chance). Rows are numbered as in your data. A flag is a reason to check that row, not to delete it. The plots stay in the window; the table goes into exported reports and the Data Log.
  • ANCOVA: compares group means on Y after adjusting for one or more continuous covariates. Pick which checked predictor column is the categorical factor. It must hold small numeric group codes (0, 1, 2, …), since text labels can't be parsed; every other checked predictor is treated as a continuous covariate.
  • ANCOVA's table reports each term's partial F-test and partial η² (each adjusted for every other term; the factor's partial η² and Cohen's f also get 90% CIs, noncentral F), the covariate-adjusted group means, and post-hoc pairwise comparisons of those means: mean difference with a 95% CI adjusted in the same way as its p-value (ANCOVA has its own post-hoc picker: Tukey-Kramer by default, as in R's emmeans, or Holm-Bonferroni, Šidák or Bonferroni), so each CI excludes zero exactly when its adjusted p is significant (for Holm, the Bonferroni simultaneous CI that goes with it), and Cohen's d. d divides the adjusted difference by the pooled within-group SD of Y before adjustment, not the covariate-adjusted residual SD, which is smaller and would inflate d relative to the same difference in a study without the covariate.
  • Homogeneity of slopes: checked automatically. If the factor × covariate interaction is significant (p < 0.05), a warning appears: the covariate's relationship with Y differs across groups, so the adjusted-means comparison isn't valid as reported. Consider analyzing groups separately or modeling the interaction. If there is too little data to test this, the table says so; the comparison still runs, but on an unverified assumption.
  • Variance homogeneity: Levene's test (Brown-Forsythe version) on the model's residuals across the factor groups is shown alongside, with a warning if their spread differs. ANCOVA assumes equal error variance, the spread around each group's line; the raw outcome's spread also reflects how much the covariate varies in each group, so it is not tested. Treat a violation as a reason to look closer, not proof the model is invalid.
  • Scope: one categorical factor plus one or more covariates. ANCOVA with more than one categorical factor isn't supported.
  • Dose-Response (4-parameter logistic): one X (dose/concentration) column and one Y (response) column, like Correlation. Fits a 4-parameter logistic (Hill) curve (Bottom, Top, LogEC50, Hill slope), reporting each parameter's SE and 95% CI, R², and EC50 on the raw dose scale. EC50 gets both a Wald and a profile-likelihood CI, and an F test says whether there is any dose-response at all. The fit is the same in any unit (amperes, counts, molar or nanomolar doses), as it runs on rescaled axes. "Converged: yes" means the fit reached a true minimum (R's relative-offset test) with every SE computable; otherwise a warning replaces the SEs and CIs. When the doses never reach one plateau, the profile CI can be one-sided (for example 0 – 2.1e-6), meaning the data cannot rule out an EC50 beyond that side. A checkbox says whether X already holds log10(dose) or should be log-transformed (raw doses must be > 0). This fit is separate from the Boltzmann (sigmoid) fit in a Graph window's Curve Fitting panel and shares no settings with it.
  • Dose-Response constraints and weighting: check Bottom, Top or Hill slope under Constraints to hold it at the value you type (for example Bottom = 0 and Top = 100 for data normalized to percent). A fixed parameter is not fitted, so it has no SE or CI and gives its degree of freedom back to the residuals; the SEs, the profile CI and the F test all use the reduced model. Weighting by 1/Y² (scatter that grows with the response) or 1/Y (Poisson-like counts) uses the fitted curve's response, as Prism 5 and later do, re-estimated from each fit until it settles (iteratively reweighted least squares, the consistent estimator; Carroll & Ruppert 1988). Minimizing the weighted sum of squares outright, weights and all, pulls the curve up instead, so a program that does that gives slightly different numbers. R's nls weights by each point's own response instead, which pulls the curve toward the points that happen to fall low: in simulations with 20% scatter it left Top 7% low and 95% CIs covering 90%. R², the SEs, the CIs and every test then use the weighted sum of squares, which the table reports. With any option on, the table also lists the fixed values, the weighting and the residual SS with its df.
  • Replicates test for lack of fit: offered when some dose is repeated. Pure error is the scatter of replicates around their own dose mean, which no curve can remove; the F test asks whether the curve misses the dose means by more than that scatter (Prism's replicates test, and what R's anova gives against a one-way ANOVA on dose). A small p means another model may fit better.
  • Comparing curves: with Compare set, each checked column other than X is a data set at the X column's doses (or, with "Checked columns as X,Y pairs", the checked columns in order are X1, Y1, X2, Y2, …, as a Scatter lays them out). Ask whether one curve fits all data sets, or whether one parameter (LogEC50, Bottom, Top or Hill slope) differs while the others stay separate. The effect comes first: each data set's LogEC50 difference from the first, with its CI, and the matching EC50 ratio. Then the extra sum-of-squares F test of the shared model against separate curves (F, df, p and both models' SS), the shared fit's parameters with SEs and CIs, and, if checked, AICc: the difference and each model's probability of being correct. Constraints and weighting apply to every data set.
  • Missing data: all three (Multiple Regression, ANCOVA, Dose-Response) use complete-case analysis: a row missing a value in Y, X, or any included predictor is dropped from that fit entirely.

Charts and the Data Log

Linked to a Chart

Opened from a chart (Open Full Statistics Window), the window is linked to it: Paired / Repeated and every Compare setting (Compare, Spreads, Question, effect size, post-hoc, tails) are shared both ways, including with the chart's brackets, and so are the comparisons and the control while the window compares exactly the chart's groups. The Show Chart button in the header brings the linked chart to the front. A window with no linked chart shows Make Chart instead: it opens a chart of the data and links the window to it, carrying the window's current settings over. Edit as Spreadsheet… links the window the same way to the spreadsheet it opens, and edits there update the statistics right away. While the chart is a time course (Split-plot or Mixed model), its spreadsheet shows the subject table: one row per subject, with the group column, the time points and any source column.

Using the Data Log and Spreadsheets with Statistics

Any numeric table in the Data Log or a Spreadsheet can be analyzed. In the Data Log, type or paste tab-delimited columns (one group per column), select the block including any header row, and press ⌘T. In a Spreadsheet, use the Data Statistics toolbar button. A first row of text is read as headers. Results go to the Data Log only when you click Send to Data Log.

The Text Tools menu provides List Stats (⇧⌘T) to compute mean/SD/SEM/count for each column directly in the Data Log without opening the Statistics window.

Brackets and the Estimation Plot

A chart's Statistics tab uses the same zones and settings to draw effect size brackets (see Charts → Effect Size Brackets), and its Show Estimation Plot button plots each Compare estimate with its bootstrap CI and sampling distribution (see Charts → Estimation Plot).
