Exporting

The Export button in a graph or chart window's toolbar (the first of its tool buttons) opens one menu for everything: under Image, Image… (PNG, JPEG, TIFF, GIF, PDF, SVG), Copy as PDF and Send to Keynote…; under Data, @Graph (.@graph) and the data formats below. File → Export… (⇧⌘E) opens the same image panel.

Exporting a graph happens in the background, so the app stays responsive while a large figure is written. If an export takes more than a moment, a progress indicator appears; click Cancel to stop it, and no file is written.

Export (⇧⌘E)

  • PNG: lossless raster, good for presentations and the web. A PNG, JPEG or TIFF records its resolution in sRGB color, so it places at its intended size in Word or a journal system.
  • JPEG: compressed raster with a quality slider (50–100%), for smaller files.
  • GIF: 256-color raster, for simple figures.
  • PDF: vector, resolution-independent. Best for publications and Illustrator/Affinity.
  • SVG: vector with individually selectable elements and editable text, for Illustrator, Affinity Designer and Inkscape. Its size is in points (72 per inch), so it opens at the same physical size in Illustrator, Inkscape, Affinity and browsers.
  • TIFF: lossless raster.

The exported image matches what's on screen, including axis labels, group layout, colors, effect size brackets, data overlays, scale bars, cursors A and B, and minor ticks. PDF and SVG exports are clean vector with no clipping masks. The panel starts from the format, size and resolution set in Settings → Export. Transparent background leaves the background out of PNG, TIFF and GIF images.

Graphs: set the width and height (pixels for images, points for PDF and SVG); the panel shows the placed size in millimeters and inches. For images, choose the resolution (72 to 1,200 dpi); 300 dpi suits photos and color figures, 600 dpi line art. The panel shows the output in pixels and warns when an image would be too large to make (over 30,000 pixels a side or 250 million pixels). Match window resets the resolution to 72 dpi; raise it afterwards for a sharper image with the same layout. PDF and SVG are sized in points (72 per inch), so a figure places at the size shown in Illustrator, Inkscape and Affinity. Match window, Copy as PDF and Send to Keynote use the size of the graph area on screen.

  • Every graph export (PNG, JPEG, TIFF, GIF, PDF and SVG) is drawn by the same code as the window: the same axis ranges, tick values and units (such as ms), junction-potential-corrected Y labels, log axes, legend, trend line, scale bars, interval bars, AP markers and error bars.
  • Each sweep is one editable path. Parts of a trace outside the plot area are cut off exactly at its edge. For PDF and SVG, Data resolution sets how many points per inch of printed plot width each sweep keeps (200, 400, 600, All or a custom number; the panel shows the points per sweep). Every sweep keeps the highest and lowest value of each stretch and its first and last points, and gaps stay gaps, so peaks and gaps survive at any setting.
  • What only matters while you work is left out: the selected sweep's highlight and a panel's highlighted analysis window.
  • PDF exports of graphs embed the fonts they use, as subsets with just the characters needed, so text looks the same on every computer, passes journal checks for embedded fonts, and stays searchable and editable. Each label uses the font you chose (Helvetica for the system font), and symbols such as µ, Δ, Ω, Greek letters, subscripts and non-Latin scripts print correctly. If a font's license doesn't allow embedding, its text is drawn as outlines instead and the Data Log says so. SVG text uses the fonts chosen in the inspector. SVG text names each font's family with its weight (from Thin to Black), italic and width, so faces such as Avenir Next Medium or Helvetica Neue Condensed Bold look the same in browsers, Inkscape and Illustrator as on screen.

Printing (⌘P)

File → Print… (⌘P) prints the front window: a graph, chart, image, spreadsheet or data table, a Statistics window, the Protocols window, a Text Editor or the Data Log. It's dimmed when the front window has nothing to print. Graphs and charts print as vector graphics that fill the page, so lines and labels stay crisp. Tables print across as many pages as needed, with their header rows and columns on every page, and text never splits a line between pages. In the print dialog, Print header and footer adds the title, date and page numbers, and Fit to page width shrinks wide tables to fit. File → Page Setup… (⇧⌘P) sets the paper size, orientation and scale for the front window, which keeps them until it closes. The User Guide prints too: the page you're reading, or the whole guide with Print all sections in the print dialog. Help → Open User Guide in Browser shows the whole guide as one web page in your default browser, with a contents list linking to each section. To keep or share a copy, save it from the browser (in Safari, File → Save As… with Format set to Page Source). A graph's Spreadsheet prints every row, with row numbers and the Time column on each page. If that would take more than 2,000 pages, @Graph asks whether to print just the selection or the first 500 pages.

Copy as PDF

Copy as PDF (in the Export menu, or right-click the graph or chart) copies the current view to the clipboard as a vector PDF, ready to paste into Keynote, Pages, Word or any app that accepts PDF, with an image alongside for apps that paste only images. A graph is copied at the size of its graph area on screen, with a 2× PNG. A chart is copied as PDF, PNG and TIFF, at the size and resolution set in Settings → Export. In a Spreadsheet window, Copy as PDF and Send to Keynote… make a clean table of the sheet or the selected cells (bold headers, grid lines), not a screenshot.

Send to Keynote

Send to Keynote… places the current graph or chart on the current slide of the frontmost Keynote presentation as a vector image. If no presentation is open, a new one is created. If Keynote can't take the image, the PDF opens in Preview instead.

The first time you use Send to Keynote, macOS asks whether @Graph may control Keynote. Click OK; this is needed only once.

Export Data

  • ABF (.abf): the Export ABF sheet asks for the sample format first. 32-bit (exact) saves every sample as it is, as ABF 2 (pCLAMP 10 and later), because pyABF can't open 32-bit ABF 1 files; 16-bit (smaller) is ABF 1.8 at half the size, with each channel rounded to 1/65,534 of its range, except that a 16-bit ABF recording keeps its own scaling and stays exact. The choice starts at the one that matches where the samples came from, and the sheet says why. ABF holds at most 16 channels, one sample rate, and the same number of sweeps on every channel; a graph outside that is refused with the reason, and a format that can hold it (NWB or HDF5) is suggested. 32-bit holds up to 4 GB of samples (pCLAMP's limit), keeps blank samples, and ends a sweep shorter than the others in blank samples (the Data Log says so). 16-bit needs one sweep length and no gaps: blank samples at the end of sweeps (SutterPatch leaves 16 on current and aux inputs) are dropped, every sweep is cut to the last recorded sample, and if another channel at the same rate loses a few real samples at its end to keep the lengths equal, the Data Log says so (a cut of more than 1% of a sweep is refused).
  • AxoGraph (.axgx): AxoGraph X format with one time column and one column per sweep, in volts and amperes. A file opened from AxoGraph and exported with the same channels and sweeps keeps AxoGraph's display settings; a recording of several episodes otherwise gets trace and group headers and its episode count, so neo reads each episode as a segment. The first-sample time is kept, and the file reads back with exactly the same values and the recording's date, even for a single episode. Every sweep must share one sample rate; a table of measurements is written as Y columns against its X column.
  • MATLAB (.mat): level 5 format; per channel a [samples × sweeps] matrix with its sample rate, units and (when they differ) sweep lengths, or one spike-times vector per unit for a raster. MATLAB and SciPy read it, and @Graph reads it back as it was. A variable over 2 GB is refused, with NWB or HDF5 suggested.
  • HDF5 (.h5): one dataset per channel, [sweeps × samples], with channel_name, units and sample_rate attributes (and each sweep's length when they differ); a raster writes one dataset of spike times per unit under /units, with its name, recorded span and sweep start as attributes. h5py and MATLAB read it, and @Graph reads it back exactly. Names and units are UTF-8 text, so symbols such as µ read correctly in Python, MATLAB and HDFView. It is plain HDF5, not NWB.
  • NWB (.nwb): a Neurodata Without Borders 2 file, which pynwb opens and its validator passes with no errors. The file carries the NWB schema it follows, so NWB tools and DANDI can check it on their own, and @Graph reopens its NWB exports exactly, sweep names included. The session starts at the recording's date and time, with its time zone, when the file has one (else at the time of export). Only this export writes NWB: a .@graph file, and the HDF5 export below, are plain HDF5 that NWB software won't open.
  • NWB from a waveform window: one series per channel and sweep under /acquisition/, with the data in the graph's own unit, the NWB unit (volts or amperes) and conversion factor, its sample rate and its start time; each sweep keeps its own rate and length.
  • NWB intracellular channels: a graph doesn't record the amplifier's mode, so the units guess it (the metadata sheet can change the guess): the first voltage or current channel sets it (voltage: current clamp; current: voltage clamp). Channels of that unit are the responses (CurrentClampSeries or VoltageClampSeries), each with its own electrode on the amplifier device. A channel of the other unit recorded alongside (Im beside Vm) is a response's stimulus, paired in order and kept in the file's stimulus section; any channel left over (a light monitor in mV, say) is a plain TimeSeries. When no command channel was recorded, an ABF protocol's command waveform is written as the stimulus. The intracellular recordings and simultaneous recordings tables list each sweep's electrode, stimulus and response.
  • NWB extracellular channels (a voltage in µV, or in mV from an Intan, Open Ephys, Neuralynx, Blackrock, Plexon or TDT file) are ElectricalSeries: each sweep's channels of one sample rate share a series, one column per channel, so a recording with several sweeps or rates becomes a series per sweep and rate, each starting at its own time. Every extracellular channel is a row of the electrodes table, with its location and electrode group, and the group's device (the recording system). Channels marked LFP in the metadata sheet go into processing/ecephys/LFP instead. Other units are plain TimeSeries.
  • NWB from a raster window (from Detect Spikes → Raster, the Spike Sorter or a spike-sorted file): the Units table, with each train's spike times in seconds from the session start (its sweep's start added), its name, and the span it was recorded over when known. A raster's spike-time resolution is set for you: the sampling interval of the recording its spikes were found in (Detect Spikes, the Spike Sorter), or the clock of a Plexon or Blackrock file.
  • CSV (.csv): one column per visible sweep, in group order (prefixed with the group name when more than one group is visible), holding the data's own values (not junction-potential shifted) in full precision. Each value column's header includes its unit. A gap inside a sweep is an empty cell, as is the end of a sweep shorter than the others. Sweeps sharing one sample rate share a first Time (s) column, measured from the start of each sweep. Otherwise, and for X-Y data, each sweep has its own X column beside it. Data to Graph… reads this layout back in. Every CSV @Graph writes (graphs, charts, spreadsheets, image data, and Data Log or Text Editor text saved with a .csv name) opens correctly in Excel: a file with a symbol such as µ or ° starts with a UTF-8 byte-order mark, and a plain ASCII file has none.

ABF, AxoGraph, MATLAB, HDF5 and NWB keep channel names, units, sample rates and sweep structure, so the file reopens in @Graph and in tools that read that format. X-Y data (such as an I-V curve) can only be exported as CSV or AxoGraph, and HDF5 needs every sweep of a channel at one sample rate.

NWB Metadata

Export → NWB first opens a sheet for what the data can't say but NWB tools and the DANDI Archive ask for. Everything is optional; a small orange diamond marks what DANDI needs to accept the file. Export… writes what you entered, Skip exports without any of it, and Cancel stops.

  • Subject: subject ID, species (pick a common lab species or type a Latin binomial such as Mus musculus, or an NCBI taxonomy link), sex (M, F, U for unknown, O for other), and the age as a number of days, weeks, months or years (exact, a range, or that age or older, counted from birth or conception) or a date of birth instead. Strain, genotype, description and weight (g, kg or mg) are optional. The age is written in ISO 8601 form (90 days: P90D; 90 to 120 days: P90D/P120D).
  • Session: experimenters (Last, First; separate several with semicolons), institution, lab, experiment description, session ID, keywords (separated by commas) and related publications (DOIs, such as doi:10.1523/…).
  • Channels: each channel's role, guessed from its unit and the recording system (Automatic) or set to current clamp, voltage clamp, stimulus, extracellular (wideband), extracellular (LFP) or other. Set it when the guess is wrong: a file holding only the command current is a stimulus, not a voltage-clamp recording, and LFP channels are never guessed. For a patch-clamp electrode: cell ID, location (for mouse, an Allen Mouse Brain Atlas name or acronym such as CA1 or VISp), slice, electrode description, seal and resistance. For an extracellular channel: location, electrode group (channels with the same group name share one; the first to give a group description or device sets it), device (the recording system unless you name one), position (x, y, z in µm) and filtering. Intan and Neuralynx files fill in the filtering from their headers, and an NWB file fills in what its electrodes table says.
  • Spike Times (a raster): the resolution is filled in when @Graph knows the sampling rate; otherwise enter the rate the spikes were detected at.
  • The sheet starts from what you entered for this graph last time (a saved .@graph keeps it) or what an NWB file it came from says, and fills blank experimenters, institution, lab, species and strain from your last export.
  • The bottom of the sheet lists what DANDI will refuse and what the NWB Inspector's best practices note. With the DANDI fields and each electrode's cell ID filled in (and, for mouse, an Atlas location), the export passes pynwb's validator, the NWB Inspector with no critical issues or best-practice violations, and dandi validate once dandi organize has named the files. If you export with blanks, the Data Log says what DANDI will refuse.

Export Visible Sweeps

The Export Visible… button, in the bar above the plot, sends only the visible sweeps to a new window (Open in new window), a file (Save to file, as .@graph), or Both. It is unavailable while View → Plot Sweeps at Recording Time is on. The panels stay as they are (each group keeps its name, style, Y range and axis title, whatever the grouping), and so do the graph's look, labels, overlays and junction-potential setting. Each sweep keeps its own sample rate and length, and X-Y data such as I-V curves keep their X values. A spike-train raster exports its visible trains.

When the X axis is zoomed, 'Only the X range on screen' (on by default) cuts each sweep to that range; times keep reading as they do now. With sweeps at different sample rates, the cut starts at a time every rate shares (for example, a whole millisecond for 20 kHz and 1 kHz), so every sample keeps its exact time. Turn it off to export whole sweeps.

To export a single group as its own file, hide all other groups in the Groups panel, then use Export Visible… → Save to file.

Chart Windows

Chart windows use the same Save (⌘S) and Save As (⇧⌘S) as graph windows, and the .@graph file reopens with all series and formatting. Saved graphs and charts (.@graph) open in the background, as recordings do: a large one shows a progress indicator while it loads, and @Graph stays responsive. File → Export… (⇧⌘E) opens Export Chart, with the same image formats as for graphs (PNG, JPEG, TIFF, GIF, PDF, SVG), and reproduces all chart settings.

  • Size: set it in points, inches, centimeters or millimeters. Journal width sets a journal's column width (Nature, Cell, Science, PNAS, eLife / PLOS) and keeps the chart's proportions. Text keeps its point size, so check that labels still fit.
  • Resolution (images): 72, 144, 150, 300, 600 or 1,200 dpi; the panel shows the output in pixels.
  • PDF and SVG files are laid out like the chart window, title included, and SVG text stays editable. Heatmaps of more than 250,000 cells (or 2,000 rows or columns) export their cells as an embedded image, with labels, dendrograms and legend still vector.
  • CSV (.csv) / TSV (.tsv): every chart type (bar/dot/pie/line series, volcano plots, heatmaps) can also be saved as delimited text from the Export menu. Headers match the on-screen spreadsheet view.
  • Large heatmap matrices (tens of millions of cells) export without running out of memory.

CSV/TSV is the most portable way to take chart or genomics data (differential-expression tables, expression matrices) into R, Python/pandas, Excel or other tools.

Lab Tools

The Lab Tools menu opens a single window with seven calculators on tabs. Values you enter are kept when you switch tabs. A result shows a dash (—) until every value it needs is a valid number. In fields that show a unit, a value can be typed with a unit and is converted to the field's (50 mg in a g field, 100 mM or 100 mmol/L in an M field, 5 ml or 500 µl in an mL field); a unit of another kind, or one on a plain number such as NA, shows the dash. α and power take 0.05 or 5%.

Molarity Calculator

Choose what to solve for (mass, molecular weight, volume or concentration) and enter the other three. A list of common compounds (NaCl, KCl, CaCl₂ and MgCl₂ anhydrous and as their usual hydrates, HEPES, EGTA, Na₂ATP, Na₂GTP and others) fills in the molecular weight of the form you weigh.

Dilution Calculator

Solves C₁V₁ = C₂V₂ for whichever value you choose (for example, the stock volume needed for a given final concentration and volume). A result that would make the final concentration higher than the stock is flagged, since that usually means swapped values or mismatched units. The Serial Dilution section lists the concentration at each step for a chosen starting concentration, dilution factor and number of steps (a whole number; the table shows up to 100 steps and says so when it stops there). Enter plain numbers, with both concentrations in the same unit and both volumes in the same unit; the result uses that unit.

Nernst Equation

Calculates an ion's reversal potential (Erev) from its inside and outside concentrations and the temperature. Presets for K⁺, Na⁺, Ca²⁺, Cl⁻ and H⁺ fill in the valence and typical mammalian-neuron concentrations. The valence must be a nonzero whole number (+1, −1, +2). R and F are the CODATA 2018 values. The result updates as you type.

Henderson–Hasselbalch

Calculates buffer pH from the pKa and the base and acid concentrations, or the base-to-acid ratio needed for a target pH. A table of common buffers (HEPES, PIPES, MES, MOPS, Tris, phosphate, bicarbonate and others) has a Use button that fills in the pKa. Its values are for 25 °C: pKa falls as the temperature rises (Tris by about 0.028 per °C, so a Tris buffer made to pH 7.4 at 25 °C is near pH 7.1 at 37 °C) and shifts with ionic strength, so check it for your conditions. A very small or large ratio is shown in scientific notation (8.71e-04).

Ohm's Law

Relates voltage (V), current (I), resistance (R) and power (P) through V = IR and P = VI. Choose what to solve for and enter the two values it needs (for power, any two of V, I and R); power is always shown too, in the unit chosen beside it. The solved quantity's unit menu sets the unit its result is shown in. Units default to millivolts, picoamperes and megaohms; current goes down to femtoamperes.

Microscopy Resolution

Calculates theoretical optical limits and recommended imaging parameters for a given objective, camera and wavelength (550 nm to start). Enter the NA and refractive index (n) under Illumination, and the camera's pixel size, sensor pixels, objective magnification, coupler/relay and binning under Camera & Magnification.

  • Abbe resolution limit (d = λ / 2NA, for incoherent light or a condenser matching the objective's NA) and Rayleigh criterion (d = 0.61λ / NA).
  • Nyquist pixel size (λ / 4NA): the largest pixel at the sample that samples the finest detail a widefield (incoherent) image carries, its cutoff 2NA/λ, at twice its frequency, as SVI's Nyquist calculator gives it. Half the Rayleigh limit (0.305λ/NA), a common rule of thumb, is about 22% larger and undersamples. A confocal image carries finer detail and needs smaller pixels still.
  • An NA above the refractive index is flagged: it can't be reached in that medium. The refractive index has a Preset menu (Air, Water, Glycerol, Immersion oil).
  • A Sampling status line says whether the pixel size at the sample meets Nyquist, with the ratio, and Min magnification for Nyquist gives the total magnification needed. The effective pixel size and total magnification are shown too.
  • Depth of field (DoF = λ·n / NA² + n / (M·NA) · e, where M is the total magnification and e the image pixel on the sensor: the camera's pixel size times the binning).
  • Field of view from the camera's pixel size and pixel count and the total magnification (objective × coupler, with a coupler Preset menu from 0.35× to 2.0×).
  • Binning (1 × 1, 2 × 2, 3 × 3, 4 × 4 or 8 × 8): with n × n on-chip binning each image pixel is n camera pixels wide, so the pixel size at the sample, the sampling status and the objective recommendations all use n × the camera pixel; the sensor line gives the binned image size.
  • Wavelength (300–1300 nm): drag the spectrum slider, step it with the arrows, or type a value, which applies as you type (in nm, or with a unit such as 0.488 µm). The slider spans visible light (380–700 nm); an ultraviolet or near-infrared wavelength, such as IR-DIC or a NIR dye, can be typed. The formulas are for one-photon imaging, so they don't give two-photon resolution.
  • An Objective menu (air, water and oil objectives) that sets magnification, NA and refractive index.
  • A Camera menu of common sCMOS, CMOS and EMCCD cameras that fills in pixel size and sensor dimensions. The Andor Sona comes in two models, the 4.2B-6 (6.5 µm pixels) and the 4.2B-11 (11 µm).
  • The tab opens with the camera and objective chosen in Settings → Imaging, once per session; after that it keeps what you enter.

The Objective Recommendations box at the bottom of the Microscopy tab lists the objectives that meet Nyquist sampling with the current camera and wavelength, with each one's pixel size at the sample and field of view. Objectives that undersample are listed below them.

Power Analysis

Estimates the sample size needed for a target power, the power of a given sample size, or the smallest effect a given sample size detects, for an independent (two-sample), paired or one-sample design. Enter the effect size directly, or turn on Compute from group means. What the effect size divides by depends on the design:

  • Independent: Cohen's d, the mean difference over the common SD (or two means and a common SD).
  • Paired: the same Question as the Statistics module, Magnitude (the default) or Consistency; the ⓘ beside it explains both. Magnitude takes Cohen's dav, the mean difference over the average of the two conditions' SDs (or two condition means and that average SD), and the expected correlation r between the conditions (default 0.5, between −1 and 1; take it from pilot data or earlier studies). It is converted to dz = dav / √(2(1 − r)), taking the two SDs as about equal, and the dz used is shown with the result. At r = 0.5 dav and dz are equal. Consistency takes Cohen's dz, the mean difference over the SD of the differences, as R's pwr (type = "paired") and G*Power take it (or two condition means and the SD of the differences).
  • One-sample: Cohen's d, (mean − μ₀) / SD (or the sample mean, μ₀ and the SD).
  • Solve for Sample Size: enter the effect size, significance level (α) and target power to get N per group (independent), N pairs (paired) or N (one-sample).
  • Solve for Power: enter the effect size, α and a sample size to see the power it achieves. Power of 99.95% or more shows as > 99.9%.
  • Solve for Detectable Effect Size: enter α, the target power and a sample size to get the smallest effect the test detects with that power: Cohen's d, or for a paired design dz (Consistency) or dav with the dz it comes from (Magnitude, using r). The target power must be above α.
  • Exact for the two-sided t-test: power from the noncentral t distribution, as G*Power, R's pwr and statsmodels compute it; N is the smallest whole number that reaches the target power. α must be between 0 and 1 (type 0.05 or 5%, not 5).
  • For a very small effect the required N can exceed 1,000,000, and is shown as such.

Cohen's conventions: d ≈ 0.2 is a small effect, 0.5 medium, 0.8 large. They are for d and dav, not dz: the SD of the differences shrinks as the two conditions correlate, so dz equals dav at r = 0.5, is larger above it and smaller below it. Enter an independent-groups or average-SD effect under Magnitude with the expected r, and dz from paired pilot data under Consistency; entering an independent-groups d as dz misstates the N needed.

References

Cohen J (1988). Statistical Power Analysis for the Behavioral Sciences, 2nd edition. Hillsdale, NJ: Lawrence Erlbaum Associates.

Keyboard Shortcuts

The Keyboard Shortcuts menu in the menu bar lists the zoom, inspector-navigation, grouping and signal-processing commands below, plus Focus Review Sweeps Field, so you can also click them. Its last item, See All Keyboard Shortcuts, opens this page. A few keys do something different when a text editor or the Data Log has the focus; those are noted in the Text Tools table.

File

⌘O Open File…

⌘N New Data Importer window: a drop-zone for any data or image file, grouped by kind

⇧⌘N New Text Editor window

⌥⌘N New Spreadsheet window

⇧⌘G Graph Selected Text: graph highlighted numbers from the Data Log or any text editor

⇧⌘H Chart Selected Text: chart highlighted table from the Data Log or any text editor

⌥⌘L Volcano Plot from Selected Text (File → Omics Plot): build a volcano plot from highlighted data (replicates, or computed fold changes and p-values)

⌥⌘M Heatmap from Selected Text (File → Omics Plot): build a heatmap from a highlighted table

⇧⌘V Smart Visualization (Text Editor and Data Log): opens the selected text as whichever kind of Graph or Chart suits it

⌘S Save: a .@graph document for Graph, Chart, Spreadsheet and Image windows; plain text for Text Editor and Data Log windows

⇧⌘S Save As… (works in all window types)

⇧⌘K Duplicate: open a copy of the window with the same data (Graph, Chart, Spreadsheet, Text Editor and Image windows; the Data Log's copy opens in a Text Editor)

⇧⌘E Export…: Graph, Chart and Image windows as an image; Spreadsheet windows as CSV, TSV or Excel

⌘P Print the front window

⇧⌘P Page Setup… for the front window

⌘W Close window (asks to save unsaved changes)

⇧⌘W Close Window: the front window with all its tabs (⌘W closes one tab)

⌥⌘W Close All: close every window

⌘Q Quit. If anything is unsaved, choose Discard Changes and Quit, Cancel, or Review Changes… (the default), which brings up each unsaved window in turn with Save / Don't Save / Cancel

Dbl-click title bar Rename the window: edit the title in place, then press Return to confirm or Escape to cancel

⌥⇧-click on plot Log the selected sweep's value at that X position ('x = … y = …', in the axes' units, Y junction-potential corrected) to the Data Log; on X-Y data, the point nearest the click. With no sweep selected, it logs the pointer's position. Needs 'Log routine file and window activity' on in Settings → General. Not available on a spike-train raster.

Double-click-to-rename works in Graph, Chart, Image, Spreadsheet, Text Editor and Statistics windows, including those opened from a chart. A Text Editor with a file renames the file too.

If you rename or move a saved file in the Finder while it is open in @Graph (Graph, Chart, Image, Spreadsheet or Text Editor window), the window's title and save location follow it automatically.

Settings (⌘,)

Open Settings from the @Graph menu (⌘,) to set app-wide defaults. The window has seven tabs: General, Appearance, Neurophysiology, Imaging, Export, Statistics and AI Assistant. Most settings apply to new windows, and the exceptions are noted.

General

  • Restore last session on launch (off by default): reopens each window that was open with a file when you quit: a graph from its .@graph file or its recording, and charts, images, spreadsheets and text files from theirs. Windows never saved to a file (results, new charts) aren't reopened.
  • Show start-up window at launch (on by default): shows the Welcome window at every launch. This is the same setting as the 'Show this window at launch' checkbox in the Welcome window.
  • Show sample data buttons (on by default): shows or hides the Welcome window's Sample Graph, Sample Chart and Sample T-Series buttons.

Data Log

  • Default font size (13 pt; 9–24 pt): the Data Log's text size. With the Data Log in front, ⌘= and ⌘− change it for the session, and ⌘0 returns to this value.
  • Timestamp each log entry (off by default): prefixes every Data Log entry with the time (HH:MM:SS).
  • Include filename header on file open (on by default): adds a line with the file name and recording details to the Data Log when a file opens. This is independent of routine logging.
  • Log routine file and window activity (on by default): when off, routine Data Log entries (files opened, saved or exported, styles copied or pasted, cursor readings) are left out. Analysis results, errors and warnings are always logged.

File Handling

  • Opening Files: 'Open as tabs (same window)' (the default: new files join the front window as tabs) or 'Open as separate windows'.
  • Daughter Files: 'Open as tabs (in the parent window)' (the default) or 'Open as separate windows', for result windows made from an existing Graph or Chart window, such as a Venn Diagram from a Volcano Plot, or a Spectrogram, Cross-Correlation, Histogram or Slow AHP Analysis result.

Appearance: App

  • App appearance: Dark (default), Light or System Default, for menus, panels and windows. Takes effect immediately, and the View menu's Dark Mode, Light Mode and System Default items set it too. The graph background is set separately (Appearance → Graphs).
  • Show a line under the front tab (on by default): a thin line in your accent color under the front tab of a window with tabs, also when the window is in the background, where the front tab otherwise looks much like the others.

Appearance: Font

Settings → Appearance → Font sets the default font. New graphs and charts use it for titles, axis labels, tick labels, legends, scale bars and pie labels, and titles stay bold. Text you add to a chart or graph starts in its tick label font. Graphs and charts you already have keep their fonts; change those in the inspector.

Appearance: Numbers

Settings → Appearance → Numbers sets how graphs and charts write their numbers. Match System Region (the default) uses the decimal and thousands separators of your region in System Settings → General → Language & Region, so a German Mac shows 1,5. Always 1,234.5 keeps the point and comma whatever the region. It applies to tick labels, scale bars, legends and statistics labels in open and new windows, on screen and in exported figures. Negative numbers always use a true minus sign (−). Spreadsheets, the Data Log and exported data keep plain numbers.

Appearance: Graphs

These defaults apply to new graph windows. Open windows are unchanged unless noted.

  • Dark background: new graph windows open with a black (on) or white (off) background. Changing it also updates open graph windows.
  • Sweep color palette (Rainbow by default): the colors overlaid sweeps take while a graph's 'Use … spectrum for overlaid sweeps' option (Plot Style) is on: Rainbow (red through the spectrum to purple), Warm (red → orange), Cool (cyan → blue), Monochrome (white → gray) or Viridis (purple → blue → green → yellow). Open graphs change at once.
  • Default sweep line width (1 pt) and Default axis line width (2.5 pt), each 0.5–5 pt.
  • Default sweep line color / Default sweep symbol color: the colors a new sweep starts with when the spectrum option is off. While it is on, the palette above decides. Reset returns to the built-in color.
  • Default sweep symbol (None by default): the marker shape new sweeps start with, from the same list as the Plot Style panel.

Appearance: Charts

  • Default chart axis line width (1.5 pt; 0.5–5 pt): axis line thickness for new charts.
  • Default bar/chart color and Default data point color: the bar (mean) color and data-point color of the first series in a new chart, however it is created (including by the AI Assistant, unless you ask it for a specific color). Further series use @Graph's built-in palette so they stay distinct. Reset returns to the built-in color (blue), and the data points then match the bars.
  • Default data point symbol (Circle by default): the marker shape for a new chart's data points. To change it on one chart, see Data Point Symbol under Charts.

Default effect-size brackets and error bar type for new charts are in Settings → Statistics → New Chart Defaults (below).

Neurophysiology

  • Enable JP correction by default (off by default): new graph windows open with junction potential correction on, and the JP Correction button in the status bar highlighted.
  • Default JP value (12 mV; −100 to 100 mV): the junction potential new windows start with, whether or not correction starts on. It's subtracted only from channels that look like a membrane potential. Typical patch-clamp values are 5–15 mV, and a liquid junction potential calculator (such as JPCalc) gives the value for your solutions.
  • Default dV/dt threshold (20 mV/ms, the Allen Institute IPFX's cutoff): the rate-of-rise threshold the AP Detection panel, Batch Processing and Protocol steps start with. Typical values are 10–50 mV/ms, and you can change it per experiment in the panel.
  • Default threshold method: the method AP Detection opens with, and the one new Batch Processing and Protocols steps start with.
  • Input Resistance (VI and IV Plots): fit the line through the origin, allow for noise with 5 or 6 steps (VI Plot only), and flag outlier sweeps with a robust fit. All off by default; see VI Plot under E-Phys Tools.

Imaging

  • Default Hardware: the camera and objective the Microscopy Calculator opens with. Choosing others in the Calculator leaves these defaults as they are.
  • Color Cameras: how a color camera's exposure (ND2, CZI) opens. One channel (the default): its red, green and blue added together, shown in the color that holds its signal, or in gray when none does (DIC, brightfield). Red, green and blue channels: its three colors separately, for a stained brightfield image whose colors are the data. Applies to files opened afterward.

Export

  • Default format (PNG by default): PNG, JPEG, TIFF, GIF, PDF or SVG, the format selected when the Export panel opens (⇧⌘E). PDF and SVG are vector formats; the others are raster.
  • Resolution (raster formats): 72, 96, 144, 192, 288, 300 or 600 dpi. 144 dpi (2×, the default) suits screen and web, 300 dpi photographs and color figures in print, and 600 dpi line art. Vector formats are resolution-independent.
  • Default canvas size (1200 × 800): the width × height the Export panel starts with, in points (1/72 inch, so pixels at 72 dpi), from 1 to 20,000. Copying a chart uses this size and resolution too.
  • Transparent background (off by default): PNG, TIFF and GIF exports of graphs and charts get a see-through background instead of the background color, handy for placing figures on slides. JPEG can't be transparent.
  • In the Export panel itself, 'Match window' sets the canvas to the on-screen graph's size and proportions, and the Resolution menu (72 to 1,200 dpi) sharpens an image without changing the proportions.

For publication figures, export PDF or SVG (vector, sharp at any size), or PNG or TIFF at 300 dpi (600 for line art). Use 'Match window' to keep the on-screen proportions; a chart's Journal width menu sets a journal's column width.

Statistics

These settings change how statistical results are computed or reported, so they're kept together in their own tab, where they're easy to find and check.

  • Default confidence level: 90%, 95% (default) or 99%. Applies to every confidence interval @Graph reports, closed-form and bootstrap: descriptive statistics, the estimate and effect size of every Compare choice, post-hoc comparisons (Tukey, Games-Howell, Dunnett, Dunnett's T3, Šidák, Bonferroni, Holm), Multiple Regression coefficients, correlation (Pearson, Spearman, Kendall), McNemar/Fisher exact/relative-risk tables, every interval on an Estimation Plot and Kaplan-Meier hazard-ratio CIs. With 3 or more groups, pairwise CIs are simultaneous at 1 − α/m. The variance-explained effect sizes (η², partial η², ω², Cohen's f and R²) get a 1 − 2α interval, 90% at the default 95% (Steiger 2004), so each excludes zero exactly when its F test is significant. It also sets the level of a chart's CI error bars and the new-chart default below. The AP phase plot's band is the one exception and stays at 95%. Table headers and notes (such as "95% CI") show the active level.
  • Bootstrap CI method: BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated, the default and recommended) or Percentile (choose it only to reproduce results made with it). It applies to every confidence interval that is bootstrapped because no formula fits: AKP (Algina–Keselman–Penfield) robust d (Trimmed means), dav/gav for paired means, the Estimation Plot's intervals for means and dominance δ (its medians and trimmed means use the smoothed bootstrap's percentile interval), Kendall's W for Cochran's Q, Cramér's V, Cohen's w, and Spearman/Kendall correlation. Test-based intervals (t, noncentral t and F, Yuen, the rank-inversion Hodges-Lehmann CI, Clopper-Pearson) are never bootstrapped. The setting applies the next time an analysis runs, and labels show '(BCa bootstrap)' or '(Percentile bootstrap)'. See 'Which bootstrap?' under Statistics → Confidence Intervals.
  • Warn on small-sample bootstrap CIs (on by default; 'Warn below n =' 5, from 3 to 20): adds a note to a Statistics window result table when one of those bootstrap CIs is built from fewer values per group or pair than the threshold. The default is below the 6–12 per group typical of routine work, so it rarely appears. The note is a reminder that the interval is imprecise, not a sign that it is wrong.
  • Always show effect sizes as positive (off by default): effect sizes are normally signed (negative when the first group is lower). When on, a result-table row with a negative estimate is restated the other way round: the group names are swapped and the estimate, its CI, the test statistic and the effect size are all given for the reversed order, never just the sign of the effect size flipped. A chart bracket, which has no direction, shows its effect size and CI for the order that makes them positive; Estimation Plot labels stay signed as plotted (later minus earlier group). It also applies to Cohen's g and ln(odds ratio) on proportions charts. η², ω², Cramér's V and Kendall's W are never changed.

New Chart Defaults

These only set what a new chart starts with, and you can change them on any chart.

  • Effect-size brackets: Show effect size, Show p-value and Show test name (all on by default), and Effect size position (Above by default) and p-value position (Below by default) relative to the bracket line. These match the Display and Position controls under Brackets in a chart's Statistics tab.
  • Default error bar type (SD by default): None, SD, SEM, or a confidence interval at the Default confidence level above, for every series of a new chart. Change an existing chart's error bars in its Plot Style panel.

AI Assistant

  • Enable AI Assistant (off by default): turns on the AI Assistant window and menu (⌥⌘I). You're still asked for consent the first time you send data to each AI provider.
  • Default Backend: the Backend the AI Assistant starts with, and its Model when it offers more than one. Changing it doesn't change a conversation already open. Until you choose one, it's Apple Private Cloud Compute, which is free, on a Mac that can use it (macOS 27 or later with Apple Intelligence), and Claude otherwise.
  • API Keys: shows which backends have a saved key, with a Clear button for each. You enter a key in the AI Assistant window the first time you use that backend. Keys are kept in the macOS Keychain and sent only to that backend's own service.
  • Attach window screenshots automatically (off by default): when the AI Assistant works with a window, it also receives a picture of that window, not just the numbers.
  • Reset Remembered Data-Sharing Consent…: the next request to each backend asks for consent again.
